他几乎是魂飞魄散地驰回家去。来了。斧子就要落下了，兄长到底要被逼得开口了。到了佩纳罗，一记新的震击又等着他：老尼古拉斯告诉他，奥利弗爵士不在家，骑马往戈多尔芬府去了。

莱昂内尔被恐怖揿着，立时得出结论：消息已经到了兄长耳朵里，他立刻行动了；他想不出兄长还能为别的什么事去戈多尔芬府。

他这份疑心其实是白担。奥利弗爵士实在是不堪再这样熬下去，才骑马去把那件证据摆到罗莎蒙德面前——那证据他早留心备下了。如今拿出去，已不怕伤着莱昂内尔了。然而他这一趟全然白跑。她一口回绝不见他；他拿出全然不合他性子的低声下气，求一个仆人替他把一封十万火急的信再递回去，还是吃了闭门羹。他带着创伤回到佩纳罗，却见弟弟正焦躁万状地等着他。

“怎么样？”莱昂内尔迎上去。“现在你打算怎么办？”

奥利弗爵士从眼睑下看他，眉宇阴沉地锁着，映出他的心思。

“怎么办？你说什么？”他道。

“你还没听说？”莱昂内尔便把消息说了。

奥利弗爵士听完，久久盯着他，随即嘴唇一绷，一巴掌拍在额头上。

“原来如此！”他喊。“她拒不见我，莫非就为这个？她莫非以为我去是求告的？她能这样想？她能？”

他大步走到壁炉边，用靴子忿忿地拨着柴。“哦！这也太下作了。可这必定是她的手笔，这桩事。”

“那你怎么办？”莱昂内尔追着问，压不住心头最上头的那桩问题；他的声音发颤。

“怎么办？”奥利弗爵士越过肩头看他。“戳破这个泡，老天作证！

===Original===

Make an end of it for them, confound them and cover them with shame.”
He said it roughly, angrily, and Lionel recoiled, deeming that roughness and anger aimed at himself. He sank into a chair, his knees loosened by his sudden fear. So it seemed that he had had more than cause for his apprehensions. This brother of his who boasted such affection for him was not equal to bearing this matter through. And yet the thing was so unlike Oliver that a doubt still lingered with him.
“You﻿ ﻿… you will tell them the truth?” he said, in small, quavering voice.
Sir Oliver turned and considered him more attentively.
“A God’s name, Lal, what’s in thy mind now?” he asked, almost roughly. “Tell them the truth? Why, of course﻿—but only as it concerns myself. You’re not supposing that I shall tell them it was you? You’ll not be accounting me capable of that?”
“What other way is there?”
Sir Oliver explained the matter. The explanation brought Lionel relief. But this relief was ephemeral. Further reflection presented a new fear to him. It came to him that if Sir Oliver cleared himself, of necessity his own implication must follow. His terrors very swiftly magnified a risk that in itself was so slender as to be entirely negligible. In his eyes it ceased to be a risk; it became a certain and inevitable danger. If Sir Oliver put forward this proof that the trail of blood had not proceeded from himself, it must, thought Lionel, inevitably be concluded that it was his own. As well might Sir Oliver tell them the whole truth, for surely they could not fail to infer it. Thus he reasoned in his terror, accounting himself lost irrevocably.
Had he but gone with those fears of his to his brother, or had he but been able to abate them sufficiently to allow reason to prevail, he must have been brought to understand how much further they carried him than was at all justified by probability. Oliver would have shown him this, would have told him that with the collapsing of the charge against himself no fresh charge could be levelled against any there, that no scrap of suspicion had ever attached to Lionel, or ever could. But Lionel dared not seek his brother in this matter. In his heart he was ashamed of his fears; in his heart he knew himself for a craven. He realized to the full the hideousness of his selfishness, and yet, as before, he was not strong enough to conquer it.

叫他们收场，叫他们乱作一团、无地自容。”

他说得粗暴、忿怒，莱昂内尔直往后缩，只当那粗暴忿怒是冲他来的。他跌坐进椅子，双膝被这突如其来的恐惧泡软了。看来他的忧惧果然不是空穴来风。这位口口声声疼他的兄长，扛不动这桩事了。可这又太不像奥利弗的为人，他心里到底还悬着一丝疑。

“你……你会把真相告诉他们？”他说，声音又小又颤。

奥利弗爵士转过身，更留意地端详他。

“老天在上，拉尔，你这会儿又打的什么主意？”他问，口气近乎粗暴。“告诉他们真相？啊，当然——但只说与我相关的部分。你总不会以为，我会告诉他们那是你干的吧？你总不至于把我看成那种人？”

“那还有什么别的法子？”

奥利弗爵士把事情讲了。这番解释让莱昂内尔松了口气。可这松快转瞬即逝。再一寻思，新的恐惧又来了。他想到：奥利弗爵士若洗清了自己，他自己的干系势必跟着暴露。这风险本细弱得全然可以不计，他的恐怖却飞快把它放大。在他眼里它不再是风险，而成了一种确定无疑、势所必至的危险。奥利弗爵士若拿出证据，证明那血迹并非出自他身，人们——莱昂内尔想——势必推断那是他自己的。这同把全部真相告诉他们有什么两样，因为人们不可能推不出来。他在恐怖里这样推理，认定自己无可挽回地完了。

他若是带着这份恐惧去找兄长，或是能把恐惧压一压、让理性说话，兄长本会让他明白：他把事情推到了概率全然担不出的远处。奥利弗会指给他看：对自己的指控一倒，谁也别想再往在场任何人头上立新的指控；从没有一丝疑影沾过莱昂内尔，也永远沾不上。可莱昂内尔不敢为这事去寻兄长。他心里为他的恐惧害臊；他心里清楚自己是个懦夫。他全然明白自己的自私有多丑恶，然而一如既往，他没有力气制服它。

===Original===

In short, his love of himself was greater than his love of his brother, or of twenty brothers.
The morrow﻿—a blustering day of late March found him again at that alehouse at Penycumwick in the company of Jasper Leigh. A course had occurred to him, as the only course now possible. Last night his brother had muttered something of going to Killigrew with his proofs since Rosamund refused to receive him. Through Killigrew he would reach her, he had said; and he would yet see her on her knees craving his pardon for the wrong she had done him, for the cruelty she had shown him.
Lionel knew that Killigrew was absent from home just then; but he was expected to return by Easter, and to Easter there was but a week. Therefore he had little time in which to act, little time in which to execute the project that had come into his mind. He cursed himself for conceiving it, but held to it with all the strength of a weak nature.
Yet when he came to sit face to face with Jasper Leigh in that little inn-parlour with the scrubbed table of plain deal between them, he lacked the courage to set his proposal forth. They drank sherry sack stiffly laced with brandy by Lionel’s suggestion, instead of the more customary mulled ale. Yet not until he had consumed the best part of a pint of it did Lionel feel himself heartened to broaching his loathsome business. Through his head hummed the words his brother had said some time ago when first the name of Jasper Leigh had passed between them﻿—“a desperate adventurer ripe for anything. So the price be high enough you may buy him body and soul.” Money enough to buy Jasper Leigh was ready to Lionel’s hand; but it was Sir Oliver’s money﻿—the money that was placed at Lionel’s disposal by his half-brother’s openhanded bounty. And this money he was to employ for Oliver’s utter ruin! He cursed himself for a filthy, contemptible hound; he cursed the foul fiend that whispered such suggestions into his mind; he knew himself, despised himself and reviled himself until he came to swear to be strong and to go through with whatever might await him sooner than be guilty of such a baseness; the next moment that same resolve would set him shuddering again as he viewed the inevitable consequences that must attend it.
Suddenly the captain set him a question, very softly, that fired the train and blew all his lingering self-resistance into shreds.

===Chinese===

一句话，他爱自己，胜过爱他的兄长，胜过爱二十个兄长。

次日——三月末一个狂风大作的日子——他又坐在佩尼坎威克那家酒馆里，同贾斯珀·利作伴。一条路子已在他脑中成形，是如今唯一行得通的路子。昨夜兄长咕哝过什么要带着证据去见基利格鲁，既然罗莎蒙德拒不接待。他说要通过基利格鲁够到她；还说迟早要看见她跪下来，求他饶恕她冤他的罪、给他的狠心。

莱昂内尔知道基利格鲁眼下不在家；可他复活节前该回来，离复活节只有一个星期。因此他没多少时间行动，没多少时间施展心里冒出来的那个计划。他咒自己竟起这样的念头，却又以一副软弱天性的全部力气抓住不放。

可真到了那间小客栈的雅座里，同贾斯珀·利面对面坐下，中间隔一张擦得干干净净的白松桌，他又没了提出那件事的勇气。按莱昂内尔的主意，他们喝的是雪利白葡萄酒兑足白兰地，而不是更惯常的热麦酒。可直灌下大半品脱，莱昂内尔才觉得自己提得起精神，敢开那门腌臜的营生。他脑子里嗡嗡响着兄长前些时候说过的话——那还是贾斯珀·利这名字头一回在他们中间提起的时候——“一个亡命之徒，什么都敢接。只要价钱够高，你能把他连人带魂买下。”买下贾斯珀·利的钱，莱昂内尔手边就是；可那是奥利弗爵士的钱——是异母兄长慷慨大方地交由他支配的钱。而他要拿这钱，去办奥利弗的彻底毁灭！他咒自己是条肮脏下作的狗；咒那个往他心里灌这种主意的恶鬼；他认清自己、作践自己、痛骂自己，直骂到自己发誓要硬起来，无论等着他的是什么，也硬着头皮干到底，免得犯这样的卑污；可转念间，一想到必然的后果，同一个决心又叫他发起抖来。

忽然，船长轻轻抛过来一个问题，点着了引信，把他残存的一点自我抵抗炸了个粉碎。

===Original===

“You’ll ha’ borne my warning to Sir Oliver?” he asked, lowering his voice so as not to be overheard by the vintner who was stirring beyond the thin wooden partition.
Master Lionel nodded, nervously fingering the jewel in his ear, his eyes shifting from their consideration of the seaman’s coarse, weather-tanned and hairy countenance.
“I did,” he said. “But Sir Oliver is headstrong. He will not stir.”
“Will he not?” The captain stroked his bushy red beard and cursed profusely and horribly after the fashion of the sea. “Od’s wounds! He’s very like to swing if he bides him here.”
“Ay,” said Lionel, “if he bides.” He felt his mouth turn dry as he spoke; his heart thudded, but its thuds were softened by a slight insensibility which the liquor had produced in him.
He uttered the words in so curious a tone that the sailor’s dark eyes peered at him from under his heavy sandy eyebrows. There was alert inquiry in that glance. Master Lionel got up suddenly.
“Let us take a turn outside, captain,” said he.
The captain’s eyes narrowed. He scented business. There was something plaguily odd about this young gentleman’s manner. He tossed off the remains of his sack, slapped down the pot and rose.
“Your servant, Master Tressilian,” said he.
Outside our gentleman untethered his horse from the iron ring to which he had attached the bridle; leading his horse he turned seaward and strode down the road that wound along the estuary towards Smithick.
A sharp breeze from the north was whipping the water into white peaks of foam; the sky was of a hard brightness and the sun shone brilliantly. The tide was running out, and the rock in the very neck of the haven was thrusting its black crest above the water. A cable’s length this side of it rode the black hull and naked spars of the *Swallow*﻿—Captain Leigh’s ship.
Lionel stepped along in silence, very gloomy and pensive, hesitating even now. And the crafty mariner reading this hesitation, and anxious to conquer it for the sake of such profit as he conceived might lie in the proposal which he scented, paved the way for him at last.
“I think that ye’ll have some matter to propose to me,” said he slyly. “Out with it, sir, for there never was a man more ready to serve you.”
“The fact is,” said Lionel, watching the other’s face with a sidelong glance, “I am in a difficult position, Master Leigh.”

莱昂内尔少爷点点头，手指紧张地捻着耳环，眼睛躲开对那张水手粗砺、黝黑、多毛的脸的打量。

“带到了。”他说。“可奥利弗爵士倔得很。他不肯动。”

“他不肯？”船长捋着他浓密的红胡子，照海上的做派痛痛快快、不堪入耳地咒骂了一通。“伤了他的皮！他再待下去，保准得去荡秋千。”

“是啊，”莱昂内尔说，“只要他待着。”说这话时他觉得嘴里发干；心口咚咚跳，只是酒劲带来的一点麻木把跳声压软了。

他说这话的调门那样古怪，水手的黑眼睛从浓密的红眉毛底下眯起来打量他。那一瞟里有警觉的探问。莱昂内尔少爷猛地站起来。

“咱们出去遛遛吧，船长。”他说。

船长的眼睛眯了眯。他嗅到了买卖。这位年轻少爷的做派蹊跷得很。他把余下的酒一饮而尽，啪地放下酒壶，站起身。

“听您差遣，特雷西利安少爷。”他说。

到了外头，我们的少爷从铁环上解开缰绳——方才他把马缰拴在那环上；他牵着马，转朝海的方向，沿河湾边那条蜿蜒向史密蒂克去的路大步走下去。

北面来的疾风把水面抽出一峰峰白沫；天穹是一种硬亮，太阳照得耀眼。潮水正往外退，港湾咽喉处那块礁石的黑顶探出水面。离它一链之内，泊着黑船身、光桅杆的“燕子号”——利船长的船。

莱昂内尔闷头走，满面阴沉忧郁，直到此刻还在打摆子。狡猾的水手读出了这份犹疑，又为他嗅出的这桩买卖里可能埋着的利头着急，索性替他把路铺平了。

“我寻思，您有桩什么事要同我提。”他狡黠地说。“抖出来吧，少爷，天底下再没有比我更乐意效劳的人了。”

“实情是，”莱昂内尔说，斜眼觑着对方的脸，“我的处境为难，利船长。”

===Original===

“I’ve been in a many,” laughed the captain, “but never yet in one through which I could not win. Strip forth your own, and haply I can do as much for you as I am wont to do for myself.”
“Why, it is this wise,” said the other. “My brother will assuredly hang as you have said if he bides him here. He is lost if they bring him to trial. And in that case, faith, I am lost too. It dishonours a man’s family to have a member of it hanged. ’Tis a horrible thing to have happen.”
“Indeed, indeed!” the sailor agreed encouragingly.
“I would abstract him from this,” pursued Lionel, and at the same time cursed the foul fiend that prompted him such specious words to cloak his villainy. “I would abstract him from it, and yet ’tis against my conscience that he should go unpunished for I swear to you, Master Leigh, that I abhor the deed﻿—a cowardly, murderous deed!”
“Ah!” said the captain. And lest that grim ejaculation should check his gentleman he made haste to add﻿—“To be sure! To be sure!”
Master Lionel stopped and faced the other squarely, his shoulders to his horse. They were quite alone in as lonely a spot as any conspirator could desire. Behind him stretched the empty beach, ahead of him the ruddy cliffs that rise gently to the wooded heights of Arwenack.
“I’ll be quite plain and open with you, Master Leigh. Peter Godolphin was my friend. Sir Oliver is no more than my half-brother. I would give a deal to the man who would abstract Sir Oliver secretly from the doom that hangs over him, and yet do the thing in such a way that Sir Oliver should not thereby escape the punishment he deserves.”
It was strange, he thought, even as he said it, that he could bring his lips so glibly to utter words that his heart detested.
The captain looked grim. He laid a finger upon Master Lionel’s velvet doublet in line with that false heart of his.
“I am your man,” said he. “But the risk is great. Yet ye say that ye’ld give a deal.﻿ ﻿…”
“Yourself shall name the price,” said Lionel quickly, his eyes burning feverishly, his cheeks white.
“Oh I can contrive it, never fear,” said the captain. “I know to a nicety what you require. How say you now: if I was to carry him overseas to the plantations where they lack toilers of just such thews as his?” He lowered his voice and spoke with some slight hesitation, fearing that he proposed perhaps more than his prospective employer might desire.

===Chinese===

“我陷过不知多少回，”船长笑道，“可还没有一回闯不过去的。把您那桩亮出来吧，少爷，兴许我能为您办成我惯常为自己办成的事。”

“咳，是这样，”对方说。“我兄长要是留在这儿，真就照您说的得上绞架了。一旦开审，他就完了。真到那一步，说实话，我也完了。家里出了个绞死的人，是要辱没门庭的。那样的事，想想都可怕。”

“正是，正是！”水手打着气附和。

“我想把他从这事里摘出去，”莱昂内尔往下说，一面在心里咒那怂恿他、给他这类体面话遮丑的恶鬼。“我想把他摘出去——可又不能让他逍遥于惩罚之外，因为我对您发誓，利船长，我痛恨那桩事——一桩怯懦的、凶杀的事！”

“啊！”船长说。他生怕这声冷哼吓住这位少爷，赶紧添上一句——“自然！自然！”

莱昂内尔少爷停下脚，背靠着马，同对方正面相对。此处幽僻之极，正合一切密谋者的心意。他身后铺展着空荡荡的沙滩，前方是赭红的崖壁，缓缓升向阿文纳克的林莽高地。

“我跟您摊开说吧，利船长。彼得·戈多尔芬是我的朋友。奥利弗爵士不过是我异母的兄长。谁能把奥利弗爵士从悬头顶的厄运里悄悄摘出去，同时又办得叫他不致逃脱他应得的惩罚，我就肯给谁一大笔。”

真奇怪，他一边说一边想，那些他心里深恶的话，唇舌竟说得这样溜。

船长的脸色严峻起来。他伸出一根指头，点在莱昂内尔少爷的天鹅绒紧身上衣上，正对那颗黑心所在的位置。

“我就是您的人。”他说。“只是干系重大。可您方才说，肯出一大笔。……”

“价钱您自己开。”莱昂内尔急急地说，眼睛烧着热病似的光，脸颊发白。

“哦，我有法子办妥，别怕。”船长说。“您要的东西，我知道得一清二楚。这么说吧：要是我把他运到海外种植园去，那边正缺他这号筋骨的苦力？”他放低声音，略带迟疑地说，怕自己开的价超出了这位主顾的胃口。

===Original===

“He might return,” was the answer that dispelled all doubts on that score.
“Ah!” said the skipper. “What o’ the Barbary rovers, then! They lack slaves and are ever ready to trade, though they be niggardly payers. I never heard of none that returned once they had him safe aboard their galleys. I ha’ done some trading with them, bartering human freights for spices and eastern carpets and the like.”
Master Lionel breathed hard. “ ’Tis a horrible fate, is’t not?”
The captain stroked his beard. “Yet ’tis the only really safe bestowal, and when all is said ’tis not so horrible as hanging, and certainly less dishonouring to a man’s kin. Ye’ld be serving Sir Oliver and yourself.”
“ ’Tis so, ’tis so,” cried Master Lionel almost fiercely. “And the price?”
The seaman shifted on his short, sturdy legs, and his face grew pensive. “A hundred pound?” he suggested tentatively.
“Done with you for a hundred pounds,” was the prompt answer﻿—so prompt that Captain Leigh realized he had driven a fool’s bargain which it was incumbent upon him to amend.
“That is, a hundred pound for myself,” he corrected slowly. “Then there be the crew to reckon for﻿—to keep their counsel and lend a hand; ’twill mean another hundred at the least.”
Master Lionel considered a moment. “It is more than I can lay my hands on at short notice. But, look you, you shall have a hundred and fifty pounds in coin and the balance in jewels. You shall not be the loser in that, I promise you. And when you come again, and bring me word that all is done as you now undertake there shall be the like again.”
Upon that the bargain was settled. And when Lionel came to talk of ways and means he found that he had allied himself to a man who understood his business thoroughly. All the assistance that the skipper asked was that Master Lionel should lure his gentleman to some concerted spot conveniently near the waterside. There Leigh would have a boat and his men in readiness, and the rest might very safely be left to him.
In a flash Lionel bethought him of the proper place for this. He swung round, and pointed across the water to Trefusis Point and the grey pile of Godolphin Court all bathed in sunshine now.
“Yonder, at Trefusis Point in the shadow of Godolphin Court at eight tomorrow night, when there will be no moon. I’ll see that he is there. But on your life do not miss him.”
“Trust me,” said Master Leigh.

===Chinese===

“他兴许会回来。”这一句，把那点疑虑扫了个干净。

“啊！”船长说。“那巴巴里海盗呢！他们缺奴隶，随来做随交易，虽说出手抠门儿。可只要人一上他们的桨帆船，我还没听说有谁回来过。我跟他们做过几注买卖，拿人口换香料、东方地毯这一类。”

莱昂内尔少爷呼吸粗重起来。“那可是个可怕的命运，不是么？”

船长捋着胡子。“可也只有这一条真正稳妥的去处；再说到底，它总没有绞刑可怕，也肯定不至于那么辱没亲族。您这是成全奥利弗爵士，也是成全您自己。”

“正是，正是。”莱昂内尔少爷几乎是凶狠地喊。“价钱呢？”

水手在他短壮的腿上换了个重心，脸沉吟下来。“一百镑？”他试探着说。

“一百镑，成交。”回答来得飞快——快得贾斯珀·利船长立时明白自己做了桩蠢人的买卖，有义务修补。

“就是说，一百镑归我本人。”他慢吞吞地更正。“还有船上那伙人要打点——叫他们闭嘴、搭把手，怎么也还得再一百。”

莱昂内尔少爷想了想。“一时半会儿我拿不出这么多现钱。可这样吧，我付您一百五十镑现款，余数拿珠宝补足。这一头我担保您不吃亏。等您回来，捎话给我，说一切都照您现在应下的办妥了，另外还有同样的数目。”

买卖就这样成了。待到谈起办法和门路，莱昂内尔才发现，他结交的是个把行当摸得透熟的人。船长只提一样帮衬：请莱昂内尔少爷把他的老爷诱到一处约好的地方，要离水边近便。利在那里备一条船、埋伏好人手，其余的尽管放心交给他。

灵光一闪，莱昂内尔想起了那个合适的地方。他转过身，隔着水一指特雷富西斯角，指着如今正浴在阳光里的戈多尔芬府那一片灰色楼群。

“那边，特雷富西斯角，戈多尔芬府的影子底下，明晚八点，那时没有月亮。我保证他到。可你务必要截住他，脑袋押上也得截住。”

“信我。”利船长说。

===Original===

“And the money?”
“When you have him safely aboard come to me at Penarrow,” he replied, which showed that after all he did not trust Master Leigh any further than he was compelled.
The captain was quite satisfied. For should his gentleman fail to disburse he could always return Sir Oliver to shore.
On that they parted. Lionel mounted and rode away, whilst Master Leigh made a trumpet of his hands and hallooed to the ship.
As he stood waiting for the boat that came off to fetch him, a smile slowly overspread the adventurer’s rugged face. Had Master Lionel seen it he might have asked himself how far it was safe to drive such bargains with a rogue who kept faith only in so far as it was profitable. And in this matter Master Leigh saw a way to break faith with profit. He had no conscience, but he loved as all rogues love to turn the tables upon a superior rogue. He would play Master Lionel most finely, most poetically false; and he found a deal to chuckle over in the contemplation of it.

===Chinese===

“那钱呢？”

“等你把他平安弄上了船，到佩纳罗来找我。”他答——可见说到底，他对利船长的信任，也止于不得不信的那一点。

船长相当满意。这位少爷若到时赖账，他大可以把奥利弗爵士送回岸上便是。

两人就此分手。莱昂内尔上马驰去，利船长则拢起双手作个喇叭，朝那条船呼喊。

他立着等小艇来接，一道笑纹慢慢爬满这冒险家粗砺的脸。莱昂内尔少爷若是看见，兴许要自问：同一个守信用只守到有利可图为止的恶棍做这种买卖，到底能押多远。而在这桩事上，利船长瞧出了一条背信而又有利可图的路。他没有良心，却同一切恶棍一样，最爱以其人之道还治高于他的恶棍。他要漂漂亮亮地、富于诗意地耍莱昂内尔少爷一道；细细玩味着这件事，他有得乐了。

## V Lion of the Faith / 五 信士之狮
===Original===

Asad-ed-Din, the Lion of the Faith, Basha of Algiers, walked in the evening cool in the orchard of the Kasbah upon the heights above the city, and at his side, stepping daintily, came Fenzileh, his wife, the first lady of his harem, whom eighteen years ago he had carried off in his mighty arms from that little whitewashed village above the Straits of Messina which his followers had raided.
She had been a lissom maid of sixteen in those far-off days, the child of humble peasant-folk, and she had gone uncomplaining to the arms of her swarthy ravisher. Today, at thirty-four, she was still beautiful, more beautiful indeed than when first she had fired the passion of Asad-Reis﻿—as he then was, one of the captains of the famous Ali-Basha. There were streaks of red in her heavy black tresses, her skin was of a soft pearliness that seemed translucent, her eyes were large, of a golden-brown, agleam with sombre fires, her lips were full and sensuous. She was tall and of a shape that in Europe would have been accounted perfect, which is to say that she was a thought too slender for Oriental taste; she moved along beside her lord with a sinuous, languorous grace, gently stirring her fan of ostrich plumes. She was unveiled; indeed it was her immodest habit to go naked of face more often than was seemly, which is but the least of the many undesirable infidel ways which had survived her induction into the Faith of Islam﻿—a necessary step before Asad, who was devout to the point of bigotry, would consent to make her his wife. He had found her such a wife as it is certain he could never have procured at home; a woman who, not content to be his toy, the plaything of his idle hour, insinuated herself into affairs, demanded and obtained his confidences, and exerted over him much the same influence as the wife of a European prince might exert over her consort. In the years during which he had lain under the spell of her ripening beauty he had accepted the situation willingly enough; later, when he would have curtailed her interferences, it was too late; she had taken a firm grip of the reins, and Asad was in no better case than many a European husband﻿—an anomalous and outrageous condition this for a Basha of the Prophet’s House.

===Chinese===

信士之狮、阿尔及尔帕夏阿萨德-艾德-丁，在傍晚的凉意里散步，走在城池高处卡斯巴的果园里；在他身侧踏着碎步的是芬齐莱——他的妻子，后宫之首。十八年前，他的部众袭击墨西拿海峡上那座粉墙小村，是他用一双铁臂把她掳来的。

在那些遥远的岁月里，她是个十六岁的娉婷少女，寒门农家的女儿，一言不发地顺从了她那黝黑的掳掠者的怀抱。如今三十四岁，她依然美丽——实在比她初次点燃阿萨德-雷斯的欲火那时更美；那时他还只是赫赫有名的阿里-帕夏麾下的一名船长。她浓重的黑发里透着几绺红，肌肤是一种柔和的珍珠色，仿佛透亮；眼睛大，金褐色，燃着沉沉的光；嘴唇丰润而性感。她个子高挑，身段在欧洲要算十全十美——也就是说，照东方的口味还略嫌单薄了些；她伴随夫君款款而行，腰身婀娜慵懒，轻轻摇着一把鸵鸟毛扇子。她没有蒙面；不错，她有个不检点的习惯，动不动就裸着一张脸，比得体的次数多得多——这只是她入伊斯兰教后残存的诸多不像样的异教习气里最轻的一种；入教是必要的一步，不然阿萨德——他虔诚到近乎偏执——绝不肯娶她为妻。他发现她这样一位妻子，在家门里肯定是讨不到的：这女人不甘心只做他的玩物、消闲的摆设，她把手伸进事务里，讨要并得到了他的信任，在他身上施加的影响，同一个欧洲君主之妻施于其夫君的并无二致。在她日臻成熟的美的魔力之下那些年，他倒也情愿接受这个局面；后来想收束她的插手，已经迟了；她牢牢攥住了缰绳，阿萨德的处境不见得比多少欧洲丈夫更好——这对先知之家的帕夏来说，是一种不伦不类、大逆不道的境况。

===Original===

It was also a dangerous one for Fenzileh; for should the burden of her at any time become too heavy for her lord there was a short and easy way by which he could be rid of it. Do not suppose her so foolish as not to have realized this﻿—she realized it fully; but her Sicilian spirit was daring to the point of recklessness; her very dauntlessness which had enabled her to seize a control so unprecedented in a Muslim wife urged her to maintain it in the face of all risks.
Dauntless was she now, as she paced there in the cool of the orchard, under the pink and white petals of the apricots, the flaming scarlet of pomegranate blossoms, and through orange-groves where the golden fruit glowed and amid foliage of sombre green. She was at her eternal work of poisoning the mind of her lord against Sakr-el-Bahr, and in her maternal jealousy she braved the dangers of such an undertaking, fully aware of how dear to the heart of Asad-ed-Din was that absent renegade corsair. It was this very affection of the Basha’s for his lieutenant that was the fomenter of her own hate of Sakr-el-Bahr, for it was an affection that transcended Asad’s love for his own son and hers, and it led to the common rumour that for Sakr-el-Bahr was reserved the high destiny of succeeding Asad in the Bashalik.
“I tell thee thou’rt abused by him, O source of my life.”
“I hear thee,” answered Asad sourly. “And were thine own hearing less infirm, woman, thou wouldst have heard me answer thee that thy words weigh for naught with me against his deeds. Words may be but a mask upon our thoughts; deeds are ever the expression of them. Bear thou that in mind, O Fenzileh.”
“Do I not bear in mind thine every word, O fount of wisdom?” she protested, and left him, as she often did, in doubt whether she fawned or sneered. “And it is his deeds I would have speak for him, not indeed my poor words and still less his own.”
“Then, by the head of Allah, let those same deeds speak, and be thou silent.”
The harsh tone of his reproof and the scowl upon his haughty face, gave her pause for a moment. He turned about.
“Come!” he said. “Soon it will be the hour of prayer.” And he paced back towards the yellow huddle of walls of the Kasbah that overtopped the green of that fragrant place.

===Chinese===

对芬齐莱来说，这也是一种危险的境况；因为她这副担子哪天要是压得夫君受不住了，他有一条又短又省事的路可以甩脱。别以为她蠢得想不到这一层——她全想到了；可她的西西里脾气胆大得近于亡命；正是那股叫她攥住这份穆斯林人妻闻所未闻的权柄的无所畏惧，又逼着她冒着一切风险也不撒手。

此刻她便是无所畏惧的，在果园的凉气里踱着，头顶是杏树粉白的花瓣、石榴花烧红的殷红，穿行在金果灼灼的橘林和黛绿的枝叶间。她在干她的千秋营生：往夫君耳朵里灌毒，败坏萨克埃尔-巴尔。凭着做母亲的妒心，她甘冒这门营生的凶险——她全知道，那个远行的叛教海盗在阿萨德-艾德-丁心里有多亲。偏偏帕夏对这副手的这份情，正是她恨萨克埃尔-巴尔的酵母；因为这情分胜过了阿萨德对自己亲生儿子、她的儿子的爱，于是四下里传开一种说法：接替阿萨德的帕夏之位那份高命，是留给萨克埃尔-巴尔的。

“我告诉你，你叫他欺瞒了，哦我生命的源头。”

“我听着呢。”阿萨德酸涩地答。“要是你自己的耳朵没这么背，婆娘，你早该听见我回你了：你的话在我这里斤两全无，他有他的事迹作抵。言语尽可以是我们心思的面具；事迹永远是我们心思的表白。把这个记牢，哦芬齐莱。”

“您哪句话我不是记牢的，哦智慧的泉源？”她抗议道，又像惯常那样留他一个人捉摸：她这话是媚还是讥。“我要替他说话的，正是他的事迹——不是我这几句可怜的话，更不是他自己那张嘴。”

“那么，以安拉的头起誓，就让那些事迹自己说去，你给我闭嘴。”

他叱责的口气那样重，傲慢的脸上阴云密布，一时堵得她没了话。他转过身去。

“来吧！”他说。“快到礼拜的时辰了。”他抬步往卡斯巴那一片高踞于这芬芳园林的葱茏之上的黄色墙群走去。

===Original===

He was a tall, gaunt man, stooping slightly at the shoulders under the burden of his years; but his eagle face was masterful, and some lingering embers of his youth still glowed in his dark eyes. Thoughtfully, with a jewelled hand, he stroked his long white beard; with the other he leaned upon her soft plump arm, more from habit than for support, for he was full vigorous still.
High in the blue overhead a lark burst suddenly into song, and from the depths of the orchard came a gentle murmur of doves as if returning thanks for the lessening of the great heat now that the sun was sinking rapidly towards the world’s edge and the shadows were lengthening.
Came Fenzileh’s voice again, more musical than either, yet laden with words of evil, poison wrapped in honey.
“O my dear lord, thou’rt angered with me now. Woe me! that never may I counsel thee for thine own glory as my heart prompts me, but I must earn thy coldness.”
“Abuse not him I love,” said the Basha shortly. “I have told thee so full oft already.”
She nestled closer to him, and her voice grew softer, more akin to the amorous cooing of the doves. “And do I not love thee, O master of my soul? Is there in all the world a heart more faithful to thee than mine? Is not thy life my life? Have not my days been all devoted to the perfecting of thine happiness? And wilt thou then frown upon me if I fear for thee at the hands of an intruder of yesterday?”
“Fear for me?” he echoed, and laughed jeeringly. “What shouldst thou fear for me from Sakr-el-Bahr?”
“What all believers must ever fear from one who is no true Muslim, from one who makes a mock and travesty of the True Faith that he may gain advancement.”
The Basha checked in his stride, and turned upon her angrily.
“May thy tongue rot, thou mother of lies!”
“I am as the dust beneath thy feet, O my sweet lord, yet am I not what thine heedless anger calls me.”
“Heedless?” quoth he. “Not heedless but righteous to hear one whom the Prophet guards, who is the very javelin of Islam against the breast of the unbeliever, who carries the scourge of Allah against the infidel Frankish pigs, so maligned by thee! No more, I say! Lest I bid thee make good thy words, and pay the liar’s price if thou shouldst fail.”
“And should I fear the test?” she countered, nothing daunted. “I tell thee, O father of Marzak, that I should hail it gladly. Why, hear me now.

===Chinese===

他是个高瘦的男人，被岁月压得双肩微微前俯；可那张鹰隼似的脸依然威严，昏黑的眼睛里还有几星青春的余烬在燃。他若有所思地用一只缀珠宝的手捋着长长的白须；另一只手搭在她的软胖的胳膊上——多半出于习惯，不为搀扶，因为他仍十分硬朗。

高高的蓝天上，一只云雀忽然放歌；果园深处传来鸽子柔柔的咕哝，仿佛在感谢暑气渐消——太阳正迅速沉向天边，影子越拉越长。

芬齐莱的声音又响了，比这两样都悦耳，装的却是恶毒的话，裹了蜜的毒。

“哦我亲爱的大人，你现在恼我了。苦哇！我心里想为您争荣光，出个主意，反倒要领受您的冷脸。”

“不许作践我爱的人。”帕夏简短地说。“这话我不知说过多少遍了。”

她偎得更近，声音更软，同鸽子的咕鸣更相像了。“我难道不爱你么，哦我灵魂的主宰？普天之下，还有哪颗心比我这颗更忠于你？你的命难道不是我的命？我的日子难道不都用在成全你的幸福上？那么，我若害怕一个昨日才冒出来的外人对你下手，你便要对我皱眉头么？”

“怕我？”他跟着念，讥诮地笑了。“你能怕萨克埃尔-巴尔把我怎么样？”

“怕的是一切信士对一个假穆斯林永远要怕的事——一个把正教当儿戏、当幌子，只为往上爬的人。”

帕夏脚下顿住，忿忿地转向她。

“愿你舌头烂掉，你这谎言之母！”

“我是您脚下的尘土，哦我甜美的大人，可我不是您轻率的怒气所骂的那种人。”

“轻率？”他说。“不是轻率，是公道，听人这样受你糟蹋——他受先知庇佑，他是伊斯兰扎进不信者胸膛的那杆标枪，他替安拉掌着鞭子，抽不信教的法兰克猪猡——却被你说成这样！够了，我说！不然我就叫你把话坐实，坐实不了就付造谣的价钱。”

“那我还会怕这场考校么？”她回敬，毫无惧色。“我告诉你，哦马尔扎克之父，我会欢欢喜喜迎接它。咳，你听我说。

===Original===

Thou settest store by deeds, not words. Tell me, then, is it the deed of a true believer to waste substance upon infidel slaves, to purchase them that he may set them free?”
Asad moved on in silence. That erstwhile habit of Sakr-el-Bahr’s was one not easy to condone. It had occasioned him his moments of uneasiness, and more than once had he taxed his lieutenant with the practice ever to receive the same answer, the answer which he now made to Fenzileh. “For every slave that he so manumitted, he brought a dozen into bondage.”
“Perforce, else would he be called to account. ’Twas so much dust he flung into the face of true Muslimeen. Those manumissions prove a lingering fondness for the infidel country whence he springs. Is there room for that in the heart of a true member of the Prophet’s immortal House? Hast ever known me languish for the Sicilian shore from which in thy might thou wrested me, or have I ever besought of thee the life of a single Sicilian infidel in all these years that I have lived to serve thee? Such longings are betrayed, I say, by such a practice, and such longings could have no place in one who had uprooted infidelity from his heart. And now this voyage of his beyond the seas﻿—risking a vessel that he captured from the arch-enemy of Islam, which is not his to risk but thine in whose name he captured it; and together with it he imperils the lives of two hundred true believers. To what end? To bear him overseas, perchance that he may look again upon the unhallowed land that gave him birth. So Biskaine reported. And what if he should founder on the way?”
“Thou at least wouldst be content, thou fount of malice,” growled Asad.
“Call me harsh names, O sun that warms me! Am I not thine to use and abuse at thy sweet pleasure? Pour salt upon the heart thou woundest; since it is thy hand I’ll never murmur a complaint. But heed me﻿—heed my words; or since words are of no account with thee, then heed his deeds which I am drawing to thy tardy notice. Heed them, I say, as my love bids me even though thou shouldst give me to be whipped or slain for my temerity.”
“Woman, thy tongue is like the clapper of a bell with the devil swinging from the rope. What else dost thou impute?”
“Naught else, since thou dost but mock me, withdrawing thy love from thy fond slave.”
“The praise to Allah, then,” said he. “Come, it is the hour of prayer!”

===Chinese===

你器重事迹，不重言语。那么请问：拿钱财去贴补异教奴隶，把他们买下来放走——这是一个真信士干的事么？”

阿萨德默默往前走。萨克埃尔-巴尔这个旧日的习惯，实在不容易开脱。它不止一次叫他心里发虚；他不止一次拿这事诘问副将，每次得到的都是同一个回答——此刻他给芬齐莱的也是这一个。“他每放走一个奴隶，就掳来一打。”

“不得不如此，不然就要被查问。那是扬进真信士眼里的沙子。那些开释，泄露出他对生他养他的异教之地旧情未断。先知不朽之家的真成员心里，容得下这个么？你可曾见我念念不忘那片西西里海岸——你当年从那里把我掳来的？这些年我活着伺候你，可曾求你饶过一个西西里异教徒的命？这样的牵念，我说，就从这习惯里露了馅；而一个心里把异教连根拔掉的人，容不下这样的牵念。再说他这趟漂洋过海——拿一条从伊斯兰的头号仇敌手里夺来的船去冒险，那船不是他的，是您的，他不过是奉您的名夺的；还随带押上两百名真信士的性命。图什么？送他出洋，兴许就是为了再看一眼生他的那片污土。比斯坎是这么报的。他若半路沉了呢？”

“你倒是要称心了，你这恶毒的泉眼。”阿萨德咆哮。

“骂我狠名字吧，哦晒暖我的太阳！我难道不是您的，任您甜美地使唤也任您甜美地作践？往你刺伤的心上撒盐吧；只要是你的手撒的，我一声也不抱怨。可你要听我——听我的话；既然言语在你这里不算数，那就听他的事迹，我正把它们一件件拖到你迟钝的眼前。听它们，我说——哪怕为这份胆大你要叫人鞭我、杀我，我的爱也要我说。”

“婆娘，你那条舌头就像钟摆，魔鬼拽着绳子在晃。你还有什么要栽的？”

“没有了，既然你一味讥笑我，把你的爱从你可怜的奴隶身上收走。”

“那就感谢安拉。”他说。“来，礼拜的时辰到了！”

===Original===

But he praised Allah too soon. Womanlike, though she protested she had done, she had scarce begun as yet.
“There is thy son, O father of Marzak.”
“There is, O mother of Marzak.”
“And a man’s son should be the partner of his soul. Yet is Marzak passed over for this foreign upstart; yet does this Nasrani of yesterday hold the place in thy heart and at thy side that should be Marzak’s.”
“Could Marzak fill that place?” he asked. “Could that beardless boy lead men as Sakr-el-Bahr leads them, or wield the scimitar against the foes of Islam and increase as Sakr-el-Bahr increases the glory of the Prophet’s Holy Law upon the earth?”
“If Sakr-el-Bahr does this, he does it by thy favour, O my lord. And so might Marzak, young though he be. Sakr-el-Bahr is but what thou hast made him﻿—no more, no less.”
“There art thou wrong, indeed, O mother of error. Sakr-el-Bahr is what Allah hath made him. He is what Allah wills. He shall become what Allah wills. Hast yet to learn that Allah has bound the fate of each man about his neck?”
And then a golden glory suffused the deep sapphire of the sky heralding the setting of the sun and made an end of that altercation, conducted by her with a daring as singular as the patience that had endured it. He quickened his steps in the direction of the courtyard. That golden glow paled as swiftly as it had spread, and night fell as suddenly as if a curtain had been dropped.
In the purple gloom that followed the white cloisters of the courtyard glowed with a faintly luminous pearliness. Dark forms of slaves stirred as Asad entered from the garden followed by Fenzileh, her head now veiled in a thin blue silken gauze. She flashed across the quadrangle and vanished through one of the archways, even as the distant voice of a muezzin broke plaintively upon the brooding stillness reciting the Shehad﻿—
“*La illaha, illa Allah! Wa Muhammad er Rasool Allah!*”
A slave spread a carpet, a second held a great silver bowl, into which a third poured water. The Basha, having washed, turned his face towards Mecca, and testified to the unity of Allah, the Compassionate, the Merciful, King of the Day of Judgment, whilst the cry of the muezzin went echoing over the city from minaret to minaret.
As he rose from his devotions, there came a quick sound of steps without, and a sharp summons.

===Chinese===

可他感谢安拉感谢得太早了。妇道人家就是这样——嘴上说完了，其实才开了个头。

“你还有儿子呢，哦马尔扎克之父。”

“是有儿子，哦马尔扎克之母。”

“儿子的儿子，本该是父亲灵魂的伙伴。可马尔扎克被摆在一边，便宜了这外来暴发户；这个昨日才入教的拿撒勒人，占了你心里、你身侧那个本该属于马尔扎克的位置。”

“马尔扎克填得了那个位置么？”他问。“那个嘴上没毛的小子，能像萨克埃尔-巴尔那样带兵么？能挥刀斩伊斯兰的仇敌，能像萨克埃尔-巴尔那样，叫先知圣法在地上的荣光日增月长么？”

“萨克埃尔-巴尔能这样，靠的是你的恩宠，哦我的大人。马尔扎克也能，别看他年轻。萨克埃尔-巴尔不过是你造就的——不多，也不少。”

“这你可就错了，哦谬误之母。萨克埃尔-巴尔是安拉造就的。他是安拉的意思。他将成为什么，也是安拉的意思。你还没学会么：安拉早把每个人的命数拴在他自己脖子上了？”

这时，一派金红的光晕漫过深蓝宝石色的天穹，宣告日落，也给了这场口角一个了结——她这场舌战打得多胆大，他忍它就有多耐烦。他加快脚步，朝院子那边走去。那金光来得快，褪得也快，夜幕骤落，仿佛放下了一道帘子。

随后的紫昏里，院子那一圈白色回廊泛着微光，如珍珠莹莹。阿萨德从花园进来，芬齐莱跟在身后，头上已蒙了一方薄薄的蓝丝纱。她一阵风掠过四方院，消失在一道拱门里——恰在此时，远处宣礼员的声音哀哀地划破沉沉的寂静，诵起舍哈达——

“除安拉外，绝无应拜者；穆罕默德，是安拉的使者！”

一个奴隶铺开毯子，第二个捧上大银盆，第三个往盆里倒水。帕夏盥洗已毕，面朝麦加，见证安拉的独一——那慈悯的、怜悯的、审判日之王——与此同时，宣礼员的呼喊正从一座座宣礼塔接力般传遍全城。

他礼拜方毕，外头传来急促的脚步声和一声尖利的通报。

===Original===

Turkish janissaries of the Basha’s guard, invisible almost in their flowing black garments, moved to answer that summons and challenge those who came.
From the dark vaulted entrance of the courtyard leapt a gleam of lanterns containing tiny clay lamps in which burned a wick that was nourished by mutton fat. Asad, waiting to learn who came, halted at the foot of the white glistening steps, whilst from doors and lattices of the palace flooded light to suffuse the courtyard and set the marbles shimmering.
A dozen Nubian javelin-men advanced, then ranged themselves aside whilst into the light stepped the imposing, gorgeously robed figure of Asad’s *wazeer*, Tsamanni. After him came another figure in mail that clanked faintly and glimmered as he moved.
“Peace and the Prophet’s blessings upon thee, O mighty Asad!” was the *wazeer*’s greeting.
“And peace upon thee, Tsamanni,” was the answer. “Art the bearer of news?”
“Of great and glorious tidings, O exalted one! Sakr-el-Bahr is returned.”
“The praise to Him!” exclaimed the Basha, with uplifted hands; and there was no mistaking the thrill of his voice.
There fell a soft step behind him and a shadow from the doorway. He turned. A graceful stripling in turban and caftan of cloth of gold salaamed to him from the topmost step. And as he came upright and the light of the lanterns fell full upon his face the astonishingly white fairness of it was revealed﻿—a woman’s face it might have been, so softly rounded was it in its beardlessness.
Asad smiled wrily in his white beard, guessing that the boy had been sent by his ever-watchful mother to learn who came and what the tidings that they bore.
“Thou hast heard, Marzak?” he said. “Sakr-el-Bahr is returned.”
“Victoriously, I hope,” the lad lied glibly.
“Victorious beyond aught that was ever known,” replied Tsamanni. “He sailed at sunset into the harbour, his company aboard two mighty Frankish ships, which are but the lesser part of the great spoil he brings.”
“Allah is great,” was the Basha’s glad welcome of this answer to those insidious promptings of his Sicilian wife. “Why does he not come in person with his news?”
“His duty keeps him yet awhile aboard, my lord,” replied the *wazeer*. “But he hath sent his *kayia* Othmani here to tell the tale of it.”
“Thrice welcome be thou, Othmani.” He beat his hands together, whereat slaves placed cushions for him upon the ground.

===Chinese===

帕夏卫队的土耳其禁卫，几乎隐没在飘拂的黑袍里，移动着去应那通报，查问来者。

院子那幽暗的拱顶入口处，跃出一片灯笼的光——灯笼里是小小的陶盏，盏里燃着羊脂养的灯芯。阿萨德候在白亮的台阶脚下，等看来者是谁；宫殿的门窗里流泻出的灯光一时灌满院子，照得大理石面波光粼粼。

先上来的是十来名努比亚标枪手，随即分立两旁；灯光里跨进一个气派堂皇的身影，锦袍华服，正是阿萨德的维齐尔察曼尼。他身后跟着另一个身影，一身锁子甲，行动间微微作响，隐约闪光。

“平安与先知的祝福降于你，哦威武的阿萨德！”维齐尔问安。

“也降于你，察曼尼。”回答道。“你捎消息来了？”

“伟大而光荣的消息，哦尊贵者！萨克埃尔-巴尔回来了。”

“赞美他！”帕夏高举双手欢呼，声音里的激动错认不了。

他背后响起轻捷的足音，门口掠进一道影子。他转过身。一个俊俏的少年，戴头巾，穿金锦长袍，在最高一级台阶上向他行礼。待他直起身，灯光正照满那张脸，露出惊人的白皙——一张几乎像女人的脸，圆润无髭，柔美之至。

阿萨德捋着白须苦笑了一下，猜到这孩子是他那位时刻提防的母亲派来探听来者何人、消息何事的。

“你听见了，马尔扎克？”他说。“萨克埃尔-巴尔回来了。”

“但愿是得胜而归。”少年撒谎撒得溜。

“得胜得前所未有。”察曼尼答。“他日落时分驶进港来，他的人马分乘两条威武的法兰克船——那还只是他带回的巨额战利品里小的一份。”

“安拉伟大。”帕夏欣然道——这句欢呼，正是对他那西西里妻子阴险撺掇的回答。“那他为何不亲自来报？”

“他的职守还留他在船上待一阵，大人。”维齐尔答。“不过他派了他的副手奥斯曼尼来，来讲这事的经过。”

“三倍欢迎你，奥斯曼尼。”他拍拍手掌，奴隶们便在地上为他摆好坐垫。

===Original===

He sat, and beckoned Marzak to his side. “And now thy tale!”
And Othmani standing forth related how they had voyaged to distant England in the ship that Sakr-el-Bahr had captured, through seas that no corsair yet had ever crossed, and how on their return they had engaged a Dutchman that was their superior in strength and numbers; how none the less Sakr-el-Bahr had wrested victory by the help of Allah, his protector, how he had been dealt a wound that must have slain any but one miraculously preserved for the greater glory of Islam, and of the surpassing wealth of the booty which at dawn tomorrow should be laid at Asad’s feet for his division of it.

===Chinese===

他坐下，招手叫马尔扎克坐到身边。“现在，讲你的故事吧！”

奥斯曼尼上前一步，讲述他们如何乘萨克埃尔-巴尔夺来的那条船，远航到了天边的英格兰，穿过了海盗们从未渡过的海域；归途上又如何同一个船坚人众胜过他们的荷兰人接了仗；萨克埃尔-巴尔又如何靠着他的保佑者安拉，硬生生夺下胜利；他如何中了一处伤，那伤搁在旁人身上必死无疑，唯独他是得神迹保全，留待为伊斯兰建更大的功；还有那批战利品何等豪富——明日拂晓，就要呈到阿萨德脚前，听候他分派。

## VII Marzak-Ben-Asad / 七 马尔扎克·本·阿萨德
===Original===

It took no less than forty camels to convey the cargo of that Dutch argosy from the mole to the Kasbah, and the procession﻿—carefully marshalled by Sakr-el-Bahr, who knew the value of such pageants to impress the mob﻿—was such as never yet had been seen in the narrow streets of Algiers upon the return of any corsair. It was full worthy of the greatest Muslim conqueror that sailed the seas, of one who, not content to keep to the tideless Mediterranean as had hitherto been the rule of his kind, had ventured forth upon the wider ocean.
Ahead marched a hundred of his rovers in their short caftans of every conceivable colour, their waists swathed in gaudy scarves, some of which supported a very arsenal of assorted cutlery; many wore body armour of mail and the gleaming spike of a casque thrust up above their turbans. After them, dejected and in chains, came the five score prisoners taken aboard the Dutchman, urged along by the whips of the corsairs who flanked them. Then marched another regiment of corsairs, and after these the long line of stately, sneering camels, shuffling cumbrously along and led by shouting Saharowis. After them followed yet more corsairs, and then mounted, on a white Arab jennet, his head swathed in a turban of cloth of gold, came Sakr-el-Bahr. In the narrower streets, with their white and yellow washed houses, which presented blank windowless walls broken here and there by no more than a slit to admit light and air, the spectators huddled themselves fearfully into doorways to avoid being crushed to death by the camels, whose burdens bulging on either side entirely filled those narrow ways. But the more open spaces, such as the strand on either side of the mole, the square before the *sôk*, and the approaches of Asad’s fortress, were thronged with a motley roaring crowd. There were stately Moors in flowing robes cheek by jowl with half-naked blacks from the Sus and the Draa; lean, enduring Arabs in their spotless white djellabas rubbed shoulders with Berbers from the highlands in black camelhair cloaks; there were Levantine Turks, and Jewish refugees from Spain ostentatiously dressed in European garments, tolerated there because bound to the Moor by ties of common suffering and common exile from that land that once had been their own.

===Chinese===

要把那艘荷兰大商船的货物从防波堤运上卡斯巴，足足用了四十匹骆驼；这支行列——由萨克埃尔-巴尔精心编排，他深知这种排场对慑服群氓的分量——是阿尔及尔窄街上任何一个海盗凯旋时都不曾见过的。它完全配得上那位航海者中最伟大的穆斯林征服者，配得上这个不满足于祖辈守着无潮的地中海、敢闯更辽阔大洋的人。

走在前头的是他的一百名海盗，短短的长袍五颜六色，你想得出的颜色都有，腰里缠着艳丽的腰带，有的腰带上还插着整整一库房的各色刀剑；许多人身披锁子甲，头巾顶上冒出闪亮的盔尖。跟在他们后面的是垂头丧气、锁着铁链的一百名荷兰船上掳来的俘虏，两侧押送的海盗不时挥鞭催赶。再后头又是海盗，然后是长长一列神情倨傲的骆驼，笨重地拖着步子，由吆喝着的撒哈拉威人牵着。骆驼后面还是海盗，最后，骑着一匹白色阿拉伯骏马、头缠金锦头巾的，是萨克埃尔-巴尔。在那些窄街上——粉墙黄墙的房屋，临街是一面面不开窗的墙，只偶尔裂一道进光透气的缝——看热闹的人惊慌地挤进门洞，生怕被骆驼挤死：骆驼两侧鼓鼓囊囊的驮子把窄巷塞得满满当当。可开阔些的地方，防波堤两边的海滩、市场前的广场、阿萨德城堡的入口，都挤满了杂沓咆哮的人群。有长袍飘飘、气度庄重的摩尔人，同苏斯和德拉来的半裸黑人和肩接踵；穿一尘不染白杰拉巴的清瘦坚忍的阿拉伯人，同高原上穿黑驼毛斗篷的柏柏尔人摩肩接踵；有黎凡特土耳其人，还有从西班牙逃来的犹太人——他们招摇地穿着欧式衣裳，之所以被容忍，是因为他们同摩尔人被共同受难、共同流亡的纽带系在一起，从那片曾经也属于他们的土地上一起流亡。

===Original===

Under the glaring African sun this amazing crowd stood assembled to welcome Sakr-el-Bahr; and welcome him it did, with such vocal thunder that an echo of it from the mole reached the very Kasbah on the hilltop to herald his approach.
By the time, however, that he reached the fortress his procession had dwindled by more than half. At the *sôk* his forces had divided, and his corsairs, headed by Othmani, had marched the captives away to the bagnio﻿—or banyard, as my Lord Henry calls it﻿—whilst the camels had continued up the hill. Under the great gateway of the Kasbah they padded into the vast courtyard to be ranged along two sides of it by their Saharowi drivers, and there brought clumsily to their knees. After them followed but some two score corsairs as a guard of honour to their leader. They took their stand upon either side of the gateway after profoundly salaaming to Asad-ed-Din. The Basha sat in the shade of an awning enthroned upon a divan, attended by his *wazeer* Tsamanni and by Marzak, and guarded by a half-dozen janissaries, whose sable garments made an effective background to the green and gold of his jewelled robes. In his white turban glowed an emerald crescent.
The Basha’s countenance was dark and brooding as he watched the advent of that line of burdened camels. His thoughts were still labouring with the doubt of Sakr-el-Bahr which Fenzileh’s crafty speech and craftier reticence had planted in them. But at sight of the corsair leader himself his countenance cleared suddenly, his eyes sparkled, and he rose to his feet to welcome him as a father might welcome a son who had been through perils on a service dear to both.
Sakr-el-Bahr entered the courtyard on foot, having dismounted at the gate. Tall and imposing, with his head high and his forked beard thrusting forward, he stalked with great dignity to the foot of the divan followed by Ali and a mahogany-faced fellow, turbaned and red-bearded, in whom it needed more than a glance to recognize the rascally Jasper Leigh, now in all the panoply of your complete renegado.
Sakr-el-Bahr went down upon his knees and prostrated himself solemnly before his prince.
“The blessing of Allah and His peace upon thee, my lord,” was his greeting.

===Chinese===

在灼烈的非洲太阳底下，这一大群奇景之人聚齐了，欢迎萨克埃尔-巴尔；也真欢迎了——那一阵人声的雷鸣，从防波堤一直传到山顶的卡斯巴，为他通报行程。

然而等他到达城堡，队伍已折损过半。到了市场，他的人马分了兵：海盗们在奥斯曼尼率领下，押着俘虏往奴隶营去了——或者照亨利勋爵的叫法，奴隶栏——骆驼则继续上山。它们踏进卡斯巴的大拱门，进了宽敞的院子，由撒哈拉威人赶着沿两边排开，笨拙地屈下前膝。随后进来的只有四十来名海盗，作为首领的仪仗。他们朝阿萨德-艾德-丁深深行礼后，分立大门两侧。帕夏坐在凉棚的荫里，踞于长榻，维齐尔察曼尼和马尔扎克在旁侍坐，六名禁卫护卫，黑衣正好衬出他锦袍的绿与金。他的白头巾上，一弯翡翠的新月莹然生光。

帕夏看着那列负重的骆驼逶迤而来，脸色阴沉若思。芬齐莱那番狡黠的言语和更狡黠的缄默，在他心里种下的疑团还在作祟。可一看见海盗首领本人，他的脸色霍然开朗，双眼发亮，站起身来迎他——像一位父亲迎接一个为父子共同珍视的事业历尽艰险归来的儿子。

萨克埃尔-巴尔在门口下了马，步行走进院子。他高大威严，昂着头，叉开的胡须向前挺着，大步庄严地走到长榻前；身后跟着阿里，还有一个红木色面孔、缠头巾、红胡子的家伙——得多看几眼才认得出，那正是无赖贾斯珀·利，如今周身上下，一身十足叛教者的行头。

萨克埃尔-巴尔跪下去，庄重地伏拜在他的君公面前。

“愿安拉的祝福与平安降于你，我的大人。”是他的问安。

===Original===

And Asad, stooping to lift that splendid figure in his arms, gave him a welcome that caused the spying Fenzileh to clench her teeth behind the fretted lattice that concealed her.
“The praise to Allah and to our Lord Muhammad that thou art returned and in health, my son. Already hath my old heart been gladdened by the news of thy victories in the service of the Faith.”
Then followed the display of all those riches wrested from the Dutch, and greatly though Asad’s expectations had been fed already by Othmani, the sight now spread before his eyes by far exceeded all those expectations.
In the end all was dismissed to the treasury, and Tsamanni was bidden to go cast up the account of it and mark the share that fell to the portion of those concerned﻿—for in these ventures all were partners, from the Basha himself, who represented the State down to the meanest corsair who had manned the victorious vessels of the Faith, and each had his share of the booty, greater or less according to his rank, one twentieth of the total falling to Sakr-el-Bahr himself.
In the courtyard were left none but Asad, Marzak and the janissaries, and Sakr-el-Bahr with Ali and Jasper. It was then that Sakr-el-Bahr presented his new officer to the Bashal as one upon whom the grace of Allah had descended, a great fighter and a skilled seaman, who had offered up his talents and his life to the service of Islam, who had been accepted by Sakr-el-Bahr, and stood now before Asad to be confirmed in his office.
Marzak interposed petulantly, to exclaim that already were there too many erstwhile Nasrani dogs in the ranks of the soldiers of the Faith, and that it was unwise to increase their number and presumptuous in Sakr-el-Bahr to take so much upon himself.
Sakr-el-Bahr measured him with an eye in which scorn and surprise were nicely blended.
“Dost say that it is presumptuous to win a convert to the banner of Our Lord Muhammad?” quoth he. “Go read the Most Perspicuous Book and see what is there enjoined as a duty upon every true believer.

===Chinese===

阿萨德俯身把那堂堂的身躯搀起来抱了抱，那欢迎的热忱，叫躲在雕花格窗后窥探的芬齐莱咬碎了牙。

“赞美安拉，赞美我们的主穆罕默德，你回来了，还安康，我的儿子。你为正教建立功勋的捷报，早已把我这颗老心焐热了。”

接着便是陈列从荷兰人手里夺来的全部财富；尽管奥斯曼尼已经把阿萨德的期望撑得老高，此刻摊在他眼前的景象，还是远远超出了那些期望。

最后，一切都被打发进宝库，察曼尼奉命去核算账目，标出各人应得的一份——原来这类营生人人都是股东，上自代表国家的帕夏本人，下至在正教凯旋船上出力的最卑微的海盗，人人有份，依职位高低或多或少；萨克埃尔-巴尔自己独得总数的二十分之一。

院子里只剩下阿萨德、马尔扎克和禁卫们，还有萨克埃尔-巴尔带着阿里和贾斯珀。这时，萨克埃尔-巴尔把他的新军官引见给帕夏：说这是一个蒙安拉恩典降临的人，一员猛将，一位精通航海的好手，他已把才艺和性命一并献上，投效伊斯兰；萨克埃尔-巴尔已收纳了他，如今立在阿萨德面前，请加敕任。

马尔扎克使性子插嘴，嚷道：正教的兵行里，昔日那些拿撒勒狗已经太多，再添他们的数目不智，萨克埃尔-巴尔擅自做这么大的主，更是僭越。

萨克埃尔-巴尔拿眼量他，那眼里轻蔑和诧异配得恰到好处。

“你说，替我们的主穆罕默德的旗帜赢来一个皈依者，是僭越？”他说。“去读一读那至明的经典，看看那里把什么事定为每个真信士的本分。

===Original===

And bethink thee, O son of Asad, that when thou dost in thy little wisdom cast scorn upon those whom Allah has blessed and led from the night wherein they dwelt into the bright noontide of Faith, thou dost cast scorn upon me and upon thine own mother, which is but a little matter, and thou dost blaspheme the Blessed name of Allah, which is to tread the ways that lead unto the Pit.”
Angry but defeated and silenced, Marzak fell back a step and stood biting his lip and glowering upon the corsair, what time Asad nodded his head and smiled approval.
“Verily art thou full learned in the True Belief, Sakr-el-Bahr,” he said. “Thou art the very father of wisdom as of valour.” And thereupon he gave welcome to Master Leigh, whom he hailed to the ranks of the Faithful under the designation of Jasper-Reis.
That done, the renegade and Ali were both dismissed, as were also the janissaries, who, quitting their position behind Asad, went to take their stand on guard at the gateway. Then the Basha beat his hands together, and to the slaves who came in answer to his summons he gave orders to set food, and he bade Sakr-el-Bahr to come sit beside him on the divan.
Water was brought that they might wash. That done, the slaves placed before them a savoury stew of meat and eggs with olives, limes, and spices.
Asad broke bread with a reverently pronounced “*Bismillah!*” and dipped his fingers into the earthenware bowl, leading the way for Sakr-el-Bahr and Marzak, and as they ate he invited the corsair himself to recite the tale of his adventure.
When he had done so, and again Asad had praised him in high and loving terms, Marzak set him a question.
“Was it to obtain just these two English slaves that thou didst undertake this perilous voyage to that distant land?”
“That was but a part of my design,” was the calm reply. “I went to rove the seas in the Prophet’s service, as the result of my voyage gives proof.”
“Thou didst not know that this Dutch argosy would cross thy path,” said Marzak, in the very words his mother had prompted him.
“Did I not?” quoth Sakr-el-Bahr, and he smiled confidently, so confidently that Asad scarce needed to hear the words that so cunningly gave the lie to the innuendo. “Had I no trust in Allah the All-wise, the All-knowing?
“Well answered, by the Koran!”

===Chinese===

再想想，哦阿萨德之子：你凭你那点小聪明，讥笑那些蒙安拉赐福、从他们所居的黑夜领进正教明亮正午的人——你这是讥笑我，讥笑你自己的母亲，这还是小事；你这是亵渎安拉的圣名，那就是往通往火坑的路上走了。”

马尔扎克又怒又败，哑了口，退后一步，站在那里咬着嘴唇，恶狠狠地瞪着那海盗；这工夫，阿萨德点着头，笑眯眯地称许。

“你当真饱学正教，萨克埃尔-巴尔，”他说。“论智慧、论勇武，你都是智慧与勇武之父。”随即他欢迎了利船长，把他收进信士的行列，赐名贾斯珀-雷斯。

这件事办完，叛教者和阿里都被打发走了，禁卫们也撤了，他们离开阿萨德身后的位置，到大门去站岗。帕夏拍拍手，应声而来的奴隶们奉命摆饭，他请萨克埃尔-巴尔来，在他身旁的长榻上坐了。

先端水来盥洗。洗罢，奴隶们端上一盆香喷喷的炖肉蛋，配着橄榄、青柠和香料。

阿萨德虔敬地念一声“奉安拉之名”，掰开饼，手指伸进陶盆，给萨克埃尔-巴尔和马尔扎克带了头；三人边吃，他请海盗首领亲自讲一讲这场冒险的经过。

他讲完，阿萨德又用亲爱而崇高的言辞夸赞了他一番，马尔扎克这才发问。

“你冒这趟凶险，远航到那片远土，就为掳这两个英格兰奴隶？”

“那不过是我的谋划的一部分。”回答是平静的。“我是奉先知之命出海劫掠的——航行的结果便是明证。”

“你可不知道那艘荷兰大船会撞上你的路。”马尔扎克说——用的正是他母亲教的字眼。

“我不知道？”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，自信地笑了——自信得叫阿萨德几乎不必再听那句巧妙地戳穿影射的话。“我信不过至智至知的安拉么？”

“答得好，凭《古兰经》！”

===Original===

Asad approved him heartily, the more heartily since it rebutted insinuations which he desired above all to hear rebutted.
But Marzak did not yet own himself defeated. He had been soundly schooled by his guileful Sicilian mother.
“Yet there is something in all this I do not understand,” he murmured, with false gentleness.
“All things are possible to Allah!” said Sakr-el-Bahr, in tones of incredulity, as if he suggested﻿—not without a suspicion of irony﻿—that it was incredible there should be anything in all the world that could elude the penetration of Marzak.
The youth bowed to him in acknowledgment. “Tell me, O mighty Sakr-el-Bahr,” he begged, “how it came to pass that having reached those distant shores thou wert content to take thence but two poor slaves, since with thy followers and the favour of the All-seeing thou might easily have taken fifty times that number.” And he looked ingenuously into the corsair’s swarthy, rugged face, whilst Asad frowned thoughtfully, for the thought was one that had occurred to him already.
It became necessary that Sakr-el-Bahr should lie to clear himself. Here no high-sounding phrase of Faith would answer. And explanation was unavoidable, and he was conscious that he could not afford one that did not go a little lame.
“Why, as to that,” said he, “these prisoners were wrested from the first house upon which we came, and their capture occasioned some alarm. Moreover, it was nighttime when we landed, and I dared not adventure the lives of my followers by taking them further from the ship and attacking a village which might have risen to cut off our good retreat.”
The frown remained stamped upon the brow of Asad, as Marzak slyly observed.
“Yet Othmani,” said he, “urged thee to fall upon a slumbering village all unconscious of thy presence, and thou didst refuse.”
Asad looked up sharply at that, and Sakr-el-Bahr realized with a tightening about the heart something of the undercurrents at work against him and all the pains that had been taken to glean information that might be used to his undoing.
“Is it so?” demanded Asad, looking from his son to his lieutenant with that lowering look that rendered his face evil and cruel.
Sakr-el-Bahr took a high tone. He met Asad’s glance with an eye of challenge.
“And if it were so my lord?” he demanded.
“I asked thee is it so?”

===Chinese===

阿萨德衷心赞许——赞许得格外衷心，因为这答复驳倒的那些含沙射影，正是他最巴望听人驳倒的。

可马尔扎克还不认输。他那狡诈的西西里母亲，把他调教得扎实。

“只是这一切里头，有一桩我参不透。”他假作温顺地咕哝。

“安拉无所不能！”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，调子里透着不信，仿佛在暗示——不无一丝讥讽——天底下竟能有马尔扎克参不透的事，真是难以置信。

少年向他欠身致意。“请教我，哦威武的萨克埃尔-巴尔，”他恳求道，“既然有你的人手和全视之主的眷顾，你要掳五十倍这个数也易如反掌，怎么到了那片远岸，你竟只满足于带走两个可怜的奴隶？”他一脸天真地望着海盗黝黑粗砺的脸；阿萨德却若有所思地皱起眉——这个疑团，他自己心里也早转过。

萨克埃尔-巴尔不免要撒个谎来洗清自己了。这里，高调的正教辞令顶不了事。解释是躲不掉的，他也明白，这解释要是不带点破绽，反倒不行。

“咳，说起这个，”他说，“这两个俘虏，是从我们撞上的头一户人家里硬夺来的，掳他们已经惊动了地方。再说，我们登陆时是夜里，我不敢拿手下的性命去冒险，让他们离船太远，去攻打一座村庄——那里的人说不定会倾巢而出，抄了我们的退路。”

阿萨德眉间的疙瘩并没有解开，马尔扎克眼尖，看在心上。

“可是奥斯曼尼，”他说，“催你趁夜突袭一座睡梦正酣、浑然不觉的村子，你却回绝了。”

阿萨德闻声猛地抬头，萨克埃尔-巴尔心口一紧，这才咂摸出暗地里冲他来的潜流，咂摸出人家费了多大心思搜罗材料，好用来毁他。

“有这事？”阿萨德质问，目光从儿子扫到副将，那阴沉沉的神色使他的脸显得凶狠残忍。

萨克埃尔-巴尔摆出高姿态，用挑衅的眼光迎住阿萨德的瞪视。

“就算有，那又怎样，大人？”他反问。

“我问你，有没有这事？”

===Original===

“Ay, but knowing thy wisdom I disbelieved my ears,” said Sakr-el-Bahr. “Shall it signify what Othmani may have said? Do I take my orders or am I to be guided by Othmani? If so, best set Othmani in my place, give him the command and the responsibility for the lives of the Faithful who fight beside him.” He ended with an indignant snort.
“Thou art over-quick to anger,” Asad reproved him, scowling still.
“And by the Head of Allah, who will deny my right to it? Am I to conduct such an enterprise as this from which I am returned laden with spoils that might well be the fruits of a year’s raiding, to be questioned by a beardless stripling as to why I was not guided by Othmani?”
He heaved himself up and stood towering there in the intensity of a passion that was entirely simulated. He must bluster here, and crush down suspicion with whorling periods and broad, fierce gesture.
“To what should Othmani have guided me?” he demanded scornfully. “Could he have guided me to more than I have this day laid at thy feet? What I have done speaks eloquently with its own voice. What he would have had me do might well have ended in disaster. Had it so ended, would the blame of it have fallen upon Othmani? Nay, by Allah! but upon me. And upon me rests then the credit, and let none dare question it without better cause.”
Now these were daring words to address to the tyrant Asad, and still more daring was the tone, the light hard eyes aflash and the sweeping gestures of contempt with which they were delivered. But of his ascendancy over the Basha there was no doubt. And here now was proof of it.
Asad almost cowered before his fury. The scowl faded from his face to be replaced by an expression of dismay.
“Nay, nay, Sakr-el-Bahr, this tone!” he cried.
Sakr-el-Bahr, having slammed the door of conciliation in the face of the Basha, now opened it again. He became instantly submissive.
“Forgive it,” he said. “Blame the devotion of thy servant to thee and to the Faith he serves with little reck to life. In this very expedition was I wounded nigh unto death. The livid scar of it is a dumb witness to my zeal. Where are thy scars, Marzak?”
Marzak quailed before the sudden blaze of that question, and Sakr-el-Bahr laughed softly in contempt.
“Sit,” Asad bade him. “I have been less than just.”

===Chinese===

“有——不过我知道您的明智，简直不敢相信自己的耳朵。”萨克埃尔-巴尔说。“奥斯曼尼说过什么，要紧么？我接令行事，还是该由奥斯曼尼领着走？若该如此，索性把奥斯曼尼摆到我的位子上，把指挥权、把同他并肩作战的信士们的性命，一并交给他得了。”他忿忿地哼了一声作结。

“你也太一点就着。”阿萨德斥他，眉头依旧锁着。

“以安拉的头起誓，谁敢说我没有权利发火？我率领这样一场征伐，满载着够得上一整年劫掠的战利品回来，倒要受一个嘴上没毛的小儿盘问，问我为何不听奥斯曼尼的？”

他直起身，高高立在那里，一副勃然大怒的样子——这怒气全是装出来的。此刻他必须虚张声势，用滔滔雄辩和粗豪的手势把疑心压下去。

“奥斯曼尼能领我到哪儿去？”他轻蔑地说。“他还能领我得到比今天摆在您脚前的更多吗？我干下的事，自己会说话，说得响亮。他要我干的事，很可能落个全军覆没。真覆没了，罪会落在奥斯曼尼头上么？不，凭安拉！落在我头上。那么功劳也就归我，谁若没有更硬的凭据，休敢来问！”

对专制者阿萨德说这些，是胆大包天；那口气更胆大——那双又亮又硬的眼睛喷着火，配上轻蔑的大动作。可他对帕夏的威慑力是毋庸置疑的，此刻便有了明证。

阿萨德在他的盛怒面前几乎缩了下去。他脸上的阴沉褪成了一副惶惑。

“别，别，萨克埃尔-巴尔，这个口气！”他喊。

萨克埃尔-巴尔方才当着帕夏的面把和解的门摔上，此刻又把它拉开。他立刻恭顺下来。

“恕罪。”他说。“要怪，就怪您的仆人对您、对他所奉的正教太舍命。就在这一趟远征里，我险些丢了命。这道青黑的疤，便是我热忱的哑证人。你的疤呢，马尔扎克？”

马尔扎克被这劈面一问的火光吓得一缩，萨克埃尔-巴尔轻声笑了，满是不屑。

“坐。”阿萨德命他。“方才是我有失公道了。”

===Original===

“Thou art the very fount and spring of justice, O my lord, as this thine admission proves,” protested the corsair. He sat down again, folding his legs under him. “I will confess to you that being come so near to England in that cruise of mine I determined to land and seize one who some years ago did injure me, and between whom and me there was a score to settle. I exceeded my intentions in that I carried off two prisoners instead of one. These prisoners,” he ran on, judging that the moment of reaction in Asad’s mind was entirely favourable to the preferment of the request he had to make, “are not in the bagnio with the others. They are still confined aboard the carack I seized.”
“And why is this?” quoth Asad, but without suspicion now.
“Because, my lord, I have a boon to ask in some reward for the service I have rendered.”
“Ask it, my son.”
“Give me leave to keep these captives for myself.”
Asad considered him, frowning again slightly. Despite himself, despite his affection for Sakr-el-Bahr, and his desire to soothe him now that rankling poison of Fenzileh’s infusing was at work again in his mind.
“My leave thou hast,” said he. “But not the law’s, and the law runs that no corsair shall subtract so much as the value of an asper from his booty until the division has been made and his own share allotted him,” was the grave answer.
“The law?” quoth Sakr-el-Bahr. “But thou art the law, exalted lord.”
“Not so, my son. The law is above the Basha, who must himself conform to it so that he be just and worthy of his high office. And the law I have recited thee applies even should the corsair raider be the Basha himself. These slaves of thine must forthwith be sent to the bagnio to join the others that tomorrow all may be sold in the *sôk*. See it done, Sakr-el-Bahr.”
The corsair would have renewed his pleadings, but that his eye caught the eager white face of Marzak and the gleaming expectant eyes, looking so hopefully for his ruin. He checked, and bowed his head with an assumption of indifference.
“Name thou their price then, and forthwith will I pay it into thy treasury.”
But Asad shook his head. “It is not for me to name their price, but for the buyers,” he replied. “I might set the price too high, and that were unjust to thee, or too low, and that were unjust to others who would acquire them. Deliver them over to the bagnio.”

===Chinese===

“您正是公道的不竭泉源，哦我的大人，您这句自认便是明证。”海盗分辩道。他重新坐下，盘起双腿。“我向你们招认：那一趟巡弋，既然离英格兰那样近了，我便决意登岸，捉一个人——几年前他害过我，我们之间有一笔账要清。我超出了本意，一口气掳了两个，不是一个。这两个俘虏，”他接着说，估量着此刻阿萨德心里的回甘，正是提他那桩请求的最好时机，“没有同旁人一起关在奴隶营。他们还囚在我夺来的那条大帆船上。”

“这是为何？”阿萨德问，此刻已不带疑心。

“因为，大人，我有一桩恩典想求，算是对我效劳的一点酬赏。”

“求吧，我的儿子。”

“准我把这两个俘虏留给我自己。”

阿萨德打量他，眉头又微微锁起。不由自主地——尽管他疼萨克埃尔-巴尔，此刻也存心抚慰他——芬齐莱灌进的那点毒汁，又在心里发作起来。

“我的准许你有了。”他说。“可法律的没有。法律明定：分赃未毕、自己那一份未派定之前，任何海盗不得私吞赃物，哪怕只值一个阿斯普尔。”回答是严正的。

“法律？”萨克埃尔-巴尔说。“可大人您就是法律啊，尊贵的大人。”

“不然，我的儿子。法律高于帕夏，帕夏自己也须遵行法律，方才公道，方才配得上他的高位。我给你背的这条法律，哪怕出劫的海盗就是帕夏本人，也一样适用。你这两个奴隶，须即刻送往奴隶营，同其余的合在一处，明日一齐上市发卖。去办了，萨克埃尔-巴尔。”

海盗还要再求，眼角却撞上马尔扎克那张热切发白的脸和灼灼期冀的眼睛，正巴望看他栽倒。他刹住话头，装出满不在乎的样子低下头。

“那您给他们标个价，我即刻照数付进您的宝库。”

可阿萨德摇头。“价不是我标的，是买主出的。”他答。“我标高了，对不住你；标低了，对不住别的买主。把他们交奴隶营去。”

===Original===

“It shall be done,” said Sakr-el-Bahr, daring to insist no further and dissembling his chagrin.
Very soon thereafter he departed upon that errand, giving orders, however, that Rosamund and Lionel should be kept apart from the other prisoners until the hour of the sale on the morrow when perforce they must take their place with the rest.
Marzak lingered with his father after Oliver had taken his leave, and presently they were joined there in the courtyard by Fenzileh﻿—this woman who had brought, said many, the Frankish ways of Shaitan into Algiers.

===Chinese===

“遵命。”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，不敢再强求，把懊丧掩了过去。

随后不久，他便为这桩差事动身了；不过他传下命令：罗莎蒙德和莱昂内尔要同其他俘虏分开看管，直到明日开卖的时辰——那时他们才不得不与众人为伍。

奥利弗告辞之后，马尔扎克陪父亲又待了一阵；不久，芬齐莱也来到院中同他们会合——就是这个女人，人们说，把法兰克人沙伊坦的那套做派带进了阿尔及尔。

## XXI Moriturus / 二十一 将死之人
===Original===

He departed from her presence with bitterness in his heart, leaving a profound contrition in her own. The sense of this her last injustice to him so overwhelmed her that it became the gauge by which she measured that other earlier wrong he had suffered at her hands. Perhaps her overwrought mind falsified the perspective, exaggerating it until it seemed to her that all the suffering and evil with which this chronicle has been concerned were the direct fruits of her own sin of unfaith.
Since all sincere contrition must of necessity bring forth an ardent desire to atone, so was it now with her. Had he but refrained from departing so abruptly he might have had her on her knees to him suing for pardon for all the wrongs which her thoughts had done him, proclaiming her own utter unworthiness and baseness. But since his righteous resentment had driven him from her presence she could but sit and brood upon it all, considering the words in which to frame her plea for forgiveness when next he should return.
But the hours sped, and there was no sign of him. And then, almost with a shock of dread came the thought that ere long perhaps Sir John Killigrew’s ship would be upon them. In her distraught state of mind she had scarcely pondered that contingency. Now that it occurred to her all her concern was for the result of it to Sir Oliver. Would there be fighting, and would he perhaps perish in that conflict at the hands either of the English or of the corsairs whom for her sake he had betrayed, perhaps without ever hearing her confession of penitence, without speaking those words of forgiveness of which her soul stood in such thirsty need?
It would be towards midnight when unable longer to bear the suspense of it, she rose and softly made her way to the entrance. Very quietly she lifted the curtain, and in the act of stepping forth almost stumbled over a body that lay across the threshold. She drew back with a startled gasp; then stooped to look, and by the faint rays of the lanterns on mainmast and poop rail she recognized Sir Oliver, and saw that he slept. She never heeded the two Nubians immovable as statues who kept guard. She continued to bend over him, and then gradually and very softly sank down on her knees beside him. There were tears in her eyes﻿—tears wrung from her by a tender emotion of wonder and gratitude at so much fidelity.

===Chinese===

他怀着满心的苦涩离她而去，留给她的却是深深的悔疚。她这最后一桩对他的不义，压得她喘不过气，竟成了她量度先前那次冤屈的标尺。或许是她过度激荡的心把远近全然量错了，把那冤屈越描越大，直到在她眼里，这部纪事所载的一切苦难与灾祸，都成了她那次负心之罪的直捷果实。

一切真诚的悔疚，必得生出火热的赎罪之愿，她此刻正是如此。他若不是走得那样急，本可以看见她跪倒在他脚下，为她心里对他负过的一切冤枉求饶，宣告自己全然的不配与卑劣。可他那义正的怨愤把他从她跟前赶走了，她只能坐在那里，把这一切翻来覆去地想，斟酌着等他下次回来时，用什么话来织成求恕的哀辞。

可时辰一个个飞过去，不见他的踪影。随后，几乎带着一阵惊惧，她忽然想起：约翰·基利格鲁爵士的船，说不定转眼就要压到他们头上了。方寸已乱的她，此前几乎没掂量过这个变数。此刻它一旦浮上心头，她满心牵挂的，全是这件事落在奥利弗爵士头上的下场。会打起来么？他会不会死在那场冲突里——或死在英国人手里，或死在他为她背叛了的那些海盗手里——也许至死听不到她的忏悔，听不到她的灵魂渴得冒烟的那几句宽恕？

约莫到了午夜，这悬心再悬不下去了，她起身，轻轻摸到门口。她悄悄掀起帘子，正要跨出去，几乎绊在一个横卧门槛的身躯上。她惊得倒抽一口气，缩回来；随即俯身去看——借主桅和艉楼栏杆上灯笼的微光，她认出是奥利弗爵士，他睡着了。守在那儿的两个努比亚人一动不动如石像，她全没留意。她只是继续俯身望着他，随后缓缓地、轻轻地在他身边跪了下去。她眼里涌上泪水——这一片忠义，从她心里绞出的又惊又感的柔情化成的泪水。

===Original===

She did not know that he had slept thus last night. But it was enough for her to find him here now. It moved her oddly, profoundly, that this man whom she had ever mistrusted and misjudged should even when he slept make of his body a barrier for her greater security and protection.
A sob escaped her, and at the sound, so lightly and vigilantly did he take his rest, he came instantly if silently to a sitting attitude; and so they looked into each other’s eyes, his swarthy, bearded hawk face on a level with her white gleaming countenance.
“What is it?” he whispered.
She drew back instantly, taken with sudden panic at that question. Then recovering, and seeking womanlike to evade and dissemble the thing she was come to do, now that the chance of doing it was afforded her﻿—
“Do you think,” she faltered, “that Lionel will have reached Sir John’s ship?”
He flashed a glance in the direction of the divan under the awning where the Basha slept. There all was still. Besides, the question had been asked in English. He rose and held out a hand to help her to her feet. Then he signed to her to reenter the poop house, and followed her within.
“Anxiety keeps you wakeful?” he said, half-question, half-assertion.
“Indeed,” she replied.
“There is scarce the need,” he assured her. “Sir John will not be like to stir until dead of night, that he may make sure of taking us unawares. I have little doubt that Lionel would reach him. It is none so long a swim. Indeed, once outside the cove he could take to the land until he was abreast of the ship. Never doubt he will have done his errand.”
She sat down, her glance avoiding his; but the light falling on her face showed him the traces there of recent tears.
“There will be fighting when Sir John arrives?” she asked him presently.
“Like enough. But what can it avail? We shall be caught﻿—as was said today﻿—in just such a trap as that in which Andrea Doria caught Dragut at Jerba, saving that whilst the wily Dragut found a way out for his galleys, here none is possible. Courage, then, for the hour of your deliverance is surely at hand.” He paused, and then in a softer voice, humbly almost, “It is my prayer,” he added, “that hereafter in a happy future these last few weeks shall come to seem no more than an evil dream to you.”
To that prayer she offered no response. She sat bemused, her brow wrinkled.

===Chinese===

她不知道他昨夜也是这样睡的。可此刻在这里找到他，于她就尽够了。这个她一直猜疑、一直错判的男人，连睡着时，也把自己的身子横成一道屏障，护她周全——这份情意古怪地、深深地撼动了她。

一声呜咽脱口而出。这声响惊得他——他睡得那样轻、那样警醒——霎时无声无息地坐了起来；于是两人四目相对，他那张黝黑有须的鹰脸，正对她白莹莹的脸。

“怎么了？”他低声问。

她立刻缩回去，这问题使她一阵心慌。随后定下神，又拿出妇人的故技——既然动手的机会就在眼前，反倒闪躲起来，另寻托辞——

“你看，”她嗫嚅道，“莱昂内尔能游到约翰爵士的船上了么？”

他朝凉棚下帕夏睡的那张长榻那边疾掠了一眼。那边一切安静。再说这问题是用英语问的。他起身，伸过一只手扶她起来，然后示意她回艉楼舱去，自己跟了进去。

“忧心叫你睡不着？”他半问半断地说。

“正是。”她答。

“大可不必。”他宽她的心。“约翰爵士不到夜深不会动，为的是稳稳摸我们一个措手不及。莱昂内尔能到他那儿，我毫不怀疑。那点水路不算长。出了海湾，他完全可以上岸，走到同那条船并齐的地方再下水。他办成了他的差事，这不必怀疑。”

她坐下来，目光避开他；灯光落在她脸上，照出方才泪水的痕迹。

“约翰爵士到的时候，会有一场厮杀？”片刻后她问。

“多半会。可那又顶什么用？我们会陷入的，正是今天说过的那类口袋——安德烈亚·多利亚在杰尔巴困住德拉古特的那类，只是狡黠的德拉古特还替他的桨帆船寻出了一条生路，这里却一条也没有。鼓起勇气来，你获救的钟点就在眼前了。”他顿了顿，随后用更软的、近乎谦卑的声音补了一句：“我祈求的是——往后的幸福岁月里，这几个礼拜在你记忆中，渐渐不过是一场噩夢。”

对这一祈求，她没有回应。她坐着出神，眉心蹙起。

===Original===

“I would it might be done without fighting,” she said presently, and sighed wearily.
“You need have no fear,” he assured her. “I shall take all precautions for you. You shall remain here until all is over and the entrance will be guarded by a few whom I can trust.”
“You mistake me,” she replied, and looked up at him suddenly. “Do you suppose my fears are for myself?” She paused again, and then abruptly asked him, “What will befall you?”
“I thank you for the thought,” he replied gravely. “No doubt I shall meet with my deserts. Let it but come swiftly when it comes.”
“Ah, no, no!” she cried. “Not that!” And rose in her sudden agitation.
“What else remains?” he asked, and smiled. “What better fate could anyone desire me?”
“You shall live to return to England,” she surprised him by exclaiming. “The truth must prevail, and justice be done you.”
He looked at her with so fierce and searching a gaze that she averted her eyes. Then he laughed shortly.
“There’s but one form of justice I can look for in England,” said he. “It is a justice administered in hemp. Believe me, mistress, I am grown too notorious for mercy. Best end it here tonight. Besides,” he added, and his mockery fell from him, his tone became gloomy, “bethink you of my present act of treachery to these men of mine, who, whatever they may be, have followed me into a score of perils and but today have shown their love and loyalty to me to be greater than their devotion to the Basha himself. I shall have delivered them to the sword. Could I survive with honour? They may be but poor heathens to you and yours, but to me they are my sea hawks, my warriors, my faithful gallant followers, and I were a dog indeed did I survive the death to which I have doomed them.”
As she listened and gathered from his words the apprehension of a thing that had hitherto escaped her, her eyes grew wide in sudden horror.
“Is that to be the cost of my deliverance?” she asked him fearfully.
“I trust not,” he replied. “I have something in mind that will perhaps avoid it.”
“And save your own life as well?” she asked him quickly.
“Why waste a thought upon so poor a thing? My life was forfeit already. If I go back to Algiers they will assuredly hang me. Asad will see to it, and not all my sea hawks could save me from my fate.”
She sank down again upon the divan, and sat there rocking her arms in a gesture of hopeless distress.

===Chinese===

“但愿这事能不动刀兵就了结。”过了一会儿她说，倦倦地叹了口气。

“你什么都不必怕。”他宽慰她。“我会替你把一切都布置妥当。你待在这里，等一切过去；门口由几个我信得过的人把守。”

“你误会我了。”她答，突然抬眼看他。“你当我怕的是我自己？”她又顿了顿，随后劈头问他：“你自己会落得什么？”

“多谢你想着这个。”他正色答。“无疑我将领受我应得的。只求它来的时候，来得干脆。”

“啊，不，不！”她喊。“不要那样！”她在骤然的激动中站了起来。

“不然还有什么？”他问，笑了。“谁还能替我盼个更好的下场？”

“你要活着回英格兰。”她喊出的这句话叫他吃了一惊。“真相必得大白，必得还你公道。”

他用那样凶狠、那样逼视的目光看她，逼得她垂下了眼。随后他短促地一笑。

“我在英格兰能指望的公道只有一种，”他说，“是用麻绳执行的公道。相信我，小姐，我如今恶名太著，饶恕轮不到我。顶好就在今夜这里了结。再说，”他补了一句，讥诮褪尽，语气阴沉下来，“想想我此刻对我这班人的背叛。他们不管是什么人，总归跟着我闯过几十遭险，就在今天，他们还表明了他们对我的爱戴和忠心，胜过对帕夏本人的。我要把他们送进刀山。这样的我，还能带着体面活下去么？在你和你的人们眼里，他们不过是可怜的异教徒；在我眼里，他们是我的海鹰，我的战士，我忠勇的部曲——我若能在自己为他们判下的死里幸存，我才真是一条狗。”

她听着，从他的话里咂摸出一件她此前全然不曾察觉的事，眼睛在骤然的恐怖中睁大了。

“我获救的代价，就是这个？”她惶惶地问。

“但愿不是。”他答。“我心里有个计较，或许能避开它。”

“也救你自己的命？”她急急追问。

“何苦为这么个贱货操心？我的命早就折进去了。我若回阿尔及尔，他们一定吊死我。阿萨德会办妥这件事，我全部的海鹰也救不了我脱这个命数。”

她又跌坐回长榻，坐在那里前后摇晃着双臂，一副绝望的悲苦模样。

===Original===

“I see,” she said. “I see. I am bringing this fate upon you. When you sent Lionel upon that errand you voluntarily offered up your life to restore me to my own people. You had no right to do this without first consulting me. You had no right to suppose I would be a party to such a thing. I will not accept the sacrifice. I will not, Sir Oliver.”
“Indeed, you have no choice, thank God!” he answered her. “But you are astray in your conclusions. It is I alone who have brought this fate upon myself. It is the very proper fruit of my insensate deed. It recoils upon me as all evil must upon him that does it.” He shrugged his shoulders as if to dismiss the matter. Then in a changed voice, a voice singularly timid, soft, and gentle, “it were perhaps too much to ask,” said he, “that you should forgive me all the suffering I have brought you?”
“I think,” she answered him, “that it is for me to beg forgiveness of you.”
“Of me?”
“For my unfaith, which has been the source of all. For my readiness to believe evil of you five years ago, for having burnt unread your letter and the proof of your innocence that accompanied it.”
He smiled upon her very kindly. “I think you said your instinct guided you. Even though I had not done the thing imputed to me, your instinct knew me for evil; and your instinct was right, for evil I am﻿—I must be. These are your own words. But do not think that I mock you with them. I have come to recognize their truth.”
She stretched out her hands to him. “If﻿ ﻿… if I were to say that I have come to realize the falsehood of all that?”
“I should understand it to be the charity which your pitiful heart extends to one in my extremity. Your instinct was not at fault.”
“It was! It was!”
But he was not to be driven out of his conviction. He shook his head, his countenance gloomy. “No man who was not evil could have done by you what I have done, however deep the provocation. I perceive it clearly now﻿—as men in their last hour perceive hidden things.”
“Oh, why are you so set on death?” she cried upon a despairing note.
“I am not,” he answered with a swift resumption of his more habitual manner. “ ’Tis death that is so set on me. But at least I meet it without fear or regret. I face it as we must all face the inevitable﻿—the gifts from the hands of destiny. And I am heartened﻿—gladdened almost﻿—by your sweet forgiveness.”
She rose suddenly, and came to him.

===Chinese===

“我明白了。”她说。“我明白了。这命数是我带给你的。你打发莱昂内尔去办那桩差使时，是自愿献上你的性命，换我回到我自己人中间。你不该不先问我一声就作这个主。你无权设想我会同意做这样一桩事的同谋。我不接受这样的牺牲。我不接受，奥利弗爵士。”

“说实在的，你也没有选择的余地，感谢上帝！”他答她。“不过你的结论下错了。把我送上这命数的，是我一个人。这是我那桩丧心病狂之举应得恰如其分的果子。一切恶事怎样回到作恶者头上，它也就怎样回到我头上。”他耸耸肩，像要搁开这话题。随即换了个声口，一种出奇胆怯、柔软、温存的声口，“要你饶恕我带给你的一切苦楚，”他说，“恐怕是过分的要求吧？”

“我看，”她答他，“该求饶恕的是我。”

“求我？”

“为我的负心，它是一切的源头。为五年前我那样轻易信了你的恶；为我把你的信、连同随信寄来的清白凭据，看也没看就烧了。”

他望着她，目光十分和善。“我记得你说过，是你的直觉指的路。哪怕我没有担那个罪名，你的直觉也认定我是恶的；你的直觉没有错，因为我就是恶的——必然是恶的。这是你自己的话。可你别以为我拿这话讥诮你。我如今认出这些话是真的了。”

她向他伸出手去。“如果……如果我说，我如今明白那一切全是虚妄的呢？”

“我会把它理解为你的慈悲心，向你绝境中的一个人递来的慈悲。你的直觉没有错。”

“错了！错了！”

可他不让自己的信念被攻破。他摇头，面色阴沉。“一个不恶的人，任凭受了多深的刺激，也做不出我对你是过的那些事。这一点我现在看得清清楚楚——像人在临终的时辰看清隐秘的事一样。”

“哦，你为什么这样一门心思求死？”她绝望地喊。

“我没有。”他答，一下子又摆回惯常的做派。“是死一门心思找我。可至少我迎它不带惧，不带悔。我面对它，如同我们都得面对那无可回避的事——命运手里的馈赠。而你的宽恕给了我胆气——几乎给了我欢喜。”

她猛地站起来，走到他跟前。

===Original===

She caught his arm, and standing very close to him, looked up now into his face.
“We have need to forgive each other, you and I, Oliver,” she said. “And since forgiveness effaces all, let﻿ ﻿… let all that has stood between us these last five years be now effaced.”
He caught his breath as he looked down into her white, straining face.
“Is it impossible for us to go back five years? Is it impossible for us to go back to where we stood in those old days at Godolphin Court?”
The light that had suddenly been kindled in his face faded slowly, leaving it grey and drawn. His eyes grew clouded with sorrow and despair.
“Who has erred must abide by his error﻿—and so must the generations that come after him. There is no going back ever. The gates of the past are tight-barred against us.”
“Then let us leave them so. Let us turn our backs upon that past, you and I, and let us set out afresh together, and so make amends to each other for what our folly has lost to us in those years.”
He set his hands upon her shoulders, and held her so at arm’s length from him considering her with very tender eyes.
“Sweet lady!” he murmured, and sighed heavily. “God! How happy might we not have been but for that evil chance.﻿ ﻿…” He checked abruptly. His hands fell from her shoulders to his sides, he half-turned away, brusque now in tone and manner. “I grow maudlin. Your sweet pity has so softened me that I had almost spoke of love; and what have I to do with that? Love belongs to life; love is life; whilst I﻿ ﻿… *Moriturus te salutat!*”
“Ah, no, no!” She was clinging to him again with shaking hands, her eyes wild.
“It is too late,” he answered her. “There is no bridge can span the pit I have dug myself. I must go down into it as cheerfully as God will let me.”
“Then,” she cried in sudden exaltation, “I will go down with you. At the last, at least, we shall be together.”
“Now here is midsummer frenzy!” he protested, yet there was a tenderness in the very impatience of his accents. He stroked the golden head that lay against his shoulder. “How shall that help me?” he asked her. “Would you embitter my last hour﻿—rob death of all its glory? Nay, Rosamund, you can serve me better far by living. Return to England, and publish there the truth of what you have learnt.

===Chinese===

她抓住他的手臂，站得离他极近，仰起脸望着他。

“你我彼此都有一笔饶恕要给，奥利弗。”她说。“饶恕既能抹去一切，那就……就让横在你我之间这五年的种种，此刻一并抹去吧。”

他望着她那张苍白而竭力的脸，呼吸屏住了。

“我们回不到五年前了么？我们回不到从前在戈多尔芬府的那些日子了么？”

他脸上霎时燃起的光，慢慢黯下去，只留下一片灰败紧绷。他的眼睛蒙上了哀伤和绝望。

“铸成过错的人必受他的过错拘管——连他以后的世代也一样。回头路永远没有。往昔的大门对我们门闩紧闭。”

“那就让它闭着吧。让我们把那段往昔抛在身后，你和我，让我们重新一道上路，为我们这痴愚在那些年里丢失的东西，彼此补报。”

他双手搭上她的肩，隔着一臂把她端详，目光无限温存。

“亲爱的小姐！”他喃喃道，重重叹了口气。“上帝！若不是那场厄运，我们本可以多幸福。……”他猛地收住。他的手从她肩上滑落，半转过身去，口气和神态又变得粗硬起来。“我感伤了。你甜甜的怜悯把我泡软了，差点说出爱这个字；可这字与我何干？爱属于生；爱就是生；而我……*将死之人向你致敬！*”

“啊，不，不！”她又抓住他，双手发抖，眼神狂乱。

“太迟了。”他答她。“没有桥能跨过我自己掘下的坑。我必须下去——上帝容我怎样欢愉地下去，我就怎样下去。”

“那么，”她在骤然的激扬中喊道，“我同你一起下去。到了最后，至少我们在一起。”

“这可真是仲夏的癫狂！”他抗议道，可那不耐烦的声调里偏透着温存。他抚着偎在他肩头的金发脑袋。“那能帮我什么呢？”他问她。“你要苦我最后的一个时辰么——把死的荣光都夺去？不，罗莎蒙德，你活着，才最能帮我。回英格兰去，把你知道的真相昭告天下。

===Original===

Be yours the task of clearing my honour of this stain upon it, proclaiming the truth of what drove me to the infamy of becoming a renegade and a corsair.” He started from her. “Hark! What’s that?”
From without had come a sudden cry, “Afoot! To arms! To arms! Holâ! *Balâk!* *Balâk*!”
“It is the hour,” he said, and turning from her suddenly sprang to the entrance and plucked aside the curtain.

===Chinese===

洗清我名誉上的这个污点，宣告是什么把我逼上了叛教为盗的不归路——这差事就交给你。”他从她身边猛然一挣。“听！那是什么？”

外头传来一声呐喊：“起来！拿家伙！拿家伙！喂嗬！*巴拉亚克*！*巴拉亚克*！”

“时辰到了。”他说，猛然转身，一个箭步蹿到门口，一把掀开帘子。

## VIII Mother and Son / 八 母与子
===Original===

Early on the morrow﻿—so early that scarce had the Shehad been recited﻿—came Biskaine-el-Borak to the Basha. He had just landed from a galley which had come upon a Spanish fishing boat, aboard of which there was a young Morisco who was being conducted over seas to Algiers. The news of which the fellow was the bearer was of such urgency that for twenty hours without intermission the slaves had toiled at the oars of Biskaine’s vessel﻿—the *capitana* of his fleet﻿—to bring her swiftly home.
The Morisco had a cousin﻿—a New-Christian like himself, and like himself, it would appear, still a Muslim at heart﻿—who was employed in the Spanish treasury at Malaga. This man had knowledge that a galley was fitting out for sea to convey to Naples the gold destined for the pay of the Spanish troops in garrison there. Through parsimony this treasure galley was to be afforded no escort, but was under orders to hug the coast of Europe, where she should be safe from all piratical surprise. It was judged that she would be ready to put to sea in a week, and the Morisco had set out at once to bring word of it to his Algerine brethren that they might intercept and capture her.
Asad thanked the young Morisco for his news, bade him be housed and cared for, and promised him a handsome share of the plunder should the treasure galley be captured. That done he sent for Sakr-el-Bahr, whilst Marzak, who had been present at the interview, went with the tale of it to his mother, and beheld her fling into a passion when he added that it was Sakr-el-Bahr had been summoned that he might be entrusted with this fresh expedition, thus proving that all her crafty innuendoes and insistent warnings had been so much wasted labour.
With Marzak following at her heels, she swept like a fury into the darkened room where Asad took his ease.
“What is this I hear, O my lord?” she cried, in tone and manner more the European shrew than the submissive Eastern slave. “Is Sakr-el-Bahr to go upon this expedition against the treasure galley of Spain?”
Reclining on his divan he looked her up and down with a languid eye. “Dost know of any better fitted to succeed?” quoth he.
“I know of one whom it is my lord’s duty to prefer to that foreign adventurer. One who is entirely faithful and entirely to be trusted. One who does not attempt to retain for himself a portion of the booty garnered in the name of Islam.”
“Bah!”

===Chinese===

次日一早——早得舍哈达都还没来得及诵起——比斯坎-埃尔-博拉克就来见帕夏。他刚从一条桨帆船上岸，那船在海上撞见一条西班牙渔船，船上有个年轻的摩里斯科人，正被押送渡海去阿尔及尔。这家伙捎来的消息十万火急，为了把船赶快送回家，奴隶们在他那条船——他舰队的旗舰——的桨位上不歇手地划了二十个钟头。

那摩里斯科人有个表亲——同他一样是新基督徒，也一样，看样子，心里仍是穆斯林——在马拉加的西班牙王室财库当差。这人得知一条桨帆船正整装待发，要把发往那不勒斯驻军饷银的黄金运过去。出于吝啬，这条饷银船不派护航，只奉命贴着欧洲海岸走，以为那样就万无一失，不怕海盗的突袭。据估计一星期内即可出航，那摩里斯科人立刻动身，把这消息捎给阿尔及尔的教胞，好让他们半路截住它、拿下它。

阿萨德谢过那年轻摩里斯科人的消息，吩咐给他安置住所、好生照应，许诺饷银船若被劫下，分他一份厚利。事毕，他传萨克埃尔-巴尔来；马尔扎克方才在场，便把这事讲给他母亲听；待他添上一句：传萨克埃尔-巴尔来，正是要把这场新的出征托付给他——足见她那些狡黠的暗示和絮叨的警告全是白费唇舌——他看着她勃然大怒。

她拖着尾随在后的马尔扎克，像一股狂风杀进那间阿萨德闲坐的暗室。

“我听见的是什么事，哦我的大人？”她嚷道，那声口做派，更像欧洲的泼妇，不像恭顺的东方女奴。“萨克埃尔-巴尔要去出征，劫西班牙的饷银船？”

他斜倚在长榻上，慵懒地把她从头到脚打量了一遍。“你知道还有谁更干得了这桩事？”他说。

“我知道有一个，我的大人理当把他摆在那外路冒险家前头。一个十足忠心、十足可靠的人。一个不会扣下以伊斯兰名义掳来的赃物、私藏一份的人。”

“呸！”

===Original===

said Asad. “Wilt thou talk forever of those two slaves? And who may be this paragon of thine?”
“Marzak,” she answered fiercely, flinging out an arm to drag forward her son. “Is he to waste his youth here in softness and idleness? But yesternight that ribald mocked him with his lack of scars. Shall he take scars in the orchard of the Kasbah here? Is he to be content with those that come from the scratch of a bramble, or is he to learn to be a fighter and leader of the Children of the Faith that himself he may follow in the path his father trod?”
“Whether he so follows,” said Asad, “is as the Sultan of Istambul, the Sublime Portal, shall decree. We are but his vicegerents here.”
“But shall the Grand Sultan appoint him to succeed thee if thou hast not equipped him so to do? I cry shame on thee, O father of Marzak, for that thou art lacking in due pride in thine own son.”
“May Allah give me patience with thee! Have I not said that he is still over young.”
“At his age thyself thou wert upon the seas, serving with the great Ochiali.”
“At his age I was, by the favour of Allah, taller and stronger than is he. I cherish him too dearly to let him go forth and perchance be lost to me before his strength is full grown.”
“Look at him,” she commanded. “He is a man, Asad, and such a son as another might take pride in. Is it not time he girt a scimitar about his waist and trod the poop of one of thy galleys?”
“Indeed, indeed, O my father!” begged Marzak himself.
“What?” barked the old Moor. “And is it so? And wouldst thou go forth then against the Spaniard? What knowledge hast thou that shall equip thee for such a task?”
“What can his knowledge be since his father has never been concerned to school him?” returned Fenzileh. “Dost thou sneer at shortcomings that are the natural fruits of thine own omissions?”
“I will be patient with thee,” said Asad, showing every sign of losing patience. “I will ask thee only if in thy judgment he is in case to win a victory for Islam? Answer me straightly now.”
