“Straightly I answer thee that he is not. And, as straightly, I tell thee that it is full time he were. Thy duty is to let him go upon this expedition that he may learn the trade that lies before him.”
Asad considered a moment. Then: “Be it so,” he answered slowly. “Shalt set forth, then, with Sakr-el-Bahr, my son.”
“With Sakr-el-Bahr?” cried Fenzilch aghast.

===Chinese===

阿萨德说。“那两个奴隶你还要念叨到几时？再说，你这位完人又是谁？”

“马尔扎克。”她凶狠地答，一伸手臂把儿子拽到前头。“难道就让他的青春浪费在这儿的温软和慵懒里？就在昨晚，那个狂徒还拿他身上没疤讥笑他。难道要他到卡斯巴的果园里去挣疤？让他满足于荆棘划出来的几道印子，还是让他学做一个战士、一个信士之子的统领，好踏上他父亲踏过的路？”

“他踏不踏上，”阿萨德说，“得由伊斯坦布尔的苏丹、至高的门户裁断。我们在此地不过是他的代理人。”

“可要是你不曾把他装备起来，大苏丹又怎会任命他接你的班？我为你害羞，哦马尔扎克之父，你竟缺少对自己亲儿子应有的骄傲。”

“愿安拉赐我容忍你的耐性！我不是说了么，他还太年轻。”

“在他这个年纪，你自己已经在海上，跟着伟大的奥恰利效力了。”

“在他这个年纪，托安拉的恩，我比他高，比他壮。我疼他疼得太紧，舍不得放他出去，怕他力气还没长足就折在我手里。”

“你看看他。”她命令道。“他是个男人了，阿萨德，是别人家要以之为荣的那种儿子。他腰里束上弯刀、踏上你哪条桨帆船的艉楼，不是正当其时么？”

“正是，正是，父亲！”马尔扎克自己也恳求道。

“什么？”老摩尔人咆哮。“哦？这么说你也想去了？想去跟西班牙人交手了？你有什么本事，配当这个差？”

“他能有本事什么呢，他父亲从来就没操心过调教他？”芬齐莱回敬。“你自己荒疏出来的果子，倒好意思讥笑？”

“我且容忍你。”阿萨德说，一脸快要忍不住的样子。“我只问你一句：依你判断，他这就能为伊斯兰打胜仗了么？直截了当地答我。”

“我直截了当答你：不能。可我也直截了当告诉你：早该能了。放他去走这一趟，学学摆在他面前的行当，是你的本分。”

阿萨德沉吟了片刻。然后：“就这样吧。”他缓缓地答。“那么你就出发，随萨克埃尔-巴尔去，我的儿子。”

“随萨克埃尔-巴尔？”芬齐莱惊呼。

===Original===

“I could find him no better preceptor.”
“Shall thy son go forth as the servant of another?”
“As the pupil,” Asad amended. “What else?”
“Were I a man, O fountain of my soul,” said she, “and had I a son, none but myself should be his preceptor. I should so mould and fashion him that he should be another me. That, O my dear lord, is thy duty to Marzak. Entrust not his training to another and to one whom despite thy love for him I cannot trust. Go forth thyself upon this expedition with Marzak here for thy *kayia*.”
Asad frowned. “I grow too old,” he said. “I have not been upon the seas these two years past. Who can say that I may not have lost the art of victory. No, no.” He shook his head, and his face grew overcast and softened by wistfulness. “Sakr-el-Bahr commands this time, and if Marzak goes, he goes with him.”
“My lord.﻿ ﻿…” she began, then checked. A Nubian had entered to announce that Sakr-el-Bahr was come and was awaiting the orders of his lord in the courtyard. Asad rose instantly and for all that Fenzileh, greatly daring as ever, would still have detained him, he shook her off impatiently, and went out.
She watched his departure with anger in those dark lovely eyes of hers, an anger that went near to filming them in tears, and after he had passed out into the glaring sunshine beyond the door, a silence dwelt in the cool darkened chamber﻿—a silence disturbed only by distant trills of silvery laughter from the lesser women of the Basha’s house. The sound jarred her taut nerves. She moved with an oath and beat her hands together. To answer her came a negress, lithe and muscular as a wrestler and naked to the waist; the slave ring in her ear was of massive gold.
“Bid them make an end of that screeching,” she snapped to vent some of her fierce petulance. “Tell them I will have the rods to them if they again disturb me.”
The negress went out, and silence followed, for those other lesser ladies of the Basha’s harem were more obedient to the commands of Fenzileh than to those of the Basha himself.
Then she drew her son to the fretted lattice commanding the courtyard, a screen from behind which they could see and hear all that passed out yonder. Asad was speaking, informing Sakr-el-Bahr of what he had learnt, and what there was to do.
“How soon canst thou put to sea again?” he ended.

===Chinese===

“我给他找不到更好的师傅了。”

“你的儿子要出去给人当仆从？”

“当学生。”阿萨德更正。“不然当什么？”

“我若是个男人，哦我灵魂的泉源，”她说，“又有个儿子，那么除了我自己，谁也不配做他的师傅。我要把他陶冶成又一个我。那才是你对马尔扎克的本分，哦我亲爱的大人。别把他的调教托给旁人，托给一个——尽管你爱他——我信不过的人。你自己出征吧，带上马尔扎克做你的副手。”

阿萨德皱起眉。“我太老了。”他说。“这两年我不曾下过海。谁说我没有丢掉打胜仗的手艺？不，不。”他摇摇头，脸上罩上一层阴云，又被一种怅惘软化。“这回萨克埃尔-巴尔指挥；马尔扎克若去，就随他去。”

“大人……”她才开口，又顿住。一个努比亚人进来禀报：萨克埃尔-巴尔到了，正在院子里候他主上的吩咐。阿萨德立刻起身；芬齐莱素来大胆，还想拉住他，他不耐烦地挣开，出去了。

她用她那双可爱的黑眼睛含怒望着他的背影，那怒气险些化作一汪泪水；他跨进门外的烈日之后，这间清凉的暗室里落下一片寂静——只除了帕夏宅里低位女眷们远远荡来的一串银铃似的笑声。那声音刺得她绷紧的神经生疼。她动了动，咒骂一声，拍拍手掌。应声而来一个女黑奴，像角力手一样精壮柔韧，赤裸到腰，耳上的奴环是足金的。

“叫她们别嚎了。”她厉声说，撒出一点火气。“告诉她们，再搅扰我，就请她们吃鞭子。”

女奴出去了，寂静随之而来——后宫那些低位女眷，对芬齐莱的命令，比对帕夏本人的还服从。

随后她把儿子拉到那面俯瞰院子的雕花格栅窗前——这道屏风后头，外头的一切他们看得见、听得着。阿萨德正在说话，把他得来的消息、要办的事由，告诉萨克埃尔-巴尔。

“多快你能再出海？”他末了问。

===Original===

“As soon as the service of Allah and thyself require,” was the prompt answer.
“It is well, my son.” Asad laid a hand, affectionately upon the corsair’s shoulder, entirely conquered by this readiness. “Best set out at sunrise tomorrow. Thou’lt need so long to make thee ready for the sea.”
“Then by thy leave I go forthwith to give orders to prepare,” replied Sakr-el-Bahr, for all that he was a little troubled in his mind by this need to depart again so soon.
“What galleys shalt thou take?”
“To capture one galley of Spain? My own *galeasse*, no more; she will be full equal to such an enterprise, and I shall be the better able, then, to lurk and take cover﻿—a thing which might well prove impossible with a fleet.”
“Ay﻿—thou art wise in thy daring,” Asad approved him. “May Allah prosper thee upon the voyage.”
“Have I thy leave to go?”
“A moment yet. There is my son Marzak. He is approaching manhood, and it is time he entered the service of Allah and the State. It is my desire that he sail as thy lieutenant on this voyage, and that thou be his preceptor even as I was thine of old.”
Now here was something that pleased Sakr-el-Bahr as little as it pleased Marzak. Knowing the bitter enmity borne him by the son of Fenzileh he had every cause to fear trouble if this project of Asad’s were realized.
“As I was thine of old!” he answered with crafty wistfulness. “Wilt thou not put to sea with us tomorrow, O Asad? There is none like thee in all Islam, and what a joy were it not to stand beside thee on the prow as of old when we grapple with the Spaniard.”
Asad considered him. “Dost thou, too, urge this?” quoth he.
“Have others urged it?” The man’s sharp wits, rendered still sharper by his sufferings, were cutting deeply and swiftly into this matter. “They did well, but none could have urged it more fervently than I, for none knows so well as I the joy of battle against the infidel under thy command and the glory of prevailing in thy sight. Come, then, my lord, upon this enterprise, and be thyself thine own son’s preceptor since ’tis the highest honour thou canst bestow upon him.”
Thoughtfully Asad stroked his long white beard, his eagle eyes growing narrow. “Thou temptest me, by Allah!”
“Let me do more.﻿ ﻿…”
“Nay, more thou canst not. I am old and worn, and I am needed here. Shall an old lion hunt a young gazelle? Peace, peace! The sun has set upon my fighting day.

===Chinese===

“安拉和您用得着我多快，就多快。”回答毫不迟疑。

“这就对了，我的儿子。”阿萨德一只手亲热地搭上海盗的肩，全然被这份爽利折服。“顶好明日日出启程。备航这点时辰你总要的。”

“那么请恕我即刻去传令备航。”萨克埃尔-巴尔回答——尽管又要这么快离程，他心里其实有点发沉。

“你带哪几条船？”

“对付西班牙一条桨帆船？就我那条加莱塞帆桨船，一条足矣；对付这样的差使它绰绰有余，而且我也能更好藏身、掩袭——带一支舰队，这事多半就办不成了。”

“啊——你的大胆里见智谋。”阿萨德赞许他。“愿安拉保你此行顺逐。”

“容我告退？”

“且慢。还有我儿子马尔扎克。他快成年了，该入安拉和国家的门下效力了。我的意思是，这一趟他随行，做你的副将，而你做他的师傅，就像当年我做你的一样。”

这话让萨克埃尔-巴尔高兴的程度，实在不比马尔扎克高多少。他深知芬齐莱之子对他怀的那份死仇，阿萨德这打算若成，他有一切理由担心出乱子。

“就像当年我做你的一样！”他答道，带着机巧的怅惘。“您明日不与我们一同出海么，哦阿萨德？整个伊斯兰没有一个比得上您，同西班牙人扭杀的时候，若能像当年那样同您并肩立在船头，那是何等的快活！”

阿萨德端详他。“你也在撺掇这个？”他说。

“还有别人撺掯？”这男人的尖利心窍，经苦难磨得愈发尖利，此刻正又深又快地往这件事里剜。“那他们撺掯得好。可谁也撺掯不过我这么恳切，因为没有人比我更知道：在您的麾下同不信者厮杀是何等的快活，在您的眼前得胜是何等的荣光。来吧，大人，这一趟亲自出马，亲自做您儿子的师傅——这是您能赐他的最高的体面了。”

阿萨德若有所思地捋着长长的白须，鹰眼渐渐眯细。“你这是诱惑我，凭安拉！”

“容我做得更多。……”

“不，多不得了。我又老又乏，此地也离不得我。老狮子还去猎小羚羊么？罢了，罢了！我的战斗之日，太阳已经落了。

===Original===

Let the brood of fighters I have raised up keep that which my arm conquered and maintain my name and the glory of the Faith upon the seas.” He leaned upon Sakr-el-Bahr’s shoulder and sighed, his eyes wistfully dreamy. “It were a fond adventure in good truth. But no﻿ ﻿… I am resolved. Go thou and take Marzak with thee, and bring him safely home again.”
“I should not return myself else,” was the answer. “But my trust is in the All-knowing.”
Upon that he departed, dissembling his profound vexation both at the voyage and the company, and went to bid Othmani make ready his great *galeasse*, equipping it with carronades, three hundred slaves to row it, and three hundred fighting men.
Asad-ed-Din returned to that darkened room in the Kasbah overlooking the courtyard, where Fenzileh and Marzak still lingered. He went to tell them that in compliance with the desires of both Marzak should go forth to prove himself upon this expedition.
But where he had left impatience he found thinly veiled wrath.
“O sun that warms me,” Fenzileh greeted him, and from long experience he knew that the more endearing were her epithets the more vicious was her mood, “do then my counsels weigh as naught with thee, are they but as the dust upon thy shoes?”
“Less,” said Asad, provoked out of his habitual indulgence of her licences of speech.
“That is the truth, indeed!” she cried, bowing her head, whilst behind her the handsome face of her son was overcast.
“It is,” Asad agreed. “At dawn, Marzak, thou settest forth upon the *galeasse* of Sakr-el-Bahr to take the seas under his tutelage and to emulate the skill and valour that have rendered him the stoutest bulwark of Islam, the very javelin of Allah.”
But Marzak felt that in this matter his mother was to be supported, whilst his detestation of this adventurer who threatened to usurp the place that should rightly be his own spurred him to mad lengths of daring.
“When I take the seas with that dog-descended Nasrani,” he answered hoarsely, “he shall be where rightly he belongs﻿—at the rowers’ bench.”
“How?” It was a bellow of rage. Upon the word Asad swung to confront his son, and his face, suddenly inflamed, was so cruel and evil in its expression that it terrified that intriguing pair. “By the beard of the Prophet! what words are these to me?”

===Chinese===

让我养大的这一窝猛禽守我的臂膀打下的江山，替我在海上保住我的名声和正教的荣光吧。”他倚着萨克埃尔-巴尔的肩头叹了口气，眼里漾着怅惘的梦意。“这实在是一桩快意的壮举，不假。可是不……我拿定主意了。你去，带上马尔扎克，把他平平安安带回来。”

“不然我自己也不回来了。”回答是。“不过我信靠那全知的主。”

说罢他去了，把他对这趟航程、对这同伴的满腔懊恼一齐掩住，去吩咐奥斯曼尼备他那条大加莱塞帆桨船：装上舰炮，配三百名划桨的奴隶、三百名战士。

阿萨德-艾德-丁回到卡斯巴那间俯瞰院子的暗室，芬齐莱和马尔扎克还留在那里。他去告诉他们：顺应他们两造的心愿，马尔扎克将随这次出征出海，去证明自己。

可他离开时留下的是焦灼，迎接他的却是薄薄一层裹不住的狂怒。

“哦晒暖我的太阳，”芬齐莱迎着他——凭多年的经验他知道，她的称呼越亲热，心气就越恶毒，“这么说，我的进言在你这里斤两全无，不过是你鞋上的尘土？”

“连尘土都不如。”阿萨德说，被激得出了一向纵容她口无遮拦的惯常脾气。

“这话倒是不假！”她喊，垂下头去；她身后，她儿子那张漂亮脸也阴沉下来。

“是不假。”阿萨德承认。“天一亮，马尔扎克，你就登上萨克埃尔-巴尔的加莱塞帆桨船出海，受他的教导，学学那身本事和勇武——正是它们，使他成了伊斯兰最坚固的堡垒、安拉手中的一杆标枪。”

可马尔扎克觉得，这件事上母亲须得撑住，而他对这个威胁要篡夺他正当地位的冒险家的憎恶，更是把他刺到了发狂的地步。

“等我同那狗杂种拿撒勒人下海的时候，”他嘶声答道，“他就得待在他该待的地方——桨凳上。”

“什么？”这是一声狂怒的咆哮。话音未落，阿萨德已转身逼向儿子，那张骤然涨红的脸，神情那样凶残邪恶，吓得这对弄权的母子心胆俱裂。“凭先知的胡须！你跟我说的是什么话？”

===Original===

He advanced upon Marzak until Fenzileh in sudden terror stepped between and faced him, like a lioness springing to defend her cub. But the Basha, enraged now by this want of submission in his son, enraged both against that son and the mother who he knew had prompted him, caught her in his sinewy old hands, and flung her furiously aside, so that she stumbled and fell in a panting heap amid the cushions of her divan.
“The curse of Allah upon thee!” he screamed, and Marzak recoiled before him. “Has this presumptuous hellcat who bore thee taught thee to stand before my face, to tell me what thou wilt and wilt not do? By the Koran! too long have I endured her evil foreign ways, and now it seems she has taught thee how to tread them after her and how to beard thy very father! Tomorrow thou’lt take the sea with Sakr-el-Bahr, I have said it. Another word and thou’lt go aboard his *galeasse* even as thou saidst should be the case with him﻿—at the rowers’ bench, to learn submission under the slave master’s whip.”
Terrified, Marzak stood numb and silent, scarcely daring to draw breath. Never in all his life had he seen his father in a rage so royal. Yet it seemed to inspire no fear in Fenzileh, that congenital shrew whose tongue not even the threat of rods or hooks could silence.
“I shall pray Allah to restore sight to thy soul, O father of Marzak,” she panted, “to teach thee to discriminate between those that love thee and the self-seekers that abuse thy trust.”
“How!” he roared at her. “Art not yet done?”
“Nor ever shall be until I am lain dumb in death for having counselled thee out of my great love, O light of these poor eyes of mine.”
“Maintain this tone,” he said, with concentrated anger, “and that will soon befall.”
“I care not so that the sleek mask be plucked from the face of that dog-descended Sakr-el-Bahr. May Allah break his bones! What of those slaves of his﻿—those two from England, O Asad? I am told that one is a woman, tall and of that white beauty which is the gift of Eblis to these Northerners. What is his purpose with her﻿—that he would not show her in the *sôk* as the law prescribes, but comes slinking here to beg thee set aside the law for him? Ha! I talk in vain. I have shown thee graver things to prove his vile disloyalty, and yet thou’lt fawn upon him whilst thy fangs are bared to thine own son.”

===Chinese===

他朝马尔扎克进逼，芬齐莱骤然心慌，抢步横在中间护住儿子，像一头扑上来护崽的母狮。可帕夏这下被儿子的忤逆激怒了——对儿子怒，也对那他明知在背后挑唆的母亲怒——用他那双筋腱犹在的老手抓住她，狠狠往旁一摔，她踉跄着跌进长榻的软垫里，喘作一团。

“安拉的诅咒降在你头上！”他尖声喊，马尔扎克直往后缩。“是这个把你生出来的放肆地狱猫，教会你站在我面前，教你什么肯做、什么不肯做的么？凭《古兰经》！她那套邪恶的洋做派我忍得够久了，如今看来她还教会了你踩着她的脚印走，教会了你顶撞你的亲父亲！明天你就随萨克埃尔-巴尔出海，我说定了。再多一句嘴，你就上他的加莱塞帆桨船——照你方才说他的那样——去桨凳上，在奴隶监工的鞭子底下学学什么叫恭顺。”

马尔扎克吓呆了，麻木地站着，大气也不敢出。他一生从没见过父亲发这样雷霆之怒。可这怒火似乎一点也吓不住芬齐莱——这个天生的泼妇，鞭子和铁钩的威胁都堵不住她的舌头。

“我要求告安拉，让他照亮你灵魂的眼，哦马尔扎克之父，”她喘着气说，“教你分一分哪些人爱你，哪些自私自利的人糟蹋你的信任。”

“怎么！”他朝她咆哮。“还没完？”

“永远完不了，除非我因进忠言而被弄死闭了嘴——那忠言出于我对你浩大的爱，哦我这双苦眼的亮光。”

“再拿这个腔调，”他攒足了怒气说，“你很快就会如愿。”

“我不怕，只要能撕下那条狗杂种萨克埃尔-巴尔脸上那层油光的面皮。愿安拉断他的骨头！他那两个奴隶算什么——那两个英格兰来的，哦阿萨德？听说有一个是女人，高挑个儿，一身北方人特有的白——那白是易卜劣斯赠给北方人的礼物。他打她什么主意——不肯依法把她送上市场去亮亮相，反倒蹑手蹑脚溜到这儿来，求你为他把法律搁在一旁？哈！我说了也是白说。比这更重的、足证他那卑劣背信的事，我都摆给你看了，你却还朝他摇尾，倒把獠牙露给自己的儿子。”

===Original===

He advanced upon her, stooped, caught her by the wrist, and heaved her up.
His face showed grey under its deep tan. His aspect terrified her at last and made an end of her reckless forward courage.
He raised his voice to call.
“*Ya anta!* Ayoub!”
She gasped, livid in her turn with sudden terror. “My lord, my lord!” she whimpered. “Stream of my life, be not angry! What wilt thou do?”
He smiled evilly. “Do?” he growled. “What I should have done ten years ago and more. We’ll have the rods to thee.” And again he called, more insistently﻿—“Ayoub!”
“My lord, my lord!” she gasped in shuddering horror now that at last she found him set upon the thing to which so often she had dared him. “Pity! Pity!” She grovelled and embraced his knees. “In the name of the Pitying the Pitiful be merciful upon the excesses to which my love for thee may have driven this poor tongue of mine. O my sweet lord! O father of Marzak!”
Her distress, her beauty, and perhaps, more than either, her unusual humility and submission may have moved him. For even as at that moment Ayoub﻿—the sleek and portly eunuch, who was her *wazeer* and chamberlain﻿—loomed in the inner doorway, salaaming, he vanished again upon the instant, dismissed by a peremptory wave of the Basha’s hand.
Asad looked down upon her, sneering. “That attitude becomes thee best,” he said. “Continue it in future.” Contemptuously he shook himself free of her grasp, turned and stalked majestically out, wearing his anger like a royal mantle, and leaving behind him two terror-shaken beings, who felt as if they had looked over the very edge of death.
There was a long silence between them. Then at long length Fenzileh rose and crossed to the *meshrabiyah*﻿—the latticed window-box. She opened it and took from one of its shelves an earthenware jar, placed there so as to receive the slightest breeze. From it she poured water into a little cup and drank greedily. That she could perform this menial service for herself when a mere clapping of hands would have brought slaves to minister to her need betrayed something of her disordered state of mind.
She slammed the inner lattice and turned to Marzak.
“And now?” quoth she.
“Now?” said the lad.
“Ay, what now? What are we to do? Are we to lie crushed under his rage until we are ruined indeed? He is bewitched. That jackal has enchanted him, so that he must deem well done all that is done by him.

===Chinese===

他朝她进逼，俯身，一把攥住她的手腕，将她整个人提了起来。

他深褐的脸皮底下透出灰色。他这副神气终于吓住了她，扑灭了她那不管不顾的莽勇。

他扬声喊人。

“喂，你！阿尤布！”

她倒抽一口气，轮到她刷地没了血色。“大人，大人！”她呜咽。“我生命的溪流，别动怒！你要做什么？”

他狞笑一声。“做什么？”他咆哮。“做我十多年前就该做的事。请你吃鞭子。”他又喊，更急了——“阿尤布！”

“大人，大人！”她喘着气，一阵战栗的恐惧——他屡次受过她激将的那件事，他如今终于铁了心要办。“发发慈悲！发发慈悲！”她跪伏下去，抱住他的双膝。“看在那慈悲者的慈悲上，饶了我这条可怜舌头的放肆吧——那是爱你爱出来的呀。哦我亲爱的大人！哦马尔扎克之父！”

她的哀恳，她的美貌，也许更兼那份她罕有的低眉顺目，或许打动了他。正当此时，阿尤布——那个油光水滑、肥头大耳的宦官，她的管家兼内侍总管——已出现在内门口打躬，却在一瞬之间又不见了，被帕夏一挥手打发掉了。

阿萨德低头看她，一脸讥诮。“这个姿态最适合你。”他说。“往后保持下去。”他不屑地挣脱她的搂抱，转身大步庄严地出去，把他的怒气当一件王袍披挂着，留下两个魂飞魄散的人，只觉得自己方才在死亡的崖沿上探过头去。

两人之间是长长的沉默。终于，芬齐莱起身走到那扇雕花格栅窗箱前。她打开它，从其中一格架子上取下一只陶罐——搁在那儿是为着接住哪怕最细的一丝风。她倒出一小杯水，贪婪地喝了。她本可以拍拍手掌唤奴隶来伺候，却亲自屈尊做了这桩贱役，足见她的心绪乱到了什么地步。

她砰地关上格栅窗，转身向着马尔扎克。

“现在呢？”她说。

“现在？”少年说。

“是啊，现在怎么办？咱们就趴在他的雷霆底下，趴到彻底完蛋么？他中了邪。那条豺狼迷住了他，害得那人干的一切，在他眼里都是干得好。

===Original===

Allah guide us here, Marzak, or thou’lt be trampled into dust by Sakr-el-Bahr.”
Marzak hung his head; slowly he moved to the divan and flung himself down upon its pillows; there he lay prone, his hands cupping his chin, his heels in the air.
“What can I do?” he asked at last.
“That is what I most desire to know. Something must be done, and soon. May his bones rot! If he lives thou art destroyed.”
“Ay,” said Marzak, with sudden vigour and significance. “If he lives!” And he sat up. “Whilst we plan and plot, and our plans and plots come to naught save to provoke the anger of my father, we might be better employed in taking the shorter way.”
She stood in the middle of the chamber, pondering him with gloomy eyes.
“I too have thought of that,” said she. “I could hire me men to do the thing for a handful of gold. But the risk of it.﻿ ﻿…”
“Where would be the risk once he is dead?”
“He might pull us down with him, and then what would our profit be in his death? Thy father would avenge him terribly.”
“If it were craftily done we should not be discovered.”
“Not be discovered?” she echoed, and laughed without mirth. “How young and blind thou art, O Marzak! We should be the first to be suspected. I have made no secret of my hate of him, and the people do not love me. They would urge thy father to do justice even were he himself averse to it, which I will not credit would be the case. This Sakr-el-Bahr﻿—may Allah wither him!﻿—is a god in their eyes. Bethink thee of the welcome given him! What Basha returning in triumph was ever greeted by the like? These victories that fortune has vouchsafed him have made them account him divinely favoured and protected. I tell thee, Marzak, that did thy father die tomorrow Sakr-el-Bahr would be proclaimed Basha of Algiers in his stead, and woe betide us then. And Asad-ed-Din grows old. True, he does not go forth to fight. He clings to life and may last long. But if he should not, and if Sakr-el-Bahr should still walk the earth when thy father’s destiny is fulfilled, I dare not think what then will be thy fate and mine.”
“May his grave be defiled!” growled Matzak.
“His grave?” said she. “The difficulty is to dig it for him without hurt to ourselves. Shaitan protects the dog.”
“May he make his bed in hell!” said Marzak.
“To curse him will not help us. Up, Marzak, and consider how the thing is to be done.”

===Chinese===

安拉在这儿指点我们吧，马尔扎克，不然你就要被萨克埃尔-巴尔踩成齑粉。”

马尔扎克垂着头；他慢慢踱到长榻边，一头栽进软垫里，趴在那里，双手托着下巴，脚跟翘在空中。

“我能做什么？”他终于开口。

“这正是我最想知道的。必须做点什么，而且要快。愿他烂骨头！只要他活着，你就是完了。”

“是啊。”马尔扎克说，突然来了劲，话里带着狠意。“只要他活着！”他坐起来。“咱们又密谋又盘算，谋来算去一事无成，只惹得我父亲动怒——倒不如走那条更短的路。”

她站在屋子中央，用阴沉的眼睛打量他。

“我也想过这一条。”她说。“我舍得一把金子，雇几个人替我办了它。可这干系……”

“等他死了，还有什么干系？”

“他兴许把咱们也一并拖下去，那他的死对咱们又有什么赚头？你父亲会为他报可怕的仇。”

“只要办得巧，咱们就不会被发现。”

“不会被发现？”她跟着念，笑得没有一点笑意。“你多年轻，多瞎啊，哦马尔扎克！头一批被疑心的就是咱们。我对他的恨，从来摆在明面上，老百姓也不爱我。就算你父亲自己不情愿——我不信他会不情愿——众人也会逼他主持公道。这个萨克埃尔-巴尔——愿安拉折损他！——在他们眼里是一尊神。你没见他们怎样迎接他么！哪个凯旋的帕夏受过那样的礼遇？命运赏给他的这一场场胜利，让他们把他当成蒙上天眷顾庇佑的人。我告诉你，马尔扎克，你父亲明天若是死了，接任阿尔及尔帕夏的就是萨克埃尔-巴尔，到那时咱们就遭殃了。而阿萨德-艾德-丁日见衰老。不错，他不出海打仗了。他惜命，兴许还拖得长。可他一旦不济，而萨克埃尔-巴尔在你父亲的气数应验之日仍行走人间——我不敢想你我的下场会是什么。”

“愿他的坟遭人践踏！”马尔扎克咆哮。

“他的坟？”她说。“难就难在：怎么给他掘这座坟，又不伤着咱们自己。沙伊坦护着那条狗。”

“愿他地狱里安床！”马尔扎克说。

“咒他不顶用。起来，马尔扎克，想想这事该怎么办。”

===Original===

Marzak came to his feet, nimble and supple as a greyhound.
“Listen now,” he said. “Since I must go this voyage with him, perchance upon the seas on some dark night opportunity may serve me.”
“Wait! Let me consider it. Allah guide me to find some way!” She beat her hands together and bade the slave girl who answered her to summon her *wazeer* Ayoub, and bid a litter be prepared for her. “We’ll to the *sôk*, O Marzak, and see these slaves of his. Who knows but that something may be done by means of them! Guile will serve us better than mere strength against that misbegotten son of shame.”
“May his house be destroyed!” said Marzak.

马尔扎克一跃而起，轻捷柔韧得像条灵缇。

“你听我说。”他说。“既然我必须同他走这一趟海，兴许在海上，哪个黑夜里，机会就到了我手上。”

“等等！让我想一想。愿安拉引我找出一条路来！”她拍拍手掌，吩咐应声而来的女奴去唤她的管家阿尤布，再备一乘肩舆。“咱们去市场，哦马尔扎克，去看看他那两个奴隶。说不定在那两个身上做得成什么文章！对付那个野杂种，使巧劲比使蛮力管用。”

“愿他的家宅塌掉！”马尔扎克说。

## Note / 作者注
===Original===

Lord Henry Goade, who had, as we shall see, some personal acquaintance with Sir Oliver Tressilian, tells us quite bluntly that he was ill-favoured. But then his lordship is addicted to harsh judgments and his perceptions are not always normal. He says, for instance, of Anne of Cleves, that she was the “ugliest woman that ever I saw.” As far as we can glean from his own voluminous writings it would seem to be extremely doubtful whether he ever saw Anne of Cleves at all, and we suspect him here of being no more than a slavish echo of the common voice, which attributed Cromwell’s downfall to the ugliness of this bride he procured for his Bluebeard master. To the common voice from the brush of Holbein, which permits us to form our own opinions and shows us a lady who is certainly very far from deserving his lordship’s harsh stricture. Similarly, I like to believe that Lord Henry was wrong in his pronouncement upon Sir Oliver, and I am encouraged in this belief by the pen-portrait which he himself appends to it. “He was,” he says, “a tall, powerful fellow of a good shape, if we except that his arms were too long and that his feet and hands were of an uncomely bigness. In face he was swarthy, with black hair and a black forked beard; his nose was big and very high in the bridge, and his eyes sunk deep under beetling eyebrows were very pale-coloured and very cruel and sinister. He had﻿—and this I have ever remarked to be the sign of great virility in a man﻿—a big, deep, rough voice, better suited to, and no doubt oftener employed in, quarterdeck oaths and foulnesses than the worship of his Maker.”
Thus my Lord Henry Goade, and you observe how he permits his lingering disapproval of the man to intrude upon his description of him. The truth is that﻿—as there is ample testimony in his prolific writings﻿—his lordship was something of a misanthropist. It was, in fact, his misanthropy which drove him, as it has driven many another, to authorship. He takes up the pen, not so much that he may carry out his professed object of writing a chronicle of his own time, but to the end that he may vent the bitterness engendered in him by his fall from favour. As a consequence he has little that is good to say of anyone, and rarely mentions one of his contemporaries but to tap the sources of a picturesque invective. After all, it is possible to make excuses for him.

===Chinese===

亨利·戈德勋爵——如下文将见，他同奥利弗·特雷西利安爵士有几分私交——直截了当地告诉我们，此人相貌不佳。可这位勋爵素来偏好刻薄的论断，他的眼力也未必总靠得住。比方说，他讲克利夫斯的安妮，是“我平生所见最丑的女人”。可从他自己卷帙浩繁的著述里能钩稽出来的，倒是他究竟见没见过克利夫斯的安妮，大可怀疑；我们疑心他这句话不过是人云亦云的应声——众口相传，都把克伦威尔的倒台归咎于他替那位蓝胡子主子聘来的新娘太丑。至于众口，自有荷尔拜因的画笔在，它容我们自下判断，向我们展示的那位夫人，实在远远当不起勋爵大人的苛评。同样，我宁愿相信亨利勋爵对奥利弗爵士的断语也是错的；他本人附在那断语后面的一幅文字小像，更助长了我这个信念。“他这个人，”他说，“高大健壮，身量不错，只是胳膊太长，手脚大得不好看。脸是黝黑的，黑头发，黑胡须，胡须分叉；鼻子大，鼻梁极高；一双眼睛深陷在突出的眉骨底下，颜色极淡，极凶，极阴。他有一副——这一点我一向认为是男子大丈夫气的标志——又大又深又粗的嗓门，拿去发舰尾楼的咒骂和脏话倒合适，想必也是干这个用的多，敬拜造物主倒少了。”

亨利·戈德勋爵的话就是这样；你也看得出来，他让自己对这个人挥之不去的反感，渗进了对他的描摹。实情是——他那些洋洋大观的著述里证据充足——这位勋爵是个不大喜欢人类的人。事实上，正是这厌世把他驱向了著述，如同它驱使过许多旁人一样。他提起笔，倒不全是为他自诩的目标——为他自己的时代写一部编年史——而是为了泄一泄失宠之后郁结在胸中的苦水。于是他几乎没一句好话说人，提到同时代人，十有八九是去开他那套绘声绘色的骂人宝库。说到底，他也不是不可原谅。

===Original===

He was at once a man of thought and a man of action﻿—a combination as rare as it is usually deplorable. The man of action in him might have gone far had he not been ruined at the outset by the man of thought. A magnificent seaman, he might have become Lord High Admiral of England but for a certain proneness to intrigue. Fortunately for him﻿—since otherwise he could hardly have kept his head where nature had placed it﻿—he came betimes under a cloud of suspicion. His career suffered a check; but it was necessary to afford him some compensation since, after all, the suspicions could not be substantiated.
Consequently he was removed from his command and appointed by the Queen’s Grace her Lieutenant of Cornwall, a position in which it was judged that he could do little mischief. There, soured by this blighting of his ambitions, and living a life of comparative seclusion, he turned, as so many other men similarly placed have turned, to seek consolation in his pen. He wrote his singularly crabbed, narrow and superficial *History of Lord Henry Goade: His Own Times*﻿—which is a miracle of injuvenations, distortions, misrepresentations, and eccentric spelling. In the eighteen enormous folio volumes, which he filled with his minute and gothic characters, he gives his own version of the story of what he terms his downfall, and, having, notwithstanding his prolixity, exhausted this subject in the first five of the eighteen tomes, he proceeds to deal with so much of the history of his own day as came immediately under his notice in his Cornish retirement.
For the purposes of English history his chronicles are entirely negligible, which is the reason why they have been allowed to remain unpublished and in oblivion. But to the student who attempts to follow the history of that extraordinary man, Sir Oliver Tressilian, they are entirely invaluable. And, since I have made this history my present task, it is fitting that I should here at the outset acknowledge my extreme indebtedness to those chronicles. Without them, indeed, it were impossible to reconstruct the life of that Cornish gentleman who became a renegade and a Barbary Corsair and might have become Basha of Algiers﻿—or Argire, as his lordship terms it﻿—but for certain matters which are to be set forth.

===Chinese===

他兼是思想的人和行动的人——这样一种合体，罕见，也通常可叹。他身上那个行动的人本可以前程远大，可惜一上来就叫那个思想的人毁了。他是出色之极的海员，本可以做到英格兰海军大臣，坏就坏在一种嗜弄阴谋的癖性。算他走运——不然他那颗脑袋很难还留在天生安放它的地方——他及时招上了一层嫌疑的阴云。他的仕途受了顿挫；可这些嫌疑到底查无实据，总得给他一点补偿。

于是他被解了军职，由女王陛下改任为康沃尔郡尉——一个估量着他兴不起大浪的位子。在那儿，壮志被摧折的酸楚腌着他，过着半隐居的日子，便同许多同样处境的人一样，转身到笔杆子里寻安慰。他写出了他那部乖戾、偏狭而浅薄的《亨利·戈德勋爵本时代史》——一部集谩骂、歪曲、颠倒与怪僻拼法之大成的奇书。在那十八巨册对开本里——密密麻麻爬满他蝇头哥特体的小字——他给出了他所称的自己“倒台”经过的自家版本；饶是他那样冗长，这个题目在前五册里便写尽了，于是接下来写他康沃尔退隐期间亲见亲闻的本朝掌故。

对英国史而言，他的编年史全然不值一顾——这也正是它们始终不曾刊行、湮没无闻的缘故。可对要追踪奥利弗·特雷西利安爵士这个非凡人物生平的研究者，它们贵重无比。我既已把这部生平当作眼下的功课，便理应在这里开卷处申明：我对那些编年史亏欠极深。没有它们，实在无从重构这位康沃尔绅士的一生——他叛了教，做了巴巴里海盗，若非下文将要叙明的种种，他甚至可能做到阿尔及尔的帕夏——他的勋爵大人是把阿尔及尔拼作“阿吉尔”的。

===Original===

Lord Henry wrote with knowledge and authority, and the tale he has to tell is very complete and full of precious detail. He was, himself, an eyewitness of much that happened; he pursued a personal acquaintance with many of those who were connected with Sir Oliver’s affairs that he might amplify his chronicles, and he considered no scrap of gossip that was to be gleaned along the countryside too trivial to be recorded. I suspect him also of having received no little assistance from Jasper Leigh in the matter of those events that happened out of England, which seem to me to constitute by far the most interesting portion of his narrative.
R. S.

===Chinese===

亨利勋爵下笔有识有据，要讲的故事相当完备，细节珍贵。他本人是许多事件的目击者；为了充实编年史，他同奥利弗爵士事务相关的许多人都有私交；乡野间能钩到的一星半点闲话，他从不嫌琐碎而不录。我还疑心，凡发生在英格兰之外的那些事件，他从贾斯珀·利那里得了不少助力——依我看，那恰恰是他叙述里最有趣的部分。

拉·萨

## II Rosamund / 二 罗莎蒙德
===Original===

Anon, after his visitor had departed, Sir Oliver grew calm again. Then being able in his calm to consider his position, he became angry anew at the very thought of the rage in which he had been, a rage which had so mastered him that he had erected additional obstacles to the already considerable ones that stood between Rosamund and himself. In full blast, his anger swung round and took Sir John Killigrew for its objective. He would settle with him at once. He would so, by Heaven’s light!
He bellowed for Nick and his boots.
“Where is Master Lionel?” he asked when the boots had been fetched.
“He be just ridden in, Sir Oliver.”
“Bid him hither.”
Promptly, in answer to that summons, came Sir Oliver’s half-brother﻿—a slender lad favouring his mother the dissolute Ralph Tressilian’s second wife. He was as unlike Sir Oliver in body as in soul. He was comely in a very gentle, almost womanish way; his complexion was fair and delicate, his hair golden, and his eyes of a deep blue. He had a very charming stripling grace﻿—for he was but in his twenty-first year﻿—and he dressed with all the care of a Court-gallant.
“Has that whelp Godolphin been to visit you?” he asked as he entered.
“Aye,” growled Sir Oliver. “He came to tell me some things and to hear some others in return.”
“Ha. I passed him just beyond the gates, and he was deaf to my greeting. ’Tis a most cursed insufferable pup.”
“Art a judge of men, Lal.” Sir Oliver stood up booted. “I am for Arwenack to exchange a compliment or two with Sir John.”
His tight-pressed lips and resolute air supplemented his words so well that Lionel clutched his arm.
“You’re not﻿ ﻿… you’re not﻿ ﻿… ?”
“I am.” And affectionately, as if to soothe the lad’s obvious alarm, he patted his brother’s shoulder. “Sir John,” he explained, “talks too much. ’Tis a fault that wants correcting. I go to teach him the virtue of silence.”
“There will be trouble, Oliver.”
“So there will﻿—for him. If a man must be saying of me that I am a pirate, a slave-dealer, a murderer, and Heaven knows what else, he must be ready for the consequences. But you are late, Lal. Where have you been?”
“I rode as far as Malpas.”
“As far as Malpas?” Sir Oliver’s eyes narrowed, as was the trick with him. “I hear it whispered what magnet draws you thither,” he said. “Be wary, boy. You go too much to Malpas.”
“How?” quoth Lionel a trifle coldly.

===Chinese===

不久，客人走后，奥利弗爵士又平静下来。平静中把处境掂量了一番，他想起方才那场狂怒，心头又起一股新的怒意——那狂怒竟那样制住了他，害得他在罗莎蒙德同他自己之间原本就够多的障碍之外，又添了几重。这一腔怒火烧到最旺处，掉转头来，直取约翰·基利格鲁爵士。他要立刻同他算账。他这就去，天光作证！

他吼着叫尼克拿马靴来。

“莱昂内尔少爷呢？”靴子取来后他问。

“刚骑马回来，奥利弗爵士。”

“叫他来。”

应声即至的，是奥利弗爵士的异母弟弟——一个清瘦少年，生得像他母亲，那个浪荡子拉尔夫·特雷西利安的续弦。他无论身心，都同奥利弗爵士处处相反。他的俊美是温文一路，近乎女子；肤色白皙细腻，头发金黄，眼睛深蓝。他有一种十分讨喜的少年风致——他才二十一岁——衣着的讲究，赛过宫里的时髦客。

“戈多尔芬那小子来看过你了？”他一进门就问。

“来过。”奥利弗爵士呼噜噜地说。“他来讲了几件事，又听我回了几件。”

“哈。我在大门外刚同他错身，他竟装听不见我的招呼。好一个可恶透顶、不知天高地厚的狗崽子。”

“你倒真会看人，拉尔。”奥利弗爵士登着靴子站起来。“我去阿文纳克，同约翰爵士交换一两句恭维话。”

他紧抿的嘴唇和决绝的神气把话补得太明白了，莱昂内尔一把抓住他的手臂。

“你该不是……你该不是……？”

“正是。”他疼爱地拍拍弟弟的肩，像要抚平少年明显的惊惶。“约翰爵士，”他解释道，“话太多。这毛病得治。我去教教他沉默的美德。”

“要出乱子的，奥利弗。”

“出就出——出在他身上。一个人既要四处张扬我是海盗、是贩奴的、是杀人凶手，还有天知道些什么，那他就得备着后果。你回来得晚了，拉尔。去哪儿了？”

“骑到马尔帕斯了。”

“骑到马尔帕斯？”奥利弗爵士眼睛眯起来——这是他的习惯。“我听人嘀咕，说有个磁石在往那儿吸你呢。”他说。“当心，孩子。你去马尔帕斯去得太勤了。”

“此话怎讲？”莱昂内尔有点冷淡地说。

===Original===

“I mean that you are your father’s son. Remember it, and strive not to follow in his ways lest they bring you to his own end. I have just been reminded of these predilections of his by good Master Peter. Go not over often to Malpas, I say. No more.” But the arm which he flung about his younger brother’s shoulders and the warmth of his embrace made resentment of his warning quite impossible.
When he was gone, Lionel sat him down to dine, with Nick to wait on him. He ate but little, and never addressed the old servant in the course of that brief repast. He was very pensive. In thought he followed his brother on that avenging visit of his to Arwenack. Killigrew was no babe, but man of his hands, a soldier and a seaman. If any harm should come to Oliver﻿ ﻿… He trembled at the thought; and then almost despite him his mind ran on to calculate the consequences to himself. His fortune would be in a very different case, he refected. In a sort of horror, he sought to put so detestable a reflection from his mind; but it returned insistently. It would not be denied. It forced him to a consideration of his own circumstances.
All that he had he owed to his brother’s bounty. That dissolute father of theirs had died as such men commonly die, leaving behind him heavily encumbered estates and many debts; the very house of Penarrow was mortgaged, and the moneys raised on it had been drunk, or gambled, or spent on one or another of Ralph Tressilian’s many lights o’ love. Then Oliver had sold some little property near Helston, inherited from his mother; he had sunk the money into a venture upon the Spanish Main. He had fitted out and manned a ship, and had sailed with Hawkins upon one of those ventures, which Sir John Killigrew was perfectly entitled to account pirate raids. He had returned with enough plunder in specie and gems to disencumber the Tressilian patrimony. He had sailed again and returned still wealthier. And meanwhile, Lionel had remained at home taking his ease. He loved his ease. His nature was inherently indolent, and he had the wasteful extravagant tastes that usually go with indolence. He was not born to toil and struggle, and none had sought to correct the shortcomings of his character in that respect. Sometimes he wondered what the future might hold for him should Oliver come to marry. He feared his life might not be as easy as it was at present.

===Chinese===

“我是说，你是你父亲的儿子。记着这一点，别去踩他的脚印，免得踩到他的下场。方才好心的彼得少爷就提醒了我这一层他的旧癖。别太常去马尔帕斯，我说。就这样。”可他搂住弟弟肩头的那条手臂，和那个拥抱的温热，叫人无论如何恼不起他这番警告来。

他走后，莱昂内尔坐下用饭，尼克在旁伺候。他吃得很少，那顿短短的饭里没同老仆说过一句话。他心事重重。思绪跟着兄长踏上那趟去阿文纳克的问罪之行。基利格鲁不是襁褓小儿，是行伍出身，陆军水军都干过。奥利弗若是有什么三长两短……他想到这里就发抖；随后，几乎不由自主，他的心思又滑下去，盘算起对他自己的后果。他的境况就大不一样了，他省悟道。他一阵恐悸，想把这么卑劣的念头撵出脑子；可它执拗地回来，撵不走。它逼着他审视起自己的处境来。

他所有的一切，全亏兄长的慷慨。他们那个浪荡父亲，死得同这类人一个死法：留下一屁股重债和重重抵押的田产；连佩纳罗这座宅子都押了出去，押来的钱或喝了，或赌了，或花在拉尔夫·特雷西利安那一长串情妇的某一位身上了。后来奥利弗卖掉赫尔斯顿附近一点小小的产业——他母亲遗下的——把钱投到西班牙大陆沿岸的一趟营生里。他置了一条船，配齐人手，随霍金斯出航，干的就是约翰·基利格鲁爵士满可以称作海盗劫掠的那种营生。他带回来的金锭珠宝，足以赎清特雷西利安家的祖产。他又出航一次，回来更富。而与此同时，莱昂内尔一直待在家里享清福。他爱他的安逸。他天性懒散，又有懒散惯出的那副挥霍口味。他生来不是受苦挣命的料，也从没人想矫正他这方面的短处。有时他也琢磨，若奥利弗娶了亲，等着他的会是什么。他怕那时的日子不会像眼下这样舒坦。

===Original===

But he did not seriously fear. It was not in his nature﻿—it never is in the natures of such men﻿—to give any excess of consideration to the future. When his thoughts did turn to it in momentary uneasiness, he would abruptly dismiss them with the reflection that when all was said Oliver loved him, and Oliver would never fail to provide adequately for all his wants.
In this undoubtedly he was fully justified. Oliver was more parent than brother to him. When their father had been brought home to die from the wound dealt him by an outraged husband﻿—and a shocking spectacle that sinner’s death had been with its hasty terrified repentance﻿—he had entrusted Lionel to his elder brother’s care. At the time Oliver was seventeen and Lionel twelve. But Oliver had seemed by so many years older than his age, that the twice-widowed Ralph Tressilian had come to depend upon this steady, resolute, and masterful child of his first marriage. It was into his ear that the dying man had poured the wretched tale of his repentance for the life he had lived and the state in which he was leaving his affairs with such scant provision for his sons. For Oliver he had no fear. It was as if with the prescience that comes to men in his pass he had perceived that Oliver was of those who must prevail, a man born to make the world his oyster. His anxieties were all for Lionel, whom he also judged with that same penetrating insight vouchsafed a man in his last hours. Hence his piteous recommendation of him to Oliver, and Oliver’s ready promise to be father, mother, and brother to the youngster.
All this was in Lionel’s mind as he sat musing there, and again he struggled with that hideous insistent thought that if things should go ill with his brother at Arwenack, there would be great profit to himself; that these things he now enjoyed upon another’s bounty he would then enjoy in his own right. A devil seemed to mock him with the whispered sneer that were Oliver to die his own grief would not be long-lived.

===Chinese===

可他并不真怕。认真为将来多操心，本不合他的天性——这一路人从来不这样。偶尔想到将来而不安，他也总是猛地一挥手撇开，心想：说到底，奥利弗爱他，奥利弗永远不会亏待他的任何需求。

这一点上，他无疑全然有理。奥利弗于他，兄长之外更是父母。当年他们父亲被一个戴了绿帽的丈夫一刀捅回家来等死——那罪人死得也真够瞧，仓皇恐怖，临终匆匆悔罪——便把莱昂内尔托付给了长子。那时奥利弗十七，莱昂内尔十二。可奥利弗比他的年纪老成出那么多岁，两度丧妻的拉尔夫·特雷西利安早就倚靠起原配生的这个沉稳、果决、有主见的孩子。垂死的人对着他，把满腔悔恨倒了个净：悔他过的这一生，悔他撂下的这副烂摊子，几乎没给儿子们留下什么。为奥利弗他不担心——仿佛人在那个关口自有一副先知，他看出奥利弗属于必定出头的那类人，生来就要把世界当成自己的牡蛎。他的焦心全在莱昂内尔身上，对他，他也用了人在临终时才有的那副看透的眼力。于是有了他凄凄惨惨对奥利弗的托付，和奥利弗爽爽利利的许诺：做这孩子的父，母，兄。

这一切都在莱昂内尔此刻的沉思里；那纠缠不休的可怕念头又同他扭打起来：兄长若在阿文纳克遭了殃，于他大有好处；他如今仰人鼻息享受的这一切，届时就名正言顺归他自己了。一个魔鬼似乎贴着他的耳朵讥笑：奥利弗若死了，他自己的悲恸也长不了。

===Original===

Then in revolt against that voice of an egoism so loathsome that in his better moments it inspired even himself with horror, he bethought him of Oliver’s unvarying, unwavering affection; he pondered all the loving care and kindness that through these years past Oliver had ever showered upon him; and he cursed the rottenness of a mind that could even admit such thoughts as those which he had been entertaining. So wrought upon was he by the welter of his emotions, by that fierce strife between his conscience and his egotism, that he came abruptly to his feet, a cry upon his lips.
“*Vade retro, Sathanas!*”
Old Nicholas, looking up abruptly, saw the lad’s face, waxen, his brow bedewed with sweat.
“Master Lionel! Master Lionel!” he cried, his small bright eyes concernedly scanning his young master’s face. “What be amiss?”
Lionel mopped his brow. “Sir Oliver has gone to Arwenack upon a punitive business,” said he.
“An’ what be that, zur?” quoth Nicholas.
“He has gone to punish Sir John for having maligned him.”
A grin spread upon the weather-beaten countenance of Nicholas.
“Be that so? Marry, ’twere time. Sir John he be over long i’ th’ tongue.”
Lionel stood amazed at the man’s easy confidence and supreme assurance of how his master must acquit himself.
“You﻿ ﻿… you have no fear, Nicholas.﻿ ﻿…” He did not add of what. But the servant understood, and his grin grew broader still.
“Fear? Lackaday! I bain’t afeeard for Sir Oliver, and doan’t ee be afeeard. Sir Oliver’ll be home to sup with a sharp-set appetite﻿—’tis the only difference fighting ever made to he.”
The servant was justified of his confidence by the events, though through a slight error of judgment Sir Oliver did not quite accomplish all that promised and intended. In anger, and when he deemed that he had been affronted, he was﻿—as his chronicler never wearies of insisting, and as you shall judge before the end of this tale is reached﻿—of a tigerish ruthlessness. He rode to Arwenack fully resolved to kill his calumniator. Nothing less would satisfy him.

===Chinese===

他猛然反抗那声音——那自我私欲的声音，卑劣得连他自己在清醒时刻也为之战栗——想起奥利弗始终如一、从不动摇的手足之情；想起这些年来奥利弗泼在他身上的种种爱护和慈仁；他咒骂自己这颗烂透的心，竟容得下方才那些念头。他叫心头的乱麻、叫良心同私欲的这场恶斗搅得站了起来，唇边迸出一声喊——

“退去吧，撒旦！”

老尼古拉斯猛地抬头，看见少爷的脸蜡黄，额头沁着汗。

“莱昂内尔少爷！莱昂内尔少爷！”他喊，小小的亮眼睛担忧地打量着少东家的脸。“怎么了，这是？”

莱昂内尔抹了把额头。“奥利弗爵士去阿文纳克办一桩问罪的事了。”他说。

“那是个啥事，先生？”尼古拉斯问。

“他去惩戒约翰爵士，为他造谣中伤。”

尼古拉斯那张风吹日晒的老脸上绽开一个咧嘴笑。

“是这么回事？嗳呀，早该了。约翰爵士那张嘴，是长了些。”

莱昂内尔愕然站着，惊的是这人对自家老爷必胜的那份轻巧的信心和十足的把握。

“你……你不怕出事，尼古拉斯。……”后半句他没说。可老仆懂，咧得更大了。

“怕？才不呢！俺不替奥利弗爵士怕，您也别怕。奥利弗爵士准保回家吃晚饭，胃口还倍儿好——干架嘛，对他历来就这点儿分别。”

后来的事，证实了这老仆的信心没信错——尽管因一处小小的判断失着，奥利弗爵士没有全然办成他许下和打算的事。一旦动怒，一旦认定自己受了冒犯，他是——他的纪传作者不厌其烦地强调这一点，读到本故事结尾你也会自行裁断——虎狼般的无情。他骑马直奔阿文纳克，抱定主意要杀死那诽谤者。少于此数，难消他心头之恨。

===Original===

Arrived at that fine embattled castle of the Killigrews which commanded the entrance to the estuary of the Fal, and from whose crenels the country might be surveyed as far as the Lizard, fifteen miles away, he found Peter Godolphin there before him; and because of Peter’s presence Sir Oliver was more deliberate and formal in his accusation of Sir John than he had intended. He desired, in accusing Sir John, also to clear himself in the eyes of Rosamund’s brother, to make the latter realize how entirely odious were the calumnies which Sir John had permitted himself, and how basely prompted.
Sir John, however, came halfway to meet the quarrel. His rancour against the Pirate of Penarrow﻿—as he had come to dub Sir Oliver﻿—rendered him almost as eager to engage as was his visitor.
They found a secluded corner of the deer-park for their business, and there Sir John﻿—a slim, sallow gentleman of some thirty years of age﻿—made an onslaught with sword and dagger upon Sir Oliver, full worthy of the onslaught he had made earlier with his tongue. But his impetuosity availed him less than nothing. Sir Oliver was come there with a certain purpose, and it was his way that he never failed to carry through a thing to which he set his hand.
In three minutes it was all over and Sir Oliver was carefully wiping his blade, whilst Sir John lay coughing upon the turf tended by white-faced Peter Godolphin and a scared groom who had been bidden thither to make up the necessary tale of witnesses.
Sir Oliver sheathed his weapons and resumed his coat, then came to stand over his fallen foe, considering him critically.
“I think I have silenced him for a little time only,” he said. “And I confess that I intended to do better. I hope, however, that the lesson will suffice and that he will lie no more﻿—at least concerning me.”
“Do you mock a fallen man?” was Master Godolphin’s angry protest.
“God forbid!” said Sir Oliver soberly. “There is no mockery in my heart. There is, believe me, nothing but regret﻿—regret that I should not have done the thing more thoroughly. I will send assistance from the house as I go. Give you good day, Master Peter.”
From Arwenack he rode round by Penryn on his homeward way. But he did not go straight home. He paused at the Gates of Godolphin Court, which stood above Trefusis Point commanding the view of Carrick Roads.

===Chinese===

到了基利格鲁家那座雄伟的设防城堡——它雄踞法尔河湾的入口，从垛口望出去，十五英里外直到利泽德角一带的乡野尽收眼底——他发现彼得·戈多尔芬已先他而至；因为有彼得在场，奥利弗爵士对约翰爵士的问罪，比原打算的更克制、更拘礼数。他要问罪约翰爵士，同时也要在罗莎蒙德兄长的眼里洗清自己，要教后者看明白：约翰爵士纵容自己散布的那些诽谤何其可憎，其用心又何其卑劣。

然而约翰爵士主动迎上半个身位来接这场争吵。他对“佩纳罗海盗”——他已惯于这样称呼奥利弗爵士——的宿怨，叫他几乎同来客一样急于交手。

他们在鹿苑里寻了个僻静角落办这桩事。在那里，约翰爵士——一个三十来岁、清瘦蜡黄的绅士——挺剑持匕直取奥利弗爵士，那一手同他先前舌上的那一手正相匹配。可惜他的猛冲半点不顶用。奥利弗爵士是带着一个明确目的来的，而他的脾气，凡经手的事，从没有不办到底的。

三分钟便见了分晓：奥利弗爵士在仔细擦拭剑刃，约翰爵士躺在草皮上咳嗽，脸色发白的彼得·戈多尔芬和一个吓坏了的马夫在旁照料——那马夫是被特地叫来凑足必要的人证数目的。

奥利弗爵士收剑入鞘，穿好外衣，然后走过来俯视倒下的对手，品鉴似的端详。

“我想，我不过是让他清静一小阵。”他说。“我本想办得更利落些，这个我认。不过，但愿这一课够了，但愿他不再撒谎——至少不再编派我。”

“你讥笑一个倒下的人么？”彼得少爷怒声抗议。

“上天不容！”奥利弗爵士正色道。“我心里没有讥笑。相信我，只有遗憾——遗憾我没有把事情办得更彻底。我走时从府上派人过来相助。祝你日安，彼得少爷。”

离开阿文纳克，他绕道彭林踏上归途。可他没有径直回家。他在戈多尔芬府的大门前停了步——那门楼高踞于特雷富西斯角上，俯瞰着卡里克水道。

===Original===

He turned in under the old gateway and drew up in the courtyard. Leaping to the kidney-stones that paved it, he announced himself a visitor to Mistress Rosamund.
He found her in her bower﻿—a light, turreted chamber on the mansion’s eastern side, with windows that looked out upon that lovely sheet of water and the wooded slopes beyond. She was sitting with a book in her lap in the deep of that tall window when he entered, preceded and announced by Sally Pentreath, who, now her tire-woman, had once been her nurse.
She rose with a little exclamation of gladness when he appeared under the lintel﻿—scarce high enough to admit him without stooping﻿—and stood regarding him across the room with brightened eyes and flushing cheeks.
What need is there to describe her? In the blaze of notoriety into which she was anon to be thrust by Sir Oliver Tressilian there was scarce a poet in England who did not sing the grace and loveliness of Rosamund Godolphin, and in all conscience enough of those fragments have survived. Like her brother she was tawny headed and she was divinely tall, though as yet her figure in its girlishness was almost too slender for her height.
“I had not looked for you so early.﻿ ﻿…” she was beginning, when she observed that his countenance was oddly stern. “Why﻿ ﻿… what has happened?” she cried, her intuitions clamouring loudly of some mischance.
“Naught to alarm you, sweet; yet something that may vex you.” He set an arm about that lissom waist of hers above the swelling farthingale, and gently led her back to her chair, then flung himself upon the window-seat beside her. “You hold Sir John Killigrew in some affection?” he said between statement and inquiry.
“Why, yes. He was our guardian until my brother came of full age.”
Sir Oliver made a wry face. “Aye, there’s the rub. Well, I’ve all but killed him.”
She drew back into her chair, recoiling before him, and he saw horror leap to her eyes and blench her face. He made haste to explain the causes that had led to this, he told her briefly of the calumnies concerning him that Sir John had put about to vent his spite at having been thwarted in a matter of his coveted licence to build at Smithick.
“That mattered little,” he concluded. “I knew these tales concerning me were abroad, and I held them in the same contempt as I hold their utterer.

===Chinese===

他从古老的大门洞里拐进去，在院子里勒住马。踏上铺院的卵石，他报名求见罗莎蒙德小姐。

他在她的闺阁里找到了她——府邸东侧一间明亮的塔楼小室，窗外正对着那片可爱的水域和岸上的林坡。他进去时，她正坐在那扇高窗的深处，膝上摊着一本书；引路通名的是萨莉·彭特里斯，如今是她的梳妆侍女，从前是她的奶妈。

见他出现在门楣下——那门楣矮得他非要俯身才进得来——她轻轻欢呼一声站起来，隔着屋子望他，眼睛亮亮的，双颊绯红。

她还需要描写么？奥利弗·特雷西利安爵士不久便把她卷进那场轰动一时的风浪，风浪里英格兰几乎没有一个诗人不曾歌咏过罗莎蒙德·戈多尔芬的风姿与可爱，而流传下来的断章残句，也尽够了。她同她哥哥一样是一头黄褐色的头发，她高挑得出奇，虽然少女的身量配这高度还显得过于单薄。

“没想到你来得这么早……”她刚开口，却看见他脸色出奇地严峻。“怎么……出什么事了？”她喊，直觉大声报警，预感着某种不测。

“没什么可怕的，亲爱的；只是有一件，兴许要惹你不快。”他一条手臂揽住她裙撑上方那盈盈一握的细腰，轻轻把她送回椅子里，自己往她旁边的窗座上一坐。“你对约翰·基利格鲁爵士，是有几分感情的吧？”他半是陈述半是探问。

“嗯，有。在我哥哥成年之前，他是我们的监护人。”

奥利弗爵士做了个苦脸。“啊，麻烦就麻烦在这儿。喏，我差点把他杀了。”

她朝椅背里缩回去，整个人朝后一挫；他看见惊恐跳进她的眼睛，刷白她的脸。他急忙解释缘由，简短地讲了约翰爵士怎样因为他在史密蒂克建港的贪图受了挫、存心泄愤，才散布那些关于他的谗言。

“那倒不打紧，”他收尾道，“这些关于我的流言在外头传，我早知道；我把它同它的散布者一样，一概嗤之以鼻。

===Original===

But he went further, Rose: he poisoned your brother’s mind against me, and he stirred up in him the slumbering rancour that in my father’s time was want to lie between our houses. Today Peter came to me with the clear intent to make a quarrel. He affronted me as no man has ever dared.”
She cried out at that, her already great alarm redoubled. He smiled.
“Do not suppose that I could harm him. He is your brother, and, so, sacred to me. He came to tell me that no betrothal was possible between us, forbade me ever again to visit Godolphin Court, dubbed me pirate and vampire to my face and reviled my father’s memory. I tracked the evil of all this to its source in Killigrew, and rode straight to Arwenack to dam that source of falsehood for all time. I did not accomplish quite so much as I intended. You see, I am frank, my Rose. It may be that Sir John will live; if so I hope that he may profit by this lesson. I have come straight to you,” he concluded, “that you may hear the tale from me before another comes to malign me with false stories of this happening.”
“You﻿ ﻿… you mean Peter?” she cried.
“Alas!” he sighed.
She sat very still and white, looking straight before her and not at all at Sir Oliver. At length she spoke.
“I am not skilled in reading men,” she said in a sad, small voice. “How should I be, that am but a maid who has led a cloistered life. I was told of you that you were violent and passionate, a man of bitter enmities, easily stirred to hatreds, cruel and ruthless in the persecution of them.”
“You, too, have been listening to Sir John,” he muttered, and laughed shortly.
“All this was I told,” she pursued as if he had not spoken, “and all did I refuse to believe because my heart was given to you. Yet﻿ ﻿… yet of what have you made proof today?”
“Of forbearance,” said he shortly.
“Forbearance?” she echoed, and her lips writhed in a smile of weary irony. “Surely you mock me!”
He set himself to explain.
“I have told you what Sir John had done. I have told you that the greater part of it﻿—and matter all that touched my honour﻿—I know Sir John to have done long since. Yet I suffered it in silence and contempt. Was that to show myself easily stirred to ruthlessness? What was it but forbearance?

===Chinese===

可他还不止于此，罗丝：他往你哥哥耳朵里灌我的毒，又搅起他心里那种沉睡的宿怨——我父亲在世时，两家之间惯常横着的那种宿怨。今天彼得到我这儿来，明摆着是存心来寻衅的。他冒犯了我，冒犯得没有一个人敢那样冒犯过我。”

她听了喊出声来，本已很大的惊惶又翻了一倍。他笑了。

“别以为我会伤害他。他是你的哥哥，所以，于我是神圣的。他来告诉我，你我之间的婚约断无可能；禁止我再登戈多尔芬府的门；当面骂我海盗、骂我吸血鬼；还糟蹋我亡父的名声。我把这一切的毒追踪到源头——基利格鲁，便直奔阿文纳克，要把那谎言的源头一劳永逸地堵死。我没能全然办成我打算的那么多。你看，我坦白，我的罗丝。约翰爵士兴许能活下来；若活下来，但愿他记住这一课。我径直来找你，”他收尾道，“是要你先从我嘴里听这件事，免得旁人赶来，拿这场事的假话糟践我。”

“你……你是说彼得？”她喊。

“唉！”他叹道。

她坐着，一动不动，脸色煞白，直望着前方，全不看奥利弗爵士。终于她开口了。

“我不大会看人，”她用一种悲哀的、小小的声音说。“我又怎么会呢，一个深居简出的姑娘。人家跟我说，你暴戾，急躁，是个记死仇的人，轻易就结恨，报复起来又狠又绝。”

“你也是听了约翰爵士那一套。”他咕哝，短促地笑笑。

“这些我都听见了，”她自顾自说下去，仿佛他没有开过口，“而这一切我都拒不肯信，因为我把心给了你。可是……可是，你今天证明的又是什么呢？”

“证明了我的克制。”他简短地说。

“克制？”她跟着念，嘴唇在一个疲惫的讥讽的笑里扭了扭。“你这是在耍我吧！”

他着手解释。

“约翰爵士干了什么，我都告诉你了。我告诉过你，其中大半——凡牵涉我名誉的部分——我早知道是他干的。可我缄默地、鄙夷地受了。这算轻易动狠么？这不正是克制么？

===Original===

When, however, he carries his petty huckster’s rancour so far as to seek to choke for me my source of happiness in life and sends your brother to affront me, I am still so forbearing that I recognize your brother to be no more than a tool and go straight to the hand that wielded him. Because I know of your affection for Sir John I gave him such latitude as no man of honour in England would have given him.”
Then seeing that she still avoided his regard, still sat in that frozen attitude of horror at learning that the man she loved had imbrued his hands with the blood of another whom she also loved, his pleading quickened to a warmer note. He flung himself upon his knees beside her chair, and took in his great sinewy hands the slender fingers which she listlessly surrendered. “Rose,” he cried, and his deep voice quivered with intercession, “dismiss all that you have heard from out your mind. Consider only this thing that has befallen. Suppose that Lionel my brother came to you, and that, having some measure of power and authority to support him, he swore to you that you should never wed me, swore to prevent this marriage because he deemed you such a woman as could not bear my name with honour to myself; and suppose that to all this he added insult to the memory of your dead father, what answer would you return him? Speak, Rose! Be honest with thyself and me. Deem yourself in my place, and say in honesty if you can still condemn me for what I have done. Say if it differs much from what you would wish to do in such a case as I have named.”
Her eyes scanned now his upturned face, every line of which was pleading to her and calling for impartial judgment. Her face grew troubled, and then almost fierce. She set her hands upon his shoulders, and looked deep into his eyes.
“You swear to me, Noll, that all is as you have told it me﻿—you have added naught, you have altered naught to make the tale more favourable to yourself?”
“You need such oaths from me?” he asked, and she saw sorrow spread upon his countenance.
“If I did I should not love thee, Noll. But in such an hour I need your own assurance. Will you not be generous and bear with me, strengthen me to withstand anything that may be said hereafter?”
“As God’s my witness, I have told you true in all,” he answered solemnly.
She sank her head to his shoulder.

===Chinese===

然而，当他把那点小贩子式的宿怨使到这般地步，要掐断我此生幸福的源头，把你哥哥打发来冒犯我，我克制到什么份上呢——我认出你哥哥不过一件工具，径直去找那只挥动工具的手。因为知道你疼约翰爵士，我给他的余地，是英格兰任何一个有名誉的人都不会给的。”

见她仍旧避开他的目光，仍旧僵坐在那副惊恐里——得知她爱的这个男人染了另一个她所爱的男人的血——他的恳求转成了更热的调子。他跪倒在她椅边，用他那双筋虬的大手捧起她懒懒交出的纤指。“罗丝，”他喊，低沉的嗓音颤着恳求，“把你听来的一切从心里撵出去。只掂量掂量今天这桩事。设想我弟弟莱昂内尔来见你，身后撑着几分权势，对你起誓说你永远嫁不得我，起誓要阻这桩婚事，因为他认准了你这样的女人，嫁我便是辱没我；再设想，除了这些，他还羞辱你亡父的名声——你怎么回他？说呀，罗丝！对你自己、对我说实话。设身处地站到我的位置上，凭良心说：你还能不能责我今日所为。说说看，同你在我讲的这种境况下想做的，差得了多少。”

她的眼睛审视着他仰起的脸——脸上每一道纹路都在向她恳求，唤她下一份公允的裁断。她的脸烦乱起来，随后几乎变得凛厉。她把双手搭上他的肩，深深望进他的眼睛。

“你向我起誓，诺尔，一切同你告诉我的一模一样——你没有添一句，没有改一句，好叫这故事偏着你？”

“你需要我这样的起誓？”他问，她看见悲伤在他脸上漫开。

“若需要，我就不会爱你了，诺尔。可这样的时刻，我需要你亲口的保证。你肯不肯大度地容我一回，给我力量，抵住往后一切闲话？”

“上帝作证，我告诉你的句句是真。”他郑重地答。

她把头沉到他肩上。

===Original===

She was weeping softly, overwrought by this climax to all that in silence and in secret she had suffered since he had come a-wooing her.
“Then,” she said, “I believe you acted rightly. I believe with you that no man of honour could have acted otherwise. I must believe you, Noll, for did I not, then I could believe in naught and hope for naught. You are as a fire that has seized upon the better part of me and consumed it all to ashes that you may hold it in your heart. I am content so you be true.”
“True I shall ever be, sweetheart,” he whispered fervently. “Could I be less since you are sent to make me so?”
She looked at him again, and now she was smiling wistfully through her tears.
“And you will bear with Peter?” she implored him.
“He shall have no power to anger me,” he answered. “I swear that too. Do you know that but today he struck me?”
“Struck you? You did not tell me that!”
“My quarrel was not with him but with the rogue that sent him. I laughed at the blow. Was he not sacred to me?”
“He is good at heart, Noll,” she pursued. “In time he will come to love you as you deserve, and you will come to know that he, too, deserves your love.”
“He deserves it now for the love he bears to you.”
“And you will think ever thus during the little while of waiting that perforce must lie before us?”
“I shall never think otherwise, sweet. Meanwhile I shall avoid him, and that no harm may come should he forbid me Godolphin Court I’ll even stay away. In less than a year you will be of full age, and none may hinder you to come and go. What is a year, with such hope as mine to still impatience?”
She stroked his face. “Art very gentle with me ever, Noll,” she murmured fondly. “I cannot credit you are ever harsh to any, as they say.”
“Heed them not,” he answered her. “I may have been something of all that, but you have purified me, Rose. What man that loved you could be aught but gentle.” He kissed her, and stood up. “I had best be going now,” he said. “I shall walk along the shore towards Trefusis Point tomorrow morning. If you should chance to be similarly disposed.﻿ ﻿…”
She laughed, and rose in her turn. “I shall be there, dear Noll.”
“ ’Twere best so hereafter,” he assured her, smiling, and so took his leave.
She followed him to the stairhead, and watched him as he descended with eyes that took pride in the fine upright carriage of that stalwart, masterful lover.

===Chinese===

她低低地哭着——自从他来向她求爱，她默默暗中受下的这一切，此刻在这结局里决了堤。

“那么，”她说，“我相信你做得对。我同你想的一样：换一个有名誉的人，也只能这么做。我必须信你，诺尔，因为若不信你，我就什么都不必信、什么都不必盼了。你像一团火，把我身上最好的部分攫住，烧成了灰，好揣在你自己心里。只要你真，我就认了。”

“真，我永远真，心肝。”他热烈地低语。“你既是上天派来造就这样一个我，我还能差到哪里去？”

她又看他，此刻她泪光后头已含着怅然的笑。

“那你会容着彼得？”她恳求他。

“他将没有惹恼我的本事。”他答。“这个我也起誓。你可知道，就在今天，他打了我一记？”

“打了你？这个你没告诉我！”

“我的账不在他头上，在遣他来的那个无赖头上。那一记我一笑置之。他于我岂非神圣？”

“他心地是好的，诺尔。”她接着说。“日子久了，他会像他该的那样爱你的；你也会明白，他也配你的爱。”

“现在就配——为他对你的爱。”

“那在我们前面注定要等的那一小段日子里，你会一直这样想么？”

“我永远不会变想法，亲爱的。这期间我避开他就是；为免生事，他若不许我进戈多尔芬府，我便当真不进。不出一年你就成年，来去没人拦得住你。一年算什么，何况有我这样的盼头，压着这份急？”

她抚着他的脸。“你待我永远这样温存，诺尔。”她爱怜地喃喃。“他们说你对人凶，我怎么也不能信。”

“别理会他们。”他答她。“那些毛病我兴许都有过，可你把我洗净了，罗丝。爱你的男人，还能不是温存的？”他吻了她，站起身。“我最好这就走。”他说。“明天早晨，我沿岸边往特雷富西斯角方向散步。你要是碰巧也有这个兴致……”

她笑了，也站起来。“我会去的，亲爱的诺尔。”

“往后顶好都这样安排。”他微笑着向她担保，随即告辞。

她送他到楼梯口，目送他走下去——那目光，为这高大威严的情郎挺拔的身姿，骄傲得发亮。

## Part / 第二部 萨克埃尔-巴尔
===Original===

II Sakr-El-Bahr

===Chinese===

II 萨克埃尔-巴尔

## XVIII Sheik Mat / 十八 将军
===Original===

Under the inquisitive gaping stare of all about them stood Rosamund and Sakr-el-Bahr regarding each other in silence for a little spell after the Basha’s departure. The very galley slaves, stirred from their habitual lethargy by happenings so curious and unusual, craned their sinewy necks to peer at them with a flicker of interest in their dull, weary eyes.
Sakr-el-Bahr’s feelings as he considered Rosamunds’s white face in the fading light were most oddly conflicting. Dismay at what had befallen and some anxious dread of what must follow were leavened by a certain measure of relief.
He realized that in no case could her concealment have continued long. Eleven mortal hours had she spent in the cramped and almost suffocating space of that pannier, in which he had intended to do no more than carry her aboard. The uneasiness which had been occasioned him by the impossibility to deliver her from that close confinement when Asad had announced his resolve to accompany them upon that voyage, had steadily been increasing as hour succeeded hour, and still he found no way to release her from a situation in which sooner or later, when the limits of her endurance were reached, her presence must be betrayed. This release which he could not have contrived had been contrived for him by the suspicions and malice of Marzak. That was the one grain of consolation in the present peril﻿—to himself who mattered nothing and to her, who mattered all. Adversity had taught him to prize benefits however slight and to confront perils however overwhelming. So he hugged the present slender benefit, and resolutely braced himself to deal with the situation as he found it, taking the fullest advantage of the hesitancy which his words had sown in the heart of the Basha. He hugged, too, the thought that as things had fallen out, from being oppressor and oppressed, Rosamund and he were become fellows in misfortune, sharing now a common peril. He found it a sweet thought to dwell on. Therefore was it that he faintly smiled as he looked into Rosamund’s white, strained face.
That smile evoked from her the question that had been burdening her mind.
“What now? What now?” she asked huskily, and held out appealing hands to him.
“Now,” said he coolly, “let us be thankful that you are delivered from quarters destructive both to comfort and to dignity.

===Chinese===

帕夏走后，罗莎蒙德和萨克埃尔-巴尔在四周好奇张望的注视下，默默相对了片刻。连那些桨奴，也被这桩离奇罕见的事从惯常的麻木里搅醒，抻着筋腱的脖颈来瞟他们，疲惫迟钝的眼睛里闪过一星兴味。

暮色渐褪里，萨克埃尔-巴尔端详着罗莎蒙德苍白的脸，心头百感交集，古怪地相冲相撞。对已临之事的沮丧、对后患的忧惧，又被几分宽解发敞开来。

他明白，她的藏匿无论如何瞒不了多久。她已经在那挂篮狭窄得几乎令人窒息的空间里熬了整整十一个钟头——他原本只打算用它把她送上船而已。阿萨德宣布要随行出海之后，他没法把她从那幽闭里解脱出来，这份焦灼随着时辰一个接一个过去、随着他始终寻不出办法让她脱身——她的耐力一旦到头，行藏迟早败露——而越积越重。这份他筹划不出的解脱，竟由马尔扎克的猜忌和恶意替他筹划出来了。这是眼下这场险局里仅有的一粒安慰——于他自己，他无所谓；于她，她是一切。患难教会他珍视哪怕是极微的益处，直面哪怕是压顶的凶险。于是他抱着眼下这细细的一粒好处，硬起心肠，就眼前的局面拿主意，把他那番话在帕夏心里播下的犹疑，用到十足。他还抱着另一个念头：事到如今，罗莎蒙德同他从欺压者与被欺压者，变成了同遭厄难的人，共担着同一份危险。这念头细细品来，竟有几分甜。所以，他望着罗莎蒙德苍白紧绷的脸，唇边浮起一丝淡淡的笑。

那笑引出了她憋在心里的问题。

“现在怎么办？现在怎么办？”她声音沙哑地问，向他伸出恳求的双手。

“现在，”他平静地说，“咱们该庆幸，你总算从那既伤舒适又伤体面的住处里解脱出来了。

===Original===

Let me lead you to those I had prepared for you, which you would have occupied long since but for the ill-timed coming of Asad. Come.” And he waved an inviting hand towards the gangway leading to the poop.
She shrank back at that, for there on the poop sat Asad under his awning with Marzak, Biskaine, and his other officers in attendance.
“Come,” he repeated, “there is naught to fear so that you keep a bold countenance. For the moment it is *sheik mat*﻿—check to the king.”
“Naught to fear?” she echoed, staring.
“For the moment, naught,” he answered firmly. “Against what the future may hold, we must determine. Be sure that fear will not assist our judgment.”
She stiffened as if he had charged her unjustly.
“I do not fear,” she assured him, and if her face continued white, her eyes grew steady, her voice was resolute.
“Then come,” he repeated, and she obeyed him instantly now as if to prove the absence of all fear.
Side by side they passed up the gangway and mounted the steps of the companion to the poop, their approach watched by the group that was in possession of it with glances at once of astonishment and resentment.
Asad’s dark, smouldering eyes were all for the girl. They followed her every movement as she approached and never for a moment left her to turn upon her companion.
Outwardly she bore herself with a proud dignity and an unfaltering composure under that greedy scrutiny; but inwardly she shrank and writhed in a shame and humiliation that she could hardly define. In some measure Oliver shared her feelings, but blent with anger; and urged by them he so placed himself at last that he stood between her and the Basha’s regard to screen her from it as he would have screened her from a lethal weapon. Upon the poop he paused, and salaamed to Asad.
“Permit, exalted lord,” said he, “that my wife may occupy the quarters I had prepared for her before I knew that thou wouldst honour this enterprise with thy presence.”
Curtly, contemptuously, Asad waved a consenting hand without vouchsafing to reply in words. Sakr-el-Bahr bowed again, stepped forward, and put aside the heavy red curtain upon which the crescent was wrought in green. From within the cabin the golden light of a lamp came out to merge into the blue-gray twilight, and to set a shimmering radiance about the white-robed figure of Rosamund.

===Chinese===

随我到你该住的舱房去——那原是为你备下的，若不是阿萨德来得不合时宜，你早住进去了。来。”他朝通往艉楼的跳板一挥手。

她朝后一缩，因为艉楼上，阿萨德正坐在他的凉棚底下，马尔扎克、比斯坎和他的军官们在旁侍立。

“来。”他又说，“只要你不怯场，就没什么可怕的。眼下这一步，是*将军*——将了国王一军。”

“没什么可怕的？”她跟着念，直瞪着他。

“眼下，没有。”他斩钉截铁地答。“往后会怎样，咱们再筹算。放心，惧怕帮不了咱们的判断。”

她挺了挺身，像被他冤枉了似的。

“我不怕。”她向他担保；脸虽然还白着，眼睛却稳了，声音也决绝了。

“那就来。”他又说了一遍，她这回立刻从命，仿佛要证明那份怕真不存在。

两人并肩上了跳板，踏过舷梯登上艉楼；盘踞着艉楼的那一群人望着他们走近，眼神里又是惊，又是恨。

阿萨德那双阴沉的眼睛全在姑娘身上。她走近的每一步都随着他那双眼，一刻也没有挪开去看她的同伴。

在那贪模的注视下，她外表撑着一派骄傲的尊严和从容；内里却在一种说不清的羞耻和屈辱里蜷缩、扭动。奥利弗多少分担着她的感受，只是他的里头还搅着怒；受它们的驱使，他最后站到了她和帕夏的视线之间，把她挡在后头，就像挡一支致命的兵器那样挡着那道目光。他在艉楼上站定，向阿萨德行了一礼。

“请容我的妻，”他说，“占用我早已为她备下的舱房——那会儿我还不知大人竟肯屈尊降临这趟征伐。”

阿萨德简慢地、轻蔑地摆手允了，不屑于回一个字。萨克埃尔-巴尔再施一礼，上前撩开那面上绣绿色新月的厚红帘。舱里一盏灯的金光流出来，融进蓝灰的暮色，给罗莎蒙德一身白袍镶了一圈莹莹的光。

===Original===

Thus for a moment Asad’s fierce, devouring eyes observed her, then she passed within. Sakr-el-Bahr followed, and the screening curtain swung back into its place.
The small interior was furnished by a divan spread with silken carpets, a low Moorish table in coloured wood mosaics bearing the newly lighted lamp, and a tiny brazier in which aromatic gums were burning and spreading a sweetly pungent perfume for the fumigation of all true believers.
Out of the shadows in the farther corners rose silently Sakr-el-Bahr’s two Nubian slaves, Abiad and Zal-Zer, to salaam low before him. But for their turbans and loincloths in spotless white their dusky bodies must have remained invisible, shadowy among the shadows.
The captain issued an order briefly, and from a hanging cupboard the slaves took meat and drink and set it upon the low table﻿—a bowl of chicken cooked in rice and olives and prunes, a dish of bread, a melon, and a clay amphora of water. Then at another word from him, each took a naked scimitar and they passed out to place themselves on guard beyond the curtain. This was not an act in which there was menace or defiance, nor could Asad so interpret it. The acknowledged presence of Sakr-el-Balir’s wife in that poop house, rendered the place the equivalent of his harem, and a man defends his harem as he defends his honour; it is a spot sacred to himself which none may violate, and it is fitting that he take proper precaution against any impious attempt to do so.
Rosamund sank down upon the divan, and sat there with bowed head, her hands folded in her lap. Sakr-el-Bahr stood by in silence for a long moment contemplating her.
“Eat,” he bade her at last. “You will need strength and courage, and neither is possible to a fasting body.”
She shook her head. Despite her long fast, food was repellent. Anxiety was thrusting her heart up into her throat to choke her.
“I cannot eat,” she answered him. “To what end? Strength and courage cannot avail me now.”
“Never believe that,” he said. “I have undertaken to deliver you alive from the perils into which I have brought you, and I shall keep my word.”
So resolute was his tone that she looked up at him, and found his bearing equally resolute and confident.
“Surely,” she cried, “all chance of escape is lost to me.”
“Never count it lost whilst I am living,” he replied.

===Chinese===

阿萨德那双凶猛贪婪的眼睛就这般把她望了片刻，随后她进去了。萨克埃尔-巴尔跟进去，遮光的帘子荡回原位。

小小的舱室里，陈设是一张铺着丝毯的长榻，一张嵌彩色木镶嵌的摩尔式矮桌，桌上摆着新点的灯，还有一只小小的火盆，香胶在盆里燃着，散出一股甜而辛辣的香气——那是熏净一切真信士的香。

暗影里，萨克埃尔-巴尔的两名努比亚奴隶阿比亚德和扎尔-泽尔，无声地站起来，向他深深行礼。若不是头巾和雪白的腰布，他们黝黑的身子定还隐在暗影里，影影绰绰看不出来。

船长简短地下了令，奴隶们从吊橱里取出酒食，摆上矮桌——一盆鸡肉，同米饭、橄榄和无花果干同炖，一盘饼，一只甜瓜，一陶罐水。他又一声令下，两人各执一柄出鞘的弯刀，出去分立帘外守望。这一手不含威胁，不含挑衅，阿萨德也不会那样解读。萨克埃尔-巴尔的妻子既已公认住在这艉楼舱里，此地便等同他的内宅；男人守内宅如守名誉，那是他一个人的圣地，谁也不得侵犯，预为防范任何不敬的企图，本是应当。

罗莎蒙德颓然坐到长榻上，垂着头，双手交叠在膝上。萨克埃尔-巴尔在旁默默站了好一会儿，端详她。

“吃。”他终于吩咐。“你需要力气，需要胆气，而饿着肚子，两样都无从谈起。”

她摇头。尽管饿了那么久，食物仍叫她反胃。焦灼把她的心顶上喉咙眼，噎着她。

“我吃不下。”她答。“吃了又怎样？力气和胆气，如今都救不了我。”

“永远别信那个。”他说。“我既已应承把你活着带出我拖你进的这片险地，我就要说到做到。”

他的声气那样决绝，她抬起头来看他，见他的神气也同样决绝，同样有把握。

“肯定，”她喊，“我逃走的机会全没了。”

“只要我活着，就永远别把它算没了。”他答。

===Original===

She considered him a moment, and there was the faintest smile on her lips.
“Do you think that you will live long now?” she asked him.
“Just as long as God pleases,” he replied quite coolly. “What is written is written. So that I live long enough to deliver you, then﻿ ﻿… why, then, faith I shall have lived long enough.”
Her head sank. She clasped and unclasped the hands in her lap. She shivered slightly.
“I think we are both doomed,” she said in a dull voice. “For if you die, I have your dagger still, remember. I shall not survive you.”
He took a sudden step forward, his eyes gleaming, a faint flush glowing through the tan of his cheeks. Then he checked. Fool! How could he so have misread her meaning even for a moment? Were not its exact limits abundantly plain, even without the words which she added a moment later?
“God will forgive me if I am driven to it﻿—if I choose the easier way of honour; for honour, sir,” she added, clearly for his benefit, “is ever the easier way, believe me.”
“I know,” he replied contritely. “I would to God I had followed it.”
He paused there, as if hoping that his expression of penitence might evoke some answer from her, might spur her to vouchsafe him some word of forgiveness. Seeing that she continued, mute and absorbed, he sighed heavily, and turned to other matters.
“Here you will find all that you can require,” he said. “Should you lack aught you have but to beat your hands together, one or the other of my slaves will come to you. If you address them in French they will understand you. I would I could have brought a woman to minister to you, but that was impossible, as you’ll perceive.” He stepped to the entrance.
“You are leaving me?” she questioned him in sudden alarm.
“Naturally. But be sure that I shall be very near at hand. And meanwhile be no less sure that you have no cause for immediate fear. At least, matters are no worse than when you were in the pannier. Indeed, much better, for some measure of ease and comfort is now possible to you. So be of good heart; eat and rest. God guard you! I shall return soon after sunrise.”
Outside on the poop deck he found Asad alone now with Marzak under the awning.

===Chinese===

她打量了他一会儿，唇边浮起一丝极淡的笑。

“你以为，你如今还能活多久？”她问他。

“上帝乐意多久，就多久。”他答得极平静。“写定的就是写定的。只要我活得够把你送出去，那么……嗐，那么，说实话，我也就活够了。”

她的头垂下去。她把叠在膝上的手紧了又松，松了又紧。她微微发抖。

“我想咱们两个都完了。”她用一种发木的声音说。“因为你若死了，我还有你的匕首，记得么。我不会活在你后头。”

他猛地上前一步，眼睛发亮，黝黑的脸颊透出一层淡淡的红。随即他刹住了。傻瓜！他怎么会有一瞬间错解了她的意思？它的界限岂不明明白白，更何况她片刻之后又添的那几句？

“上帝会饶恕我的，若我是被逼到那一步——若我挑了荣誉里较易走的一条；因为荣誉，先生，”她补上一句，分明是讲给他听的，“永远是比较易走的一条，相信我。”

“我知道。”他懊悔地答。“巴不得我当初走的就是它。”

他停在那里，仿佛盼着他这番悔悟能换来她一句回答，诱她赏他一句宽恕。见她依旧默然、出神，他重重叹了口气，转到别的事上。

“你在这儿要什么有什么。”他说。“短了什么，只消拍拍手，我的两个奴隶总有一个会来。你同他们讲法语，他们听得懂。我巴不得给你带个女人来伺候，可你也看得出，那办不到。”他走到门口。

“你要丢下我？”她骤然惊慌地问。

“自然。可放心，我就在近旁。这会儿也一样放心：眼前的危险，你一概没有。至少，局面不比你挂在挂篮里那阵更糟。实在说，好得多——如今你多少能安顿、能舒坦了。所以打起精神；吃点，歇歇。上帝保佑你！日出后不久我就回来。”

外头艉楼甲板上，他见阿萨德此刻只同马尔扎克在凉棚下。

===Original===

Night had fallen, the great crescent lanterns on the stern rail were alight and cast a lurid glow along the vessel’s length, picking out the shadowy forms and gleaming faintly on the naked backs of the slaves in their serried ranks along the benches, many of them bowed already in attitudes of uneasy slumber. Another lantern swung from the mainmast, and yet another from the poop rail for the Basha’s convenience. Overhead the clustering stars glittered in a cloudless sky of deepest purple. The wind had fallen entirely, and the world was wrapped in stillness broken only by the faint rustling break of waves upon the beach at the cove’s end.
Sakr-el-Bahr crossed to Asad’s side, and begged for a word alone with him.
“I am alone,” said the Basha curtly.
“Marzak is nothing, then,” said Sakr-el-Bahr. “I have long suspected it.”
Marzak showed his teeth and growled inarticulately, whilst the Basha, taken aback by the ease reflected in the captain’s careless, mocking words, could but quote a line of the Koran with which Fenzileh of late had often nauseated him.
“A man’s son is the partner of his soul. I have no secrets from Marzak. Speak, then, before him, or else be silent and depart.”
“He may be the partner of thy soul, Asad,” replied the corsair with his bold mockery, “but I give thanks to Allah he is not the partner of mine. And what I have to say in some sense concerns my soul.”
“I thank thee,” cut in Marzak, “for the justice of thy words. To be the partner of thy soul were to be an infidel unbelieving dog.”
“Thy tongue, O Marzak, is like thine archery,” said Sakr-el-Bahr.
“Ay﻿—in that it pierces treachery,” was the swift retort.
“Nay﻿—in that it aims at what it cannot hit. Now, Allah, pardon me! Shall I grow angry at such words as thine? Hath not the One proven full oft that he who calls me infidel dog is a liar predestined to the Pit? Are such victories as mine over the fleets of the unbelievers vouchsafed by Allah to an infidel? Foolish blasphemer, teach thy tongue better ways lest the All-wise strike thee dumb.”
“Peace!” growled Asad. “Thine arrogance is out of season.”
“Haply so,” said Sakr-el-Bahr, with a laugh. “And my good sense, too, it seems. Since thou wilt retain beside thee this partner of thy soul, I must speak before him. Have I thy leave to sit?”

===Chinese===

夜已经落了，艉栏杆上那几盏巨大的新月形灯笼点起来了，一道血红的 光顺着船身照过去，照出一排排影影绰绰的身形，微微照亮桨凳上密密匝匝的奴隶们的赤背，其中许多已经歪成不安的睡态。主桅上还吊着一盏灯，艉栏杆上又为帕夏另悬一盏。头顶上，繁星成簇，在深紫无云的天幕里闪烁。风全然止了，天地裹在寂静里，只有湾底沙滩上浪头的轻碎声，偶尔响一下。

萨克埃尔-巴尔走到阿萨德身边，求他单独一谈。

“就我一个人。”帕夏简慢地说。

“那么马尔扎克什么也不是。”萨克埃尔-巴尔说。“我早疑心是这么回事。”

马尔扎克龇出牙，含混地咆哮；帕夏呢，被船长这句满不在乎、带着讥诮的话里透出的那份从容惊了一下，只好引一句《古兰经》——近来芬齐莱拿这句把他烦得想吐。

“一个人的儿子是他灵魂的伙伴。我对马尔扎克没有秘密。那么，要讲就当着他讲，不然就闭嘴走人。”

“他兴许是你灵魂的伙伴，阿萨德，”海盗以他大胆的嘲讽答道，“可我感谢安拉，他不是我灵魂的伙伴。而我要讲的，倒真有几分牵涉我的灵魂。”

“多谢你这句公道话。”马尔扎克插进来，“做你灵魂的伙伴，就是做一条不信教的异教狗。”

“你的舌头，哦马尔扎克，同你的箭术一样。”萨克埃尔-巴尔说。

“是——一样的扎穿奸诈。”对方飞快回敬。

“不——一样的瞄着它够不着的东西。罢了，安拉饶我！我岂能同你这种字眼计较？独一的主岂不已屡屡证明：叫我异教狗的人，就是个注定下火坑的撒谎者？我在不信者的舰队上赢来的这些胜利，是安拉赏给异教徒的么？愚蠢的渎神者，管教好你的舌头，不然全智者叫你哑掉。”

“住口！”阿萨德咆哮。“你的狂傲不得其时。”

“也许吧。”萨克埃尔-巴尔笑着说。“看来我的好见识也不得其时。既然你要把你灵魂的伙伴留在身边，我只好当着他讲了。容我坐么？”

===Original===

Lest such leave should be denied him he dropped forthwith to the vacant place beside Asad and tucked his legs under him.
“Lord,” he said, “there is a rift dividing us who should be united for the glory of Islam.”
“It is of thy making, Sakr-el-Bahr,” was the sullen answer, “and it is for thee to mend it.”
“To that end do I desire thine ear. The cause of this rift is yonder.” And he jerked his thumb backward over his shoulder towards the poop house. “If we remove that cause, of a surety the rift itself will vanish, and all will be well again between us.”
He knew that never could all be well again between him and Asad. He knew that by virtue of his act of defiance he was irrevocably doomed, that Asad having feared him once, having dreaded his power to stand successfully against his face and overbear his will, would see to it that he never dreaded it again. He knew that if he returned to Algiers there would be a speedy end to him. His only chance of safety lay, indeed, in stirring up mutiny upon the spot and striking swiftly, venturing all upon that desperate throw. And he knew that this was precisely what Asad had cause to fear. Out of this assurance had he conceived his present plan, deeming that if he offered to heal the breach, Asad might pretend to consent so as to weather his present danger, making doubly sure of his vengeance by waiting until they should be home again.
Asad’s gleaming eyes considered him in silence for a moment.
“How remove that cause?” he asked. “Wilt thou atone for the mockery of thy marriage, pronounce her divorced and relinquish her?”
“That were not to remove her,” replied Sakr-el-Bahr. “Consider well, Asad, what is thy duty to the Faith. Consider that upon our unity depends the glory of Islam. Were it not sinful, then, to suffer the intrusion of aught that may mar such unity? Nay, nay, what I propose is that I should be permitted﻿—assisted even﻿—to bear out the project I had formed, as already I have frankly made confession. Let us put to sea again at dawn﻿—or this very night if thou wilt﻿—make for the coast of France, and there set her ashore that she may go back to her own people and we be rid of her disturbing presence.

===Chinese===

不等许可被拒，他一屁股坐进阿萨德旁的空位，盘起双腿。

“大人，”他说，“眼下有一道裂縫，横在我们本该为伊斯兰的荣光合为一体的两人中间。”

“那是你凿出来的，萨克埃尔-巴尔。”回答阴沉，“也该由你来补。”

“要补，所以我求你的耳朵。裂缝的根由就在那边。”他用拇指往肩后一指艉楼舱。“咱们把根由挪走，裂缝自然就合了，你我之间便一切如初。”

他清楚，他同阿萨德之间永远不能再一切如初了。他清楚，凭他那一抗，他的命数已经注定；阿萨德既然怕过他一回——怕他真能顶住自己的脸面、压过自己的意志——就断不容自己再怕第二回。他清楚，他若回阿尔及尔，便是速死。他唯一的活路，其实就在于当场煽起哗变，迅疾出手，把一切押上这绝望的一掷。而他也清楚，这恰恰是阿萨德有得怕的。正是吃准了这一点，他才想出眼下的计策：他若主动补这道裂缝，阿萨德兴许会假意应允，好先捱过眼前的险，等回了家，再把仇算得加倍稳。

阿萨德闪亮的眼睛默默打量了他片刻。

“怎么个挪走法？”他问。“你可是要为你那场婚事闹剧赎罪，宣布休了她，放了她？”

“那可挪不走她。”萨克埃尔-巴尔答。“好好掂量掂量，阿萨德，你对正教的本分是什么。掂量掂量：伊斯兰的荣光，系在你我的团结上。那么，容任何可能毁掉这团结的东西横在中间，岂不是罪？不，不。我的提议是：请容我——不如说帮我——把我原本的谋划办完，这我已经坦白招认过了。天一亮咱们就再出海——你若愿意，今夜就走——驶往法兰西海岸，把她送上岸，让她回她自己人那里去，咱们也甩掉这个扰人的祸根。

===Original===

Then we will return﻿—there is time and to spare﻿—and here or elsewhere lurk in wait for this Spanish argosy, seize the booty and sail home in amity to Algiers, this incident, this little cloud in the splendour of our comradeship, behind us and forgotten as though it had never been. Wilt thou, Asad﻿—for the glory of the Prophet’s Law?”
The bait was cunningly presented, so cunningly that not for a moment did Asad or even the malicious Marzak suspect it to be just a bait and no more. It was his own life, become a menace to Asad, that Sakr-el-Bahr was offering him in exchange for the life and liberty of that Frankish slave-girl, but offering it as if unconscious that he did so.
Asad considered, temptation gripping, him. Prudence urged him to accept, so that affecting to heal the dangerous breach that now existed he might carry Sakr-el-Bahr back to Algiers, there, beyond the aid of any friendly mutineers, to have him strangled. It was the course to adopt in such a situation, the wise and sober course by which to ensure the overthrow of one who from an obedient and submissive lieutenant had suddenly shown that it was possible for him to become a serious and dangerous rival.
Sakr-el-Bahr watched the Basha’s averted, gleaming eyes under their furrowed, thoughtful brows, he saw Marzak’s face white, tense and eager in his anxiety that his father should consent. And since his father continued silent, Marzak, unable longer to contain himself, broke into speech.
“He is wise, O my father!” was his crafty appeal. “The glory of Islam above all else! Let him have his way in this, and let the infidel woman go. Thus shall all be well between us and Sakr-el-Bahr!” He laid such a stress upon these words that it was obvious he desired them to convey a second meaning.
Asad heard and understood that Marzak, too, perceived what was here to do; tighter upon him became temptation’s grip; but tighter, too, became the grip of a temptation of another sort. Before his fierce eyes there arose a vision of a tall stately maiden with softly rounded bosom, a vision so white and lovely that it enslaved him. And so he found himself torn two ways at once. On the one hand, if he relinquished the woman, he could make sure of his vengeance upon Sakr-el-Bahr, could make sure of removing that rebel from his path.

===Chinese===

然后咱们回来——时辰绰绰有余——在这儿或别处守候那条西班牙大商船，夺下财货，和和美美驶回阿尔及尔，这桩意外，咱们手足之情的光彩里这一小片云，便落在身后，忘个干净，仿佛从来没有过。你肯么，阿萨德——为了先知之法的荣光？”

这饵下得巧，巧得阿萨德、连那个满心恶毒的马尔扎克，都不曾有一刻疑心它不过是块饵。萨克埃尔-巴尔拿去换那法兰克女奴的性命和自由的，正是他自己那条已然威胁到阿萨德的命——而且递出去时装得浑然不觉。

阿萨德沉吟，诱惑攥住了他。审慎劝他收下：表面愈合眼下这道凶险的裂缝，好把萨克埃尔-巴尔带回阿尔及尔，到了那儿，任凭什么友好的哗变者都够不着他，再勒死他。处这样的局面，这是正路，是明智稳当的路，保得翻一个从恭顺的副将一变而成危险对手的人落马。

萨克埃尔-巴尔看着帕夏那双避开的眼在紧锁沉思的眉下闪光，看见马尔扎克的脸急得发白、绷紧、热切——巴望他父亲点头。父亲迟迟不语，马尔扎克按捺不住，开口了。

“他有见识，哦我的父亲！”是他狡黠的进言。“伊斯兰的荣光高于一切！这件事上由他去，放那异教女人走。这样，咱们同萨克埃尔-巴尔之间就万事大吉了！”他把这几字咬得那样重，明摆着盼它们载着第二层意思。

阿萨德听了，明白马尔扎克也看出了此间当行的路；诱惑攥他攥得更紧；可另一种诱惑也攥得更紧。他凶猛的眼睛前头，升起一个颀长端庄的少女的身影，胸脯柔圆——那身影那样白，那样可爱，把他奴役了。于是他发现自己被两头同时撕扯。一头，他若放了这女人，便可稳稳报萨克埃尔-巴尔的仇，稳稳把这叛贼从路上搬开。

===Original===

On the other hand, if he determined to hold fast to his desires and to be ruled by them, he must be prepared to risk a mutiny aboard the *galeasse*, prepared for battle and perhaps for defeat. It was a stake such as no sane Basha would have consented to set upon the board. But since his eyes had again rested upon Rosamund, Asad was no longer sane. His thwarted desires of yesterday were the despots of his wits.
He leaned forward now, looking deep into the eyes of Sakr-el-Bahr.
“Since for thyself thou dost not want her, why dost thou thwart me?” he asked, and his voice trembled with suppressed passion. “So long as I deemed thee honest in taking her to wife I respected that bond as became a good Muslim; but since ’tis manifest that it was no more than a pretence, a mockery to serve some purpose hostile to myself, a desecration of the Prophet’s Holy Law, I, before whom this blasphemous marriage was performed, do pronounce it to be no marriage. There is no need for thee to divorce her. She is no longer thine. She is for any Muslim who can take her.”
Sakr-el-Bahr laughed unpleasantly. “Such a Muslim,” he announced, “will be nearer my sword than the Paradise of Muhammad.” And on the words he stood up, as if in token of his readiness.
Asad rose with him in a bound of a vigour such as might scarce have been looked for in a man of his years.
“Dost threaten?” he cried, his eyes aflash.
“Threaten?” sneered Sakr-el-Bahr. “I prophesy.”
And on that he turned, and stalked away down the gangway to the vessel’s waist. There was no purpose in his going other than his perceiving that here argument were worse than useless, and that the wiser course were to withdraw at once, avoiding it and allowing his veiled threat to work upon the Basha’s mind.
Quivering with rage Asad watched his departure. On the point of commanding him to return, he checked, fearing lest in his present mood Sakr-el-Bahr should flout his authority and under the eyes of all refuse him the obedience due. He knew that it is not good to command where we are not sure of being obeyed or of being able to enforce obedience, that an authority once successfully flouted is in itself half-shattered.
Whilst still he hesitated, Marzak, who had also risen, caught him by the arm and poured into his ear hot, urgent arguments enjoining him to yield to Sakr-el-Bahr’s demand.
“It is the sure way,” he cried insistently.

===Chinese===

另一头，他若决意抱紧自己的欲念、受它摆布，就得备着加莱塞船上哗变的风险，备着厮杀，说不定备着败仗。这样的赌注，没有一个神志清明的帕夏肯摆上棋盘。可自从那双眼重新落在罗莎蒙德身上，阿萨德的神志就不再清明了。昨日受挫的欲念，成了他心智的暴君。

他俯身向前，深深望进萨克埃尔-巴尔的眼睛。

“既然你自己并不要她，为什么偏要阻我？”他问，声音颤着强压的欲火。“我只当她娶她是出于真心，便以一个好穆斯林应有的敬重待那桩婚约；可如今分明可见，那不过是幌子，是一场为我而设的把戏，一场对先知圣法的亵渎——那么，我，这场渎神婚礼的主婚人，宣布它不成为婚。你不必休她。她不再是你的人了。她是任何一个能拿到她的穆斯林的。”

萨克埃尔-巴尔不快地笑了。“哪一个这样的穆斯林，”他宣示，“离我的剑，都要比离穆罕默德的乐园近。”话音里他站起身，仿佛表示随时奉陪。

阿萨德一跃而起，那股劲健，在他这年纪的人身上几乎无人能料。

“你这是威胁？”他喊，双眼喷火。

“威胁？”萨克埃尔-巴尔冷笑。“我这是预言。”

说完他转身，大步下了跳板，往船腰去了。他走并无别的用意，只因为看清了在此地多讲一句都不如不讲，上策是立即抽身，避开争论，让他那句半遮半掩的威胁，自己去帕夏心里发酵。

阿萨德抖着看他的背影，险些喝令他回来，又忍住了——怕萨克埃尔-巴尔这会儿的火头上，当众折他的令，拒不从命。他懂得：没有把握令人服从或强制服从，就不该下那道令；权威一旦被人当众折了，本身就碎了一半。

他还犹豫着，也起了身的马尔扎克一把挽住他的手臂，往他耳朵里灌滚烫的、急切的道理，劝他顺着萨克埃尔-巴尔的要求办。

“这是万全的路。”他一个劲儿地喊。

===Original===

“Shall all be jeopardized for the sake of that whey-faced daughter of perdition? In the name of Shaitan, let us be rid of her; set her ashore as he demands, as the price of peace between us and him, and in the security of that peace let him be strangled when we come again to our moorings in Algiers. It is the sure way﻿—the sure way!”
Asad turned at last to look into that handsome eager face. For a moment he was at a loss; then he had recourse to sophistry. “Am I a coward that I should refuse all ways but sure ones?” he demanded in a withering tone. “Or art thou a coward who can counsel none other?”
“My anxiety is all for thee, O my father,” Marzak defended himself indignantly. “I doubt if it be safe to sleep, lest he should stir up mutiny in the night.”
