此刻她跪伏在地，一副柔顺到极处的姿态，可爱的手臂缠着他的双膝。“我对你的爱逼我说出我看见的，换来的却只有你的怒——这怒我受不住。它压得我要晕了。”

他不耐烦地把她排开。“妇人的舌头真叫人乏！”他喊，大步走了出去——凭往日的经验他知道，再待下去，就要被一场言语的洪流淹没了。

可她的毒药下得刁，慢慢地发了性。它盘踞在他心里，拿疑心——毒药的本体——熬煎他。她哪怕摆出一条编得再圆的理由，解释萨克埃尔-巴尔的反常行止，也及不上她这“其中必有缘由”的暗示一半阴毒。这暗示给了他一团模糊缥缈的东西去琢磨，一样他无从抵挡的东西——因为它没有形迹，无处下手。他焦灼地等天亮，等萨克埃尔-巴尔本人到来；可他等的，已不再是一位父亲等爱子归来的那种一片赤诚的热望。

萨克埃尔-巴尔本人，此刻正在大帆船的艉楼甲板上踱步，望着山坡上那座蔓延的小镇里灯火一盏一盏熄灭。月亮升起来，把小镇浴在一片白硬的光里，把飒飒的枣椰树和枪尖似的宣礼塔投成锋利的墨影，把一枝枝银辉掷过安详的海湾。

他的伤好了，人完全复原了。两天前，自同荷兰人交手以来他头一回上了甲板，此后大半时日都消磨在那上面。俘虏们他只去看过一回。他从卧榻上起来，径直去了艉楼囚着罗莎蒙德的舱房。他见她苍白而怅惘，勇气却半点未折。戈多尔芬家本是硬脖子的种族，罗莎蒙德那纤弱的身子里，装着一个男子的魂。他进去时她抬起眼，见他终于来了，微微一惊——自四个星期前他从阿文纳克掳走她以来，这是他头一回站在她面前。那时她别过眼去，双肘支在桌上坐着，像一段雕出来的木头，对他的存在视而不见，对他的话充耳不闻。

===Original===

To the expressions of regret﻿—and they were sincere, for already he repented him his unpremeditated act so far as she was concerned﻿—she returned no slightest answer, gave no sign indeed that she heard a word of it. Baffled, he stood gnawing his lip a moment, and gradually, unreasonably perhaps, anger welled up from his heart. He turned and went out again. Next he had visited his brother, to consider in silence a moment the haggard, wild-eyed, unshorn wretch who shrank and cowered before him in the consciousness of guilt. At last he returned to the deck, and there, as I have said, he spent the greater portion of the last three days of that strange voyage, reclining for the most part in the sun and gathering strength from its ardour.
Tonight as he paced under the moon a stealthy shadow crept up the companion to call him gently by his English name﻿—
“Sir Oliver!”
He started as if a ghost had suddenly leapt up to greet him. It was Jasper Leigh who hailed him thus.
“Come up,” he said. And when the fellow stood before him on the poop﻿—“I have told you already that here is no Sir Oliver. I am Oliver-Reis or Sakr-el-Bahr, as you please, one of the Faithful of the Prophet’s House. And now what is your will?”
“Have I not served you faithfully and well?” quoth Captain Leigh.
“Who has denied it?”
“None. But neither has any acknowledged it. When you lay wounded below it had been an easy thing for me to ha’ played the traitor. I might ha’ sailed these ships into the mouth of Tagus. I might so by God!”
“You’d have been carved in pieces on the spot,” said Sakr-el-Bahr.
“I might have hugged the land and run the risk of capture and then claimed my liberation from captivity.”
“And found yourself back on the galleys of his Catholic Majesty. But there! I grant that you have dealt loyally by me. You have kept your part of the bond. I shall keep mine, never doubt it.”
“I do not. But your part of the bond was to send me home again.”
“Well?”
“The hell of it is that I know not where to find a home, I know not where home may be after all these years. If ye send me forth, I shall become a wanderer of no account.”
“What else am I to do with you?”
“Faith now I am as full weary of Christians and Christendom as you was yourself when the Muslims took the galley on which you toiled. I am a man of parts, Sir Ol﻿—Sakr-el-Bahr.

===Chinese===

对那些惋惜的话——那是真心的，因为就她而言，他早已懊悔自己那桩临时起意的举动了——她没有半个字的回应，甚至没有一点听见了的表示。他碰了钉子，咬着嘴唇立了片刻，渐渐地——也许没有道理——一股火从心底涌上来。他转身又出去了。接着他去看弟弟，默默打量了那憔悴、瞪眼、蓬头垢面的家伙片刻——那人自知有罪，在他面前缩着、抖着。最后他回到甲板上，此后，如我所说，那趟奇异的航程里最后三天的大半时日，他都在那儿度过，多半晒着太阳，从日头的热力里攒力气。

今夜，他正在月下踱步，一道鬼祟的影子悄悄爬上舷梯，用他的英语名字轻轻唤他——

“奥利弗爵士！”

他吓了一跳，像有个鬼魅蓦地跳出来迎他。这样招呼他的，是贾斯珀·利。

“上来。”他说。等那家伙站到艉楼上他面前——“我说过了，这里没有什么奥利弗爵士。我是奥利弗-雷斯，或者萨克埃尔-巴尔，随你叫，先知之家的一名信士。说吧，你要什么？”

“我待你，不算不忠心、不出力吧？”利船长说。

“谁否认过？”

“没人否认。可也没人认账。你躺在下面养伤那阵，我要当叛徒，易如反掌。我本可以把这几条船开进塔霍河口。我对天发誓，我本来能！”

“那你当场就会被剁成肉泥。”萨克埃尔-巴尔说。

“我也可以贴着岸走，冒被俘的险，然后要求释放我这俘虏。”

“那你就重新回到天主教陛下的桨帆船上去了。行了！我认，你待我不薄。你守了约的一半。我那一半也会守，别怀疑。”

“我不怀疑。可你那一半，是送我回家。”

“怎么样？”

“要命的是，我不知道上哪儿找家。这些年过去，家在哪儿我都不知道了。你们放我走，我就成了个没着落的浪人。”

“那你要我拿你怎么办？”

“嗐，如今我对基督徒和基督教世界，也厌透了，跟你当年在穆斯林掳了你卖苦力的那条桨帆船上时一样。我是个人才，奥利弗爵——萨克埃尔-巴尔。

===Original===

No better navigator ever sailed a ship from an English port, and I ha’ seen a mort o’ fighting and know the art of it upon the sea. Can ye make naught of me here?”
“You would become a renegade like me?” His tone was bitter.
“I ha’ been thinking that ‘renegade’ is a word that depends upon which side you’re on. I’d prefer to say that I’ve a wish to be converted to the faith of Mahound.”
“Converted to the faith of piracy and plunder and robbery upon the seas is what you mean,” said Sakr-el-Bahr.
“Nay, now. To that I should need no converting, for all that I were afore,” Captain Leigh admitted frankly. “I ask but to sail under another flag than the Jolly Roger.”
“You’ll need to abjure strong drink,” said Sakr-el-Bahr.
“There be compensations,” said Master Leigh.
Sakr-el-Bahr considered. The rogue’s appeal smote a responsive chord in his heart. It would be good to have a man of his own race beside him, even though it were but such a rascal as this.
“Be it as you will,” he said at last. “You deserve to be hanged in spite of what promises I made you. But no matter for that. So that you become a Muslim I will take you to serve beside me, one of my own lieutenants to begin with, and so long as you are loyal to me, Jasper, all will be well. But at the first sign of faithlessness, a rope and the yardarm, my friend, and an airy dance into hell for you.”
The rascally skipper stooped in his emotion, caught up Sakr-el-Bahr’s hand and bore it to his lips.
“It is agreed,” he said. “Ye have shown me mercy who have little deserved it from you. Never fear for my loyalty. My life belongs to you, and worthless thing though it may be, ye may do with it as ye please.”
Despite himself Sakr-el-Bahr tightened his grip upon the rogue’s hand, and Jasper shuffled off and down the companion again, touched to the heart for once in his rough villainous life by a clemency that he knew to be undeserved, but which he swore should be deserved ere all was done.

===Chinese===

从英国港口开出来的船，没有哪个领航员胜得过我；大小恶仗我见得多了，海上的仗怎么打，我懂。你们在这儿，能拿我派上用场么？”

“你要做叛教者，像我一样？”他的声气发苦。

“我近来想明白了，“叛教”这个字眼，全看你站在哪一头。我宁愿说，我有心皈依马洪德的教门。”

“你是说皈依海盗的教门、劫掠的教门、在海上行抢的教门。”萨克埃尔-巴尔说。

“嗐，那倒不必皈依——我从前干的本来就是那个。”利船长坦白承认。“我只求换一面旗，别再挂‘快乐罗杰’。”

“那你得戒烈酒。”萨克埃尔-巴尔说。

“总有别的补償。”利船长说。

萨克埃尔-巴尔沉吟。这无赖的恳求，敲中了他心里一根应和的弦。身边有个自己种族的人，倒也不错——哪怕只是这么个流氓。

“随你吧。”他终于说。“凭我对你许的诺，你本该上绞架。可那且不管。只要你入伊斯兰，我就收你在身边效力，先做我的一名副将；只要你对我忠心，贾斯珀，一切都好。可一露出半分不忠，就是绳子和桁端，我的朋友，送你到地狱去跳一场空中的舞。”

那无赖船长感情上来，弯腰抓起萨克埃尔-巴尔的手，凑到唇边吻了。

“成了。”他说。“你们对我这个不配有怜悯的人发了慈悲。别愁我的忠心。这条命是你的了，虽然不值钱，随你怎么使唤。”

萨克埃尔-巴尔不由自主，把那无赖的手攥紧了些；贾斯珀蹭着脚退下去，又下了舷梯——这粗坯恶棍的一生里，头一遭被一份他自知不配的宽仁碰到了心，并发誓趁一切未了，挣出个配得上它的样子来。

## XVII The Dupe / 十七 受骗人
===Original===

For a little while Asad stood at gaze, speechless in his incredulity. Then to revive the anger that for a moment had been whelmed in astonishment came the reflection that he had been duped by Sakr-el-Bahr, duped by the man he trusted most. He had snarled at Fenzileh and scorned Marzak when they had jointly warned him against his lieutenant; if at times he had been in danger of heeding them, yet sooner or later he had concluded that they but spoke to vent their malice. And yet it was proven now that they had been right in their estimate of this traitor, whilst he himself had been a poor, blind dupe, needing Marzak’s wit to tear the bandage from his eyes.
Slowly he went down the gangway, followed by Marzak, Biskaine, and the others. At the point where it joined the waist deck he paused, and his dark old eyes smouldered under his beetling brows.
“So,” he snarled. “These are thy goods of price. Thou lying dog, what was thine aim in this?”
Defiantly Sakr-el-Bahr answered him: “She is my wife. It is my right to take her with me where I go.” He turned to her, and bade her veil her face, and she immediately obeyed him with fingers that shook a little in her agitation.
“None questions thy right to that,” said Asad. “But being resolved to take her with thee, why not take her openly? Why was she not housed in the poop house, as becomes the wife of Sakr-el-Bahr? Why smuggle her aboard in a pannier, and keep her there in secret?”
“And why,” added Marzak, “didst thou lie to me when I questioned thee upon her whereabouts?﻿—telling me she was left behind in thy house in Algiers?”
“All this I did,” replied Sakr-el-Bahr, with a lofty﻿—almost a disdainful﻿—dignity, “because I feared lest I should be prevented from bearing her away with me,” and his bold glance, beating full upon Asad, drew a wave of colour into the gaunt old cheeks.
“What could have caused that fear?” he asked. “Shall I tell thee? Because no man sailing upon such a voyage as this would have desired the company of his new-wedded wife. Because no man would take a wife with him upon a raid in which there is peril of life and peril of capture.”
“Allah has watched over me his servant in the past,” said Sakr-el-Bahr, “and I put my trust in Him.”
It was a specious answer. Such words﻿—laying stress upon the victories Allah sent him﻿—had aforetime served to disarm his enemies. But they served not now.

===Chinese===

阿萨德愣愣地立了一阵，惊愕得说不出话。随后他想到：骗他的正是萨克埃尔-巴尔，骗他的正是他最信任的那个人——这一想，那阵被惊愕压下去的怒火又翻腾上来。当初芬齐莱同马尔扎克联手提醒他提防他的副手时，他冲她们咆哮、嗤笑；纵然有几回他差一点听了进去，可早晚总要断定：她们不过是出口恶毒罢了。而如今事实证明，她们对这奸贼的估量是对的，他自己却是个可怜的、瞎了眼的受骗人，还得靠马尔扎克的聪明，才扯下蒙眼的布。
他缓缓下了舷梯，马尔扎克、比斯坎同众人跟着。在舷梯接舱腰甲板的地方，他停住，突出的眉弓底下，一双苍老的黑眼睛闪着阴火。
“好啊，”他狺狺道，“这就是你那贵重的货物。你这条撒谎的狗，你安的什么心？”
萨克埃尔-巴尔昂然答他：“她是我的妻子。我到哪里，带她同去，这是我的权利。”他转向她，叫她把脸罩上；她立刻照办，激荡的手指微微发抖。
“这权利没人质疑，”阿萨德说，“可你既打定主意带她同去，为何不堂堂正正地带？为何不把她安置在后楼舱里，像个萨克埃尔-巴尔的妻子该有的样子？为何把她藏在驮篮里偷运上船，又偷偷藏在那儿？”
“还有，”马尔扎克补一句，“我盘问她在哪儿时，你为何对我撒谎？——说什么她留在你阿尔及尔的宅子里？”
“这一切我这样做，”萨克埃尔回答，带着一种高——近乎倨傲——的庄严，“是因为我怕有人阻拦，不让我带她走。”他那大胆的目光直直打在阿萨德脸上，打得起颧骨枯瘦的老颊涌上一股血色。
“什么能叫你起这样的怕？”他问。“要我替你说么？因为走这样一趟航程的人，没有一个会想带着新婚的妻子。因为没有一个男人，会把妻子带上一场有性命之险、被俘之险的劫掠。”
“安拉往日一直看顾我这仆人，”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，“我信靠他。”
这回答说得圆。这样的言辞——把安拉赐他的胜利摆在头里——从前不知多少次替他卸下了敌人的兵。可这一回不灵了。

===Original===

Instead, they did but fan the flames of Asad’s wrath.
“Blaspheme not,” he croaked, and his tall form quivered with rage, his sallow old face grew vulturine. “She was brought thus aboard in secret out of fear that were her presence known thy true purpose too must stand revealed.”
“And whatever that true purpose may have been,” put in Marzak, “it was not the task entrusted thee of raiding the Spanish treasure galley.”
“ ’Tis what I mean, my son,” Asad agreed. Then with a commanding gesture: “Wilt thou tell me without further lies what thy purpose was?” he asked.
“How?” said Sakr-el-Bahr, and he smiled never so faintly. “Hast thou not said that this purpose was revealed by what I did? Rather, then, I think is it for me to ask thee for some such information. I do assure thee, my lord, that it was no part of my intention to neglect the task entrusted me. But just because I feared lest knowledge of her presence might lead my enemies to suppose what thou art now supposing, and perhaps persuade thee to forget all that I have done for the glory of Islam, I determined to bring her secretly aboard.
“My real aim, since you must know it, was to land her somewhere on the coast of France, whence she might return to her own land, and her own people. That done, I should have set about intercepting the Spanish galley, and never fear but that by Allah’s favour I should have succeeded.”
“By the horns of Shaitan,” swore Marzak, thrusting himself forward, “he is the very father and mother of lies. Wilt thou explain this desire to be rid of a wife thou hadst but wed?” he demanded.
“Ay,” growled Asad. “Canst answer that?”
“Thou shalt hear the truth,” said Sakr-el-Bahr.
“The praise to Allah!” mocked Marzak.
“But I warn you,” the corsair continued, “that to you it will seem less easy to believe by much than any falsehood I could invent. Years ago in England where I was born I loved this woman and should have taken her to wife. But there were men and circumstances that defamed me to her so that she would not wed me, and I went forth with hatred of her in my heart. Last night the love of her which I believed to be dead and turned to loathing, proved to be still a living force. Loving her, I came to see that I had used her unworthily, and I was urged by a desire above all others to undo the evil I had done.”

===Chinese===

非但不灵，反倒扇旺了阿萨德的怒火。
“休得渎神，”他嘶着嗓子说，高大的身形气得直抖，焦黄的老脸成了副鹫相。“偷偷把她运上船，就是因为怕——怕她一露面，你真正的目的也就露了。”
“而无论那真正的目的是什么，”马尔扎克插进来，“总不是托付给你的那桩差事——拦截西班牙珍宝大帆船。”
“我说的正是这个，我儿。”阿萨德附和。随即一声令下般的手势：“你肯不肯不再撒谎，痛痛快快告诉我，你的目的是什么？”他问。
“怎么说？”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，唇边浮起极淡的一丝笑。“不是您说了么——我干的事已经把它招了？这么着，倒该是我向您讨个分晓了。我向您保证，大人，怠慢托付给我的差事，绝不在我的本意里。可正因为我怕——怕她一在场，便叫我的仇人往您如今正想着的那处想，说不定还劝得您把我为伊斯兰赢下的一切光彩都忘了——我才打定主意，悄悄带她上船。
“我真正的打算，既然你们非要知道，是把她卸在法兰西海岸上哪个地方，让她从那里回她的国土、她的族人。这件事一了，我便去拦截那条西班牙大帆船；不必担心，凭安拉的眷顾，我必成功。”
“凭沙伊坦的角起誓，”马尔扎克咒骂着，抢上前来，“他真是谎言之爹谎言之娘。你才娶的老婆，就急巴巴地要打发走——这个你作何解释？”他质问。
“正是，”阿萨德咆哮，“你答得上么？”
“你们听着真相，”萨克埃尔-巴尔说。
“赞美安拉！”马尔扎克讥讽道。
“可我警告你们，”海寇继续道，“这真相，在你们听来，比我随口编的任何谎话都难信得多。多年以前，在我出生的英格兰，我爱这个女人，本要娶她。可有人有事在她面前毁谤我，叫她不肯嫁我，我怀着一腔对她的恨出了门。昨夜，那份我原以为死了、变成了恨的爱，证明还是一股活的力量。既爱她，我便看清我从前亏待了她；我心里生出一个压倒一切的念头：把我造下的孽，弥补回来。”},

===Original===

On that he paused, and after an instant’s silence Asad laughed angrily and contemptuously. “Since when has man expressed his love for a woman by putting her from him?” he asked in a voice of scorn that showed the precise value he set upon such a statement.
“I warned thee it would seem incredible,” said Sakr-el-Bahr.
“Is it not plain, O my father, that this marriage of his was no more than a pretence?” cried Marzak.
“As plain as the light of day,” replied Asad. “Thy marriage with that woman made an impious mock of the True Faith. It was no marriage. It was a blasphemous pretence, thine only aim to thwart me, abusing my regard for the Prophet’s Holy Law, and to set her beyond my reach.” He turned to Vigitello, who stood a little behind Sakr-el-Bahr. “Bid thy men put me this traitor into irons,” he said.
“Heaven hath guided thee to a wise decision, O my father!” cried Marzak, his voice jubilant. But his was the only jubilant note that was sounded, his the only voice that was raised.
“The decision is more like to guide you both to Heaven,” replied Sakr-el-Bahr, undaunted. On the instant he had resolved upon his course. “Stay!” he said, raising his hand to Vigitello, who, indeed had shown no sign of stirring. He stepped close up to Asad, and what he said did not go beyond those who stood immediately about the Basha and Rosamund, who strained her ears that she might lose no word of it.
“Do not think, Asad,” he said, “that I will submit me like a camel to its burden. Consider thy position well. If I but raise my voice to call my sea hawks to me, only Allah can tell how many will be left to obey thee. Darest thou put this matter to the test?” he asked, his countenance grave and solemn, but entirely fearless, as of a man in whom there is no doubt of the issue as it concerns himself.
Asad’s eyes glittered dully, his colour faded to a deathly ashen hue. “Thou infamous traitor.﻿ ﻿…” he began in a thick voice, his body quivering with anger.
“Ah no,” Sakr-el-Bahr interrupted him. “Were I a traitor it is what I should have done already, knowing as I do that in any division of our forces, numbers will be heavily on my side. Let then my silence prove my unswerving loyalty, Asad. Let it weigh with thee in considering my conduct, nor permit thyself to be swayed by Marzak there, who recks nothing so that he vents his petty hatred of me.”
“Do not heed him, O my father!”

===Chinese===

说到这里他顿住；片刻的沉默后，阿萨德又怒又蔑地笑了。“从几时起，男人表达对一个女人的爱，是靠把她从身边推开？”他用一种鄙夷的调子问，表明他对这套说辞的估价。
“我警告过你们，这听着不像话。”萨克埃尔-巴尔说。
“这还不明白么，我的父亲，他这桩婚事不过是装样子？”马尔扎克喊。
“明白得同白昼一样。”阿萨德答。“你同那女人的婚姻，是对正信的一场亵渎的嘲弄。那不是婚姻。那是渎神的假戏，你唯一的目标是阻挠我，滥用我敬重先知圣法之心，把她放到我够不着的地方。”他转向维吉泰洛——他立在萨克埃尔-巴尔身后不远。“叫你的人给这奸贼上镣。”他说。
“上天使您下了明断，我的父亲！”马尔扎克欢呼道。可欢的只有他一个声音，举起来的也只有他一嗓。
“这明断倒更像要把你们两个都送上天。”萨克埃尔-巴尔答，毫无惧色。转瞬之间他已拿定了主意。“慢着！”他说，朝维吉泰洛抬手——其实维吉泰洛也并没有动的意思。他逼近阿萨德，说的话没有越出紧围在帕夏同罗莎蒙德身边的人——罗莎蒙德侧着耳朵，一个字也不肯漏。
“别以为，阿萨德，我会像骆驼服负那样服你。把你的处境掂量清楚。我只要扬声唤我的海鹰，剩下多少肯听你号令的，只有安拉知道。你敢不敢拿这件事试一试？”他问，神色庄重肃穆，却全然无惧，像一个对自己这一面的结局毫不怀疑的人。
阿萨德的眼睛发着暗光，脸色褪成死灰。“你这无耻的奸贼。……”他哑着嗓子开了头，气得身子发抖。
“啊，不，”萨克埃尔-巴尔打断他。“我若是奸贼，这事我早干了；我心里清楚，咱们的力量一分为二，人数将大大压在我这一边。那么，就让我的沉默证明我不渝的忠心吧，阿萨德。让它在你掂量我的行事时有些分量；也别叫那边马尔扎克牵着你走，他什么都不在乎，只求出了他对我那点小小的恨。”
“别听他的，我的父亲！”

===Original===

cried Marzak. “It cannot be that.﻿ ﻿…”
“Peace!” growled Asad, somewhat stricken on a sudden.
And there was peace whilst the Basha stood moodily combing his white beard, his glittering eyes sweeping from Oliver to Rosamund and back again. He was weighing what Sakr-el-Bahr had said. He more than feared that it might be no more than true, and he realized that if he were to provoke a mutiny here he would be putting all to the test, setting all upon a throw in which the dice might well be cogged against him.
If Sakr-el-Bahr prevailed, he would prevail not merely aboard this galley, but throughout Algiers, and Asad would be cast down never to rise again. On the other hand, if he bared his scimitar and called upon the faithful to support him, it might chance that recognizing in him the exalted of Allah to whom their loyalty was due, they would rally to him. He even thought it might be probable. Yet the stake he put upon the board was too vast. The game appalled him, whom nothing yet had appalled, and it scarce needed a muttered caution from Biskaine to determine him to hold his hand.
He looked at Sakr-el-Bahr again, his glance now sullen. “I will consider thy words,” he announced in a voice that was unsteady. “I would not be unjust, nor steer my course by appearances alone. Allah forbid!”

===Chinese===

马尔扎克喊。“不可能是那样。……”
“住口！”阿萨德咆哮，突然间有点被打蒙了。
于是静了；帕夏阴沉着立在那里，捋着白须，闪闪的眼睛从奥利弗扫到罗莎蒙德，又扫回来。他在掂量萨克埃尔-巴尔的话。他深怕这话多半不假；他明白，若在这里激出一场兵变，就是把一切押上赌桌，而骰子很可能灌了铅、对他不利。
萨克埃尔-巴尔若赢，赢的就不单在这条桡舰上，而是整个阿尔及尔；阿萨德将被掀翻，永不能翻身。反过来，他若拔出弯刀、呼唤信众拥他，也说不定众人认出他才是安拉所立的、忠忱所归，便聚到他旗下。他甚至觉得这多半能成。可他押上桌的注太大了。这一局叫他不寒而栗——他这个至今没有什么叫他寒栗过的人；比斯坎低低一句提醒，几乎都用不着，便叫他决意缩手。
他又看向萨克埃尔-巴尔，眼神如今阴沉了。“你的话，我会考虑。”他宣布，嗓音不稳。“我不愿不公，也不愿单凭表象掌舵。安拉不容！”

## III The Forge / 铁匠铺
===Original===

Sir Oliver’s wisdom in being the first to bear Rosamund the story of that day’s happenings was established anon when Master Godolphin returned home. He went straight in quest of his sister; and in a frame of mind oppressed by fear and sorrow, for Sir John, by his general sense of discomfiture at the hands of Sir Oliver and by the anger begotten of all this he was harsh in manner and disposed to hector.
“Madam,” he announced abruptly, “Sir John is like to die.”
The astounding answer she returned him﻿—that is, astounding to him﻿—did not tend to soothe his sorely ruffled spirit.
“I know,” she said. “And I believe him to deserve no less. Who deals in calumny should be prepared for the wages of it.”
He stared at her in a long, furious silence, then exploded into oaths, and finally inveighed against her unnaturalness and pronounced her bewitched by that foul dog Tressilian.
“It is fortunate for me,” she answered him composedly, “that he was here before you to give me the truth of this affair.” Then her assumed calm and the anger with which she had met his own all fell away from her. “Oh, Peter, Peter,” she cried in anguish, “I hope that Sir John will recover. I am distraught by this event. But be just, I implore you. Sir Oliver has told me how hard-driven he had been.”
“He shall be driven harder yet, as God’s my life! If you think this deed shall go unpunished.﻿ ﻿…”
She flung herself upon his breast and implored him to carry this quarrel no further. She spoke of her love for Sir Oliver and announced her firm resolve to marry him in despite of all opposition that could be made, all of which did not tend to soften her brother’s humour. Yet because of the love that ever had held these two in closest bonds he went so far in the end as to say that should Sir John recover he would not himself pursue the matter further. But if Sir John should die﻿—as was very likely﻿—honour compelled him to seek vengeance of a deed to which he had himself so very largely contributed.
“I read that man as if he were an open book,” the boy announced, with callow boastfulness. “He has the subtlety of Satan, yet he does not delude me. It was at me he struck through Killigrew. Because he desires you, Rosamund, he could not﻿—as he bluntly told me﻿—deal with me however I provoked him, not even though I went the length of striking him.

===Chinese===

奥利弗抢在头里把当日的事讲给罗莎蒙德，这份先见之明，不多时便得到了证实——戈多尔芬少爷回来了。他径直去寻妹妹；心里压着恐惧与忧伤，又被奥利弗爵士挫败的憋屈和由此生出的怒火煎熬着，言辞便粗鲁起来，摆出寻衅的架势。
“小姐，”他劈头便说，“约翰爵士怕是活不成了。”
她给出的回答令他愕然——至少在他看来是愕然——却丝毫没能抚平他那被搅得毛糙的火气。
“我知道，”她说，“而且我认为他受这个罪是罪有应得。谁惯于诽谤人，就该备好领那诽谤的工钱。”
他瞪着她，怒极而久久无言，继而破口咒骂起来，末了痛斥她心肠悖逆冷酷，断言她是叫特雷西利安那条恶狗迷了心窍。
“算我走运，”她从容地答道，“他赶在你前头来，把这件事的真相告诉了我。”话音一落，她那强装的镇定和方才回敬他的怒气一齐卸了下来。“哦，彼得，彼得，”她痛苦地喊道，“我盼着约翰爵士能好起来。这件事把我折磨得六神无主。可你要凭良心，我求你了。奥利弗爵士已经告诉过我，他是被逼到了什么地步。”
“我以性命起誓，还要把他逼得更狠！要是你以为这件事能就此罢休……”
她扑进他怀里，恳求他别把这场仇怨再闹下去。她说到自己对奥利弗的爱，宣布自己主意已定，无论如何阻拦都要嫁给他——这些话可软化不了她哥哥的心肠。只是念着那份向来将兄妹二人紧紧维系的骨肉之情，他最后总算松了口：倘若约翰爵士活下来，他自己便不再追究此事。可要是约翰爵士死了——这多半难免——荣誉便逼着他去讨这场血债，尽管这场祸事他自己也大大地有一份。
“我读那个人，就像读一本摊开的书，”少年夸夸其谈，带着毛头小子的自负，“他有撒旦的狡诈，却蒙不了我。他借基利格鲁对我下手，冲的正是我。因为他想要你，罗莎蒙德——他跟我明说了——所以无论我怎么招惹他，他都不能把我怎么样，哪怕我一巴掌打上去也一样。

===Original===

He might have killed me for’t; but he knew that to do so would place a barrier ’twixt him and you. Oh! he is calculating as all the fiends of Hell. So, to wipe out the dishonour which I did him, he shifts the blame of it upon Killigrew and goes out to kill him, which he further thinks may act as a warning to me. But if Killigrew dies.﻿ ﻿…” And thus he rambled on, filling her gentle heart with anguish to see this feud increasing between the two men she loved best in all the world. If the outcome of it should be that either were to kill the other, she knew that she could never again look upon the survivor.
She took heart at last in the memory of Sir Oliver’s sworn promise that her brother’s life should be inviolate to him, betide what might. She trusted him; she depended upon his word and that rare strength of his which rendered possible to him a course that no weaker man would dare pursue. And in this reflection her pride in him increased, and she thanked God for a lover who in all things was a giant among men.
But Sir John Killigrew did not die. He hovered between this world and a better one for some seven days, at the end of which he began to recover. By October he was abroad again, gaunt and pale, reduced to half the bulk that had been his before, a mere shadow of a man.
One of his first visits was to Godolphin Court. He went to remonstrate with Rosamund upon her betrothal, and he did so at the request of her brother. But his remonstrances were strangely lacking in the force that she had looked for.
The odd fact is that in his near approach to death, and with his earthly interest dwindling, Sir John had looked matters frankly in the face, and had been driven to the conclusion﻿—a conclusion impossible to him in normal health﻿—that he had got no more than he deserved. He realized that he had acted unworthily, if unconscious at the time of the unworthiness of what he did; that the weapons with which he had fought Sir Oliver were not the weapons that become a Gentleman or in which there is credit to be won. He perceived that he had permitted his old enmity for the house of Tressilian, swollen by a sense of injury lately suffered in the matter of the licence to build at Smithick, to warp his judgment and to persuade him that Sir Oliver was all he had dubbed him. He realized that jealousy, too, had taken a hand in the matter.

===Chinese===

他满可以为此要了我的命；可他知道，那样一做，你和我之间便隔了一道墙。啊！他算计起来，赛过地狱里全体魔鬼。于是，为了洗刷我加给他的羞辱，他把罪责推到基利格鲁头上，出去杀他，还想着这正好给我个警告。可要是基利格鲁死了……”
他就这样絮絮叨叨说个不了，眼看自己在这世上最爱的两个男人之间的仇怨越结越深，她那颗温柔的心给填满了苦楚。倘若到头来是这一个杀了那一个，她知道，活下来的那一个，她是再也不能相见了。
后来她记起奥利弗爵士立过的誓——无论出什么事，她哥哥的性命在他那里必然无恙——这才放了心。她信得过他；她倚靠他的诺言，也倚靠他那一股罕有的刚毅，正是这股刚毅，使他敢走一条懦夫不敢走的路。想到这里，她对他的自豪愈发深了，她感谢上天赐给她一个情人，样样都称得上人中之伟丈夫。
可约翰·基利格鲁爵士并没有死。他在今世与更好的一世之间悬了约莫七天，过后便见好转。到十月里，他又出门走动了，形容枯瘦，面色苍白，身量比从前减去一半，只剩得一条人影。
他最先去的几处里，有一处是戈多尔芬府。他是受她哥哥之托，去就她那桩婚事规劝她的。可他这一场规劝，偏又缺了她意料中的那股气力。
说来也怪：在濒临死亡的当口，眼见尘世的兴味日渐淡薄，约翰爵士索性把事情摊开了正视，竟得出一个结论——一个他平日康健时万不能得的结论：他是咎由自取。他明白自己行事不体面，纵然当时不曾察觉那不体面；他用来对付奥利弗爵士的那些兵器，原不是绅士该用的兵器，用它们也赢不来体面。他看出，自己对特雷西利安家的宿怨——又添上近来在史密尼克建屋执照一事上吃的亏——已经歪了他的判断，诱他相信奥利弗爵士真就如他咒骂的那般。他还省悟到，妒忌也在里头插了一手。

===Original===

Sir Oliver’s exploits upon the seas had brought him wealth, and with this wealth he was building up once more the Tressilian sway in those parts, which Ralph Tressilian had so outrageously diminished, so that he threatened to eclipse the importance of the Killigrews of Arwenack.
Nevertheless, in the hour of reaction he did not go so far as to admit that Sir Oliver Tressilian was a fit mate for Rosamund Godolphin. She and her brother had been placed in his care by their late father, and he had nobly discharged his tutelage until such time as Peter had come to full age. His affection for Rosamund was tender as that of a lover, but tempered by a feeling entirely paternal. He went very near to worshipping her, and when all was said, when he had cleared his mind of all dishonest bias, he still found overmuch to dislike in Oliver Tressilian, and the notion of his becoming Rosamund’s husband was repellent.
First of all there was that bad Tressilian blood﻿—notoriously bad, and never more flagrantly displayed than in the case of the late Ralph Tressilian. It was impossible that Oliver should have escaped the taint of it; nor could Sir John perceive any signs that he had done so. He displayed the traditional Tressilian turbulence. He was passionate and brutal, and the pirate’s trade to which he had now set his hand was of all trades the one for which he was by nature best equipped. He was harsh and overbearing, impatient of correction and prone to trample other men’s feelings underfoot. Was this, he asked himself in all honesty, a mate for Rosamund? Could he entrust her happiness to the care of such a man? Assuredly he could not.
Therefore, being whole again, he went to remonstrate with her as he accounted it his duty and as Master Peter had besought him. Yet knowing the bias that had been his he was careful to understate rather than to overstate his reasons.
“But, Sir John,” she protested, “if every man is to be condemned for the sins of his forbears, but few could escape condemnation, and wherever shall you find me a husband deserving your approval?”
“His father.﻿ ﻿…” began Sir John.
“Tell me not of his father, but of himself,” she interrupted.
He frowned impatiently﻿—they were sitting in that bower of hers above the river.

===Chinese===

奥利弗爵士海上的功业给他挣下了家私，他正拿这份家私重新树起特雷西利安家在那一带的威势——那威势原是拉尔夫·特雷西利安败坏得不成样子的——长此下去，阿尔韦纳克基利格鲁家的体面，怕要叫他压过去了。
然而，在这反躬悔悟的时辰，他仍不肯走到那一步，承认奥利弗·特雷西利安配做罗莎蒙德·戈多尔芬的丈夫。她和她哥哥，是他们的先父托付给他照管的，他也着实高地履行了这份监护之责，直到彼得成年。他对罗莎蒙德的爱，温柔得像一个情人，却又掺着一份全然是父亲的心肠。他敬她敬得几乎近于膜拜，而说到底，待他把心里一切不诚实的偏私都清了出去，他在奥利弗·特雷西利安身上仍旧寻出太多可厌之处，一想到他要做起罗莎蒙德的丈夫来，便觉得憎恶。
头一件，便是特雷西利安家那坏血——坏得名声在外，又没有比已故的拉尔夫·特雷西利安显露得更彰明较著的。奥利弗不可能逃过这血里的秽气；约翰爵士也看不出他有半点逃过的迹象。他显的是特雷西利安家祖传的暴烈。他性子火暴、粗悍，如今又操起海盗的营生——天下营生里，天造地设再没有比这更配他的了。他严酷专横，受不得规劝，惯把旁人的情面踩在脚底下。他扪心自问：这样的人，配做罗莎蒙德的丈夫么？能把她的幸福托给这样一个人么？断然是不能的。
于是，等身体复了元，他便去规劝她，一则自认是本分，二则也是彼得少爷相恳。只是他知道自己素来怀着成见，说话便宁可把理由说轻些，不肯说重。
“可是，约翰爵士，”她分辩道，“倘使人人都要因祖宗的罪过受责，能逃过的就没有几个了，您又往哪里去给我寻一个当得起您嘉许的丈夫呢？”
“他的父亲……”约翰爵士开了腔。
“别同我讲他的父亲，讲他自己。”她打断道。
他不耐烦地皱起眉——两人正坐在她那间临河的闺阁里。

===Original===

“I was coming to ’t,” he answered, a thought testily, for these interruptions which made him keep to the point robbed him of his best arguments. “However, suffice it that many of his father’s vicious qualities he has inherited, as we see in his ways of life; that he has not inherited others only the future can assure us.”
“In other words,” she mocked him, yet very seriously, “I am to wait until he dies of old age to make quite sure that he has no such sins as must render him an unfitting husband?”
“No, no,” he cried. “Good lack! what a perverseness is thine!”
“The perverseness is your own, Sir John. I am but the mirror of it.”
He shifted in his chair and grunted. “Be it so, then,” he snapped. “We will deal with the qualities that already he displays.” And Sir John enumerated them.
“But this is no more than your judgment of him﻿—no more than what you think him.”
“ ’Tis what all the world thinks him.”
“But I shall not marry a man for what others think of him, but for what I think of him myself. And in my view you cruelly malign him. I discover no such qualities in Sir Oliver.”
“ ’Tis that you should be spared such a discovery that I am beseeching you not to wed him.”
“Yet unless I wed him I shall never make such a discovery; and until I make it I shall ever continue to love him and to desire to wed him. Is all my life to be spent so?” She laughed outright, and came to stand beside him. She put an arm about his neck as she might have put it about the neck of her father, as she had been in the habit of doing any day in these past ten years﻿—and thereby made him feel himself to have reached an unconscionable age. With her hand she rubbed his brow.
“Why, here are wicked wrinkles of ill-humour,” she cried to him. “You are all undone, and by a woman’s wit, and you do not like it.”
“I am undone by a woman’s wilfulness, by a woman’s headstrong resolve not to see.”
“You have naught to show me, Sir John.”
“Naught? Is all that I have said naught?”
“Words are not things; judgments are not facts. You say that he is so, and so and so. But when I ask you upon what facts you judge him, your only answer is that you think him to be what you say he is. Your thoughts may be honest, Sir John, but your logic is contemptible.” And she laughed again at his gaping discomfiture.

===Chinese===

“我正要说到那个，”他答道，微带着愠意，因为这些岔子逼得他离不开正题，倒把他最好的论据都夺了去。“不过，长话短说：他父亲许多恶劣的品性，他已经承袭了，这从他的行止上看得出来；至于另一些他没有承袭，这一点，只有将来能给我们担保。”
“换句话说，”她嘲弄他道，神气却极其认真，“我得等到他老死了，才好拿准他没有那些非叫他做不成丈夫的罪过？”
“不，不，”他嚷道，“天哪！你这人拗得出奇！”
“拗的是您自己，约翰爵士。我不过是照出它的一面镜子。”
他在椅子里挪了挪身子，哼了一声。“也罢，”他没好气地说，“我们就来会一会他已经摆出来的那些品性。”于是约翰爵士把它们一一数落出来。
“可这无非是您对他的评断——无非是您心里想他是这样。”
“普天下人心里想他都是这样。”
“可我嫁人，不看旁人心里怎么想他，看的是我自己心里怎么想他。依我看，您是狠命地冤枉他。我在奥利弗爵士身上寻不出这些品性来。”
“正是要叫你免于寻出它们来，我才求你别嫁他。”
“可我不嫁他，便永远寻不出来；我寻不出来，便永远要爱他、要嫁他。难道我这辈子就这么过？”她放声笑了，走过来立在他身旁。她伸出一条胳膊搂住他的颈子，就像她可能搂住父亲的颈子那样——这十年来的任何一天，她都惯这样做——这一搂，倒叫他觉出自己上了不堪回首的高龄。她用手掌揉着他的额头。
“嗳，这儿全是坏脾气的恶皱纹，”她朝他喊道，“您全给缴了械啦，缴在一个女人的巧辩手里，您还不乐意呢。”
“我是缴了械，缴在一个女人的任性手里，缴在她死活不肯睁眼的倔强手里。”
“您拿不出东西给我看，约翰爵士。”
“拿不出？我方才说的这许多，难道不算东西？”
“言语不是事实，评断不是凭据。您说他这样、那样。可我问您凭什么事实下这断语，您唯一的回答是：您心里想他是您说的那样。您的心思或许是诚实的，约翰爵士，可您的逻辑高明得可怜。”她见他张口结舌、狼狈不堪，又笑了。

===Original===

“Come, now, deal like an honest upright judge, and tell me one act of his﻿—one thing that he has ever done and of which you have sure knowledge﻿—that will bear him out to be what you say he is. Now, Sir John!”
He looked up at her impatiently. Then, at last he smiled.
“Rogue!” he cried﻿—and upon a distant day he was to bethink him of those words. “If ever he be brought to judgment I can desire him no better advocate than thou.”
Thereupon following up her advantage swiftly, she kissed him. “Nor could I desire him a more honest judge than you.”
What was the poor man to do thereafter? What he did. Live up to her pronouncement, and go forthwith to visit Sir Oliver and compose their quarrel.
The acknowledgment of his fault was handsomely made, and Sir Oliver received it in a spirit no less handsome. But when Sir John came to the matter of Mistress Rosamund he was, out of his sense of duty to her, less generous. He announced that since he could not bring himself to look upon Sir Oliver as a suitable husband for her, nothing that he had now said must mislead Sir Oliver into supposing him a consenting party to any such union.
“But that,” he added, “is not to say that I oppose it. I disapprove, but I stand aside. Until she is of full age her brother will refuse his sanction. After that, the matter will concern neither him nor myself.”
“I hope,” said Sir Oliver, “he will take as wise a view. But whatever view he takes will be no matter. For the rest, Sir John, I thank you for your frankness, and I rejoice to know that if I may not count you for my friend, at least I need not reckon you among my enemies.”
But if Sir John was thus won round to a neutral attitude, Master Peter’s rancour abated nothing; rather it increased each day, and presently there came another matter to feed it, a matter of which Sir Oliver had no suspicion.
He knew that his brother Lionel rode almost daily to Malpas, and he knew the object of those daily rides. He knew of the lady who kept a sort of court there for the rustic bucks of Truro, Penryn, and Helston, and he knew something of the ill-repute that had attached to her in town﻿—a repute, in fact, which had been the cause of her withdrawal into the country. He told his brother some frank and ugly truths, concerning her, by way of warning him, and therein, for the first time, the twain went very near to quarrelling.

===Chinese===

“来，现在拿出一位诚实正直的法官的样子来，给我举出他的一件事——一件他确实做过、您又有真知灼见的——好证实他当真是您说的那种人。来吧，约翰爵士！”
他不耐烦地抬眼望她。末了，他笑了。
“小滑头！”他喊道——日后一个遥远的日子，他还会想起这几句话来。“他日他若真个落到受审，我但愿不出比这更好的辩护人给他。”
她趁热打铁，紧追一吻。“我也不指望给他寻个比您更诚实的法官了。”
此后这可怜的人还能怎样呢？照他做的那样做：言行相顾，即刻动身去拜访奥利弗爵士，把两人的嫌隙化解了。
认错认得漂亮，奥利弗爵士受之也抱着同样漂亮的心怀。可一谈到罗莎蒙德小姐，约翰爵士出于对她的一份责任，便不那么慷慨了。他声明：他既终究不能把奥利弗爵士看作配得上她的丈夫，那么他方才这番话，万不可叫他误会成他是这桩姻缘的赞成者。
“不过，”他补了一句，“这也并不是说我反对。我不赞成，可我袖手。她成年之前，她哥哥不会点头。成年之后，这事便与他无关，也与我无关了。”
“我希望，”奥利弗爵士说，“他也能看得这样开通。可无论他怎么看，都无关紧要。此外，约翰爵士，我谢谢您的坦白，并且深感快慰：我虽不能把您算作我的朋友，至少也不必将您数进我的仇敌里去。”
约翰爵士虽这样被争取成了中立，彼得少爷的怨毒却分毫不曾消减，反倒与日俱增；不久又来了一桩事，给它添了柴薪——这桩事，奥利弗爵士毫无觉察。
他知道弟弟莱昂内尔几乎天天骑马去马尔帕斯，也知道那些日日骑马所为何来。他知道那位太太，在马尔帕斯俨然设着一座小朝廷，招接特鲁罗、彭林、赫尔斯顿一带的乡下浪子，也知道些她在城里背上的坏名声——事实上，正是那名声逼得她退居乡野。他拣了些关于她的又丑又实的真话，直统统说给弟弟听，算是警告；就为这个，兄弟俩头一回闹得险些反目。

===Original===

After that he mentioned her no more. He knew that in his indolent way Lionel could be headstrong, and he knew human nature well enough to be convinced that interference here would but set up a breach between himself and his brother without in the least achieving its real object. So Oliver shrugged resignedly, and held his peace.
There he left the affair, nor ever spoke again of Malpas and the siren who presided there. And meanwhile the autumn faded into winter, and with the coming of stormy weather Sir Oliver and Rosamund had fewer opportunities of meeting. To Godolphin Court he would not go since she did not desire it; and himself he deemed it best to remain away since otherwise he must risk a quarrel with its master, who had forbidden him the place. In those days he saw Peter Godolphin but little, and on the rare occasions when they did meet they passed each other with a very meagre salute.
Sir Oliver was entirely happy, and men noticed how gentler were his accents, how sunnier had become a countenance that they had known for haughty and forbidding. He waited for his coming happiness with the confidence of an immortal in the future. Patience was all the service Fate asked of him, and he gave that service blithely, depending upon the reward that soon now would be his own. Indeed, the year drew near its close; and ere another winter should come round Penarrow House would own a mistress. That to him seemed as inevitable as the season itself. And yet for all his supreme confidence, for all his patience and the happiness he culled from it, there were moments when he seemed oppressed by some elusive sense of overhanging doom, by some subconsciousness of an evil in the womb of Destiny. Did he challenge his oppression, did he seek to translate it into terms of reason, he found nothing upon which his wits could fasten﻿—and he came ever to conclude that it was his very happiness by its excessiveness that was oppressing him, giving him at times that sense of premonitory weight about the heart as if to check its joyous soarings.
One day, a week from Christmas, he had occasion to ride to Helston on some trifling affair. For half a week a blizzard had whirled about the coast, and he had been kept chafing indoors what time layer upon layer of snow was spread upon the countryside.

===Chinese===

此后他再不提她。他知道，莱昂内尔虽一副懒散性子，倔起来却也执拗得很；他又深谙人情，明白这时候去干涉，除了在自己和弟弟之间生出一道裂痕，那真正的目的分毫达不到。于是奥利弗听天由命地耸耸肩，缄口不言。
这事他便撂下了，从此再不提马尔帕斯，不提那位坐镇在那里的海妖。不知不觉，秋色淡成了冬令；风雨的天气一来，奥利弗爵士同罗莎蒙德见面的机会便少了。戈多尔芬府他不去，因为她不愿意；他自己也认定还是不去的好，不然便免不了同府里的主人起一场冲突——那里的门早已对他下了禁令。那些日子里，他难得见着彼得·戈多尔芬，偶尔当真遇上，彼此也不过淡淡地颔一颔首。
奥利弗爵士快活得很。人们都留意到，他的谈吐变得多么和婉，那张素来以倨傲冷峻闻名的脸，变得多么晴朗。他怀着不朽者对将来的那份信心，等候他将至的幸福。命运向他征发的差役只有忍耐一项，这项差他也当得欢欢喜喜，指望的是那笔不久便归他名下的犒赏。的确，这一年行将收梢；再来一个冬天之前，彭纳罗府就该有一位女主人了。这在他看来，就像节令本身一样躲不开。然而，任凭他信心何等十足，任凭他怎样忍耐、又从忍耐里采得多少快活，也有些时刻，他仿佛被一种捉摸不定的大祸压顶之感、被一种对命运胎中正在孕育的灾殃的隐隐知觉压迫着。他若向这压迫挑战，若想把它翻译成道理，却寻不出一点下嘴的地方——于是他每每归结到：正是他这份幸福过了量，才压得他受不住，时时叫他心口坠着一股先兆似的分量，仿佛要煞一煞它欢腾的高飞。
圣诞节前一个星期，他为一桩琐事要骑马去赫尔斯顿。接连半周，一场暴风雪在海岸上打旋，他憋在屋里焦躁难耐，眼看一层压一层的积雪铺满了四野。

===Original===

On the fourth day, the storm being spent, the sun came forth, the skies were swept clear of clouds and all the countryside lay robed in a sun-drenched, dazzling whiteness. Sir Oliver called for his horse and rode forth alone through the crisp snow. He turned homeward very early in the afternoon, but when a couple of miles from Helston he found that his horse had cast a shoe. He dismounted, and bridle over arm tramped on through the sunlit vale between the heights of Pendennis and Arwenack, singing as he went. He came thus to Smithick and the door of the forge. About it stood a group of fishermen and rustics, for, in the absence of any inn just there, this forge was ever a point of congregation. In addition to the rustics and an itinerant merchant with his packhorses, there were present Sir Andrew Flack, the parson from Penryn, and Master Gregory Baine, one of the Justices from the neighbourhood of Truro. Both were well known to Sir Oliver, and he stood in friendly gossip with them what time he waited for his horse.
It was all very unfortunate, from the casting of that shoe to the meeting with those gentlemen; for as Sir Oliver stood there, down the gentle slope from Arwenack rode Master Peter Godolphin.
It was said afterwards by Sir Andrew and Master Baine that Master Peter appeared to have been carousing, so flushed was his face, so unnatural the brightness of his eye, so thick his speech and so extravagant and foolish what he said. There can be little doubt that it was so. He was addicted to Canary, and so indeed was Sir John Killigrew, and he had been dining with Sir John. He was of those who turn quarrelsome in wine﻿—which is but another way of saying that when the wine was in and the restraint out, his natural humour came uppermost untrammelled. The sight of Sir Oliver standing there gave the lad precisely what he needed to indulge that evil humour of his, and he may have been quickened in his purpose by the presence of those other gentlemen. In his half-fuddled state of mind he may have recalled that once he had struck Sir Oliver and Sir Oliver had laughed and told him that none would believe it.
He drew rein suddenly as he came abreast of the group, so suddenly that he pulled his horse until it almost sat down like a cat; yet he retained his saddle.

===Chinese===

到第四天，风雪用尽，太阳出来了，长空一碧，四野披着一身沐日的、晃眼的白。奥利弗爵士唤人牵马，独自踏着脆响的积雪出了门。午后很早他便折向归途，可离赫尔斯顿还有两英里，他发觉马掌脱落了一只。他下了马，缰绳搭在臂弯，徒步走在彭登尼斯与阿尔韦纳克两处高地间那道洒满阳光的谷里，一路走一路唱。就这样他走到史密尼克，走到那铁匠铺门口。铺子跟前聚着一群渔夫和乡人——那一带眼前没有客栈，这铁匠铺素来便是聚脚的所在。除了乡人和一个带着驮马的行脚商，在场的还有彭林的牧师安德鲁·弗拉克爵士，和特鲁罗左近的一位治安官格雷戈里·贝恩老爷。两位都同奥利弗爵士相熟，他便趁等马的当儿，站在那里同他们闲话家常。
从那枚脱落的马掌起，到同这几位先生的相遇，一切都倒霉透顶；因为奥利弗爵士正立在那儿，彼得·戈多尔芬少爷骑着马，从阿尔韦纳克那面的缓坡上下来了。
事后安德鲁爵士和贝恩老爷都说，彼得少爷那模样像是方才纵过酒：脸红得那样厉害，眼里的光那样不自然，口齿那样含混，说出的话又那样放肆、那样蠢。这几乎无可怀疑。他嗜好加那利酒——约翰·基利格鲁爵士其实也一样——而他方才正是在约翰爵士府上用的饭。他是那种酒一下肚便要寻衅的人——换句话说，酒一入腹、拘束一撤，他天生的那副劣性便毫无遮拦地浮上头。一望见奥利弗爵士立在那里，这少年恰好得了由头，好发泄他那一腔邪火，而在场那几位绅士，说不定更催快了他的念头。在他那半醉半昏的脑子里，他也许记起了从前他打过奥利弗爵士，而奥利弗爵士一笑了之，告诉他这话谁也不会相信。
行到那群人的侧旁，他猛地收缰，收得那样急，那匹马险些像猫一样坐倒下去；他到底还是稳住了鞍子。

===Original===

Then he came through the snow that was all squelched and mudded just about the forge, and leered at Sir Oliver.
“I am from Arwenack,” he announced unnecessarily. “We have been talking of you.”
“You could have had no better subject of discourse,” said Sir Oliver, smiling, for all that his eyes were hard and something scared﻿—though his fears did not concern himself.
“Marry, you are right; you make an engrossing topic﻿—you and your debauched father.”
“Sir,” replied Sir Oliver, “once already have I deplored your mother’s utter want of discretion.”
The words were out of him in a flash under the spur of the gross insult flung at him, uttered in the momentary blind rage aroused by that inflamed and taunting face above him. No sooner were they sped than he repented them, the more bitterly because they were greeted by a guffaw from the rustics. He would have given half his fortune in that moment to have recalled them.
Master Godolphin’s face had changed as utterly as if he had removed a mask. From flushed that it had been it was livid now and the eyes were blazing, the mouth twitching. Thus a moment he glowered upon his enemy. Then standing in his stirrups he swung aloft his whip.
“You dog!” he cried, in a snarling sob. “You dog!” And his lash came down and cut a long red wheal across Sir Oliver’s dark face.
With cries of dismay and anger the others, the parson, the Justice and the rustics got between the pair, for Sir Oliver was looking very wicked, and all the world knew him for a man to be feared.
“Master Godolphin, I cry shame upon you,” exclaimed the parson. “If evil comes of this I shall testify to the grossness of your aggression. Get you gone from here!”
“Go to the devil, sir,” said Master Godolphin thickly. “Is my mother’s name to be upon the lips of that bastard? By God, man, the matter rests not here. He shall send his friends to me, or I will horsewhip him every time we meet. You hear, Sir Oliver?”
Sir Oliver made him no reply.
“You hear?” he roared. “There is no Sir John Killigrew this time upon whom you can shift the quarrel. Come you to me and get the punishment of which that whiplash is but an earnest.” Then with a thick laugh he drove spurs into his horse’s flanks, so furiously that he all but sent the parson and another sprawling.
“Stay but a little while for me,” roared Sir Oliver after him. “You’ll ride no more, my drunken fool!”

===Chinese===

随后他踏过铁匠铺跟前那片踩得稀烂和泥的雪，斜着眼瞅奥利弗爵士。
“我打阿尔韦纳克来，”他没头没脑地宣布，“我们方才正说着你呢。”
“那你们再也挑不出比这更好的话题了。”奥利弗爵士说，含着笑，只是他的眼神又冷又带着几分惶乱——虽然他惧的并不是自己。
“可不是，你说着了；你就是个叫人入迷的话题——你，还有你那位荒唐的老子。”
“少爷，”奥利弗爵士答道，“令堂有失检点，这已是我第二回替她扼腕了。”
那句话是脱口而出的，是被泼到他脸上的那记粗鄙的羞辱刺出来的，是那张居高临下望着他的、烧红的、挑衅的脸霎时激起的一阵盲目的狂怒。话一出口他便悔了，悔得分外苦，因为乡人们报之以一阵哄笑。那一刻，他情愿拿出一半家财来收回这句话。
戈多尔芬少爷的脸变了，变得像摘掉了一副面具。方才还烧得通红，此刻已经发青，两眼冒火，嘴角抽搐。他就这样阴森森地对他的仇敌怒视了片刻。随后他踏着马镫立起来，把马鞭高高抡起。
“你这狗东西！”他嚎道，声音里带着哭腔。“你这狗东西！”鞭梢劈落，在奥利弗爵士黝黑的脸上抽出一条长长的血痕。
其余的人——牧师、治安官和乡人们——惊呼怒喝，一齐挤到两人中间，因为奥利弗爵士那副神色凶得吓人，而普天下都知道他是个惹不起的人。
“戈多尔芬少爷，你这事干得可耻！”牧师喝道，“倘若这事生出祸来，我必出来证明你挑衅得如何粗鄙。你快走开！”
“去见鬼吧，先生，”戈多尔芬少爷含混地说，“我母亲的名字，岂容那条野种挂在嘴上？老天在上，这事不算完。他要么打发他的朋友来见我，不然我逢着一回便抽他一回。你听见没有，奥利弗爵士？”
奥利弗爵士不答理他。
“你听见没有？”他吼道。“这一回可没有约翰·基利格鲁爵士好让你把争端推过去了。你自己到我跟前来，领你那份刑罚吧，方才那一鞭不过是定钱。”说罢他哑着嗓子笑了一声，狠狠把马刺磕进马腹，险些把牧师和另外一个人撞了个趔趄。
“给我稍待片刻！”奥利弗爵士在他背后吼道。“你骑不成马了，我的醉鬼傻瓜！”

===Original===

And in a rage he bellowed for his horse, flinging off the parson and Master Baine, who endeavoured to detain and calm him. He vaulted to the saddle when the nag was brought him, and whirled away in furious pursuit.
The parson looked at the Justice and the Justice shrugged, his lips tight-pressed.
“The young fool is drunk,” said Sir Andrew, shaking his white head. “He’s in no case to meet his Maker.”
“Yet he seems very eager,” quoth Master Justice Baine. “I doubt I shall hear more of the matter.” He turned and looked into the forge where the bellows now stood idle, the smith himself grimy and aproned in leather in the doorway, listening to the rustics account of the happening. Master Baine it seems had a taste for analogies. “Faith,” he said, “the place was excellently well chosen. They have forged here today a sword which it will need blood to temper.”

他怒冲冲吼人牵马，一把甩开想要拦他劝他的牧师和贝恩老爷。马一到，他翻身跨上鞍，旋风一般狂追下去。
牧师望望治安官，治安官耸耸肩，两片嘴唇抿得紧紧的。
“这傻小子醉了，”安德鲁爵士摇着他那颗白头说，“这副样子可去不得见他的造物主。”
“可他瞧着急得很呢，”治安官贝恩老爷说，“只怕这事还有下文。”他转过身，朝铁匠铺里望去：风箱此刻静着，铁匠系着皮围裙、满脸乌黑，立在门口，听乡人们讲述方才的经过。贝恩老爷这个人，看来颇有些好打比方。“嗳，”他说，“这地方挑得再好没有了。他们今天在这儿锻成了一柄剑，而这柄剑，须得用血来淬。”

## XXIII The Heathen Creed / 异教信条
===Original===

Sakr-el-Bahr was shut up in a black hole in the forecastle of the *Silver Heron* to await the dawn and to spend the time in making his soul. No words had passed between him and Sir John since his surrender. With wrists pinioned behind him, he had been hoisted aboard the English ship, and in the waist of her he had stood for a moment face to face with an old acquaintance﻿—our chronicler, Lord Henry Goade. I imagine the florid countenance of the Queen’s Lieutenant wearing a preternaturally grave expression, his eyes forbidding as they rested upon the renegade. I know﻿—from Lord Henry’s own pen﻿—that no word had passed between them during those brief moments before Sakr-el-Bahr was hurried away by his guards to be flung into those dark, cramped quarters reeking of tar and bilge.
For a long hour he lay where he had fallen, believing himself alone; and time and place would no doubt conduce to philosophical reflection upon his condition. I like to think that he found that when all was considered, he had little with which to reproach himself. If he had done evil he had made ample amends. It can scarcely be pretended that he had betrayed those loyal Muslimeen followers of his, or, if it is, at least it must be added that he himself had paid the price of that betrayal. Rosamund was safe, Lionel would meet the justice due to him, and as for himself, being as good as dead already, he was worth little thought. He must have derived some measure of content from the reflection that he was spending his life to the very best advantage. Ruined it had been long since. True, but for his ill-starred expedition of vengeance he might long have continued to wage war as a corsair, might even have risen to the proud Muslim eminence of the Bashalik of Algiers and become a feudatory prince of the Grand Turk. But for one who was born a Christian gentleman that would have been an unworthy way to have ended his days. The present was the better course.
A faint rustle in the impenetrable blackness of his prison turned the current of his thoughts. A rat, he thought, and drew himself to a sitting attitude, and beat his slippered heels upon the ground to drive away the loathly creature. Instead, a voice challenged him out of the gloom.
“Who’s there?”
It startled him for a moment, in his complete assurance that he had been alone.
“Who’s there?”

===Chinese===

萨克埃尔-巴尔被关进“银鹭号”前舱一个黑洞洞的舱室里，等着天亮，用好这段时光来预备自己的灵魂。自他投降之后，他与约翰爵士之间再没说过一句话。他双腕反缚，被吊上了那艘英国船；在船腰处，他曾与一位旧相识照过一瞬间的面——也就是我们这位编年史家，亨利·戈德勋爵。我可以想见女王副官那张红润的脸膛上罩着一副超乎寻常的凝重，目光落在那叛教者身上时，冷得拒人千里。我知道——这得自亨利勋爵的亲笔——在那短短片刻之间，两人不曾交一语，萨克埃尔-巴尔便被看守们匆匆带走，扔进了那散发着焦油与污水恶臭的、又暗又窄的舱间。
他倒在哪里就在哪里躺了许久，只道舱中唯他一人；这样的时地，免不了引人就着自己的处境作一番哲思。我宁愿相信，他把种种情形都掂量过后，发觉自己并没什么可自责的。倘若他造过孽，他也已用丰厚的代价抵赎了。很难说他背弃了那些忠心的穆斯林部众；纵然可以这么说，至少也得补上一句：那背弃的代价，他自己已经付过了。罗莎蒙德平安了，莱昂内尔将得到他应得的公道；至于他自己，反正早已半只脚踏进鬼门关，犯不着多想。想到自己这条性命正花在最划算的用处上，他想必也得到了几分慰藉。这条命早就毁了，不假。诚然，若不是那趟时乖命蹇的复仇之行，他本可以长久地继续干他的海盗营生，甚至爬上阿尔及尔帕夏那煊赫的穆斯林高位，做大土耳其君主麾下的一方藩王。可对一个生来便是基督徒绅士的人，那样的下场终究不光彩。眼前这条路，倒是更好的一条。
囚牢里那化不开的黑暗中起了一阵窸窣，打断了他的思绪。他只当是耗子，撑起身子坐了起来，趿着便鞋的脚跟在地上顿响，好把那讨厌的东西赶走。不想黑暗里却响起一声喝问。
“谁在那儿？”
他原本笃定舱中只他一人，一时竟吃了一惊。
“谁在那儿？”

===Original===

the voice repeated, querulously to add: “What black hell be this? Where am I?”
And now he recognized the voice for Jasper Leigh’s, and marvelled how that latest of his recruits to the ranks of Mohammed should be sharing this prison with him.
“Faith,” said he, “you’re in the forecastle of the *Silver Heron*; though how you come here is more than I can answer.”
“Who are ye?” the voice asked.
“I have been known in Barbary as Sakr-el-Bahr.”
“Sir Oliver!”
“I suppose that is what they will call me now. It is as well perhaps that I am to be buried at sea, else it might plague these Christian gentlemen what legend to inscribe upon my headstone. But you﻿—how come you hither? My bargain with Sir John was that none should be molested, and I cannot think Sir John would be forsworn.”
“As to that I know nothing, since I did not even know where I was bestowed until ye informed me. I was knocked senseless in the fight, after I had put my bilbo through your comely brother. That is the sum of my knowledge.”
Sir Oliver caught his breath. “What do you say? You killed Lionel?”
“I believe so,” was the cool answer. “At least I sent a couple of feet of steel through him﻿—’twas in the press of the fight when first the English dropped aboard the galley; Master Lionel was in the van﻿—the last place in which I should have looked to see him.”
There fell a long silence. At length Sir Oliver spoke in a small voice.
“Not a doubt but you gave him no more than he was seeking. You are right, Master Leigh; the van was the last place in which to look for him, unless he came deliberately to seek steel that he might escape a rope. Best so, no doubt. Best so! God rest him!”
“Do you believe in God?” asked the sinful skipper on an anxious note.
“No doubt they took you because of that,” Sir Oliver pursued, as if communing with himself. “Being in ignorance perhaps of his deserts, deeming him a saint and martyr, they resolved to avenge him upon you, and dragged you hither for that purpose.” He sighed. “Well, well, Master Leigh, I make no doubt that knowing yourself for a rascal you have all your life been preparing your neck for a noose; so this will come as no surprise to you.”
The skipper stirred uneasily, and groaned. “Lord, how my head aches!” he complained.
“They’ve a sure remedy for that,” Sir Oliver comforted him.

===Chinese===

那声音又问了一遍，继而暴躁地添上一句：“这是哪门子黑地狱？我这是在哪儿？”
这一听，他认出那是贾斯珀·利的声音，纳闷自己这新近才收进穆罕默德门下的信徒，怎会跟他同囚一室。
“说实话，”他说，“你这是在‘银鹭号’的前舱里；至于你怎么会到这儿来，我就答不上来了。”
“你是谁？”那声音问。
“在巴巴里，人们叫我萨克埃尔-巴尔。”
“奥利弗爵士！”
“想必他们如今要这么叫我了。我将被海葬，这或许倒是好事，不然这些基督徒绅士还犯愁该在我的墓碑上刻什么铭文呢。可你——你怎么上这儿来的？我与约翰爵士讲好的条件是谁也不得侵害，我想约翰爵士总不至于背誓。”
“这个我一无所知，因为直到你告诉我，我才知道自己被扔在了什么地方。打斗里我被人打昏了——在那之前，我刚一剑捅穿了你那位俊俏的老弟。我知道的就这么些。”
奥利弗爵士倒吸一口气。“你说什么？你杀了莱昂内尔？”
“我看是杀了，”回答得冷冷的。“至少我送了两英尺钢铁进他的身子——那是在英国人刚跳上大船、混战正酣的当口；莱昂内尔少爷冲在最前头——那正是我最想不到会瞧见他的地方。”
接着是好一阵沉默。末了，奥利弗爵士低声开了口。
“毫无疑问，你给他的，正是他自己寻的。你说得对，利船长；最前排原是最不该找到他的地方——除非他是故意去寻剑，好逃过一条绞索。这样最好，毫无疑问。这样最好！愿上帝让他安息！”
“你信上帝吗？”那罪孽深重的船长问，语气里透着惶急。
“他们抓你，无疑正是为的这个，”奥利弗爵士自顾自说下去，像是心里嘀咕。“他们或许不知道他的劣迹，把他当成了圣徒和殉道者，便决意在你身上替他报仇，为这个才把你拖到这儿来。”他叹了口气。“唉，唉，利船长，我毫不怀疑：你既自知是个无赖，这辈子早就在给脖颈备着绞索了；所以这事儿到来，你也不至于吃惊。”
船长不安地动了动，呻吟起来。“老天，我头疼得厉害！”他抱怨道。
“他们有治这个的万灵药，”奥利弗爵士安慰他道。

===Original===

“And you’ll swing in better company than you deserve, for I am to be hanged in the morning too. You’ve earned it as fully as have I, Master Leigh. Yet I am sorry for you﻿—sorry you should suffer where I had not so intended.”
Master Leigh sucked in a shuddering breath, and was silent for a while.
Then he repeated an earlier question.
“Do you believe in God, Sir Oliver?”
“There is no God but God, and Mohammed is his Prophet,” was the answer, and from his tone Master Leigh could not be sure that he did not mock.
“That’s a heathen creed,” said he in fear and loathing.
“Nay, now; it’s a creed by which men live. They perform as they preach, which is more than can be said of any Christians I have ever met.”
“How can you talk so upon the eve of death?” cried Leigh in protest.
“Faith,” said Sir Oliver, “it’s considered the season of truth above all others.”
“Then ye don’t believe in God?”
“On the contrary, I do.”
“But not in the real God?” the skipper insisted.
“There can be no God but the real God﻿—it matters little what men call Him.”
“Then if ye believe, are ye not afraid?”
“Of what?”
“Of hell, damnation, and eternal fire,” roared the skipper, voicing his own belated terrors.
“I have but fulfilled the destiny which in His Omniscience He marked out for me,” replied Sir Oliver. “My life hath been as He designed it, since naught may exist or happen save by His Will. Shall I then fear damnation for having been as God fashioned me?”
“ ’Tis the heathen Muslim creed!” Master Leigh protested.
“ ’Tis a comforting one,” said Sir Oliver, “and it should comfort such a sinner as thou.”
But Master Leigh refused to be comforted. “Oh!” he groaned miserably. “I would that I did not believe in God!”
“Your disbelief could no more abolish Him than can your fear create Him,” replied Sir Oliver. “But your mood being what it is, were it not best you prayed?”
“Will not you pray with me?” quoth that rascal in his sudden fear of the hereafter.
“I shall do better,” said Sir Oliver at last. “I shall pray for you﻿—to Sir John Killigrew, that your life be spared.”
“Sure he’ll never heed you!” said Master Leigh with a catch in his breath.
“He shall. His honour is concerned in it. The terms of my surrender were that none else aboard the galley should suffer any hurt.”
“But I killed Master Lionel.”
“True﻿—but that was in the scrimmage that preceded my making terms.

===Chinese===

“而且陪你上绞架的，还是你高攀不上的体面人呢，因为明早我也要被绞。你挣来的这份报应，跟我挣来的一样足，利船长。可我为你难过——我本无意叫你跟着受这个罪。”
利船长抽了口冷气，打了个寒噤，半晌无言。
然后，他把先前的问题又问了一遍。
“你信上帝吗，奥利弗爵士？”
“万物非主，唯有真主；穆罕默德，是其先知，”他这样回答；听他那口气，利船长吃不准他是不是在嘲弄。
“那是异教的信条，”他又惧又恶地说。
“不然；那是一门叫人活得像样的信条。他们言行如一，这可比我平生见过的任何基督徒都强。”
“你怎么能在死到临头还说这种话？”利嚷道，表示抗议。
“说实话，”奥利弗爵士道，“人们公认，这正是最该讲真话的时节。”
“那么你是不信上帝了？”
“恰恰相反，我信。”
“可不信真神？”船长追问道。
“除了真神，本无别神——世人管他叫什么，无关紧要。”
“那你既然信，就不害怕吗？”
“怕什么？”
“怕地狱、怕天谴、怕永火，”船长大吼道，吼出了他自己那迟迟才来的恐惧。
“我不过是走完了他以全知为我划定的命数，”奥利弗爵士答道。“我这一生，是他设计的模样，因为万事万物，非凭他的意旨，不能存在，不能发生。既是上帝把我造成这样，我倒要为这个怕天谴么？”
“这就是异教穆斯林的信条！”利船长抗议道。
“这是一门叫人心安的信条，”奥利弗爵士说，“像你这样的罪人，正该从中得些安慰。”
可利船长不肯领这份安慰。“唉！”他凄凄惨惨地哼道。“我巴不得不信上帝才好！”
“你不信，废不掉他；正如你害怕，也造不出他，”奥利弗爵士答道。“不过你如今这心绪，不如祷告祷告，可好？”
“你不跟我一起祷告吗？”那无赖说，忽然怕起了身后事。
“我有更好的办法，”奥利弗爵士终于说道。“我替你求情——求约翰·基利格鲁爵士，饶你一命。”
“他断断不会听你的！”利船长说着，倒抽一口气。
“他会的。这关乎他的荣誉。我投降的条件，便是船上其余人等一概不受伤害。”
“可我杀了莱昂内尔少爷。”
“不错——可那是在我谈条件之前的混战里。

===Original===

Sir John pledged me his word, and Sir John will keep to it when I have made it clear to him that honour demands it.”
A great burden was lifted from the skipper’s mind﻿—that great shadow of the fear of death that had overhung him. With it, it is greatly to be feared that his desperate penitence also departed. At least he talked no more of damnation, nor took any further thought for Sir Oliver’s opinions and beliefs concerning the hereafter. He may rightly have supposed that Sir Oliver’s creed was Sir Oliver’s affair, and that should it happen to be wrong he was scarcely himself a qualified person to correct it. As for himself, the making of his soul could wait until another day, when the necessity for it should be more imminent.
Upon that he lay down and attempted to compose himself to sleep, though the pain in his head proved a difficulty. Finding slumber impossible after a while he would have talked again; but by that time his companion’s regular breathing warned him that Sir Oliver had fallen asleep during the silence.
Now this surprised and shocked the skipper. He was utterly at a loss to understand how one who had lived Sir Oliver’s life, been a renegade and a heathen, should be able to sleep tranquilly in the knowledge that at dawn he was to hang. His belated Christian zeal prompted him to rouse the sleeper and to urge him to spend the little time that yet remained him in making his peace with God. Humane compassion on the other hand suggested to him that he had best leave him in the peace of that oblivion. Considering matters he was profoundly touched to reflect that in such a season Sir Oliver could have found room in his mind to think of him and his fate and to undertake to contrive that he should be saved from the rope. He was the more touched when he bethought him of the extent to which he had himself been responsible for all that happened to Sir Oliver. Out of the consideration of heroism, a certain heroism came to be begotten in him, and he fell to pondering how in his turn he might perhaps serve Sir Oliver by a frank confession of all that he knew of the influences that had gone to make Sir Oliver what he was. This resolve uplifted him, and oddly enough it uplifted him all the more when he reflected that perhaps he would be jeopardizing his own neck by the confession upon which he had determined.

===Chinese===

约翰爵士向我作过保，等我向他言明此事关涉荣誉，他必然践约。”
船长心头卸下了一副重担——那一直压在他头顶的、怕死的大片阴霾。只怕随着它一道散去的，还有他那番痛切的悔罪。至少，他不再谈天谴了，也不再关心奥利弗爵士对身后事的见解和信仰。他多半是这么想的：奥利弗爵士的信条是奥利弗爵士自己的事，那信条万一错了，他本人也断算不上有资格去矫正的人。至于他自己，预备灵魂的事，不妨另择吉日——等到那事真个迫在眉睫的时候再说。
于是他躺下身，想凑合着入睡，怎奈头疼碍事。过了一阵，看看睡是睡不成了，他本想再聊聊天；可这时同伴匀净的呼吸声提醒他：就在这静默里，奥利弗爵士已经睡着了。
这一觉可叫船长又惊又愕。他全然想不明白：一个过着奥利弗爵士那种生活的人，一个叛教者、异教徒，明知天一亮就要上绞架，怎么还能睡得这样安稳。他那姗姗来迟的基督徒热忱撺掇他叫醒睡者，劝他把剩下的这一点点光阴拿去与上帝讲和；可另一头，人性的恻隐又劝他还是让他安享那忘忧的酣睡好。他前思后想，深深为之动容：在这样的时节，奥利弗爵士心里竟然还腾得出地方来惦记他和他的命数，还肯设法保他逃过绞索。待他想到自己在这场落到奥利弗爵士头上的一切祸事里担了多重的干系，就愈发感动了。在这番对英雄气概的感念中，他自己心里也生出几分英雄气概来，遂琢磨起自己这一回能否投桃报李，把所知的、一切铸成今日之奥利弗爵士的种种前因，向人和盘托出，以此效力。这个决心鼓舞了他；说也奇怪，待他想到自己拿定的这场招供兴许会赔上自己的脖颈，他反倒愈发振奋。

===Original===

So through that endless night he sat, nursing his aching head, and enheartened by the first purpose he had ever conceived of a truly good and altruistic deed. Yet fate it seemed was bent upon frustrating that purpose of his. For when at dawn they came to hale Sir Oliver to his doom, they paid no heed to Jasper Leigh’s demands that he, too, should be taken before Sir John.
“Thee bean’t included in our orders,” said a seaman shortly.
“Maybe not,” retorted Master Leigh, “because Sir John little knows what it is in my power to tell him. Take me before him, I say, that he may hear from me the truth of certain matters ere it be too late.”
“Be still,” the seaman bade him, and struck him heavily across the face, so that he reeled and collapsed into a corner. “Thee turn will come soon. Just now our business be with this other heathen.”
“Naught that you can say would avail,” Sir Oliver assured him quietly. “But I thank you for the thought that marks you for my friend. My hands are bound, Jasper. Were it otherwise I would beg leave to clasp your own. Fare you well!”
Sir Oliver was led out into the golden sunlight which almost blinded him after his long confinement in that dark hole. They were, he gathered, to conduct him to the cabin where a short mockery of a trial was to be held. But in the waist their progress was arrested by an officer, who bade them wait.
Sir Oliver sat down upon a coil of rope, his guard about him, an object of curious inspection to the rude seamen. They thronged the forecastle and the hatchways to stare at this formidable corsair who once had been a Cornish gentleman and who had become a renegade Muslim and a terror to Christianity.
Truth to tell, the sometime Cornish gentleman was difficult to discern in him as he sat there still wearing the caftan of cloth of silver over his white tunic and a turban of the same material swathed about his steel headpiece that ended in a spike. Idly he swung his brown sinewy legs, naked from knee to ankle, with the inscrutable calm of the fatalist upon his swarthy hawk face with its light agate eyes and black forked beard; and those callous seamen who had assembled there to jeer and mock him were stricken silent by the intrepidity and stoicism of his bearing in the face of death.
If the delay chafed him, he gave no outward sign of it. If his hard, light eyes glanced hither and thither it was upon no idle quest.

===Chinese===

就这样，在那漫漫长夜里，他捧着作疼的脑袋坐着，为他平生头一桩真正善良好心、毫不利己的念头打着气。然而命运仿佛铁了心要挫败他这桩心愿。天亮时分，人们来拖奥利弗爵士去处决，贾斯珀·利要求把自己也一并带到约翰爵士面前，却无人理会。
“咱们的差事里没你这个人，”一个水手没好气地说。
“或许是没有，”利船长回嘴道，“可那是因为约翰爵士全然不知我有话能告诉他。我说，带我去见他，趁还来得及，让他从我嘴里听听几件事的真相。”
“闭嘴，”那水手喝道，一记重拳揍在他脸上，打得他一个趔趄，栽倒在角落里。“很快就轮到你。眼下的正经事，是处置这个异教徒。”
“你说什么都没用了，”奥利弗爵士安安静静地对他说。“可你这份心，够得上做我的朋友，我谢谢你。我的手绑着呢，贾斯珀。若不然，我定要握一握你的手才别。愿你好好的！”
奥利弗爵士被带了出来，走进金灿灿的日光里；在那黑洞里闷了这许久，日光几乎晃瞎他的眼。他估摸着，他们要押他去舱房，那边要开一场走走过场的短审。可到了船腰，一名军官拦住了去路，叫他们稍候。
奥利弗爵士在一盘缆绳上坐下，看守围在四周，成了粗鲁水手们好奇围观的对象。他们挤满了前舱和舱口，争睹这个凶名赫赫的海盗——此人从前本是康沃尔绅士，后来成了叛教的穆斯林、基督世界的一大祸患。
说实在的，坐在那里的他，已很难认出当年那位康沃尔绅士了：白色束腰内袍外面仍旧罩着银锦缎的长袍，同样料子的头巾裹在尖顶钢盔上。他悠然晃着膝盖到脚踝精赤着的那双棕色筋结的腿，黝黑的鹰脸上——浅色的玛瑙眼、黑色的叉形须——透着宿命者莫测的平静。那些聚拢来本想嘲弄取笑他的冷漠水手，竟被他面对死亡时那股无畏与坚忍镇得出不了声。
这一耽搁若叫他心焦，他脸上可没有半点表露。他那双浅色的冷眼四下里扫视，却绝非无所用心。

===Original===

He was seeking Rosamund, hoping for a last sight of her before they launched him upon his last dread voyage.
But Rosamund was not to be seen. She was in the cabin at the time. She had been there for this hour past, and it was to her that the present delay was due.

===Chinese===

他在找罗莎蒙德，盼着在他们送他踏上最后那趟可怖的航程之前，能再看她一眼。
可罗莎蒙德不见踪影。她那时正在舱房里。一个钟头之前她就在那儿了——眼下这场耽搁，正是因她而起的。

## I The Huckster / 贩子
===Original===

Sir Oliver Tressilian sat at his ease in the lofty dining room of the handsome house of Penarrow, which he owed to the enterprise of his father of lamented and lamentable memory and to the skill and invention of an Italian engineer named Bagnolo who had come to England half a century ago as one of the assistants of the famous Torrigiani.
This house of such a startlingly singular and Italianate grace for so remote a corner of Cornwall deserves, together with the story of its construction, a word in passing.
The Italian Bagnolo who combined with his salient artistic talents a quarrelsome, volcanic humour had the mischance to kill a man in a brawl in a Southwark tavern. As a result he fled the town, nor paused in his headlong flight from the consequences of that murderous deed until he had all but reached the very ends of England. Under what circumstances he became acquainted with Tressilian the elder I do not know. But certain it is that the meeting was a very timely one for both of them. To the fugitive, Ralph Tressilian﻿—who appears to have been inveterately partial to the company of rascals of all denominations﻿—afforded shelter; and Bagnolo repaid the service by offering to rebuild the decaying half-timbered house of Penarrow. Having taken the task in hand he went about it with all the enthusiasm of your true artist, and achieved for his protector a residence that was a marvel of grace in that crude age and outlandish district. There arose under the supervision of the gifted engineer, worthy associate of Messer Torrigiani, a noble two-storied mansion of mellow red brick, flooded with light and sunshine by the enormously tall mullioned windows that rose almost from base to summit of each pilastered façade. The main doorway was set in a projecting wing and was overhung by a massive balcony, the whole surmounted by a pillared pediment of extraordinary grace, now partly clad in a green mantle of creepers. Above the burnt red tiles of the roof soared massive twisted chimneys in lofty majesty.
But the glory of Penarrow﻿—that is, of the new Penarrow begotten of the fertile brain of Bagnolo﻿—was the garden fashioned out of the tangled wilderness about the old house that had crowned the heights above Penarrow point. To the labours of Bagnolo, Time and Nature had added their own.

===Chinese===

奥利弗·特雷西利安爵士（Sir Oliver Tressilian）舒舒服服地坐在佩纳罗那座漂亮宅子高敞的餐厅里。这座宅子，一半得感谢他那位“遗爱”与“遗祸”兼具的先父的进取心，一半得感谢一位意大利工程师的本事和巧思——那人名叫巴尼奥洛，半世纪前随着大名鼎鼎的托里贾尼的门下来到英格兰。
这座宅子在康沃尔这么个偏僻角落里，竟有着那么出人意表的、意式的秀雅，连同它营建的始末，值得在此顺带交代一笔。
那个意大利人巴尼奥洛，一身过人的艺术才华之外，还兼着一副好斗的火暴性子，时运不济，在南华克一家酒馆的斗殴里伤了人命。为躲避这场人命官司，他仓皇出逃，一路狂奔，几乎跑到英格兰的天涯海角才歇脚。他是在什么情形下结识老特雷西利安的，我不得而知。但可以断定，那场相逢对两个人都来得正是时候。对亡命之徒来说，拉尔夫·特雷西利安——此人素来同各路无赖混得火热——给了他一个藏身之所；巴尼奥洛则提出把佩纳罗那座行将倾颓的半木结构老宅翻建一新，以为报答。他一旦接手，便拿出真正艺术家的一腔热忱，替他的庇护人造出一座宅邸，在那粗陋的年代、荒僻的地界，堪称风致的一大奇迹。在这位有才华的工程师、托里贾尼大师的合格同侪的督造之下，崛起了一座雍容的两层砖厦，砖色红润，一扇扇高及整面的直棂巨窗几乎从墙脚开到墙顶，把光明和日照满满地灌进屋内。正门设在一座凸出的翼楼里，门上方挑着一方厚重的露台，再上面冠着一座秀逸出尘的柱式山花，如今已半披着攀藤的绿氅。屋顶焦红的瓦上，一道道粗大的螺旋烟囱昂然高耸。
但佩纳罗真正的荣华——也就是巴尼奥洛那颗富饶的头脑所孕育的新佩纳罗——是那座花园：它是由老宅四周缠结的荒野开辟出来的，老宅原踞在佩纳罗角上方的高地之巅。巴尼奥洛的劳绩之外，时间与大自然又各自添上了自己的一笔。

===Original===

Bagnolo had cut those handsome esplanades, had built those noble balustrades bordering the three terraces with their fine connecting flights of steps; himself he had planned the fountain, and with his own hands had carved the granite faun presiding over it and the dozen other statues of nymphs and sylvan gods in a marble that gleamed in white brilliance amid the dusky green. But Time and Nature had smoothed the lawns to a velvet surface, had thickened the handsome boxwood hedges, and thrust up those black spearlike poplars that completed the very Italianate appearance of that Cornish demesne.
Sir Oliver took his ease in his dining room considering all this as it was displayed before him in the mellowing September sunshine, and found it all very good to see, and life very good to live. Now no man has ever been known so to find life without some immediate cause, other than that of his environment, for his optimism. Sir Oliver had several causes. The first of these﻿—although it was one which he may have been far from suspecting﻿—was his equipment of youth, wealth, and good digestion; the second was that he had achieved honour and renown both upon the Spanish Main and in the late harrying of the Invincible Armada﻿—or, more aptly perhaps might it be said, in the harrying of the late Invincible Armada﻿—and that he had received in that the twenty-fifth year of his life the honour of knighthood from the Virgin Queen; the third and last contributor to his pleasant mood﻿—and I have reserved it for the end as I account this to be the proper place for the most important factor﻿—was Dan Cupid who for once seemed compounded entirely of benignity and who had so contrived matters that Sir Oliver’s wooing Of Mistress Rosamund Godolphin ran an entirely smooth and happy course.
So, then, Sir Oliver sat at his ease in his tall, carved chair, his doublet untrussed, his long legs stretched before him, a pensive smile about the firm lips that as yet were darkened by no more than a small black line of moustachios. (Lord Henry’s portrait of him was drawn at a much later period.) It was noon, and our gentleman had just dined, as the platters, the broken meats and the half-empty flagon on the board beside him testified.

===Chinese===

那几道气派的露台是巴尼奥洛开辟的，环着三层台地的那些华美的石栏、连同衔接台地的精致石级，也是他筑的；喷泉是他亲手规划的，还有统御泉眼的那个花岗岩农牧之神，和另外十几尊宁芙与林神的石像，都出自他亲手雕琢，白大理石的光泽在黝黑的绿荫间灼灼照眼。而时间与大自然把草坪熨成了天鹅绒，把一堵堵黄杨树篱养得丰茂，又擎起那一株株黑矛也似的白杨，替这处康沃尔的产业补足了十足的意大利气象。
奥利弗爵士在他的餐厅里悠然而坐，把眼前这一切尽收眼底——九月的天光正给它镀上一层温煦的金黄——只觉得样样都好看，这日子也过得有滋味。从无一人单凭周遭的顺遂，便对人生这样心满意足；奥利弗爵士那份好心境，来头却有好几桩。头一桩——尽管他多半毫无察觉——是他那份年富、财足、消化又好的本钱；第二桩，是他在西班牙大陆沿岸立过功名，又在围剿无敌舰队一役中赢过声威——或者更恰当地说，是在围剿那支业已覆灭的无敌舰队一役中——并在他二十五岁那年，蒙童贞女王赐封爵士之荣；第三桩、也是末一桩好心境的来由——我把它压在最后，因为要紧的砝码理应放在秤盘的末尾——是丘比特老儿：这位爱神这一回仿佛全用慈惠捏成，把事情安排得妥妥帖帖，叫奥利弗爵士对罗莎蒙德·戈多尔芬小姐的追求一路顺遂美满。
于是，奥利弗爵士安然坐在他那把高大的雕花椅里，紧身上衣的系带松着，两条长腿朝前伸直，唇边噙着一抹悠悠的笑——那两片坚毅的嘴唇，当时不过描着一线黑黑的小髭。（亨利勋爵为他画的像，是多年以后的事了。）时当正午，我们的绅士刚用过饭，案上残肴、空盘和那只斟了半盏的酒壶便是明证。

===Original===

He pulled thoughtfully at a long pipe﻿—for he had acquired this newly imported habit of tobacco-drinking﻿—and dreamed of his mistress, and was properly and gallantly grateful that fortune had used him so handsomely as to enable him to toss a title and some measure of renown into his Rosamund’s lap.
By nature Sir Oliver was a shrewd fellow (“cunning as twenty devils,” is my Lord Henry’s phrase) and he was also a man of some not inconsiderable learning. Yet neither his natural wit nor his acquired endowments appear to have taught him that of all the gods that rule the destinies of mankind there is none more ironic and malicious than that same Dan Cupid in whose honour, as it were, he was now burning the incense of that pipe of his. The ancients knew that innocent-seeming boy for a cruel, impish knave, and they mistrusted him. Sir Oliver either did not know or did not heed that sound piece of ancient wisdom. It was to be borne in upon him by grim experience, and even as his light pensive eyes smiled upon the sunshine that flooded the terrace beyond the long mullioned window, a shadow fell athwart it which he little dreamed to be symbolic of the shadow that was even falling across the sunshine of his life.
After that shadow came the substance﻿—tall and gay of raiment under a broad black Spanish hat decked with bloodred plumes. Swinging a long beribboned cane the figure passed the windows, stalking deliberately as Fate.
The smile perished on Sir Oliver’s lips. His swarthy face grew thoughtful, his black brows contracted until no more than a single deep furrow stood between them. Then slowly the smile came forth again, but no longer that erstwhile gentle pensive smile. It was transformed into a smile of resolve and determination, a smile that tightened his lips even as his brows relaxed, and invested his brooding eyes with a gleam that was mocking, crafty and almost wicked.
Came Nicholas his servant to announce Master Peter Godolphin, and close upon the lackey’s heels came Master Godolphin himself, leaning upon his beribboned cane and carrying his broad Spanish hat. He was a tall, slender gentleman, with a shaven, handsome countenance, stamped with an air of haughtiness; like Sir Oliver, he had a high-bridged, intrepid nose, and in age he was the younger by some two or three years.

===Chinese===

他若有所思地抽着一杆长烟斗——抽烟这新近舶来的习气他已沾上了——梦想着他的情人，并且很有骑士风度地感念命运待他如此厚道，让他能把一个爵位、几分声名，抛进他的罗莎蒙德的裙兜里。
奥利弗爵士天性是个精明人（“狡猾得赛过二十个魔鬼”，这是亨利勋爵的原话），学问也不算浅。然而，无论是他的天资还是后天的学养，似乎都不曾教给他一条道理：在掌管人类命数的众神之中，再没有哪一位，比他此刻用这杆烟斗焚香敬奉的丘比特老儿更爱讥诮、更促狭的了。古人早识破那貌似无辜的顽童是个狠毒的捣蛋鬼，对他存着戒心。奥利弗爵士要么不知道、要么没把这条扎实的古人智慧放在心上。这要靠惨痛的阅历来教会他；而且，正当他那双若有所思的浅色眼睛，笑望着长窗之外漫上露台的阳光时，一道阴影横截过那片阳光——他做梦也想不到，这阴影正是他生命里的阳光上正落下的那道阴影的先兆。
影子过后，来了实体——一个高高的身影，衣衫鲜丽，头上顶着宽边黑西班牙帽，帽上插着血红的翎毛。他摆着一根系彩带的长手杖，从窗前踱过，步子从容，一如命运。
奥利弗爵士唇边的笑意敛去了。他黝黑的脸沉了下来，两道黑眉拧紧，眉心只挤出深深一道竖纹。随后，笑容又缓缓回到唇边，却不再是先前那抹温软的、悠然的笑。它变成了一副下了决心的笑，一副收紧双唇的笑——两眉反倒舒开了——给那双沉思的眼睛添上一缕讥诮的、狡黠的、近乎凶狠的光。
仆人尼古拉斯进来禀报彼得·戈多尔芬少爷到，而紧跟着仆人的脚跟，戈多尔芬少爷本人便进来了，拄着那根系彩带的手杖，手里拿着宽边西班牙帽。他是个瘦高的绅士，脸刮得干净，相貌英俊，眉宇间印着一股倨傲；同奥利弗爵士一样，也生着一只高挺的无畏的鼻子；年纪比爵士小着两三岁。

===Original===

He wore his auburn hair rather longer than was the mode just then, but in his apparel there was no more foppishness than is tolerable in a gentleman of his years.
Sir Oliver rose and bowed from his great height in welcome. But a wave of tobacco-smoke took his graceful visitor in the throat and set him coughing and grimacing.
“I see,” he choked, “that ye have acquired that filthy habit.”
“I have known filthier,” said Sir Oliver composedly.
“I nothing doubt it,” rejoined Master Godolphin, thus early giving indications of his humour and the object of his visit.
Sir Oliver checked an answer that must have helped his visitor to his ends, which was no part of the knight’s intent.
“Therefore,” said he ironically, “I hope you will be patient with my shortcomings. Nick, a chair for Master Godolphin and another cup. I bid you welcome to Penarrow.”
A sneer flickered over the younger man’s white face. “You pay me a compliment, sir, which I fear me ’tis not mine to return to you.”
“Time enough for that when I come to seek it,” said Sir Oliver, with easy, if assumed, good humour.
“When you come to seek it?”
“The hospitality of your house,” Sir Oliver explained.
“It is on that very matter I am come to talk with you.”
“Will you sit?” Sir Oliver invited him, and spread a hand towards the chair which Nicholas had set. In the same gesture he waved the servant away.
Master Godolphin ignored the invitation. “You were,” he said, “at Godolphin Court but yesterday, I hear.” He paused, and as Sir Oliver offered no denial, he added stiffly: “I am come, sir, to inform you that the honour of your visits is one we shall be happy to forgo.”
In the effort he made to preserve his self-control before so direct an affront Sir Oliver paled a little under his tan.
“You will understand, Peter,” he replied slowly, “that you have said too much unless you add something more.” He paused, considering his visitor a moment. “I do not know whether Rosamund has told you that yesterday she did me the honour to consent to become my wife.﻿ ﻿…”
“She is a child that does not know her mind,” broke in the other.
“Do you know of any good reason why she should come to change it?” asked Sir Oliver, with a slight air of challenge.
Master Godolphin sat down, crossed his legs and placed his hat on his knee.
“I know a dozen,” he answered. “But I need not urge them.

===Chinese===

他的栗色头发留得比时兴的略长了些，衣着上倒还端正，没有超出他这年纪的绅士所能容许的那种浮华。
奥利弗爵士站起身，居高临下地一躬身，以示欢迎。可一缕烟草的青烟偏偏扑上这位风雅来客的喉咙，呛得他连咳带皱眉。
“我看出来了，”他咳着说，“您染上这恶习了。”
“比这更恶的我也见识过。”奥利弗爵士从容答道。
“这我毫不怀疑，”戈多尔芬少爷回敬道，这么早就露出了他的来意和脾气。
奥利弗爵士把一句到了嘴边的、正好给来客递刀把子的话咽了回去——爵士可没打算成全他。
“所以说，”他话里带刺地说，“但愿您多多包涵我的短处。尼克，给戈多尔芬少爷搬把椅子，再拿一只杯子来。我欢迎您光临佩纳罗。”
年轻些的那张白脸上掠过一丝冷笑。“您这是抬举我，先生，只怕我无福照样回敬您。”
“等我上门去讨的时候，日子还长着呢。”奥利弗爵士说，装出一副随和的好脾气。
“等您上门去讨什么？”
“讨贵府的款待啊。”奥利弗爵士解释道。
“我正是为这件事来同您谈谈的。”
“您请坐呀？”奥利弗爵士相让道，朝尼古拉斯摆好的椅子摊了摊手，顺势一挥，叫仆人退下了。
戈多尔芬少爷却不领这份相让。“听说您昨天到戈多尔芬宅去了。”他顿了顿，见奥利弗爵士并不否认，便又板着脸添了一句：“先生，我是来知会您的：您肯赏光光临，这份体面我们宁可不要。”
面对这样直露的羞辱，奥利弗爵士竭力自持，棕黑的脸皮下微微泛了白。
“彼得，你要明白，”他慢条斯理地答道，“不补上几句，你这话就说得太过头了。”他停了停，把来客端详了片刻。“罗莎蒙德有没有告诉你，昨天她赏脸答应做我的妻子了……”
“她是个孩子，自己还拿不定主意。”对方插嘴道。
“那你知道她有什么该改主意的正经理由么？”奥利弗爵士问，语气里带着一点挑衅。
戈多尔芬少爷坐了下来，架起腿，把帽子搁在膝上。
“我知道一打理由，”他答道，“可我不必一一细数。

===Original===

Sufficient should it be to remind you that Rosamund is but seventeen and that she is under my guardianship and that of Sir John Killigrew. Neither Sir John nor I can sanction this betrothal.”
“Good lack!” broke out Sir Oliver. “Who asks your sanction or Sir John’s? By God’s grace your sister will grow to be a woman soon and mistress of herself. I am in no desperate haste to get me wed, and by nature﻿—as you may be observing﻿—I am a wondrous patient man. I’ll even wait.” And he pulled at his pipe.
“Waiting cannot avail you in this, Sir Oliver. ’Tis best you should understand. We are resolved, Sir John and I.”
“Are you so? God’s light. Send Sir John to me to tell me of his resolves and I’ll tell him something of mine. Tell him from me, Master Godolphin, that if he will trouble to come as far as Penarrow I’ll do by him what the hangman should have done long since. I’ll crop his pimpish ears for him, by this hand!”
“Meanwhile,” said Master Godolphin whettingly, “will you not essay your rover’s prowess upon me?”
“You?” quoth Sir Oliver, and looked him over with good-humoured contempt. “I’m no butcher of fledgelings, my lad. Besides, you are your sister’s brother, and ’tis no aim of mine to increase the obstacles already in my path.” Then his tone changed. He leaned across the table. “Come, now, Peter. What is at the root of all this matter? Can we not compose such differences as you conceive exist? Out with them. ’Tis no matter for Sir John. He’s a curmudgeon who signifies not a finger’s snap. But you, ’tis different. You are her brother. Out with your plaints, then. Let us be frank and friendly.”
“Friendly?” The other sneered again. “Our fathers set us an example in that.”
“Does it matter what our fathers did? More shame to them if, being neighbours, they could not be friends. Shall we follow so deplorable an example?”
“You’ll not impute that the fault lay with my father,” cried the other, with a show of ready anger.
“I impute nothing, lad. I cry shame upon them both.”
“ ’Swounds!” swore Master Peter. “Do you malign the dead?”
“If I do, I malign them both. But I do not. I no more than condemn a fault that both must acknowledge could they return to life.”
“Then, sir, confine your condemnings to your own father with whom no man of honour could have lived at peace.﻿ ﻿…”
“Softly, softly, good Sir.﻿ ﻿…”
“There’s no call to go softly.

===Chinese===

只消提醒您一句也就够了：罗莎蒙德才十七岁，她的监护是我和约翰·基利格鲁爵士。这门亲事，约翰爵士和我都做不得主，也断断不能应承。”
“老天爷！”奥利弗爵士脱口嚷道，“谁要你们应承？谁求了你的准、还是约翰爵士的准？托上帝的福，你妹妹不久就是大人，自己做自己的主了。我又不是急猴猴地要成亲；再说，我这人天生——你也瞧见了——耐性好得出奇。我等得起。”说着他又抽了一口烟斗。
“等，在这件事上救不了您，奥利弗爵士，您最好明白这一点。我和约翰爵士主意已定。”
“是吗？老天作证。叫约翰爵士亲自来对我说他的主意，我也跟他说说我的。你就替我带话，戈多尔芬少爷：他若肯屈尊到佩纳罗来一趟，我就替刽子手办一桩他早该办的事——亲手割了他那对纨绔的耳朵，我说话算数！”
“那么眼下，”戈多尔芬少爷拿话激他，“您何不在我的身上试试您海盗的本事？”
“你？”奥利弗爵士说着，把他从头到脚好脾气地轻蔑打量了一遍。“我可不宰雏儿，小伙子。再说，你是她哥哥，我犯不上再给自己的路上添绊脚石。”说到这里，他换了口气，隔着桌子俯过身去。“来，彼得，这究竟是怎么回事？你心里认定的那些疙瘩，难道咱们解不开？都摊出来吧。这事跟约翰爵士不相干，那是个守财奴，屁事不顶。可你不同，你是她的哥哥。有什么冤屈，都说出来。咱们开诚布公，好好儿地谈。”
“好好儿地谈？”对方又是一声冷笑，“咱们的父亲在这上头可是做出了榜样。”
“父辈们干了什么，要紧吗？身为近邻却做不成朋友，是他们丢人。难道咱们要照着那么个糟糕的榜样学？”
“您可别想把错处栽到我父亲头上！”对方嚷起来，火气来得倒快。
“我谁的头也不栽，小伙子。他们两个，我都替他们臊得慌。”
“该死！”彼得少爷骂道，“您竟糟践死人？”
“要糟践，也是两个一起糟践。可我没有。我不过是责一桩过错，一桩他们俩若能还阳、自己也认账的过错。”
“那么，先生，您那些责难，还是留给你自己的父亲吧——跟他那种人，任何一个体面人都处不来……”
“轻些，轻些，好先生……”
“用不着轻。

===Original===

Ralph Tressilian was a dishonour, a scandal to the countryside. Not a hamlet between here and Truro, or between here and Helston, but swarms with big Tressilian noses like your own, in memory of your debauched parent.”
Sir Oliver’s eyes grew narrower: he smiled. “I wonder how you came by your own nose?” he wondered.
Master Godolphin got to his feet in a passion, and his chair crashed over behind him. “Sir,” he blazed, “you insult my mother’s memory!”
Sir Oliver laughed. “I make a little free with it, perhaps, in return for your pleasantries on the score of my father.”
Master Godolphin pondered him in speechless anger, then swayed by his passion he leaned across the board, raised his long cane and struck Sir Oliver sharply on the shoulder.
That done, he strode off magnificently towards the door. Halfway thither he paused.
“I shall expect your friends and the length of your sword,” said he.
Sir Oliver laughed again. “I don’t think I shall trouble to send them,” said he.
Master Godolphin wheeled, fully to face him again. “How? You will take a blow?”
Sir Oliver shrugged. “None saw it given,” said he.
“But I shall publish it abroad that I have caned you.”
“You’ll publish yourself a liar if you do; for none will believe you.” Then he changed his tone yet again. “Come, Peter, we are behaving unworthily. As for the blow, I confess that I deserved it. A man’s mother is more sacred than his father. So we may cry quits on that score. Can we not cry quits on all else? What can it profit us to perpetuate a foolish quarrel that sprang up between our fathers?”
“There is more than that between us,” answered Master Godolphin. “I’ll not have my sister wed a pirate.”
“A pirate? God’s light! I am glad there’s none to hear you for since her grace has knighted me for my doings upon the seas, your words go very near to treason. Surely, lad, what the Queen approves, Master Peter Godolphin may approve and even your mentor Sir John Killigrew. You’ve been listening to him. ’Twas he sent you hither.”
“I am no man’s lackey,” answered the other hotly, resenting the imputation﻿—and resenting it the more because of the truth in it.
“To call me a pirate is to say a foolish thing. Hawkins with whom I sailed has also received the accolade, and who dubs us pirates insults the Queen herself. Apart from that, which, as you see, is a very empty charge, what else have you against me?

===Chinese===

拉尔夫·特雷西利安是这一带的耻辱，是乡里的丑闻。从这里到特鲁罗，从这里到赫尔森，没有一座村子不遍布您这种特雷西利安式的大鼻子——全拜您那位荒唐父亲所赐，那是他留下的纪念。”
奥利弗爵士的眼睛眯细了，他笑了。“倒想请教，你那只鼻子又是打哪儿来的？”他故作纳罕道。
戈多尔芬少爷勃然大怒，霍地站起，椅子在他身后哗啦翻倒。“先生，”他炸了，“您侮辱我亡母的名声！”
奥利弗爵士笑了。“兴许是稍稍放肆了些——就算回敬你方才拿我父亲说的那些俏皮话吧。”
戈多尔芬少爷气得说不出话，直勾勾瞪着他；随即被怒火推着，隔着桌子探过身来，举起长手杖，在奥利弗爵士肩上不轻不重地抽了一记。
打完，他大摇大摆地朝门口走去。走到半路，又停住了。
“我就等着您的朋友和您剑的长度了。”他说。
奥利弗爵士又笑了。“我看，不必费那个事。”他说。
戈多尔芬少爷转过身来，正对着他。“什么？您甘愿挨这一杖？”
奥利弗爵士耸耸肩。“没人瞧见你打。”他说。
“可我要把您挨了我手杖的事四处张扬出去。”
“那你就把自己张扬成一个说谎的人；因为没人会信你。”说到这里，他又换了口气。“来吧，彼得，咱们这样太不像话了。那一杖，我认了，是我该受的。母亲比父亲更碰不得，这一层咱们就算两讫。别的难道不能也两讫么？为着父辈们闹出来的那点糊涂口角，咱们没完没了地闹下去，有什么好处？”
“咱们之间的事，不止那一点，”戈多尔芬少爷答道，“我不能让我妹妹嫁给一个海盗。”
“海盗？老天作证！幸亏没人听见你这话——女王为我在海上的功业赐了我爵位，你这话说出去，离叛国罪只差一层纸了。真的，小伙子，女王都认可的事，彼得·戈多尔芬少爷尽可以认可，就连你那位太傅约翰·基利格鲁爵士也尽可以认可。你是听了他来的吧。是他叫你上这儿来的。”
“我不是谁的马弁，”对方涨红了脸答道，对这话着恼——着恼更因为这话说着了。
“骂我海盗，是一句蠢话。当年同我一道航海的霍金斯也受过封爵；管我们叫海盗，就是骂女王本人。这一条且撇开——你也看见了，这指控空洞得很——你还有别的什么跟我过不去？

===Original===

I am, I hope, as good as any other here in Cornwall; Rosamund honours me with her affection and I am rich and shall be richer still ere the wedding bells are heard.”
“Rich with the fruit of thieving upon the seas, rich with the treasures of scuttled ships and the price of slaves captured in Africa and sold to the plantations, rich as the vampire is glutted﻿—with the blood of dead men.”
“Does Sir John say that?” asked Sir Oliver, in a soft deadly voice.
“I say it.”
“I heard you; but I am asking where you learnt that pretty lesson. Is Sir John your preceptor? He is, he is. No need to tell me. I’ll deal with him. Meanwhile let me disclose to you the pure and disinterested source of Sir John’s rancour. You shall see what an upright and honest gentleman is Sir John, who was your father’s friend and has been your guardian.”
“I’ll not listen to what you say of him.”
“Nay, but you shall, in return for having made me listen to what he says of me. Sir John desires to obtain a licence to build at the mouth of the Fal. He hopes to see a town spring up above the haven there under the shadow of his own Manor of Arwenack. He represents himself as nobly disinterested and all concerned for the prosperity of the country, and he neglects to mention that the land is his own and that it is his own prosperity and that of his family which he is concerned to foster. We met in London by a fortunate chance whilst Sir John was about this business at the Court.
