Now it happens that I, too, have interests in Truro and Penryn; but, unlike Sir John, I am honest in the matter, and proclaim it. If any growth should take place about Smithick it follows from its more advantageous situation that Truro and Penryn must suffer, and that suits me as little as the other matter would suit Sir John. I told him so, for I can be blunt, and I told the Queen in the form of a counter-petition to Sir John’s.” He shrugged. “The moment was propitious to me. I was one of the seamen who had helped to conquer the unconquerable Armada of King Philip. I was therefore not to be denied, and Sir John was sent home as empty-handed as he went to Court. D’ye marvel that he hates me? Knowing him for what he is, d’ye marvel that he dubs me pirate and worse? ’Tis natural enough so to misrepresent my doings upon the sea, since it is those doings have afforded me the power to hurt his profit.

===Chinese===

我想，在康沃尔我不输给这里任何人；罗莎蒙德赏脸垂爱于我；我有家财，而且不等婚礼的钟声响起来，还会更有。”
“好一份家财——海上劫掠的赃物，凿沉的船里的财货，非洲掳来、卖给种植园的奴隶换来的钱——富得像吸血鬼，靠死人的血吃肥的。”
“这是约翰爵士说的？”奥利弗爵士问，声音又轻又狠。
“是我说的。”
“我听见了；可我问的是，你这一课上得跟谁学的。约翰爵士是你的太傅吧？是了，是了，不必告诉我。我来收拾他。趁现在，也让我向你披露一下约翰爵士那份怨毒的又纯洁又无私的来路。你倒看看，这位正直诚实的约翰爵士——你父亲的旧友、你的监护人——是个什么东西。”
“您说他什么，我一概不听。”
“不成，你非听不可——就当回敬你方才逼我听他说我的那些话。约翰爵士想弄一张法尔河口的建屋执照。他巴望看着一座镇子在那片港湾旁崛起，卧在他阿文纳克庄園的荫庇之下。他把自己标榜得大公无私、一心为乡里兴旺，却绝口不提那片地是他自家的，他要操心的兴旺，是他自家的兴旺、他自家的门楣。凑巧，他在伦敦朝廷里奔走这件事的时候，我们有幸撞见了。不巧我又在特鲁罗和彭林两处都有营生；可同约翰爵士不一样，我在这件事上是亮在明处的。史密尼克那一带倘若有起色，凭它地段的便宜，特鲁罗和彭林必受挤压——这对我半点不合算，正如那件事对约翰爵士不合算。我把这话明明白白告诉了他，我这人嘴上不来虚的；又用一份驳约翰爵士的呈文，把话禀到了女王御前。”他耸了耸肩。“那阵子我正走红运。我是打败了打不垮的腓力国王的无敌舰队的海员之一，御前没有不允的道理；于是约翰爵士两手空空打道回府，同他进京时一般空。你奇怪他恨我么？再知道他是何等人物，你奇怪他骂我海盗、骂我更难听的么？把我海上的营生编排成这样，再自然不过——正是那些营生，给了我伤他财路的力量。”

===Original===

He has chosen the weapons of calumny for this combat, but those weapons are not mine, as I shall show him this very day. If you do not credit what I say, come with me and be present at the little talk I hope to have with that curmudgeon.”
“You forget,” said Master Godolphin, “that I, too, have interests in the neighbourhood of Smithick, and that you are hurting those.”
“Soho!” crowed Sir Oliver. “Now at last the sun of truth peeps forth from all this cloud of righteous indignation at my bad Tressilian blood and pirate’s ways! You, too, are but a trafficker. Now see what a fool I am to have believed you sincere, and to have stood here in talk with you as with an honest man.” His voice swelled and his lip curled in a contempt that struck the other like a blow. “I swear I had not wasted breath with you had I known you for so mean and pitiful a fellow.”
“These words.﻿ ﻿…” began Master Godolphin, drawing himself up very stiffly.
“Are a deal less than your deserts,” cut in the other, and he raised his voice to call﻿—“Nick.”
“You shall answer to them,” snapped his visitor.
“I am answering now,” was the stern answer. “To come here and prate to me of my dead father’s dissoluteness and of an ancient quarrel between him and yours, to bleat of my trumped-up course of piracy and my own ways of life as a just cause why I may not wed your sister whilst the real consideration in your mind, the real spur to your hostility is not more than the matter of some few paltry pounds a year that I hinder you from pocketing. A God’s name get you gone.”
Nick entered at that moment.
“You shall hear from me again, Sir Oliver,” said the other, white with anger. “You shall account to me for these words.”
“I do not fight with﻿ ﻿… with hucksters,” flashed Sir Oliver.
“D’ye dare call me that?”
“Indeed, ’tis to discredit an honourable class, I confess it. Nick, the door for Master Godolphin.”

===Chinese===

这一仗他挑了诽谤做兵器；可那兵器我使不来，今天我就要让他亲眼看看。你要不信我的话，就跟我走一趟，听听我同那个守财奴的那场小小的谈话。”
“您忘了，”戈多尔芬少爷说，“我在史密尼克左近也有营生，您伤着的正是我的。”
“好哇！”奥利弗爵士朗声一笑。“如今总算露出真章了——什么特雷西利安家的坏血统、什么海盗行径，那一大团义正词严的乌云背后，原来探出这一点真话的日头！你，也不过是条做买卖的。瞧瞧我这人多蠢，竟拿你当真心人，站在这儿同你像同体面人似的有来有去地讲道理。”他的嗓门涨起来，嘴唇一撇，那份轻蔑劈头盖脸砸向对方。“我要早知道你是这么个下三滥的可怜虫，我对你说，我连一口气也犯不上为你浪费。”
“这话……”戈多尔芬少爷挺直了身子，开口道。
“比你的实价还便宜了一大截呢，”对方截住他，随即扬声唤人——“尼克。”
“这账您是要清算的，奥利弗爵士。”来客咬着牙说。
“我现在就在清算，”回答斩钉截铁。“跑到我家来，念叨我亡父的荒唐、他同令尊的那场陈年旧怨，数落我凭空捏造的海盗行径、我的生活做派，说这些是我不配娶你妹妹的正当理由——而你心里真正的盘算、你这份敌意的真正由头，不过是那么一年几个臭钱，是我碍着你往兜里揣的那几个。看在上帝份上，请吧。”
尼克恰在这时进来了。
“咱们没完，奥利弗爵士，”那位说，脸都气白了。“这几句话，您要向我交账的。”
“我不跟……跟贩子过招。”奥利弗爵士厉声道。
“您敢这么叫我？”
“敢，这实在是辱没了体面的行当，我认。尼克，替戈多尔芬少爷开门。”

## IV The Intervener / 插手人
===Original===

The parson had notions of riding after Sir Oliver, and begged Master Baine to join him. But the Justice looked down his long nose and opined that no good purpose was to be served; that Tressilians were ever wild and bloody men; and that an angry Tressilian was a thing to be avoided. Sir Andrew, who was far from valorous, thought there might be wisdom in the Justice’s words, and remembered that he had troubles enough of his own with a froward wife without taking up the burdens of others. Master Godolphin and Sir Oliver between them, quoth the Justice, had got up this storm of theirs. A God’s name let them settle it, and if in the settling they should cut each other’s throats haply the countryside would be well rid of a brace of turbulent fellows. The pedlar deemed them a couple of madmen, whose ways were beyond the understanding of a sober citizen. The others﻿—the fishermen and the rustics﻿—had not the means to follow even had they had the will.
They dispersed to put abroad the news of that short furious quarrel and to prophesy that blood would be let in the adjusting of it. This prognostication they based entirely upon their knowledge of the short Tressilian way. But it was a matter in which they were entirely wrong. It is true that Sir Oliver went galloping along that road that follows the Penryn river and that he pounded over the bridge in the town of Penryn in Master Godolphin’s wake with murder in his heart. Men who saw him riding wildly thus with the red wheal across his white furious face said that he looked a very devil.
He crossed the bridge at Penryn a half-hour after sunset, as dusk was closing into night, and it may be that the sharp, frosty air had a hand in the cooling of his blood. For as he reached the river’s eastern bank he slackened his breakneck pace, even as he slackened the angry galloping of his thoughts. The memory of that oath he had sworn three months ago to Rosamund smote him like a physical blow. It checked his purpose, and, reflecting this, his pace fell to an amble. He shivered to think how near he had gone to wrecking all the happiness that lay ahead of him. What was a boy’s whiplash, that his resentment of it should set all his future life in jeopardy? Even though men should call him a coward for submitting to it and leaving the insult unavenged, what should that matter?

===Chinese===

牧师本想骑马追上奥利弗爵士，还央贝恩少爷同去。可治安官顺着他的长鼻子往下望了望，说去了也没用处：特雷西利安家的人向来蛮横嗜血；一个动了怒的特雷西利安，更是能躲多远躲多远的东西。安德鲁爵士本就不是勇武之辈，觉得治安官这话未尝没有道理，又想起自己家里那个悍妇已经够他操心的了，犯不上再去背别人的包袱。这场风波，治安官说，是戈多尔芬少爷和奥利弗爵士两个人自己掀起来的；成全他们自己了断去吧，倘若了断的时候他们彼此割了对方的喉咙，乡里平白少了一对刺头，倒也干净。行脚商觉得他们是一对疯子，疯子的行径，不是他这样安分市民所能理解的。其余的人——渔夫和乡民——纵然想追，也没有追的本钱。
大家散了，四下传扬这场短促而凶暴的口角，还预言这场梁子收场时必要见血。这预言，全是从他们对特雷西利安家那副急脾气的了解里推出来的。可在这件事上，他们错了个干净。的确，奥利弗爵士是顺着那条沿彭林河的路一路狂奔，在彭林镇上一声巨响碾过石桥，跟在戈多尔芬少爷身后，心里揣着杀机。瞧见他这样没命地骑、狂怒的白脸上横着那道红痕的人都说，他活脱脱一个恶鬼。
日落之后半小时，夜色四合，他过了彭林的桥；也许是那凛冽的霜气掺了一手，给他退了退血里的热。一到河东岸，他便放缓了那不要命的速度，连脑子里怒马的奔腾也一并缓了下来。三个月前他对罗莎蒙德起的那个誓，像一记实打实的拳头砸醒了他。那誓言勒住了他的念头；念头一缓，马步也落成了碎步。想到自己险些把眼前的幸福毁于一旦，他打了个寒噤。不过是一个毛头小子的一马鞭，为泄这点怨气，值得押上自己一辈子的前程么？哪怕世人骂他忍气吞声、骂他不雪此辱是懦夫，又有什么要紧？

===Original===

Moreover, upon the body of him who did so proclaim him he could brand the lie of a charge so foolish. Sir Oliver raised his eyes to the deep sapphire dome of heaven where an odd star was glittering frostily, and thanked God from a swelling heart that he had not overtaken Peter Godolphin whilst his madness was upon him.
A mile or so below Penryn, he turned up the road that ran down to the ferry there, and took his way home over the shoulder of the hill with a slack rein. It was not his usual way. He was wont ever to go round by Trefusis Point that he might take a glimpse at the walls of the house that harboured Rosamund and a glance at the window of her bower. But tonight he thought the shorter road over the hill would be the safer way. If he went by Godolphin Court he might chance to meet Peter again, and his past anger warned him against courting such a meeting, warned him to avoid it lest evil should betide. Indeed, so imperious was the warning, and such were his fears of himself after what had just passed, that he resolved to leave Penarrow on the next day. Whither he would go he did not then determine. He might repair to London, and he might even go upon another cruise﻿—an idea which he had lately dismissed under Rosamund’s earnest intercession. But it was imperative that he should quit the neighbourhood, and place a distance between Peter Godolphin and himself until such time as he might take Rosamund to wife. Eight months or so of exile; but what matter? Better so than that he should be driven into some deed that would compel him to spend his whole lifetime apart from her. He would write, and she would understand and approve when he told her what had passed that day.
The resolve was firmly implanted in him by the time he reached Penarrow, and he felt himself uplifted by it and by the promise it afforded him that thus his future happiness would be assured.
Himself he stabled his horse; for of the two grooms he kept, one had by his leave set out yesterday to spend Christmas in Devon with his parents, the other had taken a chill and had been ordered to bed that very day by Sir Oliver, who was considerate with those that served him.

===Chinese===

再说了，谁敢真这么张扬，他尽可以在那人的皮肉上，烙下这个蠢指控的谎印。奥利弗爵士抬眼望向深蓝宝石穹顶也似的夜空，一颗疏星正凛凛地眨着寒光；他心头发热，感谢上帝，幸而没有在自己疯魔大作的当口追上彼得·戈多尔芬。
在彭林下游一英里上下，他拐上那条通渡口的路，翻过山肩，松着缰绳取道回家。这不是他惯走的路。往常他总要绕特雷富西斯角那面走，好望一眼收留罗莎蒙德的那座宅子的墙，望一眼她闺阁的窗。可今晚他觉得，翻山的近路更稳妥。若打戈多尔芬宅前过，没准又撞上彼得；方才那场怒火警告他别去招惹这样的相逢，警告他躲着走，免得生出祸事。这警告来得如此急切，而经过方才那一遭，他又这样怕自己，以致他决意明天就离开佩纳罗。去哪里，他还没想定。可以上伦敦去，甚至可以再出海跑一趟——这主意他近来才在罗莎蒙德的恳切劝止下撂下。但离开这一带是刻不容缓的，得在他把罗莎蒙德娶回家之前，在自己同彼得·戈多尔芬之间隔出一段距离来。八个月上下的避走；可那又何妨？总好过被逼出一桩血案，害自己一辈子同她分离。他会写信；待他把今天的事写给她，她自会明白，自会赞成。
这个决心，到家时已在他心里扎了根，他觉得它托着自己往上升，它给了他一个许诺：如此，来日的幸福便保住了。
他亲自把马牵进厩里；他养着两个马夫，一个告了假，昨天动身去德文郡爹娘那里过圣诞；另一个染了风寒，就在今天，被体贴下人的奥利弗爵士打发上了床。

===Original===

In the dining room he found supper spread, and a great log fire blazed in the enormous cowled fireplace, diffusing a pleasant warmth through the vast room and flickering ruddily upon the trophies of weapons that adorned the walls, upon the tapestries and the portraits of dead Tressilians. Hearing his step, old Nicholas entered bearing a great candle-branch which he set upon the table.
“You’m late, Sir Oliver,” said the servant, “and Master Lionel bain’t home yet neither.”
Sir Oliver grunted and scowled as he crunched a log and set it sizzling under his wet heel. He thought of Malpas and cursed Lionel’s folly, as, without a word, he loosed his cloak and flung it on an oaken coffer by the wall where already he had cast his hat. Then he sat down, and Nicholas came forward to draw off his boots.
When that was done and the old servant stood up again, Sir Oliver shortly bade him to serve supper.
“Master Lionel cannot be long now,” said he. “And give me to drink, Nick. ’Tis what I most require.”
“I’ve brewed ee a posset o’ canary sack,” announced Nicholas; “there’m no better supping o’ a frosty winter’s night, Sir Oliver.”
He departed to return presently with a black jack that was steaming fragrantly. He found his master still in the same attitude, staring at the fire, and frowning darkly. Sir Oliver’s thoughts were still of his brother and Malpas, and so insistent were they that his own concerns were for the moment quite neglected; he was considering whether it was not his duty, after all, to attempt a word of remonstrance. At length he rose with a sigh and got to table. There he bethought him of his sick groom, and asked Nicholas for news of him. Nicholas reported the fellow to be much as he had been, whereupon Sir Oliver took up a cup and brimmed it with the steaming posset.
“Take him that,” he said. “There’s no better medicine for such an ailment.”
Outside fell a clatter of hooves.
“Here be Master Lionel at last,” said the servant.
“No doubt,” agreed Sir Oliver. “No need to stay for him. Here is all he needs. Carry that to Tom ere it cools.”
It was his object to procure the servant’s absence when Lionel should arrive, resolved as he was to greet him with a sound rating for his folly.

===Chinese===

餐厅里，晚饭已经摆开，巨大的罩式壁炉里一炉大柴烧得正旺，暖意融融地漫满宽大的屋子，红光跳动着，映上四壁悬挂的兵刃陈设、挂毯，和特雷西利安家列祖列宗的画像。听见脚步声，老尼古拉斯端着一架大烛台进来，搁在桌上。
“您回来得晚哩，奥利弗爵士，”仆人说，“莱昂内尔少爷也还没家来。”
奥利弗爵士咕哝了一声，沉着脸，一脚踩碎一段柴疙瘩，湿鞋跟底下把它踩得滋滋作响。他想起马尔帕斯，心里暗骂莱昂内尔的糊涂，一言不发地解下斗篷，撂在墙边一只橡木箱上——帽子早扔在那儿了。随后他坐下，尼古拉斯上前替他褪靴子。
褪完了，老仆人直起身，奥利弗爵士简短地吩咐上晚饭。
“莱昂内尔少爷这该快到了，”他说，“先给我倒酒来，尼克。我现在顶缺的就是这个。”
“俺给您温了一壶加那利蛋酒，”尼古拉斯道，“霜天寒夜，再没有比这更受用的了，奥利弗爵士。”
他退出去，不多时便捧着一只热气腾腾、香喷喷的皮酒壶回来。只见主人还是方才那副坐相，瞪着炉火，眉头黑黑地锁着。奥利弗爵士心里想的仍是弟弟和马尔帕斯，这念头缠得这样紧，连自己的心事都一时撂下了；他正在掂量，到底该不该去劝弟弟一句。末了他叹口气站起身，挪到桌边落座，又记起那个病着的马夫，问尼古拉斯他的情形。尼古拉斯回说那人还是老样子，于是奥利弗爵士拿起一只杯子，斟得满满的，热腾腾的蛋酒直冒气。
“给他送去，”他说，“这种病痛，没有比这更好的药了。”
门外响起一阵嘚嘚的马蹄声。
“莱昂内尔少爷可算家来了。”仆人说。
“可不是，”奥利弗爵士应道，“不用等他。他要的这儿全有。趁热给汤姆送去吧。”
他存心支开仆人，好等莱昂内尔进来时屋里没有外人——他拿定主意，见了弟弟，先要为他的荒唐结结实实训他一顿。

===Original===

Reflection had brought him the assurance that this was become his duty in view of his projected absence from Penarrow; and in his brother’s interest he was determined not to spare him.
He took a deep draught of the posset, and as he set it down he heard Lionel’s step without. Then the door was flung open, and his brother stood on the threshold a moment at gaze.
Sir Oliver looked round with a scowl, the well-considered reproof already on his lips.
“So.﻿ ﻿…” he began, and got no further. The sight that met his eyes drove the ready words from his lips and mind; instead it was with a sharp gasp of dismay that he came immediately to his feet. “Lionel!”
Lionel lurched in, closed the door, and shot home one of its bolts. Then he leaned against it, facing his brother again. He was deathly pale, with great dark stains under his eyes; his ungloved right hand was pressed to his side, and the fingers of it were all smeared with blood that was still oozing and dripping from between them. Over his yellow doublet on the right side there was a spreading dark stain whose nature did not intrigue Sir Oliver a moment.
“My God!” he cried, and ran to his brother. “What’s happened, Lal? Who has done this?”
“Peter Godolphin,” came the answer from lips that writhed in a curious smile.
Never a word said Sir Oliver, but he set his teeth and clenched his hands until the nails cut into his palms. Then he put an arm about this lad he loved above all save one in the whole world, and with anguish in his mind he supported him forward to the fire. There Lionel dropped to the chair that Sir Oliver had lately occupied.
“What is your hurt, lad? Has it gone deep?” he asked, in terror almost.
“ ’Tis naught﻿—a flesh wound; but I have lost a mort of blood. I thought I should have been drained or ever I got me home.”
With fearful speed Sir Oliver drew his dagger and ripped away doublet, vest, and shirt, laying bare the lad’s white flesh. A moment’s examination, and he breathed more freely.
“Art a very babe, Lal,” he cried in his relief. “To ride without thought to stanch so simple a wound, and so lose all this blood﻿—bad Tressilian blood though it be.” He laughed in the immensity of his reaction from that momentary terror. “Stay thou there whilst I call Nick to help us dress this scratch.”
“No, no!” There was note of sudden fear in the lad’s voice, and his hand clutched at his brother’s sleeve.

===Chinese===

左思右想，他认定既然自己要避走佩纳罗，这番训诫便成了他的本分；为了弟弟好，他打定主意绝不含糊。
他深饮了一口蛋酒，放下杯子时，听见了门外莱昂内尔的脚步。接着门被一把推开，弟弟立在门槛上，怔怔地朝里望了片刻。
奥利弗爵士皱着眉回头，那句斟酌好的责备已然到了嘴边。
“好啊，你……”他开了腔，却再没能说下去。眼前的景象把到了嘴边的话从唇上、从心里一并轰了出去；他倒抽一口凉气，霍地站了起来。“莱昂内尔！”
莱昂内尔踉跄着进来，关上门，插上了一根门闩，然后背倚着门，重新面向哥哥。他脸色死人似的惨白，眼睛底下两大团青黑；没戴手套的右手按着腰侧，指缝间还在渗血、滴血，满手糊着血污。他黄色紧身上衣的右襟上，一团黑渍正洇开——那是什么，奥利弗爵士一眼便知，不劳猜第二眼。
“我的天！”他叫出声，抢到弟弟跟前。“出什么事了，拉尔？这是谁干的？”
“彼得·戈多尔芬。”回答从唇间挤出来，那嘴唇扭出一个古怪的笑。
奥利弗爵士一言不发，只是咬紧牙关，攥紧双拳，指甲掐进了掌心。随即他伸出手臂，揽住这个他在世上除了一个人之外最爱不过的少年，怀着满心的痛楚，把他扶到炉火跟前。莱昂内尔一歪身，跌坐在奥利弗爵士方才坐的椅子里。
“伤在哪儿，孩子？深不深？”他问，几乎是惶恐了。
“不碍事——一处皮肉伤；可血淌掉了好大一摊。我还当没到家血就要流干了呢。”
奥利弗爵士抽出匕首，以可怕的手快撕开紧身上衣、背心和衬衣，露出少年白皙的皮肉。验看片刻，他才喘匀了气。
“你真是个娃娃，拉尔，”他松了口气，嚷道，“这么点小伤也不知道先裹一裹就往回骑，白白流掉这么多血——虽说流的是特雷西利安家的坏血。”他从方才那阵惊惶里回过神来，后怕之大，反叫他笑出了声。“你坐着别动，我去叫尼克来帮咱们包扎这点擦伤。”
“不，不！”少年的声音里透出突兀的惶恐，一只手死死抓住哥哥的衣袖。

===Original===

“Nick must not know. None must know, or I am undone else.”
Sir Oliver stared, bewildered. Lionel smiled again that curious twisted, rather frightened smile.
“I gave better than I took, Noll,” said he. “Master Godolphin is as cold by now as the snow on which I left him.”
His brother’s sudden start and the fixed stare from out of his slowly paling face scared Lionel a little. He observed, almost subconsciously, the dull red wheal that came into prominence as the colour faded out of Sir Oliver’s face, yet never thought to ask how it came there. His own affairs possessed him too completely.
“What’s this?” quoth Oliver at last, hoarsely.
Lionel dropped his eyes, unable longer to meet a glance that was becoming terrible.
“He would have it,” he growled almost sullenly, answering the reproach that was written in every line of his brother’s taut body. “I had warned him not to cross my path. But tonight I think some madness had seized upon him. He affronted me, Noll; he said things which it was beyond human power to endure, and.﻿ ﻿…” He shrugged to complete his sentence.
“Well, well,” said Oliver in a small voice. “First let us tend this wound of yours.”
“Do not call Nick,” was the other’s swift admonition. “Don’t you see, Noll?” he explained in answer to the inquiry of his brother’s stare, “don’t you see that we fought there almost in the dark and without witnesses. It.﻿ ﻿…” he swallowed, “it will be called murder, fair fight though it was; and should it be discovered that it was I.﻿ ﻿…” He shivered and his glance grew wild; his lips twitched.
“I see,” said Oliver, who understood at last, and he added bitterly: “You fool!”
“I had no choice,” protested Lionel. “He came at me with his drawn sword. Indeed, I think he was half-drunk. I warned him of what must happen to the other did either of us fall, but he bade me not concern myself with the fear of any such consequences to himself. He was full of foul words of me and you and all whoever bore our name. He struck me with the flat of his blade and threatened to run me through as I stood unless I drew to defend myself. What choice had I? I did not mean to kill him﻿—as God’s my witness, I did not, Noll.”
Without a word Oliver turned to a side table, where stood a metal basin and ewer. He poured water, then came in the same silence to treat his brother’s wound.

===Chinese===

“不能叫尼克知道。谁也不能知道，不然我就完了。”
奥利弗爵士瞪着眼，摸不着头脑。莱昂内尔又堆起那副古怪的、扭歪的、带着惧意的笑。
“我讨回的利息可比付出的多，诺尔，”他说，“戈多尔芬少爷这会儿已经凉了，同我撂下他的那片雪一样凉。”
哥哥蓦地一震，那张脸慢慢褪尽血色、直勾勾瞪过来的眼神，叫莱昂内尔有点发毛。他几乎是下意识地望见，奥利弗爵士脸上血色褪去时，那道暗红的鞭痕愈发凸了出来；可他压根没想起来问它是哪儿来的。他自己的事，已经把他整个人占满了。
“这是怎么说？”奥利弗爵士终于哑着嗓子开了口。
莱昂内尔垂下眼，再也受不住那道越来越怕人的逼视。
“是他自找的，”他近乎愠怒地嘟囔道，回答的是哥哥绷紧的身架里每一根线条写着的责问。“我警告过他，别挡我的道。可今晚我看他是发了失心疯。他冲撞了我，诺尔；他说的话，不是人力所能忍受的，于是……”他耸了耸肩，算是把话说完了。
“好吧，好吧，”奥利弗爵士低低地说，“先把你这伤料理了。”
“别叫尼克。”对方急急叮嘱。见哥哥瞪着眼追问，他便解释道：“你还不明白么，诺尔？我们那一场是在黑地里打的，没有见证。这……”他咽了口唾沫，“到时候会算作谋杀——虽说是一场公平决斗；可一旦查出来是我……”他打了个寒噤，眼神发了野，嘴唇直抽。
“我明白了。”奥利弗爵士说，他终于懂了，又苦涩地添了一句：“你这个傻瓜！”
“我没有选择，”莱昂内尔分辩道，“他拔着剑朝我扑过来。真的，我看他喝了个半醉。我警告过他，我们哪个倒下了，另一个会有什么下场；可他叫我别替他操心那种后果。他嘴里满是脏话，骂我、骂你、骂凡是姓我们这个姓的人。他用剑脊抽我，还威胁说，我要不拔剑自卫，他就当场把我捅个透心凉。你说我还有什么选择？我没想杀他——上帝作证，我真的没想，诺尔。”
奥利弗爵士一言不发，转身走向一张边桌，那儿摆着一只铜盆和一把水壶。他倒了水，又同样一言不发地回来，替弟弟清洗伤口。

===Original===

The tale that Lionel told made blame impossible, at least from Oliver. He had but to recall the mood in which he himself had ridden after Peter Godolphin; he had but to remember, that only the consideration of Rosamund﻿—only, indeed, the consideration of his future﻿—had set a curb upon his own bloodthirsty humour.
When he had washed the wound he fetched some table linen from a press and ripped it into strips with his dagger; he threaded out one of these and made a preliminary crisscross of the threads across the lips of the wound﻿—for the blade had gone right through the muscles of the breast, grazing the ribs; these threads would help the formation of a clot. Then with the infinite skill and cunning acquired in the course of his rovings he proceeded to the bandaging.
That done, he opened the window and flung out the blood-tinted water. The cloths with which he had mopped the wound and all other similar evidences of the treatment he cast upon the fire. He must remove all traces even from the eyes of Nicholas. He had the most implicit trust in the old servant’s fidelity. But the matter was too grave to permit of the slightest risk. He realized fully the justice of Lionel’s fears that however fair the fight might have been, a thing done thus in secret must be accounted murder by the law.
Bidding Lionel wrap himself in his cloak, Sir Oliver unbarred the door, and went upstairs in quest of a fresh shirt and doublet for his brother. On the landing he met Nicholas descending. He held him a moment in talk of the sick man above, and outwardly at least he was now entirely composed. He dispatched him upstairs again upon a trumped-up errand that must keep him absent for some little time, whilst himself he went to get the things he needed.
He returned below with them, and when he had assisted his brother into fresh garments with as little movement as possible so as not to disturb his dressing of the wound or set it bleeding afresh, he took the bloodstained doublet, vest, and shirt which he had ripped and flung them, too, into the great fire.
When some moments later Nicholas entered the vast room he found the brothers sitting composedly at table. Had he faced Lionel he would have observed little amiss with him beyond the deep pallor of his face. But he did not even do so much.

===Chinese===

莱昂内尔讲的这一套，叫人无从责备——至少奥利弗爵士责备不得。他只消回想一下自己方才是揣着什么心气追赶彼得·戈多尔芬的；他只消记起，若不是顾着罗莎蒙德——说实在的，是顾着自己的前程——他自己的嗜血脾气也早收不住了。
洗净伤口后，他从柜里取了些桌布，用匕首撕成布条；又从布条里抽出一根线，在创口两缘先交十字穿上几针——剑刃是穿透了胸肌、擦着肋骨过去的，这几根线能帮着结痂。随后，凭着航海闯荡多年练下的一手精巧功夫，他动手包扎。
包扎完，他推开窗，把染血的脏水泼了出去。揩拭伤口的布片，和处置伤处留下的一切痕迹，统统投进炉火。他必须把所有痕迹抹得干干净净，连尼古拉斯的眼睛也瞒过。他对老仆人的忠心信得十足；只是这桩事太重，重到容不得一丝一毫的风险。莱昂内尔的顾虑，他掂得出其中的分量：那一战无论多么公平，既然是背着人干的，到了法司那里就只能是谋杀。
他叫莱昂内尔裹紧斗篷，自己拔了门闩，上楼去给弟弟找干净的衬衣和紧身上衣。楼梯口正撞见尼古拉斯下来。他拉着老仆人扯了几句楼上病人的情形，至少表面上已经完全恢复了镇定。他支使老仆再上楼去办一桩编造出来的差事，那差事足能绊住他一阵子，自己则去取需要的东西。
他抱着东西回到楼下，帮着弟弟换上干净衣裳，动作放得又轻又少，怕牵动包扎、惹得伤口重新出血；随后他把那件撕坏的、浸血的紧身上衣、背心和衬衣，也一并投进了大火炉。
片刻之后尼古拉斯进来，见兄弟二人神色安然地坐在桌边。他若是正眼瞧一瞧莱昂内尔，除了那满脸的苍白，也看不出什么异样。可他连这一眼也没有看。

===Original===

Lionel sat with his back to the door and the servant’s advance into the room was checked by Sir Oliver with the assurance that they did not require him. Nicholas withdrew again, and the brothers were once more alone.
Lionel ate very sparingly. He thirsted and would have emptied the measure of posset, but that Sir Oliver restrained him, and refused him anything but water lest he should contract a fever. Such a sparing meal as they made﻿—for neither had much appetite﻿—was made in silence. At last Sir Oliver rose, and with slow, heavy steps, suggestive of his humour, he crossed to the fireplace. He threw fresh logs on the blaze, and took from the tall mantelshelf his pipe and a leaden jar of tobacco. He filled the pipe pensively, then with the short iron tongs seized a fragment of glowing wood and applied it to the herb.
He returned to the table, and standing over his brother, he broke at last the silence that had now endured some time.
“What,” he asked gruffly, “was the cause of your quarrel?”
Lionel started and shrank a little; between finger and thumb he kneaded a fragment of bread, his eyes upon it. “I scarce know,” he replied.
“Lal, that is not the truth.”
“How?”
“ ’Tis not the truth. I am not to be put off with such an answer. Yourself you said that you had warned him not to cross your path. What path was in your mind?”
Lionel leaned his elbows on the table and took his head in his hands. Weak from loss of blood, overwrought mentally as well, in a state of revulsion and reaction also from the pursuit which had been the cause of tonight’s tragic affair, he had not strength to withhold the confidence his brother asked. On the contrary, it seemed to him that in making such a confidence, he would find a haven and refuge in Sir Oliver.
“ ’Twas that wanton at Malpas was the cause of all,” he complained. And Sir Oliver’s eye flashed at the words. “I deemed her quite other; I was a fool, a fool! I”﻿—he choked, and a sob shook him﻿—“I thought she loved me. I would have married her, I would so, by God.”
Sir Oliver swore softly under his breath.
“I believed her pure and good, and.﻿ ﻿…” He checked. “After all, who am I to say even now that she was not? ’Twas no fault of hers. ’Twas he, that foul dog Godolphin, who perverted her. Until he came all was well between us. And then.﻿ ﻿…”
“I see,” said Sir Oliver quietly.

===Chinese===

莱昂内尔背朝房门坐着，仆人往屋里刚一迈步，就被奥利弗爵士挡了，说他们用不着伺候。尼古拉斯又退了出去，兄弟俩重新独处。
莱昂内尔吃得极少。他口渴，本想把那杯蛋酒一饮而尽，却被奥利弗爵士拦了，除清水以外什么都不给他，怕他发起热来。这一顿寡淡的饭——两人都没什么胃口——吃得意兴阑珊，谁也不开口。末了，奥利弗爵士站起身，迈着迟缓沉重的步子，那步子正透着他的心事，踱到壁炉前。他往火里添了几段新柴，从高高的炉台架上取下烟斗和一只铅皮烟草罐。他沉沉地装了一斗烟，用短铁钳夹起一块烧红的柴禾，点燃了烟丝。
他回到桌边，立在弟弟身旁，终于打破了这盘桓许久的沉默。
“你们到底为什么吵起来？”他哑声问。
莱昂内尔一惊，微微一缩；指间捻着一小块面包，眼睛盯着它。“我也说不清。”他答。
“拉尔，这不是实话。”
“怎么？”
“这不是实话。这种话搪塞不了我。你自己说过，你早警告过他，叫别挡你的道。你心里想的是什么道？”
莱昂内尔把两肘支在桌上，双手抱住头。血亏得他身子虚，心绪也熬得紧张；再加上今晚这场祸事的根由——那一场追逐——激起的反胃和后怕，他实在没力气再把哥哥要的这番吐露扣住了。恰恰相反，他倒觉得，把这心事说给奥利弗爵士听，自己便算找到了一处避风的港。
“祸根就是马尔帕斯那个贱人。”他诉起苦来。一听这话，奥利弗爵士眼里闪过一道光。“我从前把她当成另一种人；我是个傻瓜，傻瓜！我”——他哽住了，一声抽噎震得他直抖——“我以为她爱我。我要娶她的，真的，我对天起誓。”
奥利弗爵士低低咒了一声。
“我信她贞洁善良，还有……”他顿住。“说到底，我算老几，到今天还咬定她不是？那不是她的过错。是他，是戈多尔芬那条恶狗毁了她。他没来之前，我们俩好好的。后来……”
“我明白了。”奥利弗爵士静静地说。

===Original===

“I think you have something for which to thank him, if he revealed to you the truth of that strumpet’s nature. I would have warned thee, lad. But﻿ ﻿… Perhaps I have been weak in that.”
“It was not so; it was not she.﻿ ﻿…”
“I say it was, and if I say so I am to be believed, Lionel. I’d smirch no woman’s reputation without just cause. Be very sure of that.”
Lionel stared up at him. “O God!” he cried presently, “I know not what to believe. I am a shuttlecock flung this way and that way.”
“Believe me,” said Sir Oliver grimly. “And set all doubts to rest.” Then he smiled. “So that was the virtuous Master Peter’s secret pastime, eh? The hypocrisy of man! There is no plumbing the endless depths of it!”
He laughed outright, remembering all the things that Master Peter had said of Ralph Tressilian﻿—delivering himself as though he were some chaste and self-denying anchorite. Then on that laugh he caught his breath quite suddenly. “Would she know?” he asked fearfully. “Would that harlot know, would she suspect that ’twas your hand did this?”
“Aye﻿—would she,” replied the other. “I told her tonight, when she flouted me and spoke of him, that I went straight to find him and pay the score between us. I was on my way to Godolphin Court when I came upon him in the park.”
“Then you lied to me again, Lionel. For you said ’twas he attacked you.”
“And so he did.” Lionel countered instantly. “He never gave me time to speak, but flung down from his horse and came at me snarling like a cross-grained mongrel. Oh, he was as ready for the fight as I﻿—as eager.”
“But the woman at Malpas knows,” said Sir Oliver gloomily. “And if she tells.﻿ ﻿…”
“She’ll not,” cried Lionel. “She dare not for her reputation’s sake.”
“Indeed, I think you are right,” agreed his brother with relief. “She dare not for other reasons, when I come to think of it. Her reputation is already such, and so well detested is she that were it known she had been the cause, however indirect, of this, the countryside would satisfy certain longings that it entertains concerning her. You are sure none saw you either going or returning?”
“None.”
Sir Oliver strode the length of the room and back, pulling at his pipe. “All should be well, then, I think,” said he at last. “You were best abed. I’ll carry you thither.”
He took up his stripling brother in his powerful arms and bore him upstairs as though he were a babe.

===Chinese===

“依我看，他把那娼妇的真面目揭给你看，你还该谢他才是。我本想提醒你的，孩子。可……大概在那一头，是我心软了。”
“不是那样的；不是她……”
“我说就是她；我既然说出口，你就该信，莱昂内尔。没有实据，我断不糟践一个女人的名声。这一点，你只管放心。”
莱昂内尔抬头怔怔望他。“哦，上帝！”过了一会儿他喊道，“我都不知道该信谁了。我成了个毽子，被踢过来又踢过去。”
“信我，”奥利弗爵士斩钉截铁地说，“把疑心全撂下。”随后他笑了。“原来这就是那位道学的彼得少爷私底下的消遣，嗯？人的假道学！真真是深不见底，探不到底啊！”
他想起彼得少爷数说拉尔夫·特雷西利安的那些话——说得自己仿佛是个持斋苦修的隐士——不由放声大笑。笑到一半，他猛地倒抽一口气，屏住了。“她会不会知道？”他惊惶地问，“那个淫妇会不会知道、会不会疑心，这事儿是你的手笔？”
“会——她会。”对方答道。“今晚她讥诮我、夸他的当口，我对她说了，我这就去找他，把我们之间的账清了。我正往戈多尔芬宅去，就在园子里撞上了他。”
“那你又对我撒了一回谎，莱昂内尔。你方才说，是他先动手攻击你。”
“本来就是他先动的手。”莱昂内尔应声反驳，“他半句不容我分说，翻身下马，像条恶狗似的狂吠着朝我扑来。哦，他跟我一样巴不得打这一场——一样急。”
“可马尔帕斯那个女人知道哇，”奥利弗爵士郁郁地说，“她要是说出去……”
“她不会的，”莱昂内尔嚷道，“为她自己的名声，她也不敢。”
“可不是，我看你说得对，”哥哥松了口气，附和道，“细想起来，为着别的原因她也不敢。她的名声本来就已经那样，恨她的人又那样多；一旦叫人知道这场事的根由——哪怕只是拐着弯的——是她，乡里人积在心里的那些怨气，可就要在她身上出了。你来去的路上，确定没被人瞧见？”
“没有。”
奥利弗爵士在屋里大步走了个来回，抽着烟斗。“那么，我想，该是万全的了，”他末了说，“你最好上床躺着。我抱你上去。”
他伸出有力的双臂，托起他那个细胳膊细腿的弟弟，像抱个婴孩似的，把他抱上了楼。

===Original===

When he had seen him safely disposed for slumber, he returned below, shut the door in the hall, drew up the great oaken chair to the fire, and sat there far into the night smoking and thinking.
He had said to Lionel that all should be well. All should be well for Lionel. But what of himself with the burden of this secret on his soul? Were the victim another than Rosamund’s brother the matter would have plagued him but little. The fact that Godolphin was slain, it must be confessed, was not in itself the source of his oppression. Godolphin had more than deserved his end, and he would have come by it months ago at Sir Oliver’s own hand but for the fact that he was Rosamund’s brother, as we know. There was the rub, the bitter, cruel rub. Her own brother had fallen by the hand of his. She loved her brother more than any living being next to himself, just as he loved Lionel above any other but herself. The pain that must be hers he knew; he experienced some of it in anticipation, participating it because it was hers and because all things that were hers he must account in some measure his own.
He rose up at last, cursing that wanton at Malpas who had come to fling this fresh and terrible difficulty where already he had to face so many. He stood leaning upon the overmantel, his foot upon one of the dogs of the fender, and considered what to do. He must bear his burden in silence, that was all. He must keep this secret even from Rosamund. It split his heart to think that he must practise this deceit with her. But naught else was possible short of relinquishing her, and that was far beyond his strength.
The resolve adopted, he took up a taper and went off to bed.

===Chinese===

安置弟弟睡妥之后，他回到楼下，关上厅门，把那把大橡木椅拖到炉火前，在那里枯坐到深夜，一口一口抽烟，一桩一桩地想。
他对莱昂内尔说，该是万全的了。对莱昂内尔，该是万全的了。可他自己呢——这桩秘密压在心口，他怎么办？倘若死的不是罗莎蒙德的哥哥，这件事原烦不着他多少。说句实话，压得他喘不过气的，并不是戈多尔芬死了这件事本身。戈多尔芬横死，是他咎由自取；若不是他是罗莎蒙德的哥哥，正如我们所知，几个月前他早死在奥利弗爵士自己手里了。难就难在这里，难就难在这又苦又狠的一道坎上：她的亲哥哥，死在他亲哥哥手里。这世上除了他自己，她最爱便是她的哥哥；正同他除了她，最爱便是莱昂内尔一样。她将尝到怎样的痛，他清楚；那痛他已先替她尝到了几分——只因那是她的痛，而凡是她的，他都或多或少当成自己的。
末了他站起身，咒骂马尔帕斯那个荡妇：他已经有一大堆难关要闯，她还偏要来添上这一桩又新又可怕的。他倚着炉台上架，一只脚踏着火围的铁架上，盘算该怎么办。把这副担子默然担下去，仅此而已。这秘密，连罗莎蒙德也得瞒。一想到自己竟要对她玩弄心机，他的心像撕成了两半。可除了舍弃她，别无他法——而舍弃她，远非他的力气所能做到。
主意打定，他端起一支细蜡烛，回房睡了。

## XXVI The Judgement / 审判
===Original===

To that cabin below the whole company repaired in all speed in the surgeon’s wake, Sir Oliver coming last between his guards. They assembled about the couch where Lionel lay, leaden-hued of face, his breathing laboured, his eyes dull and glazing.
Sir John ran to him, went down upon one knee to put loving arms about that chilling clay, and very gently raised him in them, and held him so resting against his breast.
“Lionel!” he cried in stricken accents. And then as if thoughts of vengeance were to soothe and comfort his sinking friend’s last moments, he added: “We have the villain fast.”
Very slowly and with obvious effort Lionel turned his head to the right, and his dull eyes went beyond Sir John and made quest in the ranks of those that stood about him.
“Oliver?” he said in a hoarse whisper. “Where is Oliver?”
“There is not the need to distress you.﻿ ﻿…” Sir John was beginning, when Lionel interrupted him.
“Wait!” he commanded in a louder tone. “Is Oliver safe?”
“I am here,” said Sir Oliver’s deep voice, and those who stood between him and his brother drew aside that they might cease from screening him.
Lionel looked at him for a long moment in silence, sitting up a little. Then he sank back again slowly against Sir John’s breast.
“God has been merciful to me a sinner,” he said, “since He accords me the means to make amends, tardily though it be.” Then he struggled up again, and held out his arms to Sir Oliver, and his voice came in a great pleading cry. “Noll! My brother! Forgive!”
Oliver advanced, none hindering until, with his hands still pinioned behind him he stood towering there above his brother, so tall that his turban brushed the low ceiling of the cabin. His countenance was stern and grim.
“What is it that you ask me to forgive?” he asked.
Lionel struggled to answer, and sank back again into Sir John’s arms, fighting for breath; there was a trace of bloodstained foam about his lips.
“Speak! Oh, speak, in God’s name!” Rosamund exhorted him from the other side, and her voice was wrung with agony.
He looked at her, and smiled faintly. “Never fear,” he whispered, “I shall speak. God has spared me to that end. Take your arms from me, Killigrew. I am the﻿ ﻿… the vilest of men. It﻿ ﻿… it was I who killed Peter Godolphin.”
“My God!” groaned Sir John, whilst Lord Henry drew a sharp breath of dismay and realization.

===Chinese===

众人跟着外科医生，急急赶到下面那间舱房，奥利弗爵士由看守夹在当中走在最后。他们围拢到那张卧榻四旁，莱昂内尔躺在那里，脸色灰败如铅，呼吸吃力，眼神呆滞，正渐渐失了光。
约翰爵士抢到他跟前，单膝跪倒，伸出疼爱的臂膀兜住那具正在冷却的躯壳，极轻极轻地把他拢起来，让他靠在自己胸膛上。
“莱昂内尔！”他悲声喊道。接着，仿佛想到复仇的许诺能给他垂死的朋友最后一点安抚和慰藉，又添了一句：“那恶棍已经拿住了。”
莱昂内尔缓慢地、显而易见地费着力气，把头转向右边，呆滞的眼睛越过约翰爵士，在围立的人们中间搜寻。
“奥利弗？”他嘶哑地低声问，“奥利弗在哪儿？”
“犯不上叫你伤心……”约翰爵士刚开口，莱昂内尔便打断了他。
“等等！”他放高声音命令道，“奥利弗平安么？”
“我在这里。”奥利弗爵士低沉的嗓音响起，挡在他和弟弟之间的人们闪开身，不再遮蔽他。
莱昂内尔微微撑起身，默然望着他，望了许久。随后又缓缓倒回约翰爵士怀里。
“上帝怜悯了我这个罪人，”他说，“祂竟还给我一个补赎的机会，虽然迟了。”接着他又挣扎着撑起来，朝奥利弗伸出双臂，嗓音化作一声大大的、哀恳的呼喊。“诺尔！我的哥哥！饶恕！”
奥利弗上前，无人阻拦，直到他负着反缚的双手、巍巍立在弟弟上方——他那样高，头巾几乎扫着舱室低矮的顶篷。他的面容严峻而冷厉。
“你要我饶恕什么？”他问。
莱昂内尔挣扎着想答话，又倒回约翰爵士臂弯里喘息；他唇边浮着一线带血的沫子。
“说呀！哦，看在上帝份上，说呀！”罗莎蒙德在另一侧催他，声音痛苦得绞紧了。
他望向她，淡淡一笑。“别怕，”他悄声道，“我会说的。上帝留我一口气，正为的这个。放开我，基利格鲁。我是……是天下最卑污的人。是……是我杀了彼得·戈多尔芬。”
“我的上帝！”约翰爵士呻吟出声，亨利勋爵猛抽一口气，又是惊骇，又是恍然。

===Original===

“Ah, but that is not my sin,” Lionel continued. “There was no sin in that. We fought, and in self-defence I slew him﻿—fighting fair. My sin came afterwards. When suspicion fell on Oliver, I nourished it﻿ ﻿… Oliver knew the deed was mine, and kept silent that he might screen me. I feared the truth might become known for all that﻿ ﻿… and﻿ ﻿… and I was jealous of him, and﻿ ﻿… and I had him kidnapped to be sold.﻿ ﻿…”
His fading voice trailed away into silence. A cough shook him, and the faint crimson foam on his lips was increased. But he rallied again, and lay there panting, his fingers plucking at the coverlet.
“Tell them,” said Rosamund, who in her desperate fight for Sir Oliver’s life kept her mind cool and steady and directed towards essentials, “tell them the name of the man you hired to kidnap him.”
“Jasper Leigh, the skipper of the *Swallow*,” he answered, whereupon she flashed upon Lord Henry a look that contained a gleam of triumph for all that her face was ashen and her lips trembled.
Then she turned again to the dying man, relentlessly almost in her determination to extract all vital truth from him ere he fell silent.
“Tell them,” she bade him, “under what circumstances Sir Oliver sent you last night to the *Silver Heron*.”
“Nay, there is no need to harass him,” Lord Henry interposed. “He has said enough already. May God forgive us our blindness, Killigrew!”
Sir John bowed his head in silence over Lionel.
“Is it you, Sir John?” whispered the dying man. “What? Still there? Ha!” he seemed to laugh faintly, then checked. “I am going.﻿ ﻿…” he muttered, and again his voice grew stronger, obeying the last flicker of his shrinking will. “Noll! I am going! I﻿ ﻿… I have made reparation﻿ ﻿… all that I could. Give me﻿ ﻿… give me thy hand!” Gropingly he put forth his right.
“I should have given it you ere this but that my wrists are bound,” cried Oliver in a sudden frenzy. And then exerting that colossal strength of his, he suddenly snapped the cords that pinioned him as if they had been thread. He caught his brother’s extended hand, and dropped upon his knees beside him. “Lionel﻿ ﻿… Boy!” he cried. It was as if all that had befallen in the last five years had been wiped out of existence. His fierce relentless hatred of his half-brother, his burning sense of wrong, his parching thirst for vengeance, became on the instant all dead, buried, and forgotten.

===Chinese===

“啊，可那不是我的罪，”莱昂内尔续道，“那上头没有罪。我们交了手，我是自卫杀的他——公平交手。我的罪在后头。嫌疑落到奥利弗身上时，是我把它养大的……奥利弗早知道那件事是我干的，却缄口不言，为的是庇护我。我还是怕真相终究败露……还有……还有我嫉妒他，于是……于是我雇人绑了他去卖……”
他渐弱的声音拖成了寂静。一阵咳嗽撼动他，唇边淡红的沫子又添了些。可他再度回过神来，躺在那里喘息，手指揪着床单。
“告诉他们，”罗莎蒙德说——她为了奥利弗爵士的性命作殊死之战，头脑反倒冷静坚定，只往要害上引——“把你雇来绑他的人的名字告诉他们。”
“贾斯珀·利，‘燕子号’的船长。”他答道。她立刻向亨利勋爵闪去一眼，眼里含着一丝得胜的光，尽管她面如死灰、双唇打颤。
随后她重新俯向那个垂死的人，那份执意几乎近于无情——定要趁他还能出声，把所有紧要的真相榨出来。
“告诉他们，”她吩咐道，“昨夜奥利弗爵士打发你上‘银鹭号’去，是怎么一回事。”
“不，犯不着再逼他了，”亨利勋爵插言道，“他说得够多了。愿上帝宽恕我们的瞎眼，基利格鲁！”
约翰爵士低头对着莱昂内尔，默然无语。
“是您么，约翰爵士？”垂死的人悄声问。“怎么？还在？哈！”他似乎淡淡笑了一声，随即止住。“我要去了……”他喃喃道，随后那声音竟又壮了起来，听命于他那节节败退的意志最后一次的闪耀。“诺尔！我要去了！我……我已经补赎了……尽我所能了。把手……把手给我！”他摸索着伸出右手。
“若不是双腕绑着，这只手早给你了！”奥利弗狂喊一声。随即他鼓起那一身神力，猛然崩断缚腕的绳索，仿佛那不过是线。他一把攥住弟弟伸来的手，跪倒在他身旁。“莱昂内尔……孩子！”他喊道。这五年间发生的一切，仿佛霎时从天地间抹去了。他对这位异母弟弟那股凶狠不熄的恨、那腔灼痛的冤屈、那灼人的复仇渴火，霎时间尽数死去、埋葬、遗忘了。

===Original===

More, it was as if they had never been. Lionel in that moment was again the weak, comely, beloved brother whom he had cherished and screened and guarded, and for whom when the hour arrived he had sacrificed his good name, and the woman he loved, and placed his life itself in jeopardy.
“Lionel, boy!” was all that for a moment he could say. Then: “Poor lad! Poor lad!” he added. “Temptation was too strong for thee.” And reaching forth he took the other white hand that lay beyond the couch, and so held both tight-clasped within his own.
From one of the ports a ray of sunshine was creeping upwards towards the dying man’s face. But the radiance that now overspread it was from an inward source. Feebly he returned the clasp of his brother’s hands.
“Oliver, Oliver!” he whispered. “There is none like thee! I ever knew thee as noble as I was base. Have I said enough to make you safe? Say that he will be safe now,” he appealed to the others, “that no.﻿ ﻿…”
“He will be safe,” said Lord Henry stoutly. “My word on’t.”
“It is well. The past is past. The future is in your hands, Oliver. God’s blessing on’t.” He seemed to collapse, to rally yet again. He smiled pensively, his mind already wandering. “That was a long swim last night﻿—the longest I ever swam. From Penarrow to Trefusis﻿—a fine long swim. But you were with me, Noll. Had my strength given out﻿ ﻿… I could have depended on you. I am still chill from it, for it was cold﻿ ﻿… cold﻿ ﻿… ugh!” He shuddered, and lay still.
Gently Sir John lowered him to his couch. Beyond it Rosamund fell upon her knees and covered her face, whilst by Sir John’s side Oliver continued to kneel, clasping in his own his brother’s chilling hands.
There ensued a long spell of silence. Then with a heavy sigh Sir Oliver folded Lionel’s hands across his breast, and slowly, heavily rose to his feet.
The others seemed to take this for a signal. It was as if they had but waited mute and still out of deference to Oliver. Lord Henry moved softly round to Rosamund and touched her lightly upon the shoulder. She rose and went out in the wake of the others, Lord Henry following her, and none remaining but the surgeon.
Outside in the sunshine they checked. Sir John stood with bent head and hunched shoulders, his eyes upon the white deck. Timidly almost﻿—a thing never seen before in this bold man﻿—he looked at Sir Oliver.

===Chinese===

岂止如此——仿佛它们从来不曾有过。此刻的莱昂内尔，又是那个柔弱、俊美、惹人怜爱的弟弟，那个他一贯疼着、护着、守着的人；时辰一到，他为他牺牲了清名，牺牲了他心爱的女人，还把性命本身押了上去。
“莱昂内尔，孩子！”半晌，他只说得出这一句。随后又添道：“可怜的孩子！可怜的孩子！那诱惑太强，你受不住啊。”他探身过去，握住榻那边放着的那只苍白的手，把两只手一齐紧紧攥在自己掌中。
一舷窗里漏进一线阳光，正缓缓爬向垂死者的脸。而此刻罩上那张脸的光辉，却来自更里面的地方。他弱弱地回握着哥哥的手。
“奥利弗，奥利弗！”他低语道，“没有人比得上你！我从来就知道，你怎样高贵，我就怎样卑污。我说的话，够保你平安了么？说他这下平安了，”他向众人恳求道，“说再没有……”
“他会平安的，”亨利勋爵慨然道，“我以人格担保。”
“那就好了。过去的，都过去了。将来，交在你手里了，奥利弗。愿上帝赐福。”他仿佛垮了下去，又挣扎着回转来。他怅然微笑着，神志已经开始飘了。“昨夜那一趟游水真远——我这辈子游得最远的一趟。从佩纳罗到特雷富西斯——好长一趟水。可你陪着我呢，诺尔。我的力气要是断了……我尽可以指望你。我到现在还冷着呢，水里冷……冷……”他一哆嗦，静了下来。
约翰爵士轻轻把他放平在卧榻上。榻那旁，罗莎蒙德双膝跪倒，掩住了脸；约翰爵士身侧，奥利弗仍旧跪着，攥着弟弟那双渐冷的手，合在自己手中。
长长一段静默。末了，奥利弗爵士沉沉一声叹息，把莱昂内尔的双手交叠在他胸前，缓慢而沉重地站起身来。
旁人似乎把这当作了信号，仿佛方才那一阵屏息静立，全是出于对他的敬重。亨利勋爵轻轻绕到罗莎蒙德身边，在她肩上碰了一碰。她起身，随众人出去，亨利勋爵跟在后面；留下的只有外科医生。
外面阳光底下，他们停了步。约翰爵士垂着头、缩着肩站着，眼睛盯着雪白的甲板。几乎是怯怯地——这在这条硬汉是从未见过的——他望向奥利弗爵士。

===Original===

“He was my friend,” he said sorrowfully, and as if to excuse and explain himself, “and﻿ ﻿… and I was misled through love of him.”
“He was my brother,” replied Sir Oliver solemnly. “God rest him!”
Sir John, resolved, drew himself up into an attitude preparatory to receiving with dignity a rebuff should it be administered him.
“Can you find it in your generosity, sir, to forgive me?” he asked, and his air was almost one of challenge.
Silently Sir Oliver held out his hand. Sir John fell upon it almost in eagerness.
“We are like to be neighbours again,” he said, “and I give you my word I shall strive to be a more neighbourly one than in the past.”
“Then, sirs,” said Sir Oliver, looking from Sir John to Lord Henry, “I am to understand that I am no longer a prisoner.”
“You need not hesitate to return with us to England, Sir Oliver,” replied his lordship. “The Queen shall hear your story, and we have Jasper Leigh to confirm it if need be, and I will go warranty for your complete reinstatement. Count me your friend, Sir Oliver, I beg.” And he, too, held out his hand. Then turning to the others: “Come, sirs,” he said, “we have duties elsewhere, I think.”
They tramped away, leaving Oliver and Rosamund alone. The twain looked long each at the other. There was so much to say, so much to ask, so much to explain, that neither knew with what words to begin. Then Rosamund suddenly came up to him, holding out her hands. “Oh, my dear!” she said, and that, after all, summed up a deal.
One or two over-inquisitive seamen, lounging on the forecastle and peeping through the shrouds, were disgusted to see the lady of Godolphin Court in the arms of a beturbaned bare-legged follower of Mahound.

===Chinese===

“他是我的朋友，”他悲戚地说，又像是为自己开脱、辩白，“而我……而我是因为爱他，才叫人领错了路。”
“他是我的弟弟，”奥利弗爵士庄重地答道，“愿上帝让他安息！”
约翰爵士横下心，挺直了身子，摆出一副从容受教的架势——倘若对方真要给他一顿没脸。
“您大人大量，先生，”他问，那神气倒近乎挑战，“可能宽恕我？”
奥利弗爵士默默伸出手去。约翰爵士几乎是急切地一把攥住。
“看来咱们又要做邻居了，”他说，“我向您保证：往后我定尽力，做一个比从前更够意思的邻居。”
“那么，诸位，”奥利弗爵士从约翰爵士望到亨利勋爵，“我可以认为，我不再是囚徒了。”
“您尽管放心同我们一起回英格兰，奥利弗爵士，”勋爵大人答道，“您的经历女王陛下自会听取；倘有需要，还有贾斯珀·利作证；至于您恢复名誉、官复原职一节，我一力承保。请把我当作您的朋友，奥利弗爵士，我恳求您。”他也伸出手来。随后他转向众人：“来吧，诸位，”他说，“我想，别处还有咱们的差事。”
他们沉重的脚步声远去，只留下奥利弗和罗莎蒙德。两人久久相望。要说的那么多，要问的那么多，要解释的那么多，反而谁也不知从哪个字起头。末了，罗莎蒙德忽然抢上前来，朝他伸出双手。“哦，我亲爱的！”她说——这一句，到底抵过了千言万语。
一两个过分好奇的水手，正在前舱上闲倚着、从桅索缝里张望，看到戈多尔芬宅的小姐竟扑进一个裹头巾、赤着腿的穆罕默德信徒怀里，登时倒尽了胃口。

## XXIV The Judges / 审判官
===Original===

In the absence of any woman into whose care they might entrust her, Lord Henry, Sir John, and Master Tobias, the ship’s surgeon, had amongst them tended Rosamund as best they could when numbed and half-dazed she was brought aboard the *Silver Heron*.
Master Tobias had applied such rude restoratives as he commanded, and having made her as comfortable as possible upon a couch in the spacious cabin astern, he had suggested that she should be allowed the rest of which she appeared so sorely to stand in need. He had ushered out the commander and the Queen’s Lieutenant, and himself had gone below to a still more urgent case that was demanding his attention﻿—that of Lionel Tressilian, who had been brought limp and unconscious from the *galeasse* together with some four other wounded members of the *Silver Heron*’s crew.
At dawn Sir John had come below, seeking news of his wounded friend. He found the surgeon kneeling over Lionel.
As he entered, Master Tobias turned aside, rinsed his hands in a metal basin placed upon the floor, and rose wiping them on a napkin.
“I can do no more, Sir John,” he muttered in a desponding voice. “He is sped.”
“Dead, d’ye mean?” cried Sir John, a catch in his voice.
The surgeon tossed aside the napkin, and slowly drew down the upturned sleeves of his black doublet. “All but dead,” he answered. “The wonder is that any spark of life should still linger in a body with that hole in it. He is bleeding inwardly, and his pulse is steadily weakening. It must continue so until imperceptibly he passes away. You may count him dead already, Sir John.” He paused. “A merciful, painless end,” he added, and sighed perfunctorily, his pale shaven face decently grave, for all that such scenes as these were commonplaces in his life. “Of the other four,” he continued, “Blair is dead; the other three should all recover.”
But Sir John gave little heed to the matter of those others. His grief and dismay at this quenching of all hope for his friend precluded any other consideration at the moment.
“And he will not even recover consciousness?” he asked insisting, although already he had been answered.
“As I have said, you may count him dead already, Sir John. My skill can do nothing for him.”
Sir John’s head drooped, his countenance drawn and grave. “Nor can my justice,” he added gloomily. “Though it avenge him, it cannot give me back my friend.”

===Chinese===

船上没有可以托付她的女眷，罗莎蒙德麻木失魂地被带上“银鹭号”后，便由亨利勋爵、约翰爵士和船上的外科医生托比亚斯少爷三个人，尽力照看了她一阵。
托比亚斯少爷使出了他手头那些简陋的提神药，又把她在船尾宽敞的舱房里安置得尽量舒坦，随后便提议：她显然亟需休息，该让她静养。他把舰长和女王副官送出舱去，自己则下到更急的病人那里——莱昂内尔·特雷西利安，他是同“银鹭号”水手中另外四名伤员一道，软塌塌、不省人事地从那艘桨帆船上抬过来的。
天亮时分，约翰爵士下来探问伤友的消息，见外科医生正跪在莱昂内尔身旁。
他一进门，托比亚斯少爷便侧过身，在置于地上的一只铜盆里净了手，站起来在餐巾上擦着。
“我无能为力了，约翰爵士，”他丧气地嘟囔道，“他不行了。”
“你是说，死了？”约翰爵士喊道，嗓音一哽。
外科医生把餐巾一丢，慢慢放下黑色紧身上衣挽起的袖子。“差不多是死了，”他答道，“奇怪的是，身上带那么一个窟窿，居然还残着一线生气。他在内出血，脉息越来越弱。只能这样下去，直到他不知不觉地咽气。您现在就可以把他当作死人了，约翰爵士。”他顿了顿。“也算一场仁慈的、没有痛苦的死，”他添了一句，敷衍地叹口气，那张刮得净白的脸上带着得体的凝重——这样的场面，在他一生里原是家常便饭。“另外四个里，”他接着说，“布莱尔死了；其余三个都能好。”
可约翰爵士顾不上旁人。朋友生还的最后一点指望就此熄灭，悲恸与惊惶把别的念头一时全堵在了门外。
“他连知觉都恢复不了了？”他还问，虽然已经得了答复。
“我说过了，您只当他已经死了，约翰爵士。我的本事救不了他。”
约翰爵士垂下头，面容愁苦而凝重。“我的法司也救不了，”他黯然道，“它纵能替他报仇，却还不了我这位朋友。”

===Original===

He looked at the surgeon. “Vengeance, sir, is the hollowest of all the mockeries that go to make up life.”
“Your task, Sir John,” replied the surgeon, “is one of justice, not vengeance.”
“A quibble, when all is said.” He stepped to Lionel’s side, and looked down at the pale handsome face over which the dark shadows of death were already creeping. “If he would but speak in the interests of this justice that is to do! If we might but have the evidence of his own words, lest I should ever be asked to justify the hanging of Oliver Tressilian.”
“Surely, sir,” the surgeon ventured, “there can be no such question ever. Mistress Rosamund’s word alone should suffice, if indeed so much as that even were required.”
“Ay! His offenses against God and man are too notorious to leave grounds upon which any should ever question my right to deal with him out of hand.”
There was a tap at the door and Sir John’s own body servant entered with the announcement that Mistress Rosamund was asking urgently to see him.
“She will be impatient for news of him,” Sir John concluded, and he groaned. “My God! How am I to tell her? To crush her in the very hour of her deliverance with such news as this! Was ever irony so cruel?” He turned, and stepped heavily to the door. There he paused. “You will remain by him to the end?” he bade the surgeon interrogatively.
Master Tobias bowed. “Of course, Sir John.” And he added, “ ’Twill not be long.”
Sir John looked across at Lionel again﻿—a glance of valediction. “God rest him!” he said hoarsely, and passed out.
In the waist he paused a moment, turned to a knot of lounging seamen, and bade them throw a halter over the yardarm, and hale the renegade Oliver Tressilian from his prison. Then with slow heavy step and heavier heart he went up the companion to the vessel’s castellated poop.
The sun, new risen in a faint golden haze, shone over a sea faintly rippled by the fresh clean winds of dawn to which their every stitch of canvas was now spread. Away on the larboard quarter, a faint cloudy outline, was the coast of Spain.
Sir John’s long sallow face was preternaturally grave when he entered the cabin, where Rosamund awaited him. He bowed to her with a grave courtesy, doffing his hat and casting it upon a chair.

===Chinese===

他看看外科医生。“复仇，先生，是拼成人生的那一切虚妄里，最空的一场虚妄。”
“您的差事，约翰爵士，”外科医生答道，“是执法，不是复仇。”
“说到底，不过是咬文嚼字。”他踱到莱昂内尔床边，低头望着那张苍白英俊的脸，死亡的暗影已经在上面悄悄爬开。“他要是肯为这场即将施行的公道开口该多好！我们要是能拿到他亲口的话作凭据，免得日后有人要我为绞死奥利弗·特雷西利安给出说法。”
“想来不会有人问这个的，先生，”外科医生大着胆子说，“单凭罗莎蒙德小姐一句话就尽够了——倘若当真连这一句都用得着的话。”
“是啊！他触犯天条和人法的罪孽天下皆知，谁也寻不出由头，来质疑我就地处置他的权柄。”
门上叩了一响，约翰爵士的贴身仆人进来禀报：罗莎蒙德小姐急求一见。
“她准是急着打听他的消息，”约翰爵士推断道，随即呻吟了一声。“我的上帝！叫我怎么对她说？在她脱难的时辰，拿这样的消息去砸她！天底下可有这样狠的反讽？”他转过身，拖着沉重的步子朝门口走去，到那儿又停了停。“您守到他最后？”他问外科医生。
托比亚斯少爷躬了躬身。“那是自然，约翰爵士。”他又添一句，“用不了多久了。”
约翰爵士又望了莱昂内尔一眼——诀别的一眼。“愿上帝让他安息！”他哑声道，随后出了门。
到了船腰，他略停了停，吩咐一群闲站着的水手：在桁端上挂一条绞索，把那个叛教者奥利弗·特雷西利安从监牢里提出来。然后他迈着迟缓沉重的步子、揣着更沉重的心，登上扶梯，走向船尾那城堡似的艉楼。
初升的太阳笼着一层淡淡的金霭，照着清晨新鲜干净的风吹起细纹的海面；船帆一一是张满了的。左舷后方远处，一带朦朦胧胧的轮廓，是西班牙海岸。
约翰爵士走进舱房时，那张蜡黄的长脸出奇地凝重。罗莎蒙德正在里面等他。他郑重而有礼地向她一躬，摘下帽子，撂在椅子上。

===Original===

The last five years had brought some strands of white into his thick black hair, and at the temples in particular it showed very grey, giving him an appearance of age to which the deep lines in his brow contributed.
He advanced towards her, as she rose to receive him.
“Rosamund, my dear!” he said gently, and took both her hands. He looked with eyes of sorrow and concern into her white, agitated face. “Are you sufficiently rested, child?”
“Rested?” she echoed on a note of wonder that he should suppose it.
“Poor lamb, poor lamb!” he murmured, as a mother might have done, and drew her towards him, stroking that gleaming auburn head. “We’ll speed us back to England with every stitch of canvas spread. Take heart then, and.﻿ ﻿…”
But she broke in impetuously, drawing away from him as she spoke, and his heart sank with foreboding of the thing she was about to inquire.
“I overheard a sailor just now saying to another that it is your intent to hang Sir Oliver Tressilian out of hand﻿—this morning.”
He misunderstood her utterly. “Be comforted,” he said. “My justice shall be swift; my vengeance sure. The yardarm is charged already with the rope on which he shall leap to his eternal punishment.”
She caught her breath, and set a hand upon her bosom as if to repress its sudden tumult.
“And upon what grounds,” she asked him with an air of challenge, squarely facing him, “do you intend to do this thing?”
“Upon what grounds?” he faltered. He stared and frowned, bewildered by her question and its tone. “Upon what grounds?” he repeated, foolishly almost in the intensity of his amazement. Then he considered her more closely, and the wildness of her eyes bore to him slowly an explanation of words that at first had seemed beyond explaining.
“I see!” he said in a voice of infinite pity; for the conviction to which he had leapt was that her poor wits were all astray after the horrors through which she had lately travelled. “You must rest,” he said gently, “and give no thought to such matters as these. Leave them to me, and be very sure that I shall avenge you as is due.”
“Sir John, you mistake me, I think. I do not desire that you avenge me. I have asked you upon what grounds you intend to do this thing, and you have not answered me.”
In increasing amazement he continued to stare. He had been wrong, then. She was quite sane and mistress of her wits.

===Chinese===

五年过去，他浓黑头发里已掺了几缕白，两鬓尤其显得灰，平添了几分老态，额上深深的皱纹也来帮衬。
她起身相迎，他朝她走去。
“罗莎蒙德，我亲爱的！”他柔声说，握住她双手。他带着哀伤与关切，望着她苍白激动的脸。“歇过来了么，孩子？”
“歇？”她应了一声，奇怪他竟会这么想。
“可怜的羔羊，可怜的羔羊！”他喃喃道，像一位母亲那样，把她往怀里揽了揽，抚着那头光润的栗发。“我们这就扬起满帆，全速回英格兰。打起精神来，还有……”
可她急匆匆地打断了他，一面说一面从他怀里挣开；他心往下一沉，预感到她要问什么了。
“我方才听见一个水手对另一个说，您打算即刻绞死奥利弗·特雷西利安爵士——就在今天早上。”
他全然会错了意。“放心吧，”他说，“我的法司从速，我的复仇必至。桁端上已经挂好了绳子，他要在那上头荡去他永劫的刑罚。”
她倒抽一口气，一只手按住胸口，像要压住那里陡起的翻腾。
“那您凭什么，”她摆出挑战的架势，正面对着他问，“要行这件事？”
“凭什么？”他结巴起来。他瞪着眼，皱起眉，被她的问话和口气弄糊涂了。“凭什么？”他又说了一遍，惊愕之深，几乎显出痴态。随后他把她端详得更仔细了些，她眼里那股狂乱的神色，慢慢给他送来了答案——起初他只当这话没法解释。
“我明白了！”他说，声音里含着无限的怜惜；因为他一下子得出的结论是：她可怜的神志，叫她新近经历的种种惨事搅乱了。“您得歇着，”他温言道，“这样的事想都不要想。交给我，您只管放心，我自会替您讨回该讨的公道。”
“约翰爵士，我想您误会我了。我并不要您替我讨什么公道。我问的是，您凭什么行这件事，而您还没有答我。”
他愈发惊愕地瞪着她。看来他是想错了：她神志清楚得很，清醒自主。

===Original===

And yet instead of the fond inquiries concerning Lionel which he had been dreading came this amazing questioning of his grounds to hang his prisoner.
“Need I state to you﻿—of all living folk﻿—the offences which that dastard has committed?” he asked, expressing thus the very question that he was setting himself.
“You need to tell me,” she answered, “by what right you constitute yourself his judge and executioner; by what right you send him to his death in this peremptory fashion, without trial.” Her manner was as stern as if she were invested with all the authority of a judge.
“But you,” he faltered in his ever-growing bewilderment, “you, Rosamund, against whom he has offended so grievously, surely you should be the last to ask me such a question! Why, it is my intention to proceed with him as is the manner of the sea with all knaves taken as Oliver Tressilian was taken. If your mood be merciful towards him﻿—which as God lives, I can scarce conceive﻿—consider that this is the greatest mercy he can look for.”
“You speak of mercy and vengeance in a breath, Sir John.” She was growing calm, her agitation was quieting and a grim sternness was replacing it.
He made a gesture of impatience. “What good purpose could it serve to take him to England?” he demanded. “There he must stand his trial, and the issue is foregone. It were unnecessarily to torture him.”
“The issue may be none so foregone as you suppose,” she replied. “And that trial is his right.”
Sir John took a turn in the cabin, his wits all confused.
It was preposterous that he should stand and argue upon such a matter with Rosamund of all people, and yet she was compelling him to it against his every inclination, against common sense itself.
“If he so urges it, we’ll not deny him,” he said at last, deeming it best to humour her. “We’ll take him back to England if he demands it, and let him stand his trial there. But Oliver Tressilian must realize too well what is in store for him to make any such demand.” He passed before her, and held out his hands in entreaty. “Come, Rosamund, my dear! You are distraught, you.﻿ ﻿…”
“I am indeed distraught, Sir John,” she answered, and took the hands that he extended. “Oh, have pity!” she cried with a sudden change to utter intercession. “I implore you to have pity!”
“What pity can I show you, child? You have but to name.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

可他等来的，不是他一直发怵的那些关于莱昂内尔的缠人追问，而是这番惊人的、质问他绞死囚徒之权的盘问。
“普天之下的人里，难道还偏要我说给你听那懦夫都犯下了什么罪？”他问，把他自问的正是这一句说了出来。
“您要告诉我的，”她答道，“是您凭什么权，自任他的法官和行刑人；凭什么权，这样不容分说、不经审讯就送他去死。”她的神气严峻，俨然一位执掌全副法权的大法官。
“可是您，”他在节节上涨的惶惑里结结巴巴，“您，罗莎蒙德，他冒犯您冒犯得那样狠，偏偏该是您，最不该问我这句话！哼，我打主意照海上的规矩处置他——凡是像奥利弗·特雷西利安这样被拿住的无赖，海上的规矩便是如此。您若是对他动了菩萨心肠——老天在上，我简直想不出您怎么会——那您想想，这已经是他能指望的最大的慈悲了。”
“您一口气又说慈悲又说报仇，约翰爵士。”她渐渐平静下来，激动退了，换上一副冷峻的严峻。
他不耐烦地一挥手。“押他回英格兰顶什么用？”他质问道，“在那儿他得上法庭，结局早定了。那不过是平白多折磨他一场。”
“结局未必像您想的那么早定，”她答道，“再说，受审是他的权利。”
约翰爵士在舱里踱了个来回，心思全乱了。
跟罗莎蒙德——偏偏是跟她——站在一处抬这样的杠，岂有此理；可她逼得他不能不抬，违着他全部的心意，也违着常识本身。
“他要是自己这么要求，我们不至于不答应，”他末了说，觉得还是顺着她些好。“他要是要押回英格兰，就押回去，让他在那儿受审。可奥利弗·特雷西利安心里太清楚等着他的是什么，断不会提这个要求。”他踱到她跟前，恳求地伸出双手。“好了，罗莎蒙德，我亲爱的！您是伤心迷了心窍，您……”
“我确实是伤心迷了心窍，约翰爵士，”她答道，握住他伸来的手。“哦，发发慈悲吧！”她忽然一变，语气成了全然的哀恳。“我求您发发慈悲！”
“我能给您什么慈悲呢，孩子？您只管开口……”

===Original===

“ ’Tis not pity for me, but pity for him that I am beseeching of you.”
“For him?” he cried, frowning again.
“For Oliver Tressilian.”
He dropped her hands and stood away. “God’s light!” he swore. “You sue for pity for Oliver Tressilian, for that renegade, that incarnate devil? Oh, you are mad!” he stormed. “Mad!” and he flung away from her, whirling his arms.
“I love him,” she said simply.
That answer smote him instantly still. Under the shock of it he just stood and stared at her again, his jaw fallen.
“You love him!” he said at last below his breath. “You love him! You love a man who is a pirate, a renegade, the abductor of yourself and of Lionel, the man who murdered your brother!”
“He did not.” She was fierce in her denial of it. “I have learnt the truth of that matter.”
“From his lips, I suppose?” said Sir John, and he was unable to repress a sneer. “And you believed him?”
“Had I not believed him I should not have married him.”
“Married him?” Sudden horror came now to temper his bewilderment. Was there to be no end to these astounding revelations? Had they reached the climax yet, he wondered, or was there still more to come? “You married that infamous villain?” he asked, and his voice was expressionless.
“I did﻿—in Algiers on the night we landed there.”
He stood gaping at her whilst a man might count to a dozen, and then abruptly he exploded. “It is enough!” he roared, shaking a clenched fist at the low ceiling of the cabin. “It is enough, as God’s my Witness. If there were no other reason to hang him, that would be reason and to spare. You may look to me to make an end of this infamous marriage within the hour.”
“Ah, if you will but listen to me!” she pleaded.
“Listen to you?” He paused by the door to which he had stepped in his fury, intent upon giving the word that there and then should make an end, and summoning Oliver Tressilian before him, announce his fate to him and see it executed on the spot. “Listen to you?” he repeated, scorn and anger blending in his voice. “I have heard more than enough already!”
It was the Killigrew way, Lord Henry Goade assures us, pausing here at long length for one of those digressions into the history of families whose members chance to impinge upon his chronicle.

===Chinese===

“我求的不是给我的慈悲，是给他的。”
“给他？”他嚷起来，又皱起了眉。
“给奥利弗·特雷西利安。”
他撒开她的手，退开一步。“老天作证！”他骂道，“您替奥利弗·特雷西利安求慈悲？替那个叛教者、那个魔鬼的化身？哦，您疯了！”他勃然大怒。“疯了！”他抡着胳膊，从她面前甩身走开。
“我爱他。”她简单地说。
这一句立时把他钉在了原地。他受了这一击，只是站在那里，瞪着她，下巴都耷拉了下来。
“您爱他！”他末了压着嗓子说。“您爱他！您爱一个海盗、一个叛教者、一个劫走您和莱昂内尔的人、一个杀了您哥哥的人！”
“他没有杀。”她断然否认，语气凌厉。“那件事的真相，我已经知道了。”
“从他嘴里听来的吧，我猜？”约翰爵士说，抑制不住一声冷笑。“您就信了？”
“我若不信他，就不会嫁给他。”
“嫁给他？”这下惊骇掺进了他的惶惑。这些骇人听闻的揭秘，到底还有没有个头？他纳闷：是不是到了顶了，还是后头更有？“您嫁了那个臭名昭著的恶棍？”他问，嗓音里没了起伏。
“嫁了——在阿尔及尔，我们登岸的那天夜里。”
他张着嘴瞪了她足有十几念的工夫，随即猛地炸了。“够了！”他咆哮道，攥紧的拳头朝着舱室低矮的顶棚直晃。“够了，上帝作证！哪怕没有别的由头绞他，这一条也绰绰有余。您就瞧我的吧：不出一个钟头，我叫这门腌臜的婚事做个了断。”
“啊，您只要肯听我说！”她恳求道。
“听您说？”他在盛怒中已跨到门边，一心就要传令当场了结——传奥利弗·特雷西利安上前，当面宣判，就地行刑。“听您说？”他又说了一遍，话音里轻蔑与恼怒交并。“我已经听得太多了！”
基利格鲁家就是这个脾气——亨利·戈德勋爵在此停下叙事，照例就撞进他编年史的这些人家，插上一段家史考据，向我们担保说。

===Original===

“They were,” he says, “ever an impetuous, short-reasoning folk, honest and upright enough so far as their judgment carried them, but hampered by a lack of penetration in that judgment.”
Sir John, as much in his earlier commerce with the Tressilians as in this pregnant hour, certainly appears to justify his lordship of that criticism. There were a score of questions a man of perspicuity would not have asked, not one of which appears to have occurred to the knight of Arwenack. If anything arrested him upon the cabin’s threshold, delayed him in the execution of the thing he had resolved upon, no doubt it was sheer curiosity as to what further extravagances Rosamund might yet have it in her mind to utter.
“This man has suffered,” she told him, and was not put off by the hard laugh with which he mocked that statement. “God alone knows what he has suffered in body and in soul for sins which he never committed. Much of that suffering came to him through me. I know today that he did not murder Peter. I know that but for a disloyal act of mine he would be in a position incontestably to prove it without the aid of any man. I know that he was carried off, kidnapped before ever he could clear himself of the accusation, and that as a consequence no life remained him but the life of a renegade which he chose. Mine was the chief fault. And I must make amends. Spare him to me! If you love me.﻿ ﻿…”
But he had heard enough. His sallow face was flushed to a flaming purple.
“Not another word!” he blazed at her. “It is because I do love you﻿—love and pity you from my heart﻿—that I will not listen. It seems I must save you not only from that knave, but from yourself. I were false to my duty by you, false to your dead father and murdered brother else. Anon, you shall thank me, Rosamund.” And again he turned to depart.
“Thank you?” she cried in a ringing voice. “I shall curse you. All my life I shall loathe and hate you, holding you in horror for a murderer if you do this thing. You fool! Can you not see? You fool!”
He recoiled. Being a man of position and importance, quick, fearless, and vindictive of temperament﻿—and also, it would seem, extremely fortunate﻿—it had never happened to him in all his life to be so uncompromisingly and frankly judged.

===Chinese===

“他们家的人，”他写道，“历来是一性子火急、懒得深思的一族：论判断力所及，倒也诚实端正；只是那判断力缺了眼力，处处受制。”
约翰爵士无论早先同特雷西利安家打交道的那些事里，还是在这性命攸关的时辰，都着实没辜负勋爵大人这条考语。一个有眼力的人本有一二十个问题要问，而阿文纳克这位爵士，看来一个也不曾想到。倘若真有什么在舱房门槛上拦了他一拦，耽搁了他去办那已经拿定的事，那也无非是纯粹的好奇：罗莎蒙德嘴里接下来还会冒出怎样惊世骇俗的话来。
“这个人受够了苦，”她告诉他；他拿一声冷笑讥这句话，她却并不退缩。“只有上帝知道，他为了那些从未犯下的罪，身心受过了怎样的煎熬。那苦楚里，有好些是经我的手落到他头上的。我今天知道了他没有杀彼得。我知道，若不是我的一桩负心，他本可以不靠任何人，把这件事证得铁板钉钉。我知道，他在还没来得及洗清指控的时候就被人掳走了；其结果，摆在他面前的便只剩下他选下的那条叛教者的活路。头一等的过错在我。所以我必须补赎。把他留给我！倘若您爱我……”
可他听够了。蜡黄的脸涨成了猪肝色。
“一个字也不要再说了！”他对她厉声道。“正因为我爱您——打心底里爱您、疼您——我才不听。看来我不但要救您脱离那无赖，还得救您脱离您自己。不然我就辜负了我对您的本分，辜负了您亡故的父亲和被害的哥哥。往后，您会谢我的，罗莎蒙德。”说着他又转身要走。
“谢您？”她朗声喊道。“我要咒您。我要咒您一辈子，厌您、恨您，把您当一个杀人凶手一样恐惧——您要是行这件事的话。您这个傻瓜！您就看不见么？您这个傻瓜！”
他倒退了一步。他是个有身份有地位的人，性子急、胆子大、睚眦必报——而且，看样子运气也好得出奇——平生还从没被人这样不留情面、直截了当地评断过。

===Original===

She was by no means the first to account him a fool, but she was certainly the first to call him one to his face; and whilst to the general it might have proved her extreme sanity, to him it was no more than the culminating proof of her mental distemper.
“Pish!” he said, between anger and pity, “you are mad, stark mad! Your mind’s unhinged, your vision’s all distorted. This fiend incarnate is become a poor victim of the evil of others; and I am become a murderer in your sight﻿—a murderer and a fool. God’s Life! Bah! Anon when you are rested, when you are restored, I pray that things may once again assume their proper aspect.”
He turned, all aquiver still with indignation, and was barely in time to avoid being struck by the door which opened suddenly from without.
Lord Henry Goade, dressed﻿—as he tells us﻿—entirely in black, and with his gold chain of office﻿—an ominous sign could they have read it﻿—upon his broad chest, stood in the doorway, silhouetted sharply against the flood of morning sunlight at his back. His benign face would, no doubt, be extremely grave to match the suit he had put on, but its expression will have lightened somewhat when his glance fell upon Rosamund standing there by the table’s edge.
“I was overjoyed,” he writes, “to find her so far recovered, and seeming so much herself again, and I expressed my satisfaction.”
“She were better abed,” snapped Sir John, two hectic spots burning still in his sallow cheeks. “She is distempered, quite.”
“Sir John is mistaken, my lord,” was her calm assurance, “I am very far from suffering as he conceives.”
“I rejoice therein, my dear,” said his lordship, and I imagine his questing eyes speeding from one to the other of them, and marking the evidences of Sir John’s temper, wondering what could have passed. “It happens,” he added sombrely, “that we may require your testimony in this grave matter that is toward.” He turned to Sir John. “I have bidden them bring up the prisoner for sentence. Is the ordeal too much for you, Rosamund?”
“Indeed, no, my lord,” she replied readily. “I welcome it.” And threw back her head as one who braces herself for a trial of endurance.
“No, no,” cut in Sir John, protesting fiercely. “Do not heed her, Harry. She.﻿ ﻿…”
“Considering,” she interrupted, “that the chief count against the prisoner must concern his﻿ ﻿…

背地里骂他傻瓜的，她绝不是头一个；当面叫他傻瓜的，她确乎是头一个。在旁人看来，这没准正显出她神志再清醒不过；在他看来，这不过是她心疾大发的最后铁证。
“呸！”他又怒又怜地说，“您疯了，疯得不轻！您的神志错了位，眼睛看什么都是歪的。这个魔鬼的化身，成了别人作恶的可怜牺牲品；我呢，在您眼里成了杀人凶手——又是凶手，又是傻瓜。老天在上！呸！等您歇过来了、缓过来了，我祷告上苍，万事自会恢复它们本来的面目。”
他转过身，怒气还抖在身上，险些被从外面突然推开的门撞着。
亨利·戈德勋爵立在门口——用他自己的话说，通身黑衣，宽阔的胸前挂着职位的金链——他们若是读得懂，这本是个不祥之兆——背后晨光泼洒，把他的身影衬得轮廓分明。他和善的脸想必极其凝重，同那身衣裳正相配；可目光落到立在桌边的罗莎蒙德身上时，神色想必松了松。
“我喜出望外，”他写道，“见她恢复得这样好，人又像是回来了，我便把欣慰说了出来。”
“她还是卧床的好，”约翰爵士没好气地说，蜡黄的两颊上还烧着两团潮红。“她心绪失调，失调得很。”
“约翰爵士弄错了，大人，”她平静地保证道，“我离他所设想的，远得很呢。”
“那我更觉欣慰，亲爱的，”勋爵大人说。我可想见他那双探询的眼睛在两人之间来回逡巡，留意着约翰爵士动怒的种种痕迹，纳闷方才出了什么事。“事情凑巧，”他正色补充道，“这桩进行中的要案，我们或许需要您的证词。”他转向约翰爵士。“我已吩咐他们把囚犯提上来宣判。这场熬煎，您受得住么，罗莎蒙德？”
“受得住，大人，”她爽快地答道，“我正求之不得。”说着把头一扬，像攒足了劲要熬一场耐力考验的人。
“不，不行，”约翰爵士激烈地拦道，“别听她的，哈里。她……”
“要知道，”她打断道，“控告囚犯的头一条罪名，必然牵涉到他同我……”

his dealings with myself, surely the matter is one upon which I should be heard.”
“Surely, indeed,” Lord Henry agreed, a little bewildered, he confesses, “always provided you are certain it will not overtax your endurance and distress you overmuch. We could perhaps dispense with your testimony.”
“In that, my lord, I assure you that you are mistaken,” she answered. “You cannot dispense with it.”
“Be it so, then,” said Sir John grimly, and he strode back to the table, prepared to take his place there.
Lord Henry’s twinkling blue eyes were still considering Rosamund somewhat searchingly, his fingers tugging thoughtfully at his short tuft of ashen-coloured beard. Then he turned to the door. “Come in, gentlemen,” he said, “and bid them bring up the prisoner.”
Steps clanked upon the deck, and three of Sir John’s officers made their appearance to complete the court that was to sit in judgment upon the renegade corsair, a judgment whose issue was foregone.
