亨利勋爵那双蓝眼睛还在相当仔细地端详罗莎蒙德，手指若有所思地捻着一撮灰黄色的短须。随后他转向门口。“进来吧，诸位，”他说，“再叫他们把囚犯提上来。”
甲板上响起铁盔般的脚步声，约翰爵士手下的三名军官鱼贯而入，凑齐了即将审判那个叛教海盗的法庭——一场结局早已注定的审判。

## XX The Messenger / 送信人
===Original===

For the rest of the day she kept the cabin, chafing with anxiety to know what was toward and the more racked by it because Sakr-el-Bahr refrained through all those hours from coming to her. At last towards evening, unable longer to contain herself, she went forth again, and as it chanced she did so at an untimely moment.
The sun had set, and the evening prayer was being recited aboard the *galeasse*, her crew all prostrate. Perceiving this, she drew back again instinctively, and remained screened by the curtain until the prayer was ended. Then putting it aside, but without stepping past the Nubians who were on guard, she saw that on her left Asad-ed-Din, with Marzak, Biskaine, and one or two other officers, was again occupying the divan under the awning. Her eyes sought Sakr-el-Bahr, and presently they beheld him coming up the gangway with his long, swinging stride, in the wake of the boatswain’s mates who were doling out the meagre evening meal to the slaves.
Suddenly he halted by Lionel, who occupied a seat at the head of his oar immediately next to the gangway. He addressed him harshly in the lingua franca, which Lionel did not understand, and his words rang clearly and were heard﻿—as he intended that they should be﻿—by all upon the poop.
“Well, dog? How does galley slave fare suit thy tender stomach?”
Lionel looked up at him.
“What are you saying?” he asked in English.
Sakr-el-Bahr bent over him, and his face as all could see was evil and mocking. No doubt he spoke to him in English also, but no more than a murmur reached the straining ears of Rosamund, though from his countenance she had no doubt of the purport of his words. And yet she was far indeed from a correct surmise. The mockery in his countenance was but a mask.
“Take no heed of my looks,” he was saying. “I desire them up yonder to think that I abuse you. Look as a man would who were being abused. Cringe or snarl, but listen. Do you remember once when as lads we swam together from Penarrow to Trefusis Point?”
“What do you mean?” quoth Lionel, and the natural sullenness of his mien was all that Sakr-el-Bahr could have desired.
“I am wondering whether you could still swim as far. If so you might find a more appetizing supper awaiting you at the end﻿—aboard Sir John Killigrew’s ship. You had not heard? The *Silver Heron* is at anchor in the bay beyond that headland.

===Chinese===

这一天的其余时候，她都守在舱房里，为外面不知怎样的局面焦灼难安；萨克埃尔-巴尔一连几个时辰不来见她，更叫她心里煎熬。挨到傍晚，她再憋不住，又走了出去——偏巧赶了个不是时候的点。
夕阳已经落了，桨帆船上正在做晚祷，水手们匍匐了一船。她一见，本能地又缩了回去，隔着帘子掩着身，直到祷告完毕。随后她撩开帘子——没有跨过守卫的努比亚人——望见左手边，阿萨德-丁同马尔扎克、比斯坎和一两名军官，又坐在天棚下的软榻上。她的眼睛寻萨克埃尔-巴尔，不多时便望见他迈着大步、一荡一荡地从舷梯上来，走在水手长助手们身后——他们正给奴隶们分发那顿微薄的晚饭。
忽然，他在莱昂内尔跟前站住了。莱昂内尔的座位在桨位排头，紧挨着舷梯。他用混通语厉声对莱昂内尔说话——莱昂内尔听不懂那话，可他的话音字字清亮，艉楼上人人听得分明——他正是要他们听见。
“怎么样，狗东西？划桨奴隶的伙食，合不合你那娇贵的胃口？”
莱昂内尔抬头望他。
“你说什么？”他用英语问。
萨克埃尔-巴尔朝他俯下身，那副脸色——人人都看得见——凶恶而讥诮。无疑他是用英语同他说的，只是落到罗莎蒙德竖起的耳朵里的，不过一片低语；然而看他的脸色，那些话的用意，她毫不怀疑。可她的猜测实在错得远。他脸上的讥诮，不过是一张面具。
“别理会我的脸色，”他说的是这个，“我要上头那些人当我在作践你。你就摆出一副挨作践的架势。缩着也好，龇牙也好，只管听。你可记得，小时候有一回，咱们俩一起从佩纳罗游水到特雷富西斯角？”
“你什么意思？”莱昂内尔问；他那副天生的阴沉相，正是萨克埃尔-巴尔求之不得的。
“我在想，你如今还游不游得动那么远。游得动的话，这段水路的尽头兴许有一顿更开胃的晚饭在等你——就在约翰·基利格鲁爵士的船上。你还没听说？‘银鹭号’就泊在海岬那面的湾里。

===Original===

If I afford you the means, could you swim to her do you think?”
Lionel stared at him in profoundest amazement. “Do you mock me?” he asked at length.
“Why should I mock you on such a matter?”
“Is it not to mock me to suggest a way for my deliverance?”
Sakr-el-Bahr laughed, and he mocked now in earnest. He set his left foot upon the rowers’ stretcher, and leaned forward and down his elbow upon his raised knee so that his face was close to Lionel’s.
“For your deliverance?” said he. “God’s life! Lionel, your mind was ever one that could take in naught but your own self. ’Tis that has made a villain of you. Your deliverance! God’s wounds! Is there none but yourself whose deliverance I might desire? Look you, now I want you to swim to Sir John’s ship and bear him word of the presence here of this *galeasse* and that Rosamund is aboard it. ’Tis for her that I am concerned, and so little for you that should you chance to be drowned in the attempt my only regret will be that the message was not delivered. Will you undertake that swim? It is your one sole chance short of death itself of escaping from the rower’s bench. Will you go?”
“But how?” demanded Lionel, still mistrusting him.
“Will you go?” his brother insisted.
“Afford me the means and I will,” was the answer.
“Very well.” Sakr-el-Bahr leaned nearer still. “Naturally it will be supposed by all who are watching us that I am goading you to desperation. Act, then, your part. Up, and attempt to strike me. Then when I return the blow﻿—and I shall strike heavily that no make-believe may be suspected﻿—collapse on your oar pretending to swoon. Leave the rest to me. Now,” he added sharply, and on the word rose with a final laugh of derision as if to take his departure.
But Lionel was quick to follow the instructions. He leapt up in his bonds, and reaching out as far as they would permit him, he struck Sakr-el-Bahr heavily upon the face. On his side, too, there was to be no make-believe apparent. That done he sank down with a clank of shackles to the bench again, whilst every one of his fellow-slaves that faced his way looked on with fearful eyes.
Sakr-el-Bahr was seen to reel under the blow, and instantly there was a commotion on board. Biskaine leapt to his feet with a half-cry of astonishment; even Asad’s eyes kindled with interest at so unusual a sight as that of a galley slave attacking a corsair.

===Chinese===

我若给你门路，你游得过去么？”
莱昂内尔直勾勾瞪着他，惊愕到了极处。“你是在耍我？”他终于问。
“这样的事，我耍你做什么？”
“给我指一条脱身之路，难道不是耍我？”
萨克埃尔-巴尔笑了，这回是当真在讥诮。他左脚踏上桨手的踏板，探身向前，把胳膊肘支在抬起的膝上，脸凑近莱昂内尔。
“你的脱身？”他说，“老天在上！莱昂内尔，你这副脑子，从来只装得下你自己一个人，就是它把你造成了恶棍。你的脱身！老天在上！除了你，难道我就没有旁人的脱身好惦记？你听好：我要你游到约翰爵士船上，给他捎个信：这条桨帆船在这儿，罗莎蒙德就在船上。我惦记的是她；至于你——你要是半道淹死了，我唯一的遗憾，不过是信没送到。这一趟水，你干不干？除了死，这是你逃出这桨凳的唯一指望。你去不去？”
“可怎么去？”莱昂内尔追问，还是信不过他。
“你去不去？”哥哥执意问。
“你给我门路，我就去。”这是回答。
“很好。”萨克埃尔-巴尔又凑近些。“照理，盯着咱们的所有人都会当我在把你往绝路上逼。那你就演好你这一角。起来，动手打我。等我回手——我会打得实实在在，叫人疑不到有假——你就瘫在桨上，装作昏死。剩下的交给我。动手，”他厉声补了一句，话音一落便哈哈一笑、满脸鄙夷地直起身，仿佛要走。
莱昂内尔倒是执行吩咐不含糊。他带着镣铐一跃而起，胳膊在锁链容许的限度里伸到最远，一拳重重打在萨克埃尔-巴尔脸上。他这一头，也同样不容人看出半分做戏。打完，他铐链哗啦一响，跌坐回长凳，一众面朝这边的奴隶伙计，个个看直了眼。
众人眼见萨克埃尔-巴尔在这一拳之下踉跄后退，船上霎时炸了锅。比斯坎半声惊叫，跳将起来；连阿萨德的眼里也燃起了兴致——一个划桨奴隶动手打海盗，这样的奇景可是头一遭。

===Original===

Then with a snarl of anger, the snarl of an enraged beast almost, Sakr-el-Bahr’s great arm was swung aloft and his fist descended like a hammer upon Lionel’s head.
Lionel sank forward under the blow, his senses swimming. Sakr-el-Bahr’s arm swung up a second time.
“Thou dog!” he roared, and then checked, perceiving that Lionel appeared to have swooned.
He turned and bellowed for Vigitello and his mates in a voice that was hoarse with passion. Vigitello came at a run, a couple of his men at his heels.
“Unshackle me this carrion, and heave it overboard,” was the harsh order. “Let that serve as an example to the others. Let them learn thus the price of mutiny in their lousy ranks. To it, I say.”
Away sped a man for hammer and chisel. He returned with them at once. Four sharp metallic blows rang out, and Lionel was dragged forth from his place to the gangway-deck. Here he revived, and screamed for mercy as though he were to be drowned in earnest.
Biskaine chuckled under the awning, Asad looked on approvingly, Rosamund drew back, shuddering, choking, and near to fainting from sheer horror.
She saw Lionel borne struggling in the arms of the boatswain’s men to the starboard quarter, and flung over the side with no more compunction or care than had he been so much rubbish. She heard the final scream of terror with which he vanished, the splash of his fall, and then in the ensuing silence the laugh of Sakr-el-Bahr.
For a spell she stood there with horror and loathing of that renegade corsair in her soul. Her mind was bewildered and confused. She sought to restore order in it, that she might consider this fresh deed of his, this act of wanton brutality and fratricide. And all that she could gather was the firm conviction that hitherto he had cheated her; he had lied when he swore that his aim was to effect her deliverance. It was not in such a nature to know a gentle mood of penitence for a wrong done. What might be his purpose she could not yet perceive, but that it was an evil one she never doubted, for no purpose of his could be aught but evil. So overwrought was she now that she forgot all Lionel’s sins, and found her heart filled with compassion for him hurled in that brutal fashion to his death.
And then, quite suddenly a shout rang out from the forecastle.
“He is swimming!”
Sakr-el-Bahr had been prepared for the chance of this.
“Where? Where?”

===Chinese===

接着，萨克埃尔-巴尔怒吼一声，几乎是一头暴兽的嗥叫，粗大的手臂抡到半空，一拳像铁锤似的砸在莱昂内尔头顶。
莱昂内尔在这一拳下向前栽倒，天旋地转。萨克埃尔-巴尔的手臂又抡了起来。
“你这狗东西！”他咆哮道，随即顿住——莱昂内尔看去已经昏死。
他转身嘶声吼唤维吉特洛和他的伙计们，那嗓音怒得发哑。维吉特洛飞奔而来，身后跟着两名手下。
“把这堆烂肉给我卸了镣，扔下海去，”是那道凶狠的命令。“就给旁的人立个榜样，叫这群龌龊东西知道知道，他们那行里闹反是什么价钱。动手，我说。”
一人飞奔去取锤子和凿子，顷刻便回。四记金属的脆响过后，莱昂内尔被从座位上拖到舷梯甲板上。到这儿他醒了过来，尖声讨饶，倒真像要被沉海似的。
天棚下，比斯坎吃吃地笑，阿萨德赞许地看着；罗莎蒙德直往后缩，发着抖、噎着气，单是骇怕就几乎晕倒。
她眼见莱昂内尔在水手长手下们的臂弯里挣着、被抬到右舷后侧，被抛下船舷——那份不心疼、不在乎，仿佛抛的只是一堆垃圾。她听见他消失时最后一声恐怖的尖叫，听见他落水的水响，随后，在那随之而来的寂静里，是萨克埃尔-巴尔的笑声。
一时间，她立在那里，灵魂里满是惊骇与憎恶，冲着那个叛教海盗。她的心思乱作一团。她想理出个头绪，好掂掂他这桩新的行径——这桩肆意的凶残、这桩弑弟。可她捋来捋去，只捋出一个铁定的信念：他一直在骗她；他赌咒说一心救她脱身，那是撒谎。这样的天性，是不会为亏欠谁生出一点温软的悔意的。他安的什么心，她一时还看不出来；可那是坏心，她从不怀疑——他的心思，除了坏，不可能有别的。她激动到这步田地，竟把莱昂内尔的种种罪过忘了个干净，满心怜惜起他来——他就那样被凶残地扔出去送了死。
就在这时，前艄上陡然爆出一声喊。
“他在游水！”
这一手，萨克埃尔-巴尔早有准备。
“哪儿？哪儿？”

===Original===

he cried, and sprang to the bulwarks.
“Yonder!” A man was pointing. Others had joined him and were peering through the gathering gloom at the moving object that was Lionel’s head and the faintly visible swirl of water about it which indicated that he swam.
“Out to sea!” cried Sakr-el-Bahr. “He’ll not swim far in any case. But we will shorten his road for him.” He snatched a crossbow from the rack about the mainmast, fitted a shaft to it and took aim.
On the point of loosing the bolt he paused.
“Marzak!” he called. “Here, thou prince of marksmen, is a butt for thee!”
From the poop deck whence with his father he too was watching the swimmer’s head, which at every moment became more faint in the failing light, Marzak looked with cold disdain upon his challenger, making no reply. A titter ran through the crew.
“Come now,” cried Sakr-el-Bahr. “Take up thy bow!”
“If thou delay much longer,” put in Asad, “he will be beyond thine aim. Already he is scarcely visible.”
“The more difficult a butt, then,” answered Sakr-el-Bahr, who was but delaying to gain time. “The keener test. A hundred philips, Marzak, that thou’lt not hit me that head in three shots, and that I’ll sink him at the first! Wilt take the wager?”
“The unbeliever is forever peeping forth from thee,” was Marzak’s dignified reply. “Games of chance are forbidden by the Prophet.”
“Make haste, man!” cried Asad. “Already I can scarce discern him. Loose thy quarrel.”
“Pooh,” was the disdainful answer. “A fair mark still for such an eye as mine. I never miss﻿—not even in the dark.”
“Vain boaster,” said Marzak.
“Am I so?” Sakr-el-Bahr loosed his shaft at last into the gloom, and peered after it following its flight, which was wide of the direction of the swimmer’s head. “A hit!” he cried brazenly. “He’s gone!”
“I think I see him still,” said one.
“Thine eyes deceive thee in this light. No man was ever known to swim with an arrow through his brain.”
“Ay,” put in Jasper, who stood behind Sakr-el-Bahr. “He has vanished.”
“ ’Tis too dark to see,” said Vigitello.
And then Asad turned from the vessel’s side. “Well, well﻿—shot or drowned, he’s gone,” he said, and there the matter ended.
Sakr-el-Bahr replaced the crossbow in the rack, and came slowly up to the poop.
In the gloom he found himself confronted by Rosamund’s white face between the two dusky countenances of his Nubians.

===Chinese===

他喊着一纵身扑到船栏边。
“那儿！”一人指着。旁人凑过来，都在渐浓的暮色里眯眼盯那个移动的东西——那是莱昂内尔的头，头边一圈隐约的水纹，表明他在游。
“往海里去了！”萨克埃尔-巴尔喊道，“横竖他也游不远。不过，咱们替他把路截短些。”他从主桅旁的架上抓下一张弩，搭上箭，瞄准了。
箭在弦上，他却停了手。
“马尔扎克！”他喊，“来，神射手殿下，这儿给你备了个靶子！”
马尔扎克正同他父亲在艉楼上盯着那颗游动的头——那头在渐熄的天光里一刻比一刻模糊——闻言只拿冷冷的轻蔑打量这个挑衅的人，不作声。水手们里起了一阵窃笑。
“来嘛，”萨克埃尔-巴尔嚷道，“取你的弓来！”
“再磨蹭，”阿萨德插话，“他就出你的射程了。这会儿已经快瞧不见了。”
“那这靶子就更难了，”萨克埃尔-巴尔答——他拖着不过是为争时间，“考验就更精。一百枚金币，马尔扎克，赌你三箭射不中那颗头，赌我一箭就送他见底！接不接？”
“你身上到底藏不住那股异教气，”马尔扎克矜贵地答，“掷骰碰运，先知有禁。”
“快点，伙计！”阿萨德喊道，“我已经快认不出他了。放箭。”
“呸，”回答满是不屑，“对我这双眼，这也还算个正经靶子。我从无不中——黑地里也一样。”
“吹大话的。”马尔扎克说。
“是么？”萨克埃尔-巴尔终于把箭射进暮色里，眯眼追着它的飞行——那箭大大偏离开游泳者的方向。“中了！”他厚着脸皮喊，“完事了！”
“我好像还看得见他。”有人说。
“这光线下，你的眼骗了你。没听说过谁脑袋上插着箭还能游水的。”
“是啊，”贾斯珀凑上来一句，他就站在萨克埃尔-巴尔身后，“人没影儿了。”
“天太黑，看不清。”维吉特洛说。
随后阿萨德从船舷边转过身来。“也罢，也罢——射死也好，淹死也好，人是没了，”他说，这件事便就此收场。
萨克埃尔-巴尔把弩放回架上，慢慢踱上艉楼。
昏暗里，他迎面撞上罗莎蒙德那张白脸，夹在他两名努比亚人黝黑的面孔中间。

===Original===

She drew back before him as he approached, and he, intent upon imparting his news to her, followed her within the poop house, and bade Abiad bring lights.
When these had been kindled they faced each other, and he perceived her profound agitation and guessed the cause of it. Suddenly she broke into speech.
“You beast! You devil!” she panted. “God will punish you! I shall spend my every breath in praying Him to punish you as you deserve. You murderer! You hound! And I like a poor simpleton was heeding your false words. I was believing you sincere in your repentance of the wrong you have done me. But now you have shown me.﻿ ﻿…”
“How have I hurt you in what I have done to Lionel?” he cut in, a little amazed by so much vehemence.
“Hurt me!” she cried, and on the words grew cold and calm again with very scorn. “I thank God it is beyond your power to hurt me. And I thank you for correcting my foolish misconception of you, my belief in your pitiful pretence that it was your aim to save me. I would not accept salvation at your murderer’s hands. Though, indeed, I shall not be put to it. Rather,” she pursued, a little wildly now in her deep mortification, “are you like to sacrifice me to your own vile ends, whatever they may be. But I shall thwart you, Heaven helping me. Be sure I shall not want courage for that.” And with a shuddering moan she covered her face, and stood swaying there before him.
He looked on with a faint, bitter smile, understanding her mood just as he understood her dark threat of thwarting him.
“I came,” he said quietly, “to bring you the assurance that he has got safely away, and to tell you upon what manner of errand I have sent him.”
Something compelling in his voice, the easy assurance with which he spoke, drew her to stare at him again.
“I mean Lionel, of course,” he said, in answer to her questioning glance. “That scene between us﻿—the blow and the swoon and the rest of it﻿—was all make-believe. So afterwards the shooting. My challenge to Marzak was a ruse to gain time﻿—to avoid shooting until Lionel’s head should have become so dimly visible in the dusk that none could say whether it was still there or not. My shaft went wide of him, as I intended. He is swimming round the head with my message to Sir John Killigrew. He was a strong swimmer in the old days, and should easily reach his goal. That is what I came to tell you.”

===Chinese===

他一走近，她便朝后退；他一心要把消息告诉她，便随着她进了艉楼舱，吩咐阿比亚德掌灯。
灯点上了，两人相对而立，他看出她激荡得厉害，也猜到了缘由。忽然，她开了口。
“你这畜生！你这魔鬼！”她喘着说。“上帝会惩罚你的！我这一辈子，一口气一口气地求上帝，求他照你的罪罚你。你这杀人犯！你这猎狗！而我像个可怜的傻子，竟听信你那些鬼话。我竟当你真懊悔了亏待我。如今你可让我看清了……”
“我这样处置莱昂内尔，怎么就伤了你了？”他插进来说，这样劈头的发作叫他有点吃惊。
“伤我！”她喊道；话到这儿，那股鄙夷反倒叫她冷了、静了。“谢天谢地，伤我超出了你的本事。也多谢你纠正了我对你愚蠢的错看——我居然信了你那套可怜的把戏，说什么你的目的是救我。你那双凶手的手里递来的解救，我不要。不过说真的，也轮不到我受不受——倒更像是，”她接着说，深深的羞愤使她话音发狂，“你要把我祭了你自己的龌龊目的，管它是什么。可我会挫败你的，上天帮我。放心，我不会缺这个胆。”一声战栗的呜咽，她掩住脸，在他面前摇摇地立着。
他望着，唇边挂一丝淡淡的苦笑；她的心境他懂得，正同他懂得她那句咬牙切齿的“挫败你”一样。
“我来，”他平静地说，“是要告诉你一个准信：他已经安然脱身了；再告诉你，我差他去办的是怎样一桩差事。”
他话音里那股不容分说的镇定，引得她重又瞪住了他。
“我当然是指莱昂内尔，”他回答她询问的目光说，“咱们俩那一出——那一拳、那一昏，还有后来的种种——全是做戏。射箭也是。我激马尔扎克比箭，是个拖时间的计策——拖到莱昂内尔的头在暮色里淡得叫人说不上还在不在，才好放箭。我那一箭大大偏过了他，本就存心如此。他这会儿正绕着海岬游水，替我给约翰·基利格鲁爵士送信呢。当年他是把游水的好手，到岸不难。我要告诉你的，就是这个。”

===Original===

For a long spell she continued to stare at him in silence.
“You are speaking the truth?” she asked at last, in a small voice.
He shrugged. “You will have a difficulty in perceiving the object I might serve by falsehood.”
She sat down suddenly upon the divan; it was almost as if she collapsed bereft of strength; and as suddenly she fell to weeping softly.
“And﻿ ﻿… and I believed that you﻿ ﻿… that you.﻿ ﻿…”
“Just so,” he grimly interrupted. “You always did believe the best of me.”
And on that he turned and went out abruptly.

===Chinese===

许久，她只是默然瞪着他。
“你说的是实话？”她终于小声问。
他耸耸肩。“撒谎能给我图着什么，你怕是想不出来。”
她突然在软榻上坐倒——几乎像脱了力；又突然低低哭了起来。
“可是……可我一直当真……当真以为你……”
“正是，”他冷冷地打断，“你从来把我往最好处想。”
说完，他转身，大步走了出去。

## XIX The Mutineers / 哗变者
===Original===

Later that morning, some time after the *galeasse* had awakened to life and such languid movement as might be looked for in a waiting crew, Sakr-el-Bahr went to visit Rosamund.
He found her brightened and refreshed by sleep, and he brought her reassuring messages that all was well, encouraging her with hopes which himself he was very far from entertaining. If her reception of him was not expressedly friendly, neither was it unfriendly. She listened to the hopes he expressed of yet effecting her safe deliverance, and whilst she had no thanks to offer him for the efforts he was to exert on her behalf﻿—accepting them as her absolute due, as the inadequate liquidation of the debt that lay between them﻿—yet there was now none of that aloofness amounting almost to scorn which hitherto had marked her bearing towards him.
He came again some hours later, in the afternoon, by when his Nubians were once more at their post. He had no news to bring her beyond the fact that their sentinel on the heights reported a sail to westward, beating up towards the island before the very gentle breeze that was blowing. But the argosy they awaited was not yet in sight, and he confessed that certain proposals which he had made to Asad for landing her in France had been rejected. Still she need have no fear, he added promptly, seeing the sudden alarm that quickened in her eyes. A way would present itself. He was watching, and would miss no chance.
“And if no chance should offer?” she asked him.
“Why then I will make one,” he answered, lightly almost. “I have been making them all my life, and it would be odd if I should have lost the trick of it on my life’s most important occasion.”
This mention of his life led to a question from her.
“How did you contrive the chance that has made you what you are? I mean,” she added quickly, as if fearing that the purport of that question might be misunderstood, “that has enabled you to become a corsair captain.”
“ ’Tis a long story that,” he said. “I should weary you in the telling of it.”
“No,” she replied, and shook her head, her clear eyes solemnly meeting his clouded glance. “You would not weary me. Chances may be few in which to learn it.”
“And you would learn it?” quoth he, and added, “That you may judge me?”
“Perhaps,” she said, and her eyes fell.
With bowed head he paced the length of the small chamber, and back again.

===Chinese===

第二天上午，稍晚些时候，桨帆船醒了过来，有了些等候中的船伍该有的懒散动静，萨克埃尔-巴尔便去看罗莎蒙德。
他见她睡过一觉，容色明润了些，便带了些宽心的话来，说诸事顺遂，又拿些希望给她打气——这些希望，他自己心里可远没有。她待他算不得亲热，倒也不算冷淡。他表达说仍有把握送她安然脱身，她听着；她并不为他将出的力道谢他——只当那是他分内该还的账，还的是他们两人之间那笔债里微薄的一点——可她如今对他，已没有先前那种近乎鄙夷的疏远了。
几个钟头后，下午，他又来了，他的努比亚人那时重新守在岗位上。他没带来什么消息，只有一条：山头上的了望哨报，西边来了一张帆，趁着那阵极软的风朝岛这边蹭。但他们等候的那艘大商船还没影儿；他向阿萨德提的、送她在法国登陆的几项提议，也被驳回了。见她眼里陡然窜起的惊慌，他又赶紧添了一句：她大可不必害怕。路子总会有的。他盯着呢，一点机会也漏不了。
“要是机会不来呢？”她问。
“那我就造一个，”他答，语气几乎是轻快的。“我一辈子都在造机会；到了这辈子最要紧的关头倒忘了这门手艺，那才叫怪呢。”
提到“这辈子”，引出了她一个问题。
“你是怎么造出那个机会，把自己造成今天这个身份的？我是说，”她赶紧补上，像怕这话被误解了用意，“是什么叫你能当上海盗船长的。”
“那可说来话长了，”他说，“讲起来怕要把你讲乏了。”
“不会，”她答，摇了摇头，清亮的眼睛郑重地迎着他晦暗的目光。“你乏不了我。能听这段话的机会，怕是不多。”
“你想听？”他说着又添一句，“好来评判我？”
“也许吧。”她说，垂下了眼。
他低着头，在那间小舱里踱了个来回。

===Original===

His desire was to do her will in this, which is natural enough﻿—for if it is true that who knows all must perforce forgive all, never could it have been truer than in the case of Sir Oliver Tressilian.
So he told his tale. Pacing there he related it at length, from the days when he had toiled at an oar on one of the galleys of Spain down to that hour in which aboard the Spanish vessel taken under Cape Spartel he had determined upon that voyage to England to present his reckoning to his brother. He told his story simply and without too great a wealth of detail, yet he omitted nothing of all that had gone to place him where he stood. And she, listening, was so profoundly moved that at one moment her eyes glistened with tears which she sought vainly to repress. Yet he, pacing there, absorbed, with head bowed and eyes that never once strayed in her direction, saw none of this.
“And so,” he said, when at last that odd narrative had reached its end, “you know what the forces were that drove me. Another stronger than myself might have resisted and preferred to suffer death. But I was not strong enough. Or perhaps it is that stronger than myself was my desire to punish, to vent the bitter hatred into which my erstwhile love for Lionel was turned.”
“And for me, too﻿—as you have told me,” she added.
“Not so,” he corrected her. “I hated you for your unfaith, and most of all for your having burnt unread the letter that I sent you by the hand of Pitt. In doing that you contributed to the wrongs I was enduring, you destroyed my one chance of establishing my innocence and seeking rehabilitation, you doomed me for life to the ways which I was treading. But I did not then know what ample cause you had to believe me what I seemed. I did not know that it was believed I had fled. Therefore I forgive you freely a deed for which at one time I confess that I hated you, and which spurred me to bear you off when I found you under my hand that night at Arwenack when I went for Lionel.”
“You mean that it was no part of your intent to have done so?” she asked him.
“To carry you off together with him?” he asked. “I swear to God I had not premeditated that. Indeed, it was done because not premeditated, for had I considered it, I do think I should have been proof against any such temptation. It assailed me suddenly when I beheld you there with Lionel, and I succumbed to it.

===Chinese===

他愿意依她这一桩，这也再自然不过——倘若“知全恕全”这话当真，那再没有比奥利弗·特雷西利安爵士这桩更当真的了。
于是他讲起了自己的故事。他在那里踱着，从头到尾讲了一遍：从他在西班牙一条大帆船上支着桨当牛做马的日子起，直到他在斯帕特尔角外掳下的那条西班牙船上、打定主意驶往英格兰找弟弟清算的那个时辰。他讲得平实，细节也不铺张，可凡是把他推到今日这个地步的事，一件没有漏。她听着，听得心魂深受撼动，有一刻眼里闪起了泪光，压也压不住。可他只顾在那里踱步、低头出神，眼睛一次也不曾朝她那边偏，这一切全没有瞧见。
“就这样，”那段奇特的叙述到了头，他说，“你知道了把我推着走的都是些什么力道了。换一个比我强的人，兴许就抗住了，宁死不屈。可我不够强。也许还不如说，比我自己更强的，是我那股要惩罚的欲念，是那腔苦恨——我从前对莱昂内尔的疼爱，就化成了它。”
“也有对我的一份——照你自己说的。”她补了一句。
“不对，”他纠正她，“我恨过你，恨你的负心，最恨你把我托皮特捎给你的那封信，没拆就烧了。你那一烧，添了我正受着的冤，毁了我洗冤复名的唯一指望，把我一辈子钉死在我走的这条道上。可我当时不知道，你有的是充分的理由，信我是表里如一的坏人。我不知道旁人都当我是畏罪潜逃。所以那桩事——我认账，它一度叫我恨你，也是它撺掇着我，那夜在阿文纳克冲着莱昂内尔去、却在你落在我手边时把你劫走——如今我痛痛快快地原谅你了。”
“你是说，那原不在你的盘算里？”她问。
“连你一块儿劫走？”他说，“我对天起誓，那不是我预先打算的。说实在的，正因没打算，才做下了；真要掂量过，我自量还扛得住那样的诱惑。它是在我瞧见你同莱昂内尔在一起的那一瞬突然扑上来的，我便缴了械。

===Original===

Knowing what I now know I am punished enough, I think.”
“I think I can understand,” she murmured gently, as if to comfort him, for quick pain had trembled in his voice.
He tossed back his turbaned head. “To understand is something,” said he. “It is halfway at least to forgiveness. But ere forgiveness can be accepted the evil done must be atoned for to the full.”
“If possible,” said she.
“It must be made possible,” he answered her with heat, and on that he checked abruptly, arrested by a sound of shouting from without.
He recognized the voice of Larocque, who at dawn had returned to his sentinel’s post on the summit of the headland, relieving the man who had replaced him there during the night.
“My lord! My lord!” was the cry, in a voice shaken by excitement, and succeeded by a shouting chorus from the crew.
Sakr-el-Bahr turned swiftly to the entrance, whisked aside the curtain, and stepped out upon the poop. Larocque was in the very act of clambering over the bulwarks amidships, towards the waist deck where Asad awaited him in company with Marzak and the trusty Biskaine. The prow, on which the corsairs had lounged at ease since yesterday, was now a seething mob of inquisitive babbling men, crowding to the rail and even down the gangway in their eagerness to learn what news it was that brought the sentinel aboard in such excited haste.
From where he stood Sakr-el-Bahr heard Larocque’s loud announcement.
“The ship I sighted at dawn, my lord!”
“Well?” barked Asad.
“She is here﻿—in the bay beneath that headland. She has just dropped anchor.”
“No need for alarm in that,” replied the Basha at once. “Since she has anchored there it is plain that she has no suspicion of our presence. What manner of ship is she?”
“A tall galleon of twenty guns, flying the flag of England.”
“Of England!” cried Asad in surprise. “She’ll need be a stout vessel to hazard herself in Spanish waters.”
Sakr-el-Bahr advanced to the rail.
“Does she display no further device?” he asked.
Larocque turned at the question. “Ay,” he answered, “a narrow blue pennant on her mizzen is charged with a white bird﻿—a stork, I think.”
“A stork?” echoed Sakr-el-Bahr thoughtfully. He could call to mind no such English blazon, nor did it seem to him that it could possibly be English. He caught the sound of a quickly indrawn breath behind him.

===Chinese===

如今知道了这一切，我想，我受的罚已经够了。”
“我想我能明白，”她柔声说，像是在宽慰他——他话音里颤过一阵急痛。
他把缠头一甩，昂起头来。“明白，是一桩，”他说，“至少是宽恕的一半路。可要受下宽恕，造下的孽就得先清偿个干净。”
“倘若办得到。”她说。
“必须办到。”他答得激烈，话到这儿猛地刹住——外头起了一片喊声。
他听出那是拉罗克的声音。天亮时，拉罗克回岬顶哨位换下了夜里替他的人。
“大人！大人！”那呼声激动得发颤，跟着，水手们齐声应和，嚷成一片。
萨克埃尔-巴尔霍地转向舱门，一把撩开帘子，跨上艉楼。拉罗克正翻过船中部的舷栏，朝船腰甲板爬去——阿萨德已同马尔扎克和亲信比斯坎等在那里。船头上，自昨日以来海盗们一直闲散歪着的那个地方，如今成了一锅翻滚的、七嘴八舌的人堆，众人挤到栏边，甚至顺着舷梯往下涌，急着要打听：是什么消息，叫哨兵这样火烧火燎地跑回船来。
萨克埃尔-巴尔从他立的地方，听得见拉罗克的高声禀报。
“我天亮时望见的那条船，大人！”
“怎么样？”阿萨德喝问。
“来了——就在那海岬底下的湾里。刚下了锚。”
“这有什么可慌的，”帕夏当即答道，“她既在那里下锚，分明没疑心咱们在。是条什么船？”
“一条高大的三桅帆船，二十门炮，挂着英格兰旗。”
“英格兰！”阿萨德惊呼，“敢闯西班牙水面，那得是条硬船。”
萨克埃尔-巴尔走到栏杆前。
“她没再挂别的识别旗？”他问。
拉罗克闻声转过脸来。“挂了，”他答，“后桅上一面窄蓝燕尾旗，旗上一只白鸟——我看是只鹳。”
“鹳？”萨克埃尔-巴尔若有所思地重复。他想不起英格兰纹章里有这么一只鸟，也觉得那不大像英格兰的旗。他听见身后急促抽了一口气。

===Original===

He turned to find Rosamund standing in the entrance, not more than half concealed by the curtain. Her face showed white and eager, her eyes were wide.
“What is’t?” he asked her shortly.
“A stork, he thinks,” she said, as though that were answer enough.
“I’ faith an unlikely bird,” he commented. “The fellow is mistook.”
“Yet not by much, Sir Oliver.”
“How? Not by much?” Intrigued by something in her tone and glance, he stepped quickly up to her, whilst below the chatter of voices increased.
“That which he takes to be a stork is a heron﻿—a white heron, and white is argent in heraldry, is’t not?”
“It is. What then?”
“D’ye not see? That ship will be the *Silver Heron*.”
He looked at her. “ ’S life!” said he, “I reck little whether it be the silver heron or the golden grasshopper. What odds?”
“It is Sir John’s ship﻿—Sir John Killigrew’s,” she explained. “She was all but ready to sail when﻿ ﻿… when you came to Arwenack. He was for the Indies. Instead﻿—don’t you see?﻿—out of love for me he will have come after me upon a forlorn hope of overtaking you ere you could make Barbary.”
“God’s light!” said Sakr-el-Bahr, and fell to musing. Then he raised his head and laughed. “Faith, he’s some days late for that!”
But the jest evoked no response from her. She continued to stare at him with those eager yet timid eyes.
“And yet,” he continued, “he comes opportunely enough. If the breeze that has fetched him is faint, yet surely it blows from Heaven.”
“Were it﻿ ﻿… ?” she paused, faltering a moment. Then, “Were it possible to communicate with him?” she asked, yet with hesitation.
“Possible﻿—ay,” he answered. “Though we must needs devise the means, and that will prove none so easy.”
“And you would do it?” she inquired, an undercurrent of wonder in her question, some recollection of it in her face.
“Why, readily,” he answered, “since no other way presents itself. No doubt ’twill cost some lives,” he added, “but then.﻿ ﻿…” And he shrugged to complete the sentence.
“Ah, no, no! Not at that price!” she protested. And how was he to know that all the price she was thinking of was his own life, which she conceived would be forfeited if the assistance of the *Silver Heron* were invoked?
Before he could return her any answer his attention was diverted.

===Chinese===

他回头，见罗莎蒙德立在舱门口，帘子掩不住半边。她脸色发白，神情急切，眼睛睁得老大。
“什么事？”他简短地问。
“他说是一只鹳，”她说，仿佛这一个字就够了。
“说实在的，不像，”他品评道，“这家伙看岔了。”
“岔得不多，奥利弗爵士。”
“怎么？岔得不多？”她话音眼里有些东西勾着他，他快步走到她跟前；底下人声嘈杂，愈发高了。
“他当是鹳的那只鸟，是鹭——一只白鹭；纹章学里，白就是银，是不是？”
“是。那又怎样？”
“你还不明白？那条船准是‘银鹭号’。”
他望着她。“老天！”他说，“银鹭也好，金蚂蚱也好，我不大在乎。有什么要紧？”
“那是约翰爵士的船——约翰·基利格鲁爵士的，”她解释道，“你……你来阿文纳克那阵，她眼看就要开船了，本来是去印度群岛的。结果呢——你还不明白？——他心疼我，就追出来了，指望赶在你到巴巴里之前追上你，一线渺茫的希望。”
“老天在上！”萨克埃尔-巴尔说着，堕入沉思。随后他抬起头，笑了。“说实话，他晚了几天了！”
可这句玩笑没换来她的回应。她只顾拿那又急又怯的眼睛瞪着他。
“不过，”他接着说，“他来得倒也凑巧。送他来的这阵风虽软，吹的却是顺风——从天上来。”
“能不能……”她顿住，结巴了一下，随后问，“能不能同他通上消息？”话音里还带着迟疑。
“能——能，”他答，“只是门路得现想，这可不见得容易。”
“你肯做？”她问，问话里淌着一股暗暗的惊奇，脸上也浮着几分回想。
“怎么不肯，”他答，“眼下又没别的路。少不得要折几条人命，”他补了一句，“不过嘛……”他耸耸肩，算是把话说完。
“啊，不，不！不能拿这个作价！”她抗议道。他又哪里知道，她心里盘算的那个“价”，全是他自己的一条命——她认定，一搬“银鹭号”的救兵，他这条命就得搭进去。
他还来不及答她，注意力便被引开了。

===Original===

A sullen threatening note had crept into the babble of the crew, and suddenly one or two voices were raised to demand insistently that Asad should put to sea at once and remove his vessel from a neighbourhood become so dangerous. Now, the fault of this was Marzak’s. His was the voice that first had uttered that timid suggestion, and the infection of his panic had spread instantly through the corsair ranks.
Asad, drawn to the full of his gaunt height, turned upon them the eyes that had quelled greater clamours, and raised the voice which in its day had hurled a hundred men straight into the jaws of death without a protest.
“Silence!” he commanded. “I am your lord and need no counsellors save Allah. When I consider the time come, I will give the word to row, but not before. Back to your quarters, then, and peace!”
He disdained to argue with them, to show them what sound reasons there were for remaining in this secret cove and against putting forth into the open. Enough for them that such should be his will. Not for them to question his wisdom and his decisions.
But Asad-ed-Din had lain overlong in Algiers whilst his fleets under Sakr-el-Bahr and Biskaine had scoured the inland sea. The men were no longer accustomed to the goad of his voice, their confidence in his judgment was not built upon the sound basis of past experience. Never yet had he led into battle the men of this crew and brought them forth again in triumph and enriched by spoil.
So now they set their own judgment against his. To them it seemed a recklessness﻿—as, indeed, Marzak had suggested﻿—to linger here, and his mere announcement of his purpose was far from sufficient to dispel their doubts.
The murmurs swelled, not to be overborne by his fierce presence and scowling brow, and suddenly one of the renegades﻿—secretly prompted by the wily Vigitello﻿—raised a shout for the captain whom they knew and trusted.
“Sakr-el-Bahr! Sakr-el-Bahr! Thou’lt not leave us penned in this cove to perish like rats!”
It was as a spark to a train of powder. A score of voices instantly took up the cry; hands were flung out towards Sakr-el-Bahr, where he stood above them and in full view of all, leaning impassive and stern upon the poop rail, whilst his agile mind weighed the opportunity thus thrust upon him, and considered what profit was to be extracted from it.
Asad fell back a pace in his profound mortification.

===Chinese===

水手们的嘈杂里渗进了阴沉的威胁调子，忽然有一两嗓门拔起来，一个劲地催：阿萨德该立刻出海，把船挪出这个变得凶险的是非之地。说到这条祸根，得算马尔扎克。头一个怯生生提这主意的正是他的嘴，他那一阵恐慌当即在海盗行伍里传开了。
阿萨德挺足了他那瘦高的身量，转过那双镇压过更大风浪的眼睛，提起那副当年一声令下便把上百人直送进死神的血口、无人敢吭一声的嗓子。
“肃静！”他喝道，“我是你们的主上，除了安拉，用不着旁人进言。几时我掂量着时辰到了，自会传令开桨；不到那时，不行。各归各位，安分！”
他屑于同他们讲道理，讲讲守在这处隐秘小湾有什么实在的章程、出海露行有什么不妥——他觉得犯不上。他们只消知道：这是他的意思。轮不着他们来质疑他的睿智和裁断。
可是，阿萨德-丁在阿尔及尔闲居得太久了，他的舰队一直是萨克埃尔-巴尔和比斯坎带着在地中海里扫荡的。这些人早已不惯他嗓音的鞭策，对他判断的信心，也没有往日实绩打底。这条船上的这些人，他还从没亲自领着打过一仗、再带着他们凯旋、满载着战利品回来过。
于是他们拿自己的判断顶他的。在他们看来，赖在这儿不走是拿性命冒险——马尔扎克正是这么递的话——他一句宣布心意，远驱不散他们的疑心。
怨声涨了起来，凭他凶横的气焰、紧锁的眉头也压不住；忽然，叛教者里有一个——是狡猾的维吉特洛暗地里撺掇的——高声喊起那位他们认得、信得过的船长来。
“萨克埃尔-巴尔！萨克埃尔-巴尔！你可不能把我们圈在这湾里，叫咱们像耗子一样死绝！”
这话像一点火星落进了火药线。二十来条嗓门立时应和；一条条手臂朝萨克埃尔-巴尔伸出去——他立在他们上方的艉楼栏边，人人望得见，不动声色，冷峻地凭栏而倚；与此同时，他那副机敏的头脑正掂着这个硬送到手里的机会，盘算能从里头榨出多少好处。
阿萨德在深深的羞愤里倒退了一步。

===Original===

His face was livid, his eyes blared furiously, his hand flew to the jewelled hilt of his scimitar, yet forbore from drawing the blade. Instead he let loose upon Marzak the venom kindled in his soul by this evidence of how shrunken was his authority.
“Thou fool!” he snarled. “Look on thy craven’s work. See what a devil thou hast raised with thy woman’s counsels. Thou to command a galley! Thou to become a fighter upon the seas! I would that Allah had stricken me dead ere I begat me such a son as thou!”
Marzak recoiled before the fury of words that he feared might be followed by yet worse. He dared make no answer, offer no excuse; in that moment he scarcely dared breathe.
Meanwhile Rosamund in her eagerness had advanced until she stood at Sakr-el-Bahr’s elbow.
“God is helping us!” she said in a voice of fervent gratitude. “This is your opportunity. The men will obey you.”
He looked at her, and smiled faintly upon her eagerness. “Ay, mistress, they will obey me,” he said. But in the few moments that were sped he had taken his resolve. Whilst undoubtedly Asad was right, and the wise course was to lie close in this sheltering cove where the odds of their going unperceived were very heavily in their favour, yet the men’s judgment was not altogether at fault. If they were to put to sea, they might by steering an easterly course pass similarly unperceived, and even should the splash of their oars reach the galleon beyond the headland, yet by the time she had weighed anchor and started in pursuit they would be well away straining every ounce of muscle at the oars, whilst the breeze﻿—a heavy factor in his considerations﻿—was become so feeble that they could laugh at pursuit by a vessel that depended upon wind alone. The only danger, then, was the danger of the galleon’s cannon, and that danger was none so great as from experience Sakr-el-Bahr well knew.
Thus was he reluctantly forced to the conclusion that in the main the wiser policy was to support Asad, and since he was full confident of the obedience of the men he consoled himself with the reflection that a moral victory might be in store for him out of which some surer profit might presently be made.
In answer, then, to those who still called upon him, he leapt down the companion and strode along the gangway to the waist deck to take his stand at the Basha’s side.

===Chinese===

他脸色铁青，两眼喷火，手飞向弯刀那镶宝石的柄，却忍住没有拔。他把这桩事里烧起来的毒——这一眼便见出他的权威败落到何地步——尽数朝马尔扎克泼去。
“你这蠢货！”他咬牙道，“瞧瞧你这份懦夫的功德。听听你那些妇人之见惹出来的鬼。你，也配指挥一条战船！你，也配到海上去打仗！安拉还不如在我生出你这么个儿子之前，先把我劈死了干净！”
马尔扎克在这一顿臭骂前直往后缩，生怕骂完了还有更狠的。他不敢回嘴，不敢辩解；那一刻他连大气也不敢出。
这厢，罗莎蒙德情急之下已抢到萨克埃尔-巴尔肘边。
“上帝在帮咱们！”她说，声音里满是滚烫的感激。“这是你的机会。这些人会听你的。”
他望着她，对她这份急切淡淡一笑。“会，小姐，他们会听我的，”他说。可就在这飞逝的片刻里，他已拿定了主意。无疑，阿萨德是对的，稳妥的路是龟缩在这处藏身的湾里——不被发觉的胜算大得很；可这些人的判断也不算全错。倘若出海，取一条偏东的航线，同样可以不露行藏；即便桨声传到了岬后那条大帆船的耳朵里，等她起锚追出来，他们早已铆足了浑身筋骨划出老远；何况风——他盘算里的一个大头——已经软成那样，一条光靠风走的船来追，只管笑话她就是。那么，剩下的只有那条船炮火的危险，而那危险有多大，萨克埃尔-巴尔凭经验再清楚不过：没多大。
就这样，他不情愿地被逼到一个结论：大体上，还是支持阿萨德更明智。既然他吃准这些人服他，便拿这个念头宽慰自己：一场“道义上的胜仗”说不定正在前头等着，从里头往后还能榨出些更靠实的进项。
于是，他回应那些还在喊他的人：一跃跳下扶梯，顺着舷梯大步走向船腰甲板，到帕夏身边站定。

===Original===

Asad watched his approach with angry misgivings; it was with him a foregone conclusion that things being as they were Sakr-el-Bahr would be ranged against him to obtain complete control of these mutineers and to cull the fullest advantage from the situation. Softly and slowly he unsheathed his scimitar, and Sakr-el-Bahr seeing this out of the corner of his eye, yet affected not to see, but stood forward to address the men.
“How now?” he thundered wrathfully. “What shall this mean? Are ye all deaf that ye have not heard the commands of your Basha, the exalted of Allah, that ye dare raise your mutinous voices and say what is your will?”
Sudden and utter silence followed that exhortation. Asad listened in relieved amazement; Rosamund caught her breath in sheer dismay.
What could he mean, then? Had he but fooled and duped her? Were his intentions towards her the very opposite to his protestations? She leant upon the poop rail straining to catch every syllable of that speech of his in the lingua franca, hoping almost that her indifferent knowledge of it had led her into error on the score of what he had said.
She saw him turn with a gesture of angry command upon Larocque, who stood there by the bulwarks, waiting.
“Back to thy post up yonder, and keep watch upon that vessel’s movements, reporting them to us. We stir not hence until such be our lord Asad’s good pleasure. Away with thee!”
Larocque without a murmur threw a leg over the bulwarks and dropped to the oars, whence he clambered ashore as he had been bidden. And not a single voice was raised in protest.
Sakr-el-Bahr’s dark glance swept the ranks of the corsairs crowding the forecastle.
“Because this pet of the harem,” he said, immensely daring, indicating Marzak by a contemptuous gesture, “bleats of danger into the ears of men, are ye all to grow timid and foolish as a herd of sheep? By Allah! What are ye? Are ye the fearless sea hawks that have flown with me, and struck where the talons of my grappling-hooks were flung, or are ye but scavenging crows?”
He was answered by an old rover whom fear had rendered greatly daring.
“We are trapped here as Dragut was trapped at Jerba.”
“Thou liest,” he answered. “Dragut was not trapped, for Dragut found a way out. And against Dragut there was the whole navy of Genoa, whilst against us there is but one single galleon.

===Chinese===

阿萨德看着他走近，怒火里掺着不祥的猜度；在他看来，事情到了这一步，萨克埃尔-巴尔必会站到对面去，把这些哗变的人整个抓到手里，把这局面里的好处摘个干净。他轻轻缓缓地抽出了弯刀。萨克埃尔-巴尔眼角里瞧见了，却只做不见，上前一步，对着众人开腔。
“这是怎么说？”他雷霆似的怒喝，“算什么意思？你们全聋了么——你们巴沙、安拉所扬的巴沙的号令没听见么——竟敢扯开你们犯上的喉咙，说你们的意愿？”
这一喝之后，霎时鸦雀无声。阿萨德惊中带慰地听着；罗莎蒙德却纯是绝望，倒抽了一口气。
那么，他到底是什么意思？莫非他一直在耍她、骗她？他对她的打算，莫非同他嘴上赌的咒正相反？她伏在艉楼栏上，一个音节一个音节地捕捉他那通混通语，几乎指望是自己那半吊子水平听岔了他话里的意思。
她见他带着恼怒的威势，转向立在舷栏边候着的拉罗克。
“回你上头的哨位去，盯死那条船的动静，随时来报。咱们的主上阿萨德一日不点头，咱们一步也不挪。去！”
拉罗克一声不吭，一条腿翻过舷栏，落到桨上，从那里照吩咐爬上了岸。没有一条嗓子提出异议。
萨克埃尔-巴尔黝黑的目光扫过挤满船头的海盗行列。
“就因为这位闺帐里的宝贝儿，”他说着，朝马尔扎克轻蔑地一摆手，这份胆量可真不小，“往你们耳朵里灌了几句凶险，你们就要全体学起娘们儿，蠢得跟一群绵羊似的了？安拉在上！你们算什么？你们是随我翱翔过的海上苍鹰，我的抓钩甩到哪里、你们就扑到哪里的一群？还是一群吃腐肉的乌鸦？”
答话的是一个老水匪，恐惧给了他天大的胆子。
“咱们叫困在这儿了，就跟德拉古特困在杰尔巴那回一样。”
“你放屁，”他答道，“德拉古特没叫困住，因为德拉古特寻着了出路。对付德拉古特的是热那亚的全副水师；对付咱们的，不过一条孤零零的大帆船。

===Original===

By the Koran, if she shows fight, have we no teeth? Will it be the first galleon whose decks we have overrun? But if ye prefer a coward’s counsel, ye sons of shame, consider that once we take the open sea our discovery will be assured, and Larocque hath told you that she carries twenty guns. I tell you that if we are to be attacked by her, best be attacked at close quarters, and I tell you that if we lie close and snug in here it is long odds that we shall never be attacked at all. That she has no inkling of our presence is proven, since she has cast anchor round the headland. And consider that if we fly from a danger that doth not exist, and in our flight are so fortunate as not to render real that danger and to court it, we abandon a rich argosy that shall bring profit to us all.”
“But I waste my breath in argument,” he ended abruptly. “You have heard the commands of your lord, Asad-ed-Din, and that should be argument enough. No more of this, then.”
Without so much as waiting to see them disperse from the rail and return to their lounging attitudes about the forecastle, he turned to Asad.
“It might have been well to hang the dog who spoke of Dragut and Jerba,” he said. “But it was never in my nature to be harsh with those who follow me.” And that was all.
Asad from amazement had passed quickly to admiration and a sort of contrition, into which presently there crept a poisonous tinge of jealousy to see Sakr-el-Bahr prevail where he himself alone must utterly have failed. This jealousy spread all-pervadingly, like an oil stain. If he had come to bear ill will to Sakr-el-Bahr before, that ill will was turned of a sudden into positive hatred for one in whom he now beheld a usurper of the power and control that should reside in the Basha alone. Assuredly there was no room for both of them in the Bashalik of Algiers.
Therefore the words of commendation which had been rising to his lips froze there now that Sakr-el-Bahr and he stood face to face. In silence he considered his lieutenant through narrowing evil eyes, whose message none but a fool could have misunderstood.
Sakr-el-Bahr was not a fool, and he did not misunderstand it for a moment. He felt a tightening at the heart, and ill will sprang to life within him responding to the call of that ill will.

===Chinese===

凭《古兰经》起誓，她要是敢接仗，咱们就没牙么？踩平她一层甲板，是头一回了？可你们要是偏爱懦夫的主意——你们这些丢人现眼的东西——那就想想：一旦出了海，咱们的行藏便是十拿九稳要败露的；拉罗克可告诉了你们，她带着二十门炮。我告诉你们：她真要打咱们，近身接仗最上算；我再说一遍：咱们在这湾里安安稳稳一猫，十有八九她压根不会来打。她对咱们的存在毫无所觉，这是明摆着的——不然她怎会绕过海岬下锚。再想想：咱们要是叫一个并不存在的凶险吓跑，跑的时候又侥幸没把那凶险招成真的、撞成真的，咱们可就白白撂下一条肥得流油的大商船——那可是人人有份的花红。”
“不过，道理讲再多也是白费，”他陡然收了话头，“你们的主上阿萨德-丁的号令，你们都听见了，那道理尽够了。这事儿就到此为止。”
他连等也不等他们散回各处、歪回船头的老样子，就转过身去对着阿萨德。
“方才那个提德拉古特、提杰尔巴的家伙，吊死他本也不冤，”他说，“可我这人天生苛刻不起来，对我跟着我的人。”就这一句。
阿萨德从惊愕里很快转入叹服，又掺进几分悔意；随后，悔意里又悄悄洇开一缕毒——眼见萨克埃尔-巴尔在他一个人必输的局面里赢了，一缕妒意。这妒意四下漫开，像一片油渍。如果说他先前对萨克埃尔-巴尔还只是怀怨，这怨此刻陡然化成了真恨——他如今在这人身上看见的，是一个僭主，僭了只该归巴沙一人的权柄。阿尔及尔的帕夏之位，断然容不下他们两个。
于是，那些涌到嘴边的嘉许，在萨克埃尔-巴尔同他面对面的这一刻，冻在了那里。他默然打量他的副手，一双恶眼眯细，眼里那份口信，除了傻子谁也不会错解。
萨克埃尔-巴尔不是傻子，一刻也没错解。他觉得心口一紧，怨毒应着那怨毒的召唤，在他心里活了起来。

===Original===

Almost he repented him that he had not availed himself of that moment of weakness and mutiny on the part of the crew to attempt the entire superseding of the Basha.
The conciliatory words he had in mind to speak he now suppressed. To that venomous glance he opposed his ever ready mockery. He turned to Biskaine.
“Withdraw,” he curtly bade him, “and take that stout sea-warrior with thee.” And he indicated Marzak.
Biskaine turned to the Basha. “Is it thy wish, my lord?” he asked.
Asad nodded in silence, and motioned him away together with the cowed Marzak.
“My lord,” said Sakr-el-Bahr, when they were alone, “yesterday I made thee a proposal for the healing of this breach between us, and it was refused. But now had I been the traitor and mutineer thou hast dubbed me I could have taken full advantage of the humour of my corsairs. Had I done that it need no longer have been mine to propose or to sue. Instead it would have been mine to dictate. Since I have given thee such crowning proof of my loyalty, it is my hope and trust that I may be restored to the place I had lost in thy confidence, and that this being so thou wilt accede now to that proposal of mine concerning the Frankish woman yonder.”
It was unfortunate perhaps that she should have been standing there unveiled upon the poop within the range of Asad’s glance; for the sight of her it may have been that overcame his momentary hesitation and stifled the caution which prompted him to accede. He considered her a moment, and a faint colour kindled in his cheeks which anger had made livid.
“It is not for thee, Sakr-el-Bahr,” he answered at length, “to make me proposals. To dare it, proves thee far removed indeed from the loyalty thy lips profess. Thou knowest my will concerning her. Once hast thou thwarted and defied me, misusing to that end the Prophet’s Holy Law. Continue a barrier in my path and it shall be at thy peril.” His voice was raised and it shook with anger.
“Not so loud,” said Sakr-el-Bahr, his eyes gleaming with a response of anger. “For should my men overhear these threats of thine I will not answer for what may follow. I oppose thee at my peril sayest thou. Be it so, then.” He smiled grimly. “It is war between us, Asad, since thou hast chosen it. Remember hereafter when the consequences come to overwhelm thee that the choice was thine.”
“Thou mutinous, treacherous son of a dog!” blazed Asad.

===Chinese===

他几乎后悔，方才没趁水手们那一阵怯懦的哗变，把巴沙整个掀翻。
他原备下的那几句转圜话，此刻也咽了回去。对着那道毒眼，他祭出他从不离手的讥诮。他转向比斯坎。
“退下，”他简短地吩咐，“把那位了不起的海上豪杰也带走。”他朝马尔扎克一指。
比斯坎望向巴沙。“这是您的意思么，大人？”他问。
阿萨德默然点头，挥手叫他同那个蔫头耷脑的马尔扎克一并退下。
“大人，”只剩下两人时，萨克埃尔-巴尔开口道，“昨天我为弥合你我之间的这道裂缝，向你提过一回议，被你回了。可今天，倘若我真如你骂的那样，是个叛贼、是个乱党，我这班海盗的心气，我尽可以拿它大用一场。真用了，此刻就用不着我来提议、来恳求，该轮到我发号施令了。我既给了你这样顶天的忠心实证，便指望、也信托：我能收回在你心里失掉的位置；既然如此，如今你就该允了我那桩提议——关于那边那位法兰克女子的。”
不巧的是，她那时正不戴面纱立在艉楼上，正落在阿萨德的眼界里；多一半正是她那身姿，压倒了他一时的犹豫，掐灭了他本待应允的审慎。他把她端详了片刻，两颊——方才怒得发青的——泛起一层淡淡的红。
“轮不到你，萨克埃尔-巴尔，”他终于答道，“来向我提什么议。敢这样放肆，就证明你离你嘴上表的白差得远。她的处置，我的意思你知道。你已经拦过我、抗过我一回，还滥用了先知的圣法来达到那个目的。再挡我的道，就拿你的命来挡。”他的嗓门抬高了，气得直抖。
“小声些，”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，眼里应着怒火闪出光来，“叫我的手下听见了你这些威吓，后面出什么事，我可担保不了。你说我挡你是拿命挡。那就这样吧。”他冷冷一笑。“这是你我之间的战争了，阿萨德，既然是你选的。往后祸事临头、把你淹没的时候，记着：这个选择是你自己做的。”
“你这犯上作乱、狗娘养的贼种！”阿萨德炸了。

===Original===

Sakr-el-Bahr turned on his heel. “Pursue the path of an old man’s folly,” he said over his shoulder, “and see whither it will lead thee.”
Upon that he strode away up the gangway to the poop, leaving the Basha alone with his anger and some slight fear evoked by that last bold menace. But notwithstanding that he menaced boldly the heart of Sakr-el-Bahr was surcharged with anxiety. He had conceived a plan; but between the conception and its execution he realized that much ill might lie.
“Mistress,” he addressed Rosamund as he stepped upon the poop. “You are not wise to show yourself so openly.”
To his amazement she met him with a hostile glance.
“Not wise?” said she, her countenance scornful. “You mean that I may see more than was intended for me. What game do you play here, sir, that you tell me one thing and show me by your actions that you desire another?”
He did not need to ask her what she meant. At once he perceived how she had misread the scene she had witnessed.
“I’ll but remind you,” he said very gravely, “that once before you did me a wrong by overhasty judgment, as has been proven to you.”
It overthrew some of her confidence. “But then.﻿ ﻿…” she began.
“I do but ask you to save your judgment for the end. If I live I shall deliver you. Meanwhile I beg that you will keep your cabin. It does not help me that you be seen.”
She looked at him, a prayer for explanation trembling on her lips. But before the calm command of his tone and glance she slowly lowered her head and withdrew beyond the curtain.

===Chinese===

萨克埃尔-巴尔一转身。“那就顺着老头儿的糊涂道走到黑吧，”他回头撂下一句，“看它把你领到哪里去。”
说罢，他大步顺着舷梯走上艉楼，丢下巴沙一个人，守着他的一腔怒火，还有那最后一记大胆威吓勾起的一点惧。可他虽然威吓得响亮，萨克埃尔-巴尔的心里却塞满了焦灼。他已有了一个计划；可他明白，从计划到落成之间，还横着不知多少凶险。
“小姐，”他踏上艉楼，对罗莎蒙德说，“这样抛头露面，可不明智。”
出乎他意料，她回敬他的竟是一眼敌意。
“不明智？”她说，满脸讥诮。“你是说，怕我看见的比你要给我看的多。你在这儿玩的什么把戏，先生？嘴上同我说一套，行动上又叫我看明白你要的是另一套？”
他用不着问她指什么。他立刻看明白，她把她方才目睹的那一幕读岔了。
“我只想提醒你一句，”他说得极郑重，“从前你已经有一回，栽在贸然论断上，冤了我——这个已经证给你看了。”
这推翻了她几分底气。“可是……”她开口道。
“我只求你把论断留到最后。我只要活着，就一定送你脱身。这期间，我求你待在你的舱房里。你被人瞧见，帮不了我。”
她望着他，一句求解释的话在唇边打颤。可他语气和目光里那份沉静的号令，叫她慢慢垂下头，退到帘子后面去了。

## XVI The Pannier / 驮篮
===Original===

He was still pacing there when an hour or so before sunset﻿—some fifteen hours after setting out﻿—they stood before the entrance of a long bottlenecked cove under the shadow of the cliffs of Aquila Point on the southern coast of the Island of Formentera. He was rendered aware of this and roused from his abstraction by the voice of Asad calling to him from the poop and commanding him to make the cove.
Already the wind was failing them, and it became necessary to take to the oars, as must in any case have happened once they were through the cove’s narrow neck in the becalmed lagoon beyond. So Sakr-el-Bahr, in his turn, lifted up his voice, and in answer to his shout came Vigitello and Larocque.
A blast of Vigitello’s whistle brought his own men to heel, and they passed rapidly along the benches ordering the rowers to make ready, whilst Jasper and a half-dozen Muslim sailors set about furling the sails that already were beginning to flap in the shifting and intermittent gusts of the expiring wind. Sakr-el-Bahr gave the word to row, and Vigitello blew a second and longer blast. The oars dipped, the slaves strained and the *galeasse* ploughed forward, time being kept by a boatswain’s mate who squatted on the waist deck and beat a tom-tom rhythmically. Sakr-el-Bahr, standing on the poop deck, shouted his orders to the steersmen in their niches on either side of the stern, and skilfully the vessel was manoeuvred through the narrow passage into the calm lagoon whose depths were crystal clear. Here before coming to rest, Sakr-el-Bahr followed the invariable corsair practice of going about, so as to be ready to leave his moorings and make for the open again at a moment’s notice.
She came at last alongside the rocky buttresses of a gentle slope that was utterly deserted by all save a few wild goats browsing near the summit. There were clumps of broom, thick with golden flower, about the base of the hill. Higher, a few gnarled and aged olive trees reared their grey heads from which the rays of the westering sun struck a glint as of silver.
Larocque and a couple of sailors went over the bulwarks on the larboard quarter, dropped lightly to the horizontal shafts of the oars, which were rigidly poised, and walking out upon them gained the rocks and proceeded to make fast the vessel by ropes fore and aft.

===Chinese===

他还在那里踱着，约莫日落前一个钟头——动身后约莫十五个钟头——船已停在一处长长的、瓶颈似的湾口前，头顶是福门特拉岛南岸鹰角峭壁的阴影。把他从出神里唤醒的，是阿萨德从艉楼上唤他的声音，吩咐他把船开进湾去。
风已经不行了，只得下桨——横竖一过湾口的窄颈、进了里头风平浪静的礁湖，也免不了下桨。于是萨克埃尔-巴尔也提起嗓门一喊，维吉特洛和拉罗克应声而来。
维吉特洛一声唿哨收拢了他的人手，他们飞快地沿桨位一路传下去，叫桨手预备；贾斯珀带着六名穆斯林水手着手收帆——垂死的风一阵一阵、忽东忽西地扑打着，帆已经开始乱拍。萨克埃尔-巴尔传令开桨，维吉特洛吹了第二声、更长的一声唿哨。桨叶入水，奴隶们绷紧了身子，桨帆船犁水而前；船腰甲板上蹲着一名水手长助手，有板有眼地敲着一面小鼓定着拍子。萨克埃尔-巴尔立在艉楼甲板上，朝船尾两侧舵龛里的舵手们发号施令，船灵巧地穿过那条窄道，驶进清澈见底的静水礁湖。进湖之后、下碇之前，萨克埃尔-巴尔照海盗的老规矩掉了船头，以便随时解缆，一刻不停地就能重新冲向大海。
船终于贴上一道缓坡的岩石壁脚，那里除了坡顶附近几只啃草的野山羊，荒无人迹。山脚一带丛生着金雀花，金黄的花开得正稠。再高处，几株虬结苍老的橄榄树探出灰白的头颅，西斜的日头照在上面，闪出一层银也似的微光。
拉罗克带着两名水手翻过左舷后侧的船栏，轻巧地落在平悬着的桨杆上，踩着桨杆走到岸石上，前后系缆，把船固定住。

===Original===

Sakr-el-Bahr’s next task was to set a watch, and he appointed Larocque, sending him to take his station on the summit of the head whence a wide range of view was to be commanded.
Pacing the poop with Marzak the Basha grew reminiscent of former days when roving the seas as a simple corsair he had used this cove both for purposes of ambush and concealment. There were, he said, few harbours in all the Mediterranean so admirably suited to the corsairs’ purpose as this; it was a haven of refuge in case of peril, and an unrivalled lurking place in which to lie in wait for the prey. He remembered once having lain there with the formidable Dragut-Reis, a fleet of six galleys, their presence entirely unsuspected by the Genoese admiral, Doria, who had passed majestically along with three caravels and seven galleys.
Marzak, pacing beside his father, listened but half-heartedly to these reminiscences. His mind was all upon Sakr-el-Bahr, and his suspicions of that palmetto bale were quickened by the manner in which for the last two hours he had seen the corsair hovering thoughtfully in its neighbourhood.
He broke in suddenly upon his father’s memories with an expression of what was in his mind.
“The thanks to Allah,” he said, “that it is thou who command this expedition, else might this cove’s advantages have been neglected.”
“Not so,” said Asad. “Sakr-el-Bahr knows them as well as I do. He has used this vantage point aforetime. It was himself who suggested that this would be the very place in which to await this Spanish craft.”
“Yet had he sailed alone I doubt if the Spanish argosy had concerned him greatly. There are other matters on his mind, O my father. Observe him yonder, all lost in thought. How many hours of this voyage has he spent thus. He is as a man trapped and desperate. There is some fear rankling in him. Observe him, I say.”
“Allah pardon thee,” said his father, shaking his old head and sighing over so much impetuosity of judgment. “Must thy imagination be forever feeding on thy malice? Yet I blame not thee, but thy Sicilian mother, who has fostered this hostility in thee. Did she not hoodwink me into making this unnecessary voyage?”
“I see thou hast forgot last night and the Frankish slave-girl,” said his son.
“Nay, then thou seest wrong. I have not forgot it.

===Chinese===

萨克埃尔-巴尔的下一桩事是派哨。他点了拉罗克，打发他上海岬顶上站位——从那里，四下的海面尽收眼底。
巴沙同马尔扎克在艉楼上踱着，起了怀旧之兴：当年他还是个单干的海盗在海上闯的时候，这处湾子，设伏、藏身，两样都用过。他说，全地中海里，像这样合海盗心意的港口可不多：遇险，它是个避风的窝；守食，它是个没处比的埋伏地。他记得有一回，他同那位威名赫赫的德拉古特-莱斯就窝在这里，带着六条大帆船的舰队，而热那亚海军上将多里亚竟毫无所觉，带着三条卡拉维尔帆船和七条大帆船，堂堂皇皇地从湾外驶了过去。
马尔扎克在父亲身边踱着，这些旧话只听了个半心半意。他的心思全在萨克埃尔-巴尔身上；方才两个钟头，他眼见那海盗一副沉思的样子，围着那只棕榈席包打转，他对那只包的疑心又活了起来。
他猛地打断父亲的追忆，把心里的念头说了出来。
“感谢安拉，”他说，“这一趟是您统兵；换个旁人，这湾子的好处怕就白撂了。”
“不然，”阿萨德说，“这湾子的好处，萨克埃尔-巴尔同我一样门儿清。他从前也用过这块宝地。正是他自己提的：等那条西班牙船，就该在这儿等。”
“可他要是独个儿带队，我看那条西班牙商船他还未必放在心上。他心里装着别的事呢，父亲大人。您瞧瞧他在那边，魂不守舍的样子。这一路船行，他这样发了多少个钟头的呆？活像个落了套、急红了眼的人。他心里扎着一根怕的刺。我说，您瞧瞧他。”
“安拉饶恕你，”他父亲说着，摇了摇他那颗苍老的头，为这份急口的论断叹了口气。“你的想象，就非得拿你的恶意当饭吃么？不过我也不怪你，怪你那西西里人的娘，是她在你身上养出这份敌意。不是她哄着我，我何至于跑这一趟冤枉水路？”
“我看您是把昨夜那个法兰克女奴忘了吧。”他儿子说。
“不，这你可看岔了。我没有忘。

===Original===

But neither have I forgot that since Allah hath exalted me to be Basha of Algiers, He looks to me to deal in justice. Come, Marzak, set an end to all this. Perhaps tomorrow thou shalt see him in battle, and after such a sight as that never again wilt thou dare say evil of him. Come, make thy peace with him, and let me see better relations betwixt you hereafter.”
And raising his voice he called Sakr-el-Bahr, who immediately turned and came up the gangway. Marzak stood by in a sulky mood, with no notion of doing his father’s will by holding out an olive branch to the man who was like to cheat him of his birthright ere all was done. Yet was it he who greeted Sakr-el-Bahr when the corsair set foot upon the poop.
“Does the thought of the coming fight perturb thee, dog of war?” he asked.
“Am I perturbed, pup of peace?” was the crisp answer.
“It seems so. Thine aloofness, thine abstractions.﻿ ﻿…”
“Are signs of perturbation, dost suppose?”
“Of what else?”
Sakr-el-Bahr laughed. “Thou’lt tell me next that I am afraid. Yet I should counsel thee to wait until thou hast smelt blood and powder, and learnt precisely what fear is.”
The slight altercation drew the attention of Asad’s officers who were idling there. Biskaine and some three others lounged forward to stand behind the Basha, looking, on in some amusement, which was shared by him.
“Indeed, indeed,” said Asad, laying a hand upon Marzak’s shoulder, “his counsel is sound enough. Wait, boy, until thou hast gone beside him aboard the infidel, ere thou judge him easily perturbed.”
Petulantly Marzak shook off that gnarled old hand. “Dost thou, O my father, join with him in taunting me upon my lack of knowledge. My youth is a sufficient answer. But at least,” he added, prompted by a wicked notion suddenly conceived, “at least you cannot taunt me with lack of address with weapons.”
“Give him room,” said Sakr-el-Bahr, with ironical good-humour, “and he will show us prodigies.”
Marzak looked at him with narrowing, gleaming eyes. “Give me a crossbow,” he retorted, “and I’ll show thee how to shoot,” was his amazing boast.
“Thou’lt show him?” roared Asad. “Thou’lt show him!” And his laugh rang loud and hearty. “Go smear the sun’s face with clay, boy.”
“Reserve thy judgment, O my father,” begged Marzak, with frosty dignity.
“Boy, thou’rt mad! Why, Sakr-el-Bahr’s quarrel will check a swallow in its flight.”

===Chinese===

可我同样没忘：安拉既把我扬到阿尔及尔巴沙的位上，祂就要我行事公道。好了，马尔扎克，这一篇翻过去吧。兴许明天你就能亲眼看他上阵厮杀；看过那样一场，看你还敢不敢再说他半个不字。来，同他讲和，往后你们俩，叫我看你俩好好的。”
他扬声唤萨克埃尔-巴尔。那人应声转身，从舷梯上来。马尔扎克立在一旁，一脸的悻悻，全无照父亲的意思朝那人递橄榄枝的念头——这个人，到头来多半要骗走他名分的产业。可萨克埃尔-巴尔一脚踏上艉楼，先开口的偏偏又是他。
“想起要来的那场仗，心里发慌了么，战狗？”他问。
“我发慌了么，太平崽子？”回答干脆利落。
“看样子是。你这副避人的样子，这副魂不守舍的样子……”
“依你看，就是发慌的兆头？”
“不是这，又是什么？”
萨克埃尔-巴尔笑了。“下一步你就要说我害怕了。可我劝你一句：等你先闻过血腥和硝烟、弄明白了害怕到底是个什么东西，再说这话不迟。”
这一场小口角引来了在一旁闲晃的军官们。比斯坎和另外三四个人踱上前来，站在巴沙身后，饶有兴味地看着，巴沙自己也看得有趣。
“的确，的确，”阿萨德把手搭在马尔扎克肩上，“他这句劝的在理。等着吧，孩子，等你同他一道跳上异教徒的甲板之后，再轻易说他沉不住气不迟。”
马尔扎克一把甩开那只青筋盘结的老手，赌气似的。“父亲大人，您也同他一道，拿我未经阵来讥我？我年纪轻，这一条就是现成的回答。可至少，”他忽然起了一个歹毒的念头，补上一句，“至少你们没法讥我手上没有功夫。”
“给他让出地方来，”萨克埃尔-巴尔好脾气地带着讥诮说，“他就要显神通给咱们瞧了。”
马尔扎克眯起发亮的眼睛瞪着他。“给我一张弩，”他回敬道，“我就射给你看看”——这牛皮吹得惊人。
“你射给他看？”阿萨德吼道，“你射给他看！”他的笑声响亮而痛快。“去拿泥把太阳的脸抹了吧，孩子。”
“您先把断语收起来，父亲大人，”马尔扎克带着冷冰冰的架子求告道。
“孩子，你疯了！哼，萨克埃尔-巴尔的箭，飞着的燕子都叫得住。”

===Original===

“That is his boast, belike,” replied Marzak.
“And what may thine be?” quoth Sakr-el-Bahr. “To hit the Island of Formentera at this distance?”
“Dost dare to sneer at me?” cried Marzak, ruffling.
“What daring would that ask?” wondered Sakr-el-Bahr.
“By Allah, thou shalt learn.”
“In all humility I await the lesson.”
“And thou shalt have it,” was the answer viciously delivered. Marzak strode to the rail. “Ho there! Vigitello! A crossbow for me, and another for Sakr-el-Bahr.”
Vigitello sprang to obey him, whilst Asad shook his head and laughed again.
“An it were not against the Prophet’s law to make a wager.﻿ ﻿…” he was beginning, when Marzak interrupted him.
“Already should I have proposed one.”
“So that,” said Sakr-el-Bahr, “thy purse would come to match thine head for emptiness.”
Marzak looked at him and sneered. Then he snatched from Vigitello’s hands one of the crossbows that he bore and set a shaft to it. And then at last Sakr-el-Bahr was to learn the malice that was at the root of all this odd pretence.
“Look now,” said the youth, “there is on that palmetto bale a speck of pitch scarce larger than the pupil of my eye. Thou’lt need to strain thy sight to see it. Observe how my shaft will find it. Canst thou better such a shot?”
His eyes, upon Sakr-el-Bahr’s face, watching it closely, observed the pallor by which it was suddenly overspread. But the corsair’s recovery was almost as swift. He laughed, seeming so entirely careless that Marzak began to doubt whether he had paled indeed or whether his own imagination had led him to suppose it.
“Ay, thou’lt choose invisible marks, and wherever the arrow enters thou’lt say ’twas there! An old trick, O Marzak. Go cozen women with it.”
“Then,” said Marzak, “we will take instead the slender cord that binds the bale.” And he levelled his bow. But Sakr-el-Bahr’s hand closed upon his arm in an easy yet paralyzing grip.
“Wait,” he said. “Thou’lt choose another mark for several reasons. For one, I’ll not have thy shaft blundering through my oarsmen and haply killing one of them. Most of them are slaves specially chosen for their brawn, and I cannot spare any. Another reason is that the mark is a foolish one. The distance is not more than ten paces. A childish test, which, maybe, is the reason why thou hast chosen it.”
Marzak lowered his bow and Sakr-el-Bahr released his arm.

===Chinese===

“那是他吹的，”马尔扎克答道。
“那你的牛皮又是什么？”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，“这个距离上，一箭射中福门特拉岛？”
“你敢讥笑我？”马尔扎克炸了毛。
“这要什么胆量？”萨克埃尔-巴尔故作不解。
“凭安拉起誓，叫你领教。”
“我诚惶诚恐，恭候这一课。”
“你领教定了。”这句话咬着牙吐出来。马尔扎克大步走到栏边。“喂！维吉特洛！拿一张弩来给我，再拿一张给萨克埃尔-巴尔。”
维吉特洛飞奔照办；阿萨德摇着头又笑了。
“要不是先知的法度不许赌钱……”他刚起了个头，马尔扎克便打断了他。
“若不是法度不许，我早开赌了。”
“好叫你那钱袋，”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，“同你那脑壳一般空。”
马尔扎克白了他一眼，冷笑一声，从维吉特洛手里夺过一张弩，搭上箭。直到这时，萨克埃尔-巴尔才算看清了这一番古怪做作底下扎着的毒。
“你瞧，”那少年说，“那只棕榈席包上有一星柏油点儿，比我瞳仁大不了多少。你得眯细了眼才瞧得见。看我的箭怎么找上它。这样的箭法，你能比么？”
他的眼睛盯在萨克埃尔-巴尔脸上，盯得紧，看见了那张脸上霎时漫过的一层白。可那海盗回气也快得惊人。他哈哈一笑，那份满不在乎做足了，倒叫马尔扎克疑心起来：他到底是真白了脸，还是自己想岔了。
“是啊，你专挑看不见的靶子，箭落在哪里，你就说它原先在哪里！老把戏了，马尔扎克。拿去哄娘儿们吧。”
“那么，”马尔扎克说，“咱们就改射捆包的那根细绳。”他端平了弩。可萨克埃尔-巴尔的手已经扣上他的胳膊，那一份轻松的钳制，叫他动弹不得。
“慢着，”他说，“这靶子你得换一个，理由有好几条。头一条，我不许你的箭在我桨手堆里乱窜，没准报销掉一个。他们多半是特意挑来的壮劳力，一个也折损不起。第二条，这靶子蠢。距离不过十步。娃娃的考题——多半正为这个，你才挑它。”
马尔扎克放低了弩，萨克埃尔-巴尔松开他的胳膊。

===Original===

They looked at each other, the corsair supremely master of himself and smiling easily, no faintest trace of the terror that was in his soul showing upon his swarthy bearded countenance or in his hard pale eyes.
He pointed up the hillside to the nearest olive tree, a hundred paces distant. “Yonder,” he said, “is a man’s mark. Put me a shaft through the long branch of that first olive.”
Asad and his officers voiced approval.
“A man’s mark, indeed,” said the Basha, “so that he be a marksman.”
But Marzak shrugged his shoulders with make-believe contempt. “I knew he would refuse the mark I set,” said he. “As for the olive-branch, it is so large a butt that a child could not miss it at this distance.”
“If a child could not, then thou shouldst not,” said Sakr-el-Bahr, who had so placed himself that his body was now between Marzak and the palmetto bale. “Let us see thee hit it, O Marzak.” And as he spoke he raised his crossbow, and scarcely seeming to take aim, he loosed his shaft. It flashed away to be checked, quivering, in the branch he had indicated.
A chorus of applause and admiration greeted the shot, and drew the attention of all the crew to what was toward.
Marzak tightened his lips, realizing how completely he had been outwitted. Willy-nilly he must now shoot at that mark. The choice had been taken out of his hands by Sakr-el-Bahr. He never doubted that he must cover himself with ridicule in the performance, and that there he would be constrained to abandon this pretended match.
“By the Koran,” said Biskaine, “thou’lt need all thy skill to equal such a shot, Marzak.”
“ ’Twas not the mark I chose,” replied Marzak sullenly.
“Thou wert the challenger, O Marzak,” his father reminded him. “Therefore the choice of mark was his. He chose a man’s mark, and by the beard of Mohammed, he showed us a man’s shot.”
Marzak would have flung the bow from him in that moment, abandoning the method he had chosen to investigate the contents of that suspicious palmetto bale; but he realized that such a course must now cover him with scorn. Slowly he levelled his bow at that distant mark.
“Have a care of the sentinel on the hilltop,” Sakr-el-Bahr admonished him, provoking a titter.
Angrily the youth drew the bow. The cord hummed, and the shaft sped to bury itself in the hill’s flank a dozen yards from the mark.

两人对望着。那海盗从容到了极处，嘴角挂着轻松的笑；他灵魂里那一场惊惶，在他黝黑的须髯脸上、在他冷硬的浅色眼睛里，寻不出一丝踪影。
他抬手朝山坡上一指，指向最近的那株橄榄树，一百步开外。“那儿，”他说，“才是汉子的靶子。给我一箭，穿那头一株橄榄树的长枝。”
阿萨德和他的军官们齐声叫好。
“是汉子的靶子，”巴沙说，“——只要他是个射手。”
可马尔扎克拿做作的轻蔑耸了耸肩。“我就知道他会推了我挑的靶子，”他说，“至于那橄榄枝，靶子大得这个距离上娃娃也失不了手。”
“娃娃失不了手，那你更不该失手，”萨克埃尔-巴尔说——他不知什么时候挪了位，身子已经隔在马尔扎克同那只棕榈席包之间。“射给咱们瞧瞧，马尔扎克。”话音里他抬起弩，看也不像看地一箭放出去。箭光一闪，钉进他指的那根枝上，箭羽犹自颤动。
一片喝彩赞叹迎着这一箭，把全船人的注意都引了过来。
马尔扎克咬紧了嘴唇：他叫人彻底将了一军。愿不愿意，他都得朝那个靶子射了。选择的权柄，已经从萨克埃尔-巴尔手里回到了他手里——不，是从他手里到了萨克埃尔-巴尔手里。他毫不怀疑，这一射必把自己变成笑柄，到那儿他就只好撂下这场假比赛了。
“凭《古兰经》说，”比斯坎道，“要追平这一箭，你那手本事得全拿出来喽，马尔扎克。”
“那不是我挑的靶子，”马尔扎克绷着脸答。
“是你下的战书，马尔扎克，”他父亲提醒他，“所以挑靶子的权在他。他挑了汉子的靶子，而且凭穆罕默德的胡须说，他也射出了汉子的一箭。”
马尔扎克恨不得当场把弓摔了，撂下这个用来探那只可疑席包内里的法子；可他明白，这一撂，才真把自己撂成笑柄。他慢慢举起弩，对准那个远靶。
“留神山顶上那位放哨的，”萨克埃尔-巴尔好意叮嘱，惹起一片窃笑。
那少年恼火地张弓。弓弦嗡的一声，箭飞出去，扎进离靶一二十步的山坡上。

===Original===

Since he was the son of the Basha none dared to laugh outright save his father and Sakr-el-Bahr. But there was no suppressing a titter to express the mockery to which the proven braggart must ever be exposed.
Asad looked at him, smiling almost sadly. “See now,” he said, “what comes of boasting thyself against Sakr-el-Bahr.”
“My will was crossed in the matter of a mark,” was the bitter answer. “You angered me and made my aim untrue.”
Sakr-el-Bahr strode away to the starboard bulwarks, deeming the matter at an end. Marzak observed him.
“Yet at that small mark,” he said, “I challenge him again.” As he spoke he fitted a second shaft to his bow. “Behold!” he cried, and took aim.
But swift as thought, Sakr-el-Bahr﻿—heedless now of all consequences﻿—levelled at Marzak the bow which he still held.
“Hold!” he roared. “Loose thy shaft at that bale, and I loose this at thy throat. I never miss!” he added grimly.
There was a startled movement in the ranks of those who stood behind Marzak. In speechless amazement they stared at Sakr-el-Bahr, as he stood there, white-faced, his eyes aflash, his bow drawn taut and ready to launch that death-laden quarrel as he threatened.
Slowly then, smiling with unutterable malice, Marzak lowered his bow. He was satisfied. His true aim was reached. He had drawn his enemy into self-betrayal.
Asad’s was the voice that shattered that hush of consternation.
“*Kellamullah!*” he bellowed. “What is this? Art thou mad, too, O Sakr-el-Bahr?”
“Ay, mad indeed,” said Marzak; “mad with fear.” And he stepped quickly aside so that the body of Biskaine should shield him from any sudden consequences of his next words. “Ask him what he keeps in that pannier, O my father.”
“Ay, what, in Allah’s name?” demanded the Basha, advancing towards his captain.
Sakr-el-Bahr lowered his bow, master of himself again. His composure was beyond all belief.
“I carry in it goods of price, which I’ll not see riddled to please a pert boy,” he said.
“Goods of price?” echoed Asad, with a snort. “They’ll need to be of price indeed that are valued above the life of my son. Let us see these goods of price.” And to the men upon the waist deck he shouted, “Open me that pannier.”
Sakr-el-Bahr sprang forward, and laid a hand upon the Basha’s arm.
“Stay, my lord!” he entreated almost fiercely. “Consider that this pannier is my own.

他到底是巴沙的儿子，除了他父亲和萨克埃尔-巴尔，没人敢放声笑。可这一位当场验明的吹牛大王，免不了受人讪笑，那一阵嗤嗤的窃笑，是怎么也压不住的。
阿萨德望着他，笑得近乎惋惜。“瞧见了吧，”他说，“同萨克埃尔-巴尔逞能，是个什么下场。”
“都怪你们搅了我挑靶子的主意，”回答满是怨气。“你们惹恼了我，叫我的准头都歪了。”
萨克埃尔-巴尔大步走开，到右舷船栏边去，只当这事完了。马尔扎克拿眼睛盯着他。
“可方才那个小靶子，”他说，“我再同他比一回。”说话间他往弩上搭了第二支箭。“瞧着！”他喊一声，端起来瞄准。
可是快得像念头一样，萨克埃尔-巴尔——此时再顾不得一切后果——把他手里的弩直直对准了马尔扎克。
“住手！”他吼道。“你的箭敢朝那包放，我这箭就放你喉咙。我从无不中！”他又冷冷添了一句。
马尔扎克身后的人群里起了一阵惊动。众人哑然瞪着萨克埃尔-巴尔：他立在那里，脸色发白，两眼喷火，弓已拉满，那支索命的箭就悬在他威吓的那个地方。
马尔扎克慢慢地、带着说不出的歹毒笑着，放低了弩。他满意了。他真正的目的达到了：他钓着他的敌人自己招了。
打破这一场惊惶的死寂的，是阿萨德的声音。
“安拉在上！”他吼道，“这是干什么？你也疯了么，萨克埃尔-巴尔？”
“是啊，疯得不轻，”马尔扎克说，“吓疯了。”他迅速闪身，拿比斯坎的身子挡在自己前面，防他下一句话引来什么急变。“问问他那驮篮里装的是什么，父亲大人。”
“是啊，凭安拉说，装的什么？”巴沙追问着，朝他的船长逼近一步。
萨克埃尔-巴尔放下弩，重新做回了他自己。那份镇定，简直叫人没法相信。
“里头装的是值钱的货，”他说，“我不能看着它叫一个轻狂小子射成筛子，取他的乐。”
“值钱的货？”阿萨德哼了一声。“得值多大的价，才压得过我儿子的命？叫咱们见识见识这值钱的货。”他朝船腰甲板上的人喝道：“给我把那驮篮打开。”
萨克埃尔-巴尔抢上一步，一把按住巴沙的胳膊。
“慢着，大人！”他几乎是厉声恳求。“您想想，这驮篮是我自己的。

===Original===

That its contents are my property; that none has a right to.﻿ ﻿…”
“Wouldst babble of rights to me, who am thy lord?” blazed the Basha, now in a towering passion. “Open me that pannier, I say.”
They were quick to his bidding. The ropes were slashed away, and the front of the pannier fell open on its palmetto hinges. There was a half-repressed chorus of amazement from the men. Sakr-el-Bahr stood frozen in horror of what must follow.
“What is it? What have you found?” demanded Asad.
In silence the men swung the bale about, and disclosed to the eyes of those upon the poop deck the face and form of Rosamund Godolphin. Then Sakr-el-Bahr, rousing himself from his trance of horror, reckless of all but her, flung down the gangway to assist her from the pannier, and thrusting aside those who stood about her, took his stand at her side.

这里头的东西是我的私产；谁也没有权利……”
“你同我谈权利？我是你的主上！”巴沙炸了，怒气冲天。“我说了，把那驮篮给我打开。”
手下人奉命飞快。捆绳砍断，驮篮的正面顺着棕榈席的合页塌了开来。人群中迸出一片压不住的惊呼。萨克埃尔-巴尔僵立在那里，为着接踵必至的那一幕不寒而栗。
“是什么？你们翻出了什么？”阿萨德喝问。
众人一言不发，把那只包转了过来——罗莎蒙德·戈多尔芬的脸和身段，就此撞进艉楼上众人的眼睛。萨克埃尔-巴尔这才从那惊骇的怔忡里挣醒，什么都顾不得、只顾得她：他顺着舷梯飞奔而下，去搀她出驮篮，又拨开围着她的人，在她身旁站定。

## IV The Raid / 夜袭
===Original===

In the estuary of the River Fal a splendid ship, on the building of which the most cunning engineers had been employed and no money spared, rode proudly at anchor just off Smithick under the very shadow of the heights crowned by the fine house of Arwenack. She was fitting out for a distant voyage and for days the work of bringing stores and munitions aboard had been in progress, so that there was an unwonted bustle about the little forge and the huddle of cottages that went to make up the fishing village, as if in earnest of the great traffic that in future days was to be seen about that spot. For Sir John Killigrew seemed at last to be on the eve of prevailing and of laying there the foundations of the fine port of his dreams.
To this state of things his friendship with Master Lionel Tressilian had contributed not a little. The opposition made to his project by Sir Oliver﻿—and supported, largely at Sir Oliver’s suggestion, by Truro and Helston﻿—had been entirely withdrawn by Lionel; more, indeed Lionel had actually gone so far in the opposite direction as to support Sir John in his representations to Parliament and the Queen. It followed naturally enough that just as Sir Oliver’s opposition of that cherished project had been the seed of the hostility between Arwenack and Penarrow, so Lionel’s support of it became the root of the staunch friendship that sprang up between himself and Sir John.
What Lionel lacked of his brother’s keen intelligence he made up for in cunning. He realized that although at some future time it was possible that Helston and Truro and the Tressilian property there might come to suffer as a consequence of the development of a port so much more advantageously situated, yet that could not be in his own lifetime; and meanwhile he must earn in return Sir John’s support for his suit of Rosamund Godolphin and thus find the Godolphin estates merged with his own. This certain immediate gain was to Master Lionel well worth the other future possible loss.
It must not, however, be supposed that Lionel’s courtship had thenceforward run a smooth and easy course.

===Chinese===

法尔河口的湾水里，一条气派的船昂然泊着锚，就停在史密尼克外不远处，头顶便是阿文纳克那座漂亮宅子踞着的高地。造这条船，雇的是最能干的工程师，花的钱不设上限。她正在做出远航的整备，连日来粮秣军械源源上船；那座小铁匠铺和那簇凑成渔村的农舍四周，便有了罕见的忙碌，倒像是预先预演着日后那一片地方将有的兴旺——因为约翰·基利格鲁爵士眼看终于要如愿了，要在他梦想中的良港打地基了。
这局面，同莱昂内尔·特雷西利安少爷的交情大有干系。奥利弗爵士对那项工程曾有的反对——又拉上特鲁罗和赫尔森一起反对——莱昂内尔把它整个撤了；不止撤了，他还掉头走得更远，竟在约翰爵士向议会和女王的呈请上署了名。于是顺理成章：一如奥利弗爵士反对那项心爱的工程，播下了阿文纳克同佩纳罗结仇的种子；莱昂内尔的支持，也就成了他同约翰爵士之间深厚交情的根。
莱昂内尔缺他哥哥那份敏锐的头脑，奸猾却补上了。他掂量得明白：日后，赫尔森、特鲁罗和特雷西利安家在那边的产业，说不定当真要吃这处地段便宜得多的港口的亏；可那轮不到他这一辈子看见；眼下要紧的，是换得约翰爵士支持他向罗莎蒙德·戈多尔芬的求婚，好叫戈多尔芬家的产业并进自家的。这一笔现成的进项，抵得过那笔来世的亏空，莱昂内尔少爷觉得划算。
不过，切莫以为莱昂内尔的追求从此就一帆风顺。

===Original===

The mistress of Godolphin Court showed him no favour and it was mainly that she might abstract herself from the importunities of his suit that she had sought and obtained Sir John Killigrew’s permission to accompany the latter’s sister to France when she went there with her husband, who was appointed English ambassador to the Louvre. Sir John’s authority as her guardian had come into force with the decease of her brother.
Master Lionel moped awhile in her absence; but cheered by Sir John’s assurance that in the end he should prevail, he quitted Cornwall in his turn and went forth to see the world. He spent some time in London about the Court, where, however, he seems to have prospered little, and then he crossed to France to pay his devoirs to the lady of his longings.
His constancy, the humility with which he made his suit, the obvious intensity of his devotion, began at last to wear away that gentlewoman’s opposition, as dripping water wears away a stone. Yet she could not bring herself to forget that he was Sir Oliver’s brother﻿—the brother of the man she had loved, and the brother of the man who had killed her own brother. Between them stood, then, two things; the ghost of that old love of hers and the blood of Peter Godolphin.
Of this she reminded Sir John on her return to Cornwall after an absence of some two years, urging these matters as reasons why an alliance between herself and Lionel Tressilian must be impossible.
Sir John did not at all agree with her.
“My dear,” he said, “there is your future to be thought of. You are now of full age and mistress of your own actions. Yet it is not well for a woman and a gentlewoman to dwell alone. As long as I live, or as long as I remain in England, all will be well. You may continue indefinitely your residence here at Arwenack, and you have been wise, I think, in quitting the loneliness of Godolphin Court. Yet consider that that loneliness may be yours again when I am not here.”
“I should prefer that loneliness to the company you would thrust upon me,” she answered him.
“Ungracious speech!” he protested. “Is this your gratitude for that lad’s burning devotion, for his patience, his gentleness, and all the rest!”
“He is Oliver Tressilian’s brother,” she replied.
“And has he not suffered enough for that already? Is there to be no end to the price that he must pay for his brother’s sins?

===Chinese===

戈多尔芬宅的女主人不给他好脸；她之所以求得了约翰·基利格鲁爵士的准许、随他妹妹去法国——他妹妹的丈夫奉派出任英国驻卢浮宫大使——多半正是为了躲开他这份纠缠不休的求婚。她哥哥一死，约翰爵士作为监护人的权柄便生效了。
莱昂内尔少爷在她走后蔫了一阵；可约翰爵士打包票说他终能如愿，他得了这话打气，也离开康沃尔，出门见世面去了。他在伦敦朝廷里盘桓了些时日，看起来却没混出什么名堂，随后渡海去了法国，向他朝思暮想的那位小姐致意。
他的执着，他求婚时那份低声下气，他那份显而易见的痴狂，到底开始磨蚀那位贵妇的心防了，如滴水之穿石。可她始终忘不掉他是奥利弗的弟弟——她爱过的那个人的弟弟，也是杀了她的亲哥哥的那个人的弟弟。横在两人中间的有两样东西：她旧日恋情的亡魂，和彼得·戈多尔芬的血。
阔别约两年后她回到康沃尔，拿这两样提醒约翰爵士，力陈她同莱昂内尔·特雷西利安的结合断无可能。
约翰爵士全然不同意她。
“亲爱的，”他说，“总得想想你的往后。你如今成年了，自己的事自己做主。可一个女人、一位贵妇，独身过活总不是办法。只要我活着，只要我还在英格兰，一切都好。你尽可以一直住在阿文纳克这里；你离开戈多尔芬宅那份冷清，我看是明智的。可你得想想：等我不在了，那份冷清说不定又归你。”
“比起您要塞给我的那个人，我倒宁愿要那份冷清。”她答。
“好不识好歹的话！”他抗议道，“那孩子一片火热的痴心，他的耐性，他的温存，还有他那一切，你就这样报答他！”
“他是奥利弗·特雷西利安的弟弟。”她说。
“他为这个遭的罪还不够么？他哥哥的罪，要他付到几时才是个头？

===Original===

Besides, consider that when all is said they are not even brothers. They are but half-brothers.”
“Yet too closely kin,” she said. “If you must have me wed I beg you’ll find me another husband.”
To this he would answer that expediently considered no husband could be better than the one he had chosen her. He pointed out the contiguity of their two estates, and how fine and advantageous a thing it would be to merge these two into one.
He was persistent, and his persistence was increased when he came to conceive his notion to take the seas again. His conscience would not permit him to heave anchor until he had bestowed her safely in wedlock. Lionel too was persistent, in a quiet, almost self-effacing way that never set a strain upon her patience, and was therefore the more difficult to combat.
In the end she gave way under the pressure of these men’s wills, and did so with the best grace she could summon, resolved to drive from her heart and mind the one real obstacle of which, for very shame, she had made no mention to Sir John. The fact is that in spite of all, her love for Sir Oliver was not dead. It was stricken down, it is true, until she herself failed to recognize it for what it really was. But she caught herself thinking of him frequently and wistfully; she found herself comparing him with his brother; and for all that she had bidden Sir John find her some other husband than Lionel, she knew full well that any suitor brought before her must be submitted to that same comparison to his inevitable undoing. All this she accounted evil in herself. It was in vain that she lashed her mind with the reminder that Sir Oliver was Peter’s murderer. As time went on she found herself actually making excuses for her sometime lover; she would admit that Peter had driven him to the step, that for her sake Sir Oliver had suffered insult upon insult from Peter, until, being but human, the cup of his endurance had overflowed in the end, and weary of submitting to the other’s blows he had risen up in his anger and smitten in his turn.
She would scorn herself for such thoughts as these, yet she could not dismiss them. In act she could be strong﻿—as witness how she had dealt with that letter which Oliver sent her out of Barbary by the hand of Pitt﻿—but her thoughts she could not govern, and her thoughts were full often traitors to her will.

===Chinese===

再说，说到底，他们俩算不得亲兄弟，不过是异母兄弟罢了。”
“可也近得碍事，”她说，“您若一定嫁我，就请您另给我寻一位丈夫。”
对这话，他的回答永远是：通盘掂量，再没有比他替她挑的这位更好的了。他指出两家产业如何毗邻，并作一处是怎样一件又体面又合算的事。
他锲而不舍；待到他起了再次出海的念头，这份锲而不舍又添了三分。良心不容他在把她安安稳稳嫁出去之前起锚。莱昂内尔也锲而不舍——用一种安静的、几乎自甘退让的路数，从不磨她的耐性，因而也就格外难对付。
末了，她在这些男人们的意志的挤压下让了步，让得尽量体面，一面打定主意，要把心里那道真正的障碍赶出去——那障碍她羞于启齿，对约翰爵士只字未提。实情是：任凭怎样，她对奥利弗的爱没有死。它是被打倒了，不假——倒得连她自己都认不出它的真身。可她时时发怔地想起他；她拿他同他弟弟比来比去；她嘴上叫约翰爵士另寻佳婿，心里却明白，任何送上门来的求婚人，都得先过这一道比，过这一道就注定了败。这一切，她都当是自己心里的恶。她拿“奥利弗是杀彼得的凶手”这话鞭策自己，也是枉然。日子一久，她竟替昔日的情人开脱起来：她肯承认是彼得逼他走到那一步的；她肯承认，为了她的缘故，奥利弗受了彼得一辱又一辱，直到血肉之躯忍无可忍，忍够了那一记记的打，终于在怒火里奋起还了手。
她为这些念头瞧不起自己，可赶不走它们。行动上她可以硬气——瞧她怎样处置了奥利弗托皮特从巴巴里捎来的那封信就知道——可念头管不住；她的念头，十回里有八回是她意志的叛徒。

===Original===

There were longings in her heart for Oliver which she could not stifle, and there was ever the hope that he would one day return, although she realized that from such a return she might look for nothing.
When Sir John finally slew the hope of that return he did a wiser thing than he conceived. Never since Oliver’s disappearance had they heard any news of him until Pitt came to Arwenack with that letter and his story. They had heard, as had all the world, of the corsair Sakr-el-Bahr, but they had been far indeed from connecting him with Oliver Tressilian. Now that his identity was established by Pitt’s testimony, it was an easy matter to induce the courts to account him dead and to give Lionel the coveted inheritance.
This to Rosamund was a small matter. But a great one was that Sir Oliver was dead at law, and must be so in fact, should he ever again set foot in England. It extinguished finally that curiously hopeless and almost subconscious hope of hers that one day he would return. Thus it helped her perhaps to face and accept the future which Sir John was resolved to thrust upon her.
Her betrothal was made public, and she proved if not an ardently loving, at least a docile and gentle mistress to Lionel. He was content. He could ask no more in reason at the moment, and he was buoyed up by every lover’s confidence that given opportunity and time he could find the way to awaken a response. And it must be confessed that already during their betrothal he gave some proof of his reason for his confidence. She had been lonely, and he dispelled her loneliness by his complete surrender of himself to her; his restraint and his cautious, almost insidious creeping along a path which a more clumsy fellow would have taken at a dash made companionship possible between them and very sweet to her. Upon this foundation her affection began gradually to rise, and seeing them together and such excellent friends, Sir John congratulated himself upon his wisdom and went about the fitting out of that fine ship of his﻿—the *Silver Heron*﻿—for the coming voyage.
Thus they came within a week of the wedding, and Sir John all impatience now. The marriage bells were to be his signal for departure; as they fell silent the *Silver Heron* should spread her wings.

===Chinese===

她心里对奥利弗存着压抑不住的渴念，还存着一线指望：他总有一天回来——尽管她明白，那样的归来，她什么也指望不上。
约翰爵士最后掐灭这归来的指望时，办了一件比他自己所想的更聪明的事。自奥利弗失踪以来，他们再没得到他半点音讯，直到皮特带着那封信和他的经历来到阿文纳克。海盗萨克埃尔-巴尔的名头，他们同世人一样听说过，可把这个人同奥利弗·特雷西利安连在一起，他们却万万没有想到。如今皮特的见证坐实了他的身份，要官府判定他死亡、把那笔让人眼红的产业判给莱昂内尔，便是易事。
这在罗莎蒙德是小事。大的是：奥利弗在法理上死了，而且必须事实上也死——他若再踏上英格兰一步。这就彻底掐灭了她心底那点说不清、几乎自己都不觉得的指望：有一天他会回来。这样，她或许倒能面对、能接受约翰爵士执意要塞给她的未来了。
她的订婚公开了。她对莱昂内尔，纵算不上热恋的情人，至少是温顺和婉的一位。他满足了。眼下凭情理，他不能再多求；况且每个情人都有的那份自信托着他：只要有工夫有机会，他总能唤醒她的回应。也得承认，订婚期间，他已拿出了几分叫他自信的道理。她冷清，他把自己整个交出去，驱散了她的冷清；他有节制，他那种小心翼翼、近乎不动声色的缓步走近——换个笨人早一冲而上了——使两人之间有了做伴的可能，而且这做伴叫她受用。在这块地基上，她的情意渐渐升起来。瞧他们俩出双入对、交情这样好，约翰爵士为自己的明智自得，一门心思去督造他那条漂亮的大船——“银鹭号”——准备此番远航。
这样，婚期只剩一周了，约翰爵士如今一心急切。婚礼的钟声就是他动身的号令；钟声一歇，“银鹭号”便张翅。

===Original===

It was the evening of the first of June; the peal of the curfew had faded on the air and lights were being set in the great dining room at Arwenack where the company was to sup. It was a small party. Just Sir John and Rosamund and Lionel, who had lingered on that day, and Lord Henry Goade﻿—our chronicler﻿—the Queen’s Lieutenant of Cornwall, together with his lady. They were visiting Sir John and they were to remain yet a week his guests at Arwenack that they might grace the coming nuptials.
Above in the house there was great stir of preparation for the departure of Sir John and his ward, the latter into wedlock, the former into unknown seas. In the turret chamber a dozen sempstresses were at work upon the bridal outfit under the directions of that Sally Pentreath who had been no less assiduous in the preparation of swaddling clothes and the like on the eve of Rosamund’s appearance in this world.
At the very hour at which Sir John was leading his company to table Sir Oliver Tressilian was setting foot ashore not a mile away.
He had deemed it wiser not to round Pendennis Point. So in the bay above Swanpool on the western side of that promontory he had dropped anchor as the evening shadows were deepening. He had launched the ship’s two boats, and in these he had conveyed some thirty of his men ashore. Twice had the boats returned, until a hundred of his corsairs stood ranged along that foreign beach. The other hundred he left on guard aboard. He took so great a force upon an expedition for which a quarter of the men would have sufficed so as to ensure by overwhelming numbers the avoidance of all unnecessary violence.
