“赞颂全智的那位，赐我力量，教我未行一桩不配的事！”他朗声喊道，先把自己夸了一番，“我本可以杀了他、解了那段渎神的姻缘，可我遵了经上的字。”
“你的坚忍叫众天使喜乐，”她答道，“可偏就有男人下作到这个地步，拿它做买卖、拿你的虔诚做买卖，阿萨德！”
他甩开她的搂抱，大步走开，一颗心翻腾不止。他在月光里来回地踱；她呢，心满意足，斜倚在软榻的软垫上，一副说不尽的曼妙姿态，那双灼灼的眼睛知趣地避开他的视线——只等她的毒发作到家。
她见他站住，双臂朝天一扬，像在诘问上苍，像在质询那满天星斗——它们正在月亮四外张开的光晕里眨眼。
末了，他慢慢踱回她跟前。他还没拿定主意。她说的有理；可他也掂得出她对萨克埃尔-巴尔的那份恨，掂得出那恨必撺掇她把他的任何一举都往最坏处解；掂得出她护马尔扎克的心思；于是他疑她的理，也疑他自己。还有他自己对萨克埃尔-巴尔的那份爱，也非要在他判断的天平上占一个秤位。他心里乱作一团。
“够了，”他近乎粗声地说，“我求安拉夜里赐我一个主意。”说罢他从她身边大步走过，上了台阶，进了宅子。
她跟了进去。一整夜，她卧在他脚边，好赶在天光头一露的时辰，去加固她生怕还软弱的那份决心；他睡得断断续续，她则整夜没合眼，睁着眼守着。
宣礼员的嗓音头一响起，他便应声从榻上跃起；那声音的余音还挂在黎明的风上，他已在活动手脚，拍掌唤奴仆，发号施令——她听得出，他要即刻去港口。

===Original===

“May Allah have inspired thee, O my lord!” she cried. And asked him: “What is thy resolve?”
“I go to seek a sign,” he answered her, and upon that departed, leaving her in a frame of mind that was far from easy.
She summoned Marzak, and bade him accompany his father, breathed swift instructions of what he should do and how do it.
“Thy fate has been placed in thine own hands,” she admonished him. “See that thou grip it firmly now.”
In the courtyard Marzak found his father in the act of mounting a white mule that had been brought him. He was attended by his *wazeer* Tsamanni, Biskaine, and some other of his captains. Marzak begged leave to go with him. It was carelessly granted, and they set out, Marzak walking by his father’s stirrup, a little in advance of the others. For a while there was silence between father and son, then the latter spoke.
“It is my prayer, O my father, that thou art resolved to depose the faithless Sakr-el-Bahr from the command of this expedition.”
Asad considered his son with a sombre eye. “Even now the *galeasse* should be setting out if the argosy is to be intercepted,” he said. “If Sakr-el-Bahr does not command, who shall, in Heaven’s name?”
“Try me, O my father,” cried Marzak.
Asad smiled with grim wistfulness. “Art weary of life, O my son, that thou wouldst go to thy death and take the *galeasse* to destruction?”
“Thou art less than just, O my father,” Marzak protested.
“Yet more than kind, O my son,” replied Asad, and they went on in silence thereafter, until they came to the mole.
The splendid *galeasse* was moored alongside, and all about her there was great bustle of preparation for departure. Porters moved up and down the gangway that connected her with the shore, carrying bales of provisions, barrels of water, kegs of gunpowder, and other necessaries for the voyage, and even as Asad and his followers reached the head of that gangway, four negroes were staggering down it under the load of a huge palmetto bale that was slung from staves yoked to their shoulders.
On the poop stood Sakr-el-Bahr with Othmani, Ali, Jasper-Reis, and some other officers. Up and down the gangway paced Larocque and Vigitello, two renegade boatswains, one French and the other Italian, who had sailed with him on every voyage for the past two years.

===Chinese===

“愿安拉已把主意放进了你心里，我的主上！”她喊道，又问：“你拿定了什么主意？”
“我去寻一个征兆。”他答完便走了，撇下她一颗心七上八下。
她唤来马尔扎克，吩咐他跟着他父亲去，又飞快地口授了一番他该做什么、该怎样做。
“你的命数已经搁在你自己手里了，”她叮嘱他，“这回可要攥紧了。”
院子里，马尔扎克见父亲正在上人牵来的那头白骡。随行的是他的维齐尔察曼尼、比斯坎和他手下几名船长。马尔扎克求带他同去，获准得很随意。一行人上路，马尔扎克走在父亲镫旁，略略在前。父子俩静了一阵，随后小的开了口。
“父亲大人，我祷告的，是您已拿定主意，褫夺那背信的萨克埃尔-巴尔的帅印，不叫他统领这一趟。”
阿萨德拿阴沉沉的眼色打量儿子。“这会儿，桨帆船早该动身了，再晚就截不着那条商船了，”他说，“萨克埃尔-巴尔若不统兵，老天在上，谁统？”
“用我试试，父亲大人。”马尔扎克喊道。
阿萨德冷冷地、带着点怜惜地笑了。“嫌命长了，我的儿？你要去送死，还搭上一条桨帆船？”
“您有欠公道，父亲大人。”马尔扎克不服。
“却有富余的慈爱，我的儿。”阿萨德答。此后两人再没言语，直到防波堤前。
那条气派的桨帆船傍岸泊着，四下里是一片开拔前的大忙。挑夫们在连通船岸的舷梯上来来往往，扛着一捆捆粮秣、一桶桶淡水、一桶桶火药，和航程里别的必需；阿萨德一行刚到舷梯头，四个黑人正肩扛着杠子，压着一巨大棕榈席包的分量，在舷梯上深一步浅一步地下来——不，正往船上抬。
艉楼上站着萨克埃尔-巴尔，同他一起的有奥斯曼尼、阿里、贾斯珀-雷斯和另几名军官。舷梯上来回踱着拉罗克和维吉特洛——两名叛教的水手长，一法一意，两年来随他航过每一趟海。

===Original===

Larocque was superintending the loading of the vessel, bawling his orders for the bestowal of provisions here, of water yonder, and of powder about the mainmast. Vigitello was making a final inspection of the slaves at the oars.
As the palmetto pannier was brought aboard, Larocque shouted to the negroes to set it down by the mainmast. But here Sakr-el-Bahr interfered, bidding them, instead, to bring it up to the stern and place it in the poop house.
Asad had dismounted, and stood with Marzak at his side at the head of the gangway when the youth finally begged his father himself to take command of this expedition, allowing him to come as his lieutenant and so learn the ways of the sea.
Asad looked at him curiously, but answered nothing. He went aboard, Marzak and the others following him. It was at this moment that Sakr-el-Bahr first became aware of the Basha’s presence, and he came instantly forward to do the honours of his galley. If there was a sudden uneasiness in his heart his face was calm and his glance as arrogant and steady as ever.
“May the peace of Allah overshadow thee and thy house, O mighty Asad,” was his greeting. “We are on the point of casting off, and I shall sail the more securely for thy blessing.”
Asad considered him with eyes of wonder. So much effrontery, so much ease after their last scene together seemed to the Basha a thing incredible, unless, indeed, it were accompanied by a conscience entirely at peace.
“It has been proposed to me that I shall do more than bless this expedition﻿—that I shall command it,” he answered, watching Sakr-el-Bahr closely. He observed the sudden flicker of the corsair’s eyes, the only outward sign of his inward dismay.
“Command it?” echoed Sakr-el-Bahr. “ ’Twas proposed to thee?” And he laughed lightly as if to dismiss that suggestion.
That laugh was a tactical error. It spurred Asad. He advanced slowly along the vessel’s waist deck to the mainmast﻿—for she was rigged with main and foremasts. There he halted again to look into the face of Sakr-el-Bahr who stepped along beside him.
“Why didst thou laugh?” he questioned shortly.
“Why? At the folly of such a proposal,” said Sakr-el-Bahr in haste, too much in haste to seek a diplomatic answer.
Darker grew the Basha’s frown. “Folly?” quoth he. “Wherein lies the folly?”
Sakr-el-Bahr made haste to cover his mistake.

===Chinese===

拉罗克在督装：扯着嗓子指挥，粮放这边，水放那边，火药码主桅周围。维吉特洛在给桨位上的奴隶们做出发前的最后一遍查验。
那只棕榈席驮篮抬上船时，拉罗克喝叫黑人把它撂在主桅旁。可萨克埃尔-巴尔插了手，叫他们改抬到船尾，安进艉楼舱里。
阿萨德下了骡，同马尔扎克并立舷梯头。这时，那少年再次恳求父亲亲自统率此行，许他随行当个副手，学学海上的门道。
阿萨德古怪地看了他一眼，没有作答。他上了船，马尔扎克和其余人跟着。恰在此刻，萨克埃尔-巴尔才察觉巴沙驾到，立刻趋前，行他船上主人的礼数。他心里纵有一阵突兀的不安，脸上却平静，目光还是一如既往的倨傲和沉稳。
“愿安拉的平安覆庇你和你的一家，威严的阿萨德，”是他的迎辞，“我们正要解缆；得你一祝，我这船行得更稳。”
阿萨德拿惊奇的眼光打量他。自从两人上回那一场面之后，还有这许多的放肆、这许多的从容，在巴沙看来简直难以置信——除非，这一切背后，是一颗全然无愧的良心。
“有人向我提议，这趟我不止要祝一祝——还要亲自统率它。”他答道，眼睛紧盯着萨克埃尔-巴尔。他捕到了那海盗眼里忽的一闪——那是他心里惊惶唯一露在外头的痕迹。
“统率它？”萨克埃尔-巴尔应声道，“有人向你提议？”他轻轻一笑，仿佛要把这话岔开去。
那一笑是步战术上的错棋。它把阿萨德激了起来。他顺着船腰甲板慢慢朝主桅走去——这船是主桅前桅双桅的——到那里又站住，端详着傍着他走来的萨克埃尔-巴尔的脸。
“你笑什么？”他劈头问。
“笑什么？笑这提议的糊涂，”萨克埃尔-巴尔急急地说——急得来不及寻一句圆滑话。
巴沙的眉头更黑了。“糊涂？”他说，“糊涂在哪里？”
萨克埃尔-巴尔赶紧找补自己的失着。

===Original===

“In the suggestion that such poor quarry as waits us should be worthy thine endeavour, should warrant the Lion of the Faith to unsheathe his mighty claws. Thou,” he continued with ringing scorn, “thou the inspirer of a hundred glorious fights in which whole fleets have been engaged, to take the seas upon so trivial an errand﻿—one *galeasse* to swoop upon a single galley of Spain! It were unworthy thy great name, beneath the dignity of thy valour!” and by a gesture he contemptuously dismissed the subject.
But Asad continued to ponder him with cold eyes, his face inscrutable. “Why, here’s a change since yesterday!” he said.
“A change, my lord?”
“But yesterday in the marketplace thyself didst urge me to join this expedition and to command it,” Asad reminded him, speaking with deliberate emphasis. “Thyself invoked the memory of the days that are gone, when, scimitar in hand, we charged side by side aboard the infidel, and thou didst beseech me to engage again beside thee. And now.﻿ ﻿…” He spread his hands, anger gathered in his eyes. “Whence this change?” he demanded sternly.
Sakr-el-Bahr hesitated, caught in his own toils. He looked away from Asad a moment; he had a glimpse of the handsome flushed face of Marzak at his father’s elbow, of Biskaine, Tsamanni, and the others all staring at him in amazement, and even of some grimy sunburned faces from the rowers’ bench on his left that were looking on with dull curiosity.
He smiled, seeming outwardly to remain entirely unruffled. “Why﻿ ﻿… it is that I have come to perceive thy reasons for refusing. For the rest, it is as I say, the quarry is not worthy of the hunter.”
Marzak uttered a soft sneering laugh, as if the true reason of the corsair’s attitude were quite clear to him. He fancied too, and he was right in this, that Sakr-el-Bahr’s odd attitude had accomplished what persuasions addressed to Asad-ed-Din might to the end have failed to accomplish﻿—had afforded him the sign he was come to seek. For it was in that moment that Asad determined to take command himself.
“It almost seems,” he said slowly, smiling, “as if thou didst not want me. If so, it is unfortunate; for I have long neglected my duty to my son, and I am resolved at last to repair that error. We accompany thee upon this expedition, Sakr-el-Bahr. Myself I will command it, and Marzak shall be my apprentice in the ways of the sea.”

===Chinese===

“糊涂在：候着咱们的这点可怜猎物，竟劳动得动你的大驾，配得上信仰的雄狮亮出他的利爪。你，”他接着说，字字带着铿锵的讥诮，“你，百场堂堂之阵的灵感之源——整队整队的舰队都在那些阵里交过手——竟为这点鸡毛差使下海：一条桨帆船，扑西班牙的一条孤船！这有辱你的大名，有失你勇武的尊严！”他手一摆，轻蔑地把这话题打发掉了。
可阿萨德还在拿冷眼掂他，那张脸深不可测。“咦，这可同昨天换了天地了！”他说。
“换了天地，大人？”
“就在昨天，集市上，还是你自己劝我加入这趟出征、劝我亲自统率的，”阿萨德提点他，一字一句，“是你自己翻出旧日的光景——那时候，我们弯刀在手，并肩跳上异教徒的船——是你求我再到你身边并肩一战。可如今……”他摊开双手，怒意聚上眼底。“这变卦，打哪儿来？”他厉声质问。
萨克埃尔-巴尔迟疑了，自己织的网兜住了自己。他把目光从阿萨德身上挪开一瞬：瞥见马尔扎克在他父亲肘边那张涨红的俊脸，瞥见比斯坎、察曼尼和其余人个个惊愕地瞪着他，甚至瞥见左边桨凳上几张乌黑汗脸，正带着麻木的好奇张望。
他微微一笑，外表上仍是纹丝不乱。“这个……是我后来想通了您回绝的道理。其余的，正如我说的：这猎物，配不上这猎手。”
马尔扎克轻轻发出一声冷笑，仿佛那海盗这副做态的真正缘由，他早已洞若观火。他还——这一点上他没想错——认定萨克埃尔-巴尔这副古怪的态度，办成了对阿萨德-丁说破嘴也未必办得成的事：给了他要来寻的那个征兆。因为正是在那一刻，阿萨德拿定主意亲自统兵。
“倒像是，”他缓缓地、含笑说，“你不要我去似的。若真这样，那就可惜了：我久已疏忽了对儿子的本分，如今总算决意补上这一课。这一趟出征，我们陪你同去，萨克埃尔-巴尔。我亲自统率，马尔扎克给我当学徒，学学海上的门道。”

===Original===

Sakr-el-Bahr said not another word in protest against that proclaimed resolve. He salaamed, and when he spoke there was almost a note of gladness in his voice.
“The praise to Allah, then, since thou’rt determined. It is not for me to urge further the unworthiness of the quarry since I am the gainer by thy resolve.”

===Chinese===

萨克埃尔-巴尔对这道宣示的决心，再没多辩一个字。他深深行了个礼；开口时，话音里竟带着近乎欢喜的调子。
“既然你意已决，那就赞颂安拉。猎物既配不上你，我本也不便再多嘴——何况你这决定，进项的还是我。”

## X The Slave Market / 奴隶市场
===Original===

At the Sôk-el-Abeed it was the hour of the outcry, announced by a blast of trumpets and the thudding of tom-toms. The traders that until then had been licensed to ply within the enclosure now put up the shutters of their little booths. The Hebrew pedlar of gems closed his box and effaced himself, leaving the steps about the well clear for the most prominent patrons of the market. These hastened to assemble there, surrounding it and facing outwards, whilst the rest of the crowd was ranged against the southern and western walls of the enclosure.
Came negro water carriers in white turbans with aspersers made of palmetto leaves to sprinkle the ground and lay the dust against the tramp of slaves and buyers. The trumpets ceased for an instant, then wound a fresh imperious blast and fell permanently silent. The crowd about the gates fell back to right and left, and very slowly and stately three tall *dalals*, dressed from head to foot in white and with immaculate turbans wound about their heads, advanced into the open space. They came to a halt at the western end of the long wall, the chief *dalal* standing slightly in advance of the other two.
The chattering of voices sank upon their advent, it became a hissing whisper, then a faint drone like that of bees, and then utter silence. In the solemn and grave demeanour of the *dalals* there was something almost sacerdotal, so that when that silence fell upon the crowd the affair took on the aspect of a sacrament.
The chief *dalal* stood forward a moment as if in an abstraction with downcast eyes; then with hands outstretched to catch a blessing he raised his voice and began to pray in a monotonous chant:
“In the name of Allah the Pitying the Pitiful Who created man from clots of blood! All that is in the Heavens and in the Earth praiseth Allah, Who is the Mighty, the Wise! His the kingdom of the Heavens and of the Earth. He maketh alive and killeth, and He hath power over all things. He is the first and the last, the seen and the unseen, and He knoweth all things.”
“*Ameen*,” intoned the crowd.
“The praise to Him who sent us Muhammad His Prophet to give the world the True Belief, and curses upon Shaitan the stoned who wages war upon Allah and His children.”
“*Ameen.

===Chinese===

奴隶市场上，开市的时辰到了：一声号角、一阵鼓点报的信。此前领了执照在场内做买卖的商贩，这时纷纷上起小棚的板门。卖宝石的希伯来货郎合上箱子，悄然隐去，把井台四周腾干净，让给市场上最体面的主顾们。他们赶来聚在那里，围着井，面朝外；人群的其余部分，被排到围场的南墙和西墙跟前。
来了几名缠白头巾的黑人送水夫，拿着棕榈叶扎的洒水帚，把地面洒湿，压住奴隶和买主们的脚步扬尘。号角顿了一顿，又吹出新一轮倨傲的调子，便就此长歇。大门边的人群向左右分开，三名高大的拍卖师通身上下一色白，头上缠着一尘不染的头巾——缓慢而庄严地走进场心。他们在长墙的西端站定，为首那位略略站在另两位前头。
他们一到，嗡嗡的人语便低了下去，低成一片咝咝的耳语，又低成蜜蜂似的一线嗡鸣，然后是全然的寂静。三位拍卖师那副庄重肃穆的气度里，几乎带着几分司祭的神味；这份寂静罩下来，这桩买卖便俨然有了一场圣礼的模样。
为首的拍卖师垂着眼，出神似的立了片刻；随后双手摊开承接福佑，提起嗓门，用单调的诵腔祷告起来：
“奉那怜悯的、慈悲的、从血块造人的安拉之名！天地间的一切，都赞颂安拉——那全能的、全智的！诸天与大地的权柄，都是祂的。祂使生，使死，祂于万事万物全能有辖。祂是初，是终；是显的，是隐的；祂知晓万事。”
“阿敏。”众人应诵。
“赞颂那位差遣穆罕默德祂的先知、把真信仰带给世人的主；诅咒那被石击的撒旦——他向安拉和安拉的儿女们开战。”
“阿敏。

===Original===

*”
“The blessings of Allah and our Lord Muhammad upon this market and upon all who may buy and sell herein, and may Allah increase their wealth and grant them length of days in which to praise Him.”
“*Ameen*,” replied the crowd, as with a stir and rustle the close ranks relaxed from the tense attitude of prayer, and each man sought elbow-room.
The *dalal* beat his hands together, whereupon the curtains were drawn aside and the huddled slaves displayed﻿—some three hundred in all, occupying three several pens.
In the front rank of the middle pen﻿—the one containing Rosamund and Lionel﻿—stood a couple of stalwart young Nubians, sleek and muscular, who looked on with completest indifference, no whit appalled by the fate which had haled them thither. They caught the eye of the *dalal*, and although the usual course was for a buyer to indicate a slave he was prepared to purchase, yet to the end that good beginning should be promptly made, the *dalal* himself pointed out that stalwart pair to the corsairs who stood on guard. In compliance the two negroes were brought forth.
“Here is a noble twain,” the *dalal* announced, “strong of muscle and long of limb, as all may see, whom it were a shameful thing to separate. Who needs such a pair for strong labour let him say what he will give.” He set out on a slow circuit of the well, the corsairs urging the two slaves to follow him that all buyers might see and inspect them.
In the foremost ranks of the crowd near the gate stood Ali, sent thither by Othmani to purchase a score of stout fellows required to make up the contingent of the *galeasse* of Sakr-el-Bahr. He had been strictly enjoined to buy naught but the stoutest stuff the market could afford﻿—with one exception. Aboard that *galeasse* they wanted no weaklings who would trouble the boatswain with their swoonings. Ali announced his business forthwith.
“I need such tall fellows for the oars of Sakr-el-Bahr,” said he with loud importance, thus drawing upon himself the eyes of the assembly, and sunning himself in the admiring looks bestowed upon one of the officers of Oliver-Reis, one of the rovers who were the pride of Islam and a sword-edge to the infidel.
“They were born to toil nobly at the oar, O Ali-Reis,” replied the *dalal* in all solemnity. “What wilt thou give for them?”
“Two hundred philips for the twain.”

===Chinese===

”
“愿安拉和我们主上穆罕默德的福佑，降于这个市场，降于在此买卖的众人；愿安拉增益他们的财富，赐他们长寿，好在长年里赞颂祂。”
“阿敏。”众人应道。随着一阵窸窣耸动，紧拢的行列从祷告的紧张里松了下来，人人各自寻起了伸胳膊的地方。
拍卖师一击掌，帘幕拉开，蜷缩的奴隶们露了出来——总计约三百人，分圈在三处围栏里。
当中那处围栏的前排——正是关着罗莎蒙德和莱昂内尔的那处——站着两名壮健的年轻努比亚人，油光水滑、筋肉虬结，一副全然无所谓的神气，被命运拽到这里，半点不见惶恐。他们落进拍卖师的眼里；照惯例，本该由买主指名要买的奴隶，可为了图个开门红，拍卖师亲自把这一对壮汉指给了看守的海盗们。于是两个黑人被带上前。
“瞧这一对贵货，”拍卖师宣布，“筋长力足，诸位都看得见；拆散了他们，是桩丢人的事。哪位要这样一对出苦力的，只管出个价。”他绕着井台缓缓走起圈来，海盗们催着两个奴隶跟在他身后，好叫众买主都能看见、都能相看。
紧靠大门的人群前排站着阿里——他是受奥斯曼尼差遣，来买二十条壮汉，补齐萨克埃尔-巴尔那条桨帆船的缺额的。他得了严令：只买这市场上拿得出的最壮的货——只一个例外。那条桨帆船上不要软骨头，省得动不动昏给水手长看。阿里当场亮明了来意。
“我要这样高大的汉子，去给萨克埃尔-巴尔摇桨，”他大剌剌地说，把满场的眼睛都引到自己身上，晒着众人的艳羡——众人艳羡的是奥利弗-雷斯手下的一名军官；奥利弗-雷斯这路人，是伊斯兰的骄傲、异教徒的刀刃。
“他们生来就是要上桨的，命里注定要高贵地吃苦，阿里-雷斯。”拍卖师一本正经地答，“你出什么价？”
“两个人，二百枚金币。”

===Original===

The *dalal* paced solemnly on, the slaves following in his wake.
“Two hundred philips am I offered for a pair of the lustiest slaves that by the favour of Allah were ever brought into this market. Who will say fifty philips more?”
A portly Moor in a flowing blue *selham* rose from his seat on the step of the well as the *dalal* came abreast of him, and the slaves scenting here a buyer, and preferring any service to that of the galleys with which they were threatened, came each in turn to kiss his hands and fawn upon him, for all the world like dogs.
Calm and dignified he ran his hands over them feeling their muscles, and then forced back their lips and examined their teeth and mouths.
“Two hundred and twenty for the twain,” he said, and the *dalal* passed on with his wares, announcing the increased price he had been offered.
Thus he completed the circuit and came to stand once more before Ali.
“Two hundred and twenty is now the price, O Ali! By the Koran, they are worth three hundred at the least. Wilt say three hundred?”
“Two hundred and thirty,” was the answer.
Back to the Moor went the *dalal*. “Two hundred and thirty I am now offered, O Hamet. Thou wilt give another twenty?”
“Not I, by Allah!” said Hamet, and resumed his seat. “Let him have them.”
“Another ten philips?” pleaded the *dalal*.
“Not another *asper.*”
“They are thine, then, O Ali, for two hundred and thirty. Give thanks to Allah for so good a bargain.”
The Nubians were surrendered to Ali’s followers, whilst the *dalal*’s two assistants advanced to settle accounts with the corsair.
“Wait wait,” said he, “is not the name of Sakr-el-Bahr good warranty?”
“The inviolable law is that the purchase money be paid ere a slave leaves the market, O valiant Ali.”
“It shall be observed,” was the impatient answer, “and I will so pay before they leave. But I want others yet, and we will make one account an it please thee. That fellow yonder now. I have orders to buy him for my captain.” And he indicated Lionel, who stood at Rosamund’s side, the very incarnation of woefulness and debility.
Contemptuous surprise flickered an instant in the eyes of the *dalal*. But this he made haste to dissemble.
“Bring forth that yellow-haired infidel,” he commanded.
The corsairs laid hands on Lionel.

===Chinese===

拍卖师庄严地踱着，奴隶们跟在他身后。
“承安拉的恩典，这市场上进过顶健壮的奴隶，如今有人出二百枚金币买这样一对。哪位加五十？”
拍卖师踱到与一个阔气的摩尔人相并时，那人从井台的台阶上站起身来——他身披一领飘飘的蓝色斗篷。两个奴隶嗅出了买主，又巴不得摆脱那悬在头上的桨船营生，便一个挨一个凑上去吻他的手，摇尾乞怜，活脱脱两条狗。
他从容而有身份地伸手在他们身上捏捏摸摸，试试筋肉，又扒开他们的嘴唇，验看牙齿口腔。
“两个，二百二十。”他说。拍卖师领着他的货继续往前走，一路报着涨上去的新价。
他这样走完一圈，又站回阿里面前。
“如今的价是二百二了，阿里！凭《古兰经》说，他们少说值三百。你出三百不？”
“二百三。”这是回答。
拍卖师回到那摩尔人跟前。“如今叫价二百三了，哈米特。你再加二十？”
“不加了，安拉在上！”哈米特说，重新坐下，“让他要吧。”
“再加十枚？”拍卖师劝。
“一个铜子也不加了。”
“那就是你的了，阿里，二百三十枚。为这样合算的买卖，谢安拉吧。”
两个努比亚人交给了阿里的随从，拍卖师的两名助手上前，同那海盗结账。
“慢着慢着，”他说，“萨克埃尔-巴尔的名字，还不算顶硬的保票么？”
“铁律不破：钱货两讫，奴隶方能出场，勇武的阿里。”
“遵命就是，”回答透着不耐，“出场前我必付清。可我还要买别人，回头一并结账，可好？喏，那边那个。我奉命替我的船长把他买下。”他一指莱昂内尔——那人立在罗莎蒙德身边，活是一副愁苦虚弱的化身。
拍卖师眼里掠过一瞬轻蔑的诧异，但他赶忙掩了。
“把那个黄头发的异教徒带上来。”他吩咐。
海盗们朝莱昂内尔伸手。

===Original===

He made a vain attempt to struggle, but it was observed that the woman leaned over to him and said something quickly, whereupon his struggles ceased and he suffered himself to be dragged limply forth into the full view of all the market.
“Dost want him for the oar, Ali?” cried Ayoub-el-Samin across the quadrangle, a jest this that evoked a general laugh.
“What else?” quoth Ali. “He should be cheap at least.”
“Cheap?” quoth the *dalal* in an affectation of surprise. “Nay, now. ’Tis a comely fellow and a young one. What wilt thou give, now? a hundred philips?”
“A hundred philips!” cried Ali derisively. “A hundred philips for that skinful of bones! *Ma’sh’Allah!* Five philips is my price, O *dalal*.”
Again laughter crackled through the mob. But the *dalal* stiffened with increasing dignity. Some of that laughter seemed to touch himself, and he was not a person to be made the butt of mirth.
“ ’Tis a jest, my master,” said he, with a forgiving yet contemptuous wave. “Behold how sound he is.” He signed to one of the corsairs, and Lionel’s doublet was slit from neck to girdle and wrenched away from his body, leaving him naked to the waist, and displaying better proportions than might have been expected. In a passion at that indignity Lionel writhed in the grip of his guards, until one of the corsairs struck him a light blow with a whip in earnest of what to expect if he continued to be troublesome. “Consider him now,” said the *dalal*, pointing to that white torso. “And behold how sound he is. See how excellent are his teeth.” He seized Lionel’s head and forced the jaws apart.
“Ay,” said Ali, “but consider me those lean shanks and that woman’s arm.”
“ ’Tis a fault the oar will mend,” the *dalal* insisted.
“You filthy blackamoors!” burst from Lionel in a sob of rage.
“He is muttering curses in his infidel tongue,” said Ali. “His temper is none too good, you see. I have said five philips. I’ll say no more.”
With a shrug the *dalal* began his circuit of the well, the corsairs thrusting Lionel after him. Here one rose to handle him, there another, but none seemed disposed to purchase.
“Five philips is the foolish price offered me for this fine young Frank,” cried the *dalal*. “Will no true believer pay ten for such a slave? Wilt not thou, O Ayoub? Thou, Hamet﻿—ten philips?”
But one after another those to whom he was offered shook their heads.

===Chinese===

他徒劳地挣了几挣；可众人都看见那女子俯身对他急急说了句什么，他的挣扎便停了，任人软塌塌地拖到全场众目睽睽之下。
“你要他上桨么，阿里？”阿尤布-埃勒-萨明隔着场子喊——这句俏皮话惹来一片哄笑。
“不上桨上什么？”阿里说，“至少便宜。”
“便宜？”拍卖师拿腔作调地惊奇，“不然不然。这可是个体面小伙子，又年轻。你出多少？一百枚金币？”
“一百枚！”阿里讥诮地嚷，“一百枚买那副骨头架子！天晓得！我的价，五枚，拍卖师。”
笑声又在人堆里炸响。可拍卖师把架子端得更高了。那笑声里有些仿佛扎着他本人，他可不是给人当笑料的。
“这是玩笑话，我的主顾，”他说，宽恕而轻蔑地一挥手，“瞧他多结实。”他朝一个海盗递了个眼色，莱昂内尔的紧身上衣从领口到腰带被一剑划开，从身上扯了下来，叫他赤裸到腰，露出的身条倒比预想的齐整。莱昂内尔为这一番羞辱气得在看守手里乱扭，一个海盗拿鞭子轻轻给了他一下，算是个预告：再不老实，有他受的。“现在相看相看他，”拍卖师指着那副白生生的身躯说，“瞧他多结实。瞧他一口好牙。”他攥住莱昂内尔的头，把他的下颌掰开。
“是啊，”阿里说，“可你瞧瞧那两条细腿、那条娘们儿胳膊。”
“这点毛病，桨上自会给他磨好。”拍卖师坚称。
“你们这些龌龊的黑奴！”莱昂内尔憋出一声带哭的怒骂。
“他在用他的异教话咕哝咒语呢，”阿里说，“你们瞧，脾气还不见得好。我出了五枚，不再加了。”
拍卖师耸耸肩，绕着井台走起圈来，海盗们推着莱昂内尔跟在后头。这边一个起身捏捏他，那边一个上手摸摸，可谁也没有要买的意思。
“五枚——就这样个昏价，买这个俊俏的法兰克小伙子，”拍卖师喊，“哪个信士出十枚？你不出么，阿尤布？你呢，哈米特——十枚？”
可他递到谁跟前，谁都是摇头。

===Original===

The haggardness of Lionel’s face was too unprepossessing. They had seen slaves with that look before, and experience told them that no good was ever to be done with such fellows. Moreover, though shapely, his muscles were too slight, his flesh looked too soft and tender. Of what use a slave who must be hardened and nourished into strength, and who might very well die in the process? Even at five philips he would be dear. So the disgusted *dalal* came back to Ali.
“He is thine, then, for five philips﻿—Allah pardon thy avarice.”
Ali grinned, and his men seized upon Lionel and bore him off into the background to join the two negroes previously purchased.
And then, before Ali could bid for another of the slaves he desired to acquire, a tall, elderly Jew, dressed in black doublet and hose like a Castilian gentleman, with a ruffle at his neck, a plumed bonnet on his grey locks, and a serviceable dagger hanging from his girdle of hammered gold, had claimed the attention of the *dalal*.
In the pen that held the captives of the lesser raids conducted by Biskaine sat an Andalusian girl of perhaps some twenty years, of a beauty entirely Spanish. Her face was of the warm pallor of ivory, her massed hair of an ebony black, her eyebrows were finely pencilled, and her eyes of deepest and softest brown. She was dressed in the becoming garb of the Castilian peasant, the folded kerchief of red and yellow above her bodice leaving bare the glories of her neck. She was very pale, and her eyes were wild in their look, but this detracted nothing from her beauty.
She had attracted the Jew’s notice, and it is not impossible that there may have stirred in him a desire to avenge upon her some of the cruel wrongs, some of the rackings, burning, confiscations, and banishment suffered by the men of his race at the hands of the men of hers. He may have bethought him of invaded ghettos, of Jewish maidens ravished, and Jewish children butchered in the name of the God those Spanish Christians worshipped, for there was something almost of contemptuous fierceness in his dark eyes and in the hand he flung out to indicate her.
“Yonder is a Castilian wench for whom I will give fifty philips, O *dalal*,” he announced. The datal made a sign, whereupon the corsairs dragged her struggling forth.
“So much loveliness may not be bought for fifty philips, O Ibrahim,” said he.

===Chinese===

莱昂内尔那张脸的枯槁相太不讨喜。这种模样的奴隶他们从前见过，经验告诉他们：这种人买回去没有好事。何况他身条虽顺，筋肉太单薄，皮肉看着太娇嫩。一个还得养壮、还得炼硬、半道还兴许养死的奴隶，要来何用？白送还嫌占地方。五枚也是贵。于是满脸厌弃的拍卖师回到了阿里跟前。
“那就是你的了，五枚——安拉饶你的贪心。”
阿里咧嘴一笑，他的人抓住莱昂内尔，把他拖到后场，归进先前买下的那两个黑人堆里。
接着，阿里还没来得及开价买下一个，一个高个子的年长犹太人已经把拍卖师的注意力占了去——他黑紧身上衣、黑紧身裤，一副卡斯蒂利亚绅士的打扮，颈上一圈皱领，灰发上一顶插羽的帽，锤金腰带上挂一把趁手的匕首。
在关着比斯坎几次小仗俘虏的那处围栏里，坐着一个安达卢西亚姑娘，约莫二十岁，一副纯然的西班牙美貌。她的脸是象牙般的暖白，一头浓发黑如乌木，眉如细描，眼睛是最深最软的褐色。她穿着一身合身的卡斯蒂利亚农家衣裳，胸衣上一方红黄相间的折巾，露出颈项的风光。她脸色惨白，眼神狂乱，可这半点不减她的美。
她引住了那犹太人的目光；他心里是否翻起了几分报复的欲念，也未可知——他的族人受过的那些酷刑、火刑、抄家和放逐，他要借她讨还几分。他也许想起了被闯开的犹太区，想起了被糟蹋的犹太少女、以西班牙基督徒所拜上帝之名屠戮的犹太孩童；因为他伸手指向她时，黑眼睛里、那只手上，都带着几分近乎轻蔑的凶悍。
“那边那个卡斯蒂利亚丫头，我出五十枚金币，拍卖师。”他宣布。拍卖师一摆手，海盗们便把她拖了出来，一路挣着。
“这样的姿色，五十枚可买不来，易卜拉欣。”他说。

===Original===

“Yusuf here will pay sixty at least.” And he stood expectantly before a resplendent Moor.
The Moor, however, shook his head.
“Allah knows I have three wives who would destroy her loveliness within the hour and so leave me the loser.”
The *dalal* moved on, the girl following him but contesting every step of the way with those who impelled her forward, and reviling them too in hot Castilian. She drove her nails into the arms of one and spat fiercely into the face of another of her corsair guards. Rosamund’s weary eyes quickened to horror as she watched her﻿—a horror prompted as much by the fate awaiting that poor child as by the undignified fury of the futile battle she waged against it. But it happened that her behaviour impressed a Levantine Turk quite differently. He rose, a short squat figure, from his seat on the steps of the well.
“Sixty philips will I pay for the joy of taming that wild cat,” said he.
But Ibrahim was not to be outbidden. He offered seventy, the Turk countered with a bid of eighty, and Ibrahim again raised the price to ninety, and there fell a pause.
The *dalal* spurred on the Turk. “Wilt thou be beaten then, and by an Israelite? Shall this lovely maid be given to a perverter of the Scriptures, to an inheritor of the fire, to one of a race that would not bestow on their fellow-men so much as the speck out of a date-stone? It were a shame upon a true believer.”
Urged thus the Turk offered another five philips, but with obvious reluctance. The Jew, however, entirely unabashed by a tirade against him, the like of which he heard a score of times a day in the course of trading, pulled forth a heavy purse from his girdle.
“Here are one hundred philips,” he announced. “ ’Tis overmuch. But I offer it.”
Ere the *dalal*’s pious and seductive tongue could urge him further the Turk sat down again with a gesture of finality.
“I give him joy of her,” said he.
“She is thine, then, O Ibrahim, for one hundred philips.”
The Israelite relinquished the purse to the *dalal*’s white-robed assistants and advanced to receive the girl. The corsairs thrust her forward against him, still vainly battling, and his arms closed about her for a moment.
“Thou has cost me dear, thou daughter of Spain,” said he. “But I am content. Come.” And he made shift to lead her away. Suddenly, however, fierce as a tiger-cat she writhed her arms upwards and clawed at his face.

===Chinese===

“这边的优素福，少说出六十。”他满怀期许地立在一 位衣饰显赫的摩尔人跟前。
那摩尔人却摇头。
“安拉知道，我有三个太太，不出一个钟头就把她的姿色毁了，倒叫我赔本。”
拍卖师往前走，姑娘跟在后面，可这一路她寸步不让，同推她的人缠斗，还用火辣辣的卡斯蒂利亚话咒骂他们。她把指甲掐进一个海盗的胳膊，又狠狠啐了另一个一脸。罗莎蒙德疲惫的眼睛看着她，惊怖得睁大了——那惊怖，一半为着等着这可怜孩子的命运，一半为着她这场徒劳之战那不成体统的烈性。可偏偏，她这副做派给一个黎凡特土耳其人的观感截然不同。他从井台台阶上站起来，矮墩墩的一个。
“我出六十，买个乐子，驯驯这只野猫。”他说。
可易卜拉欣不让人压过。他出七十；土耳其人回价八十；易卜拉欣又抬到九十。场中一静。
拍卖师拿话激那土耳其人。“你就这么认了？输给一个以色列人？这可爱的姑娘，就给一个篡改经文的、火狱的嗣子、一族连枣核上一点渣都不肯分给同类的人？这是信士的耻辱。”
土耳其人被他激得又加了五枚，却明显不情愿。那犹太人呢，对这一通冲他来的数落全然不窘——做买卖，这样的话他一天听二十回——从腰带里拽出一只沉甸甸的钱袋。
“这里是一百枚金币，”他宣布，“多了。可我出。”
不等拍卖师那张虔诚又煽动的嘴再激，土耳其人一摆手坐了回去，算是认了头。
“那我把她让给他。”他说。
“她归你了，易卜拉欣，一百枚。”
以色列人把钱袋交给拍卖师的白袍助手，上前去领人。海盗们把还在徒劳挣扎的姑娘朝他一推，他的胳膊把她兜住，抱了片刻。
“你可叫我破费了，西班牙的女儿，”他说，“可我心甘。来吧。”他引她要走。可她骤然凶得像只豹猫，双臂朝上一拧，朝他脸上就抓。

===Original===

With a scream of pain he relaxed his hold of her and in that moment, quick as lightning she plucked the dagger that hung from his girdle so temptingly within her reach.
“*Válgame Dios!*” she cried, and ere a hand could be raised to prevent her she had buried the blade in her lovely breast and sank in a laughing, coughing, heap at his feet. A final convulsive heave and she lay there quite still, whilst Ibrahim glared down at her with eyes of dismay, and over all the market there hung a hush of sudden awe. Rosamund had risen in her place, and a faint colour came to warm her pallor, a faint light kindled in her eyes. God had shown her the way through this poor Spanish girl, and assuredly God would give her the means to take it when her own turn came. She felt herself suddenly uplifted and enheartened. Death was a sharp, swift severing, an easy door of escape from the horror that threatened her, and God in His mercy, she knew, would justify self-murder under such circumstances as were her own and that poor dead Andalusian maid’s.
At length Ibrahim roused himself from his momentary stupor. He stepped deliberately across the body, his face inflamed, and stood to beard the impassive *dalal*.
“She is dead!” he bleated. “I am defrauded. Give me back my gold!”
“Are we to give back the price of every slave that dies?” the *dalal* questioned him.
“But she was not yet delivered to me,” raved the Jew. “My hands had not touched her. Give me back my gold.”
“Thou liest, son of a dog,” was the answer, dispassionately delivered. “She was thine already. I had so pronounced her. Bear her hence, since she belongs to thee.”
The Jew, his face empurpling, seemed to fight for breath.
“How?” he choked. “Am I to lose a hundred philips?”
“What is written is written,” replied the serene *dalal*.
Ibrahim was frothing at the lips, his eyes were blood-injected. “But it was never written that.﻿ ﻿…”
“Peace,” said the *dalal*. “Had it not been written it could not have come to pass. It is the will of Allah! Who dares rebel against it?”
The crowd began to murmur.
“I want my hundred philips,” the Jew insisted, whereupon the murmur swelled into a sudden roar.
“Thou hearest?” said the *dalal*. “Allah pardon thee, thou art disturbing the peace of this market. Away, ere ill betide thee.”
“Hence! hence!” roared the crowd, and some advanced threateningly upon the luckless Ibrahim.

===Chinese===

他痛叫一声，一松劲；就在这一瞬，她疾如闪电，拔下了他腰间那把垂在她手边的匕首。
“上帝救我！”她喊；不等一只手来得及拦，刀已埋进她那可爱的胸膛，她笑着、咳着，瘫作一堆，跌在他脚边。最后一下痉挛的抽动过后，她躺在那里全然不动了。易卜拉欣瞪着她，一脸的错愕；整个市场罩下一阵突兀的肃然。罗莎蒙德在座位上站了起来，一层淡淡的红润了她惨白的脸，一点淡淡的光燃在她眼里。上帝借着这个可怜的西班牙姑娘，把路指给了她；轮到她自己时，上帝也必定给她走这条路的力量。她忽然觉得被托了起来，壮了起来。死是一记干脆利落的割断，是逃出那威胁着她的恐怖的一道便门；她知道，仁慈的上帝，在她和那死去的安达卢西亚姑娘这样的境地里，必会宽宥自戕。
末了，易卜拉欣从一时的麻木里醒过来。他一脚踏过尸体，涨红着脸，上前冲那位不动声色的拍卖师发作。
“她死了！”他尖着嗓子嚷，“我叫人坑了！把我的金子还我！”
“死一个奴隶就退一回钱，我们是这样的规矩么？”拍卖师反问。
“可她还没交到我手里！”犹太人狂怒道，“我的手还没碰着她！还我的金子！”
“你放屁，狗崽子，”回答得平平静静，“她已经是你的了。我已这样宣判。抬走吧，她既然归了你。”
犹太人脸色由红转紫，像喘不上气来。
“怎么？”他噎着说，“我就这么白折一百枚金币？”
“写下的就是写下的。”从容的拍卖师答。
易卜拉欣嘴角起了沫，眼里充了血。“可写下的从来没有这一条……”
“肃静，”拍卖师说，“倘若没写下，它便来不成。这是安拉的意旨！谁敢逆它？”
人群里起了怨声。
“我要我的一百枚金币。”犹太人还不依；怨声霎时涨成一片怒吼。
“听见了？”拍卖师说，“安拉饶恕你，你搅了这个市场的安宁。滚，趁还没招祸。”
“滚！滚！”人群吼着，有几个已经朝倒霉的易卜拉欣逼了上去。

===Original===

“Away, thou perverter of Holy Writ! thou filth! thou dog! Away!”
Such was the uproar, such the menace of angry countenances and clenched fists shaken in his very face, that Ibrahim quailed and forgot his loss in fear.
“I go, I go,” he said, and turned hastily to depart.
But the *dalal* summoned him back. “Take hence thy property,” said he, and pointed to the body. And so Ibrahim was forced to suffer the further mockery of summoning his slaves to bear away the lifeless body for which he had paid in lively potent gold.
Yet by the gates he paused again. “I will appeal me to the Basha,” he threatened. “Asad-ed-Din is just, and he will have my money restored to me.”
“So he will,” said the *dalal*, “when thou canst restore the dead to life,” and he turned to the portly Ayoub, who was plucking at his sleeve. He bent his head to catch the muttered words of Fenzileh’s *wazeer*. Then, in obedience to them, he ordered Rosamund to be brought forward.
She offered no least resistance, advancing in a singularly lifeless way, like a sleepwalker or one who had been drugged. In the heat and glare of the open market she stood by the *dalal*’s side at the head of the well, whilst he dilated upon her physical merits in that lingua franca which he used since it was current coin among all the assorted races represented there﻿—a language which the knowledge of French that her residence in France had taught her she was to her increasing horror and shame able to understand.
The first to make an offer for her was that same portly Moor who had sought to purchase the two Nubians. He rose to scrutinize her closely, and must have been satisfied, for the price he offered was a good one, and he offered it with contemptuous assurance that he would not be outbidden.
“One hundred philips for the milk-faced girl.”
“ ’Tis not enough. Consider me the moon-bright loveliness of her face,” said the *dalal* as he moved on. “Chigil yields us fair women, but no woman of Chigil was ever half so fair.”
“One hundred and fifty,” said the Levantine Turk with a snap.
“Not yet enough. Behold the stately height which Allah hath vouchsafed her. See the noble carriage of her head, the lustre of her eye! By Allah, she is worthy to grace the Sultan’s own harem.”
He said no more than the buyers recognized to be true, and excitement stirred faintly through their usually impassive ranks.

===Chinese===

“滚，你这篡改圣经的！你这脏东西！你这狗！滚！”
声势这样大，愤怒的脸和攥紧的拳头直晃到他鼻子底下，易卜拉欣怯了，怕压过了心疼。
“我走，我走。”他说，慌忙转身要走。
可拍卖师又把他唤住。“把你的产业带走，”他说，朝那尸首一指。于是易卜拉欣只得再受一重奚落：唤来他的奴隶，抬走那具他拿响当当的金子买下的、再无生气的身子。
到了大门口，他又站住。“我要告到巴沙跟前，”他撂下狠话，“阿萨德-丁是公正的，他必叫人还我的钱。”
“那是自然，”拍卖师说，“等你能把死人救活的时候。”说着他转向扯他袖子的胖阿尤布，俯首去听芬齐莱那位维齐尔咕哝的话。然后，遵照那话，他吩咐把罗莎蒙德带上来。
她没有半分抗拒，以一种出奇地没了魂儿的步子走上前，像梦游的，又像吃了药的。在露天市场的燥热和炫光里，她立在拍卖师身侧、井台前头，听他拿那门混通语夸耀她周身的好处——那语言是全场各色人等通行的钱，而凭旅居法国学下的法文底子，她竟能听懂，越听越是惊怖羞愤。
头一个朝她出价的，正是先前想买那两个努比亚人的胖摩尔人。他起身把她细细相看了一遍，想必满意，因为出的价不小，出得还带着一股笃定无人敢抬的傲气。
“一百枚金币，买这张奶子脸的姑娘。”
“不够。瞧瞧她那张月亮般明亮的脸，”拍卖师一面往前走一面说，“奇吉尔地方出美人，可奇吉尔的美人，也没有一半这样美。”
“一百五。”黎凡特土耳其人咬着牙说。
“还不够。瞧安拉赏她的这身量。瞧她那高贵的头姿、那双眼睛的光彩！安拉在上，她配进苏丹自己的后宫。”
他说的句句是买主们自己也认的实情；他们素来不动声色的行伍里，起了一阵轻轻的骚动。

===Original===

A Tagareen Moor named Yusuf offered at once two hundred.
But still the *dalal* continued to sing her praises. He held up one of her arms for inspection, and she submitted with lowered eyes, and no sign of resentment beyond the slow flush that spread across her face and vanished again.
“Behold me these limbs, smooth as Arabian silks and whiter than ivory. Look at those lips like pomegranate blossoms. The price is now two hundred philips. What wilt thou give, O Hamet?”
Hamet showed himself angry that his original bid should so speedily have been doubled. “By the Koran, I have purchased three sturdy girls from the Sus for less.”
“Wouldst thou compare a squat-faced girl from the Sus with this narcissus-eyed glory of womanhood?” scoffed the *dalal*.
“Two hundred and ten, then,” was Hamet’s sulky grunt.
The watchful Tsamanni considered that the time had come to buy her for his lord as he had been bidden.
“Three hundred,” he said curtly, to make an end of matters, and﻿—
“Four hundred,” instantly piped a shrill voice behind him.
He spun round in his amazement and met the leering face of Ayoub. A murmur ran through the ranks of the buyers, the people craned their necks to catch a glimpse of this openhanded purchaser.
Yusuf the Tagareen rose up in a passion. He announced angrily that never again should the dust of the *sôk* of Algiers defile his slippers, that never again would he come there to purchase slaves.
“By the Well of Zem-Zem,” he swore, “all men are bewitched in this market. Four hundred philips for a Frankish girl! May Allah increase your wealth, for verily you’ll need it.” And in his supreme disgust he stalked to the gates, and elbowed his way through the crowd, and so vanished from the *sôk*.
Yet ere he was out of earshot her price had risen further. Whilst Tsamanni was recovering from his surprise at the competitor that had suddenly appeared before him, the *dalal* had lured an increased offer from the Turk.
“ ’Tis a madness,” the latter deplored. “But she pleaseth me, and should it seem good to Allah the Merciful to lead her into the True Faith she may yet become the light of my harem. Four hundred and twenty philips, then, O *dalal*, and Allah pardon me my prodigality.”
Yet scarcely was his little speech concluded than Tsamanni with laconic eloquence rapped out: “Five hundred.”
“Y’Allah!” cried the Turk, raising his hands to heaven, and “Y’Allah!”

===Chinese===

一位塔加伦摩尔人，名叫优素福，当场出到二百。
可拍卖师还在唱她的赞。他拈起她一条胳膊举起来给人相看；她垂着眼由他摆布，除了脸上缓缓洇开又退去的一层红，没有一丝愠色。
“瞧这胳膊腿，滑得赛阿拉伯的绸，白得赛象牙。瞧那两片嘴唇，红得像石榴花。如今的价是二百枚了。你出多少，哈米特？”
哈米特见自己开的价这样快翻了一倍，登时不悦。“凭《古兰经》说，我从苏斯地方买三条壮实的丫头，也没花这个数。”
“你拿苏斯那扁脸丫头，同这水仙眼的人间尤物比？”拍卖师嗤之以鼻。
“二百一。”哈米特赌气咕哝道。
那留心伺候的察曼尼掂量着：奉命替主上买下她的时辰到了。
“三百。”他简短地说，想一锤定音。可是——
“四百。”他背后一条尖嗓子立时脆生生地叫起。
他惊讶地猛回头，正撞上阿尤布那张嬉皮笑脸。买主的行伍里起了一阵骚动，人们伸长脖子，要瞧一瞧这位出手阔绰的买主是何方神圣。
塔加伦人优素福勃然大怒。他怒气冲冲地宣布：阿尔及尔这市场的灰，从今往后休想再脏了他的鞋；他从此再不来这里买奴隶。
“凭渗渗泉起誓，”他骂道，“这市场上的人都中了邪。一个法兰克丫头，四百枚金币！愿安拉增益你们的财富——你们是真用得着。”他拂袖而去，大踏步走到门口，从人堆里挤了出去，就此出了场。
可他还没走出听不清的地方，她的价钱又涨了。趁察曼尼还没从这突然冒出的对手的惊愕里回神，拍卖师已从那土耳其人嘴里勾出了一个更高的价。
“这是疯了，”那人叹道，“可她中我的意；倘若慈悲的安拉乐意领她进真信仰，她将来未必不能做我内院的一盏灯。四百二十枚吧，拍卖师，安拉饶我这一回的挥霍。”
可他这小段话音未落，察曼尼已用他格言式的口才敲出三个字：“五百。”
“惟安拉！”土耳其人双手举天喊道；“惟安拉！”

===Original===

echoed the crowd.
“Five hundred and fifty,” shrilled Ayoub’s voice above the general din.
“Six hundred,” replied Tsamanni, still unmoved.
And now such was the general hubbub provoked by these unprecedented prices that the *dalal* was forced to raise his voice and cry for silence.
When this was restored Ayoub at once raised the price to seven hundred.
“Eight hundred,” snapped Tsamanni, showing at last a little heat.
“Nine hundred,” replied Ayoub.
Tsamanni swung round upon him again, white now with fury.
“Is this a jest, O father of wind?” he cried, and excited laughter by the taunt implicit in that appellation.
“And thou’rt the jester,” replied Ayoub with forced calm, “thou’lt find the jest a costly one.”
With a shrug Tsamanni turned again to the *dalal*. “A thousand philips,” said he shortly.
“Silence there!” cried the *dalal* again. “Silence, and praise Allah who sends good prices.”
“One thousand and one hundred,” said Ayoub the irrepressible.
And now Tsamanni not only found himself outbidden, but he had reached the outrageous limit appointed by Asad. He lacked authority to go further, dared not do so without first consulting the Basha. Yet if he left the *sôk* for that purpose Ayoub would meanwhile secure the girl. He found himself between sword and wall. On the one hand did he permit himself to be outbidden his master might visit upon him his disappointment. On the other, did he continue beyond the limit so idly mentioned as being far beyond all possibility, it might fare no less ill with him.
He turned to the crowd, waving his arms in furious gesticulation. “By the beard of the Prophet, this bladder of wind and grease makes sport of us. He has no intent to buy. What man ever heard of the half of such a price for a slave girl?”
Ayoub’s answer was eloquent; he produced a fat bag and flung it on the ground, where it fell with a mellow chink. “There is my sponsor,” he made answer, grinning in the very best of humours, savouring to the full his enemy’s rage and discomfiture, and savouring it at no cost to himself. “Shall I count out one thousand and one hundred philips, O *dalal*.”
“If the *wazeer* Tsamanni is content.”
“Dost thou know for whom I buy?” roared Tsamanni. “For the Basha himself, Asad-ed-Din, the exalted of Allah,” He advanced upon Ayoub with hands upheld.

===Chinese===

人群跟着喊。
“五百五。”阿尤布的尖嗓门压过满场喧腾。
“六百。”察曼尼答，依旧不动声色。
这一下，这样空前价钱激起的哄闹实在太大，拍卖师不得不放声喝令肃静。
静下来后，阿尤布立刻把价抬到七百。
“八百。”察曼尼斩钉截铁，终于也带了点火气。
“九百。”阿尤布回。
察曼尼又猛地转身朝他，这回气得发白。
“这是耍人么，放屁的老爷？”他喝道；这个称呼里夹枪带棒，惹起一片哄笑。
“要说耍人的是你，”阿尤布强作镇定地答，“你会发现这个玩笑价钱不菲。”
察曼尼耸耸肩，又转向拍卖师。“一千枚金币。”他干巴巴地说。
“那边肃静！”拍卖师又喊，“肃静！赞颂安拉，祂送来好价钱。”
“一千一百。”打不倒的阿尤布说。
这一下，察曼尼不光被人压了价，而且顶到了阿萨德划下的那条天价红线。再往上，他没有这个权；不经巴沙点头，他不敢。可他若为这个离场去请示，阿尤布就趁隙把姑娘拿下了。他前是剑，后是墙：一头，他若任人压价，主上或许拿他泄愤；另一头，他若越过那道“绝无可能”的线——那线原是随口一提的——他的下场未必更好。
他转向人群，气得双臂乱舞。“凭先知的胡须起誓，这个风囊、这团油膏，是在耍咱们。他根本不想买。半个这样的价钱买个女奴，谁听说过？”
阿尤布的回答很有分量：他掏出一只肥鼓鼓的钱袋，往地上一掼，落地一声圆润的脆响。“这是我的保人。”他答道，笑得再痛快没有——敌人的暴跳和狼狈，他咂摸了个够，自己还分文不用破费。“要点出一千一百枚金币来么，拍卖师？”
“只要维齐尔察曼尼没有话说。”
“你知道我是替谁买么？”察曼尼吼道，“替巴沙他本人，阿萨德-丁，安拉所扬的那位！”他高举双手，朝阿尤布逼去。

===Original===

“What shalt thou say to him, O dog, when he calls thee to account for daring to outbid him.”
But Ayoub remained unruffled before all this fury. He spread his fat hands, his eyes twinkling, his great lips pursed. “How should I know, since Allah has not made me all-knowing? Thou shouldst have said so earlier. ’Tis thus I shall answer the Basha should he question me, and the Basha is just.”
“I would not be thee, Ayoub﻿—not for the throne of Istambul.”
“Nor I thee, Tsamanni; for thou art jaundiced with rage.”
And so they stood glaring each at the other until the *dalal* called them back to the business that was to do.
“The price is now one thousand and one hundred philips. Wilt thou suffer defeat, O *wazeer*?”
“Since Allah wills. I have no authority to go further.”
“Then at one thousand and one hundred philips, Ayoub, she is.﻿ ﻿…”
But the sale was not yet to be completed. From the dense and eager throng about the gates rang a crisp voice﻿—
“One thousand and two hundred philips for the Frankish girl.”
The *dalal*, who had conceived that the limits of madness had been already reached, stood gaping now in fresh amazement. The mob crowed and cheered and roared between enthusiasm and derision, and even Tsamanni brightened to see another champion enter the lists who perhaps would avenge him upon Ayoub. The crowd parted quickly to right and left, and through it into the open strode Sakr-el-Bahr. They recognized him instantly, and his name was shouted in acclamation by that idolizing multitude.
That Barbary name of his conveyed no information to Rosamund, and her back being turned to the entrance she did not see him. But she had recognized his voice, and she had shuddered at the sound. She could make nothing of the bidding, nor what the purpose that surely underlay it to account for the extraordinary excitement of the traders. Vaguely had she been wondering what dastardly purpose Oliver might intend to serve, but now that she heard his voice that wonder ceased and understanding took its place. He had hung there somewhere in the crowd waiting until all competitors but one should have been outbidden, and now he stepped forth to buy her for his own﻿—his slave! She closed her eyes a moment and prayed God that he might not prevail in his intent.

===Chinese===

“等他找你算账、问你怎敢抬他的价的时候，你这条狗，拿什么回他的话？”
可阿尤布在这一切雷霆跟前纹丝不动。他摊开胖手，眼睛眨巴着，两片厚嘴唇努着。“我怎么会知道？安拉又没把我造得全知。你早该说破的。巴沙若问我，我就这么答他——巴沙是公正的。”
“把你换我，伊斯坦布尔的御座我也不干。”
“把你换我，我也不干，察曼尼——你已经气得发黄了。”
两人就这样彼此瞪着，直到拍卖师把扯回正题。
“如今的价是一千一百枚金币。你认输么，维齐尔？”
“安拉既如此安排。我没有权再往上走了。”
“那么，一千一百枚金币，阿尤布，她归……”
可这桩买卖还没完。大门口那密匝匝、探头探脑的人堆里，响起一个干脆的嗓门——
“一千二百枚金币，买这个法兰克姑娘。”
拍卖师原以为疯狂的顶点早到了，此刻又张着嘴怔住。人众又是喝彩、又是哄笑、又是吼叫，半是狂热半是嘲弄；连察曼尼也来了精神——场上又进来一位好汉，说不定能替他出这口阿尤布的恶气。人群飞快地左右分开，萨克埃尔-巴尔大步走了进来。众人一眼认出了他，他的名字被那群奉他为神明的人众喊得震天响。
他那个巴巴里的名字，给不了罗莎蒙德半点消息；她背朝入口，也没看见他。可她听出了他的嗓音，那声音叫她一颤。方才那场竞价，她参不透，竞价背后那个必有的用意——足以解释商贩们这阵反常疯狂的用意——她更参不透。她一直模模糊糊地纳闷：奥利弗这回又安着什么歹毒的心肠。如今一听见他的声音，纳闷没了，懂了。他一直藏在人堆里的什么地方，等着除一个之外所有的竞价者都出局，才现身出来买她——买归他自己！做他的奴隶！她闭了一瞬眼，求上帝别叫他如愿。

===Original===

Any fate but that; she would rob him even of the satisfaction of driving her to sheathe a poniard in her heart as that poor Andalusian girl had done. A wave almost of unconsciousness passed over her in the intensity of her horror. For a moment the ground seemed to rock and heave under her feet.
Then the dizziness passed, and she was herself again. She heard the crowd thundering “*Ma’sh’Allah!*” and “Sakr-el-Bahr!” and the *dalal* clamouring sternly for silence. When this was at last restored she heard his exclamation﻿—
“The glory to Allah who sends eager buyers! What sayest thou, O *wazeer* Ayoub?”
“Ay!” sneered Tsamanni, “what now?”
“One thousand and three hundred,” said Ayoub with a quaver of uneasy defiance.
“Another hundred, O *dalal*,” came from Sakr-el-Bahr in a quiet voice.
“One thousand and five hundred,” screamed Ayoub, thus reaching not only the limit imposed by his mistress, but the very limit of the resources at her immediate disposal. Gone, too, with that bid was all hope of profit to himself.
But Sakr-el-Bahr, impassive as Fate, and without so much as deigning to bestow a look upon the quivering eunuch, said again﻿—
“Another hundred, O *dalal*.”
“One thousand and six hundred philips!” cried the *dalal*, more in amazement than to announce the figure reached. Then controlling his emotions he bowed his head in reverence and made confession of his faith. “All things are possible if Allah wills them. The praise to Him who sends wealthy buyers.”
He turned to the crestfallen Ayoub, so crestfallen that in the contemplation of him Tsamanni was fast gathering consolation for his own discomfiture, vicariously tasting the sweets of vengeance. “What say you now, O perspicuous *wazeer*?”
“I say,” choked Ayoub, “that since by the favour of Shaitan he hath so much wealth he must prevail.”
But the insulting words were scarcely uttered than Sakr-el-Bahr’s great hand had taken the *wazeer* by the nape of his fat neck, a growl of anger running through the assembly to approve him.
“By the favour of Shaitan, sayest thou, thou sexless dog?” he growled, and tightened his grip so that the *wazeer* squirmed and twisted in an agony of pain. Down was his head thrust, and still down, until his fat body gave way and he lay supine and writhing in the dust of the *sôk*.

===Chinese===

怎样都好，只别那样；她连“逼她像那可怜的安达卢西亚姑娘一样把匕首捅进心口”这一份满足，也要从他手里夺走。恐惧之深，几乎叫她晕过去一阵，脚下的大地仿佛摇晃、拱动了起来。
随后晕眩过去，她回过神来。她听见人众雷鸣似的喊着“天晓得”和“萨克埃尔-巴尔”，听见拍卖师厉声喝叫肃静。静下来之后，她听见他扬声道——
“荣耀归于送来热心买主的安拉！你怎么说，维齐尔阿尤布？”
“是啊！”察曼尼冷笑，“现在怎么说？”
“一千三百。”阿尤布说，声气里带着虚虚的挑衅。
“再加一百，拍卖师。”萨克埃尔-巴尔的声音平平地说。
“一千五百！”阿尤布尖叫——这一下，他不光顶到了女主人划下的线，还顶到了她手头现银的极限。这一口喊出去，他自己那点捞头的指望也一并喊没了。
可萨克埃尔-巴尔，冷静得像命运本身，连正眼也不屑赏那哆嗦的阉人一下，只又说了一遍——
“再加一百，拍卖师。”
“一千六百枚金币！”拍卖师喊，与其说是报数，不如说是惊叹。随后他按住心绪，虔敬地垂首，表白他的信仰：“安拉若愿，万事皆能。赞颂那位送来阔绰买主的主。”
他转向垂头丧气的阿尤布——那副丧气相，叫察曼尼瞧着瞧着，很快攒出了自我安慰，隔着一层替自己尝到了复仇的甜头。“现在你怎么说，眼明心亮的维齐尔？”
“我说，”阿尤布哽着嗓子道，“撒旦既抬举他这么些钱，他必赢。”
可这侮辱的话音未落，萨克埃尔-巴尔的大手已经攥住那维齐尔肥胖的后颈，全场响起一片赞许的低吼。
“撒旦抬举，是么，你这条没性的狗？”他低吼着，手上收紧，攥得那维齐尔痛得扭来扭去。他的头被往下按，一按再按，直到那团肥肉撑不住，仰面朝天，在市场的尘土里打起滚来。

===Original===

“Shall I strangle thee, thou father of filth, or shall I fling thy soft flesh to the hooks to teach thee what is a man’s due from thee?” And as he spoke he rubbed the too daring fellow’s face roughly on the ground.
“Mercy!” squealed the *wazeer*. “Mercy, O mighty Sakr-el-Bahr, as thou lookest for mercy!”
“Unsay thy words, thou offal. Pronounce thyself a liar and a dog.”
“I do unsay them. I have foully lied. Thy wealth is the reward sent thee by Allah for thy glorious victories over the unbelieving.”
“Put out thine offending tongue,” said Sakr-el-Bahr, “and cleanse it in the dust. Put it forth, I say.”
Ayoub obeyed him in fearful alacrity, whereupon Sakr-el-Bahr released his hold and allowed the unfortunate fellow to rise at last, half-choked with dirt, livid of face, and quaking like a jelly, an object of ridicule and cruel mockery to all assembled.
“Now get thee hence, ere my sea hawks lay their talons on thee. Go!”
Ayoub departed in all haste to the increasing jeers of the multitude and the taunts of Tsamanni, whilst Sakr-el-Bahr turned him once more to the *dalal*.
“At one thousand and six hundred philips this slave is thine, O Sakr-el-Bahr, thou glory of Islam. May Allah increase thy victories!”
“Pay him, Ali,” said the corsair shortly, and he advanced to receive his purchase.
Face to face stood he now with Rosamund, for the first time since that day before the encounter with the Dutch argosy when he had sought her in the cabin of the carack.
One swift glance she bestowed on him, then, her senses reeling with horror at her circumstance she shrank back, her face of a deathly pallor. In his treatment of Ayoub she had just witnessed the lengths of brutality of which he was capable, and she was not to know that this brutality had been a deliberate piece of mummery calculated to strike terror into her.
Pondering her now he smiled a tight-lipped cruel smile that only served to increase her terror.
“Come,” he said in English.
She cowered back against the *dalal* as if for protection. Sakr-el-Bahr reached forward, caught her by the wrists, and almost tossed her to his Nubians, Abiad and Zal-Zer, who were attending him.
“Cover her face,” he bade them. “Bear her to my house. Away!”

===Chinese===

“掐死你呢，你这肮脏的老货，还是把你这身软肉扔上铁钩，教你明白明白男人该从你身上得到什么？”他说着，把这条胆子忒大的家伙的脸在地上来回蹭。
“饶命！”维齐尔尖叫，“饶命，威严的萨克埃尔-巴尔，你也指望饶命！”
“收回你的话，你这堆下水。自己招认：你是条撒谎的狗。”
“我收，我收。我说了混账话。你的财富，是安拉赏你的，赏你那一场场对不信者的大胜。”
“把你那闯祸的舌头伸出来，”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，“在土里涮干净。伸出来，我说。”
阿尤布惶恐地从速照办。萨克埃尔-巴尔这才撒手，放那倒霉蛋终于爬起来：半口泥堵着喉咙，脸色青紫，抖得像块凉粉，成了满场众人的一桩笑柄、一场残忍的取乐。
“趁我的海鹰还没把爪子搭上你，滚。滚！”
阿尤布抱头鼠窜，身后是愈来愈响的哄笑和察曼尼的奚落。萨克埃尔-巴尔重新转向拍卖师。
“一千六百枚金币，这个奴隶归你了，萨克埃尔-巴尔，伊斯兰的荣光。愿安拉增益你的胜绩！”
“付钱给他，阿里。”海盗简短地说，上前去领他的货。
此刻他同罗莎蒙德面对面站着——自打同荷兰商船接仗前、他在卡拉维尔帆船舱里寻她那一回，这是头一遭。
她飞快地瞥了他一眼，随后便被自身处境的惊怖淹得晕了神，朝后缩去，脸白得像死。方才他整治阿尤布的那番凶残，她亲眼看见了；她却无从知道，那番凶残原是一场算准了要吓她的假戏。
他把她打量了一番，抿嘴一笑，笑得残忍，只叫她更加魂飞魄散。
“来。”他用英语说。
她朝拍卖师身后缩，像求庇护。萨克埃尔-巴尔探身过去，攥住她的双腕，几乎是把她抛给了侍立在旁的努比亚人阿比亚德和扎勒-泽尔。
“给她蒙上面纱。抬到我家去。走！”

## VIII The Spaniard / 西班牙人
===Original===

The *Swallow*, having passed through a gale in the Bay of Biscay﻿—a gale which she weathered like the surprisingly steady old tub she was﻿—rounded Cape Finisterre and so emerged from tempest into peace, from leaden skies and mountainous seas into a sunny azure calm. It was like a sudden transition from winter into spring, and she ran along now, close hauled to the soft easterly breeze, with a gentle list to port.
It had never been Master Leigh’s intent to have got so far as this without coming to an understanding with his prisoner. But the wind had been stronger than his intentions, and he had been compelled to run before it and to head to southward until its fury should abate. Thus it fell out﻿—and all marvellously to Master Lionel’s advantage, as you shall see﻿—that the skipper was forced to wait until they stood along the coast of Portugal﻿—but well out to sea, for the coast of Portugal was none too healthy just then to English seamen﻿—before commanding Sir Oliver to be haled into his presence.
In the cramped quarters of the cabin in the poop of the little vessel sat her captain at a greasy table, over which a lamp was swinging faintly to the gentle heave of the ship. He was smoking a foul pipe, whose fumes hung heavily upon the air of that little chamber, and there was a bottle of Nantes at his elbow.
To him, sitting thus in state, was Sir Oliver introduced﻿—his wrists still pinioned behind him. He was haggard and hollow-eyed, and he carried a week’s growth of beard on his chin. Also his garments were still in disorder from the struggle he had made when taken, and from the fact that he had been compelled to lie in them ever since.
Since his height was such that it was impossible for him to stand upright in that low-ceilinged cabin, a stool was thrust forward for him by one of the ruffians of Leigh’s crew who had haled him from his confinement beneath the hatchway.
He sat down quite listlessly, and stared vacantly at the skipper. Master Leigh was somewhat discomposed by this odd calm when he had looked for angry outbursts. He dismissed the two seamen who fetched Sir Oliver, and when they had departed and closed the cabin door he addressed his captive.
“Sir Oliver,” said he, stroking his red beard, “ye’ve been most foully abused.”
The sunshine filtered through one of the horn windows and beat full upon Sir Oliver’s expressionless face.

===Chinese===

“燕子号”在比斯开湾里熬过了一场大风——凭着它那出奇稳当的老船身，它熬了过来——绕过菲尼斯特雷角，从风暴驶进了太平，从铅灰的天、山立的海，驶进了阳光碧蓝的平静。像一夜之间从严冬跨进了阳春，此刻它贴着软软的东风，张着满帆抢风前行，船身微微左倾。
利船长的本意，原没打算走得这么远才同他的囚徒摊牌。可风压倒了他的主意，他只得随风跑，一路朝南，等风头泄了再说。于是——结果竟大大便宜了莱昂内尔少爷，这个诸君往下便见——船长一直挨到船沿着葡萄牙海岸走的时候，才传令提奥利弗爵士来见；不过离岸远远的，因为葡萄牙那片海岸，那时节对英格兰水手来说不太平。
在那条小船艉楼低矮的舱房里，船长坐在一张油腻的桌前；一盏灯随着船身轻轻的起伏，在桌上方微微晃着。他抽着一杆呛人的烟斗，烟气压在那间小舱的空气里，肘边搁着一瓶南特酒。
奥利弗爵士就这样被引到他这位端着架子的跟前——双腕仍反缚在背后。他形容枯槁，眼窝深陷，下巴上蓄了一个星期的胡子；衣裳还乱着，从被拿时的挣扎乱起，又一路不曾换过。
他个子太高，在那顶棚低矮的舱里直不起身，利手下那个把他从舱口底下提上来的恶棍，给他推过来一张凳。
他恹恹地坐下，失神地瞪着船长。利船长本等着一场怒火，撞上这样反常的平静，倒有点不自在了。他打发押人来的两名水手出去，门一关，便对囚徒开了腔。
“奥利弗爵士，”他捋着红胡子说，“您叫人狠狠地坑了一把。”
日光从一扇角窗里滤进来，正照在奥利弗爵士那张毫无表情的脸上。

===Original===

“It was not necessary, you knave, to bring me hither to tell me so much,” he answered.
“Quite so,” said Master Leigh. “But I have something more to add. Ye’ll be thinking that I ha’ done you a disservice. There ye wrong me. Through me you are brought to know true friends from secret enemies; henceforward ye’ll know which to trust and which to mistrust.”
Sir Oliver seemed to rouse himself a little from his passivity, stimulated despite himself by the impudence of this rogue. He stretched a leg and smiled sourly.
“You’ll end by telling me that I am in your debt,” said he.
“You’ll end by saying so yourself,” the captain assured him. “D’ye know what I was bidden do with you?”
“Faith, I neither know nor care,” was the surprising answer, wearily delivered. “If it is for my entertainment that you propose to tell me, I beg you’ll spare yourself the trouble.”
It was not an answer that helped the captain. He pulled at his pipe a moment.
“I was bidden,” said he presently, “to carry you to Barbary and sell you there into the service of the Moors. That I might serve you, I made believe to accept this task.”
“God’s death!” swore Sir Oliver. “You carry make-believe to an odd length.”
“The weather has been against me. It were no intention o’ mine to ha’ come so far south with you. But we’ve been driven by the gale. That is overpast, and so that ye’ll promise to bear no plaint against me, and to make good some of the loss I’ll make by going out of my course, and missing a cargo that I wot of, I’ll put about and fetch you home again within a week.”
Sir Oliver looked at him and smiled grimly. “Now what a rogue are you that can keep faith with none!” he cried. “First you take money to carry me off; and then you bid me pay you to carry me back again.”
“Ye wrong me, sir, I vow ye do! I can keep faith when honest men employ me, and ye should know it, Sir Oliver. But who keeps faith with rogues is a fool﻿—and that I am not, as ye should also know. I ha’ done this thing that a rogue might be revealed to you and thwarted, as well as that I might make some little profit out of this ship o’ mine. I am frank with ye, Sir Oliver. I ha’ had some two hundred pounds in money and trinkets from your brother. Give me the like and.﻿ ﻿…”
But now of a sudden Sir Oliver’s listlessness was all dispelled.

===Chinese===

“这点事，也犯不着劳你把我弄到这儿来说，你这无赖。”他答。
“正是，”利船长说，“可我还有下文。您心里准在想，是我坏了您的事。这您可冤枉我了。亏得有我，您才认得出谁是真朋友、谁是暗地里的仇人；往后哪些信得过、哪些信不过，您就有数了。”
奥利弗似乎叫这无赖的厚颜激出了几分活气，不由自主地从那副死气里挣起来一点。他伸了伸腿，苦笑了一下。
“你说到末了，怕是要说我还欠着你的情。”他说。
“说到末了，这话要出自您自己的口。”船长打包票。“您知道人家雇我怎么处置您么？”
“说实话，我不知道，也不想知道，”回答倦倦的，出人意料。“你要是想拿这个给我解闷，我劝你省了这个神。”
这个回答可帮不上船长。他抽了口烟斗。
“人家雇我，”他半晌说，“把您运到巴巴里，卖给摩尔人当差。为了帮您，我假装接了这趟活。”
“该死！”奥利弗爵士骂道，“你这假戏也做得太出格了。”
“天公不作美。我本没打算带您跑这么南。可叫大风赶着，没法子。风头过去了；只要您肯应承不告我，再补上我为此误的损失——我这趟偏了航线，还错过一票我知道的货——我就掉转船头，不出一个礼拜送您回府。”
奥利弗爵士望着他，冷冷一笑。“瞧你这条谁的信都不守的无赖！”他喊道，“先收了钱把我劫走，又叫我付钱送我回来。”
“您冤枉我了，先生，我起誓！正经人雇我，我是守信的，这您该知道，奥利弗爵士。可跟无赖讲信用的人是傻子——我不是傻子，这您也该知道。我干这一票，一来叫那无赖在您跟前现形、叫他落空；二来嘛，也好叫我这条船小小的进项一点。我跟您交底，奥利弗爵士。您弟弟前后给了我大约两百镑现钱和首饰。您照样给一份，就……”
可就在这时，奥利弗爵士那副恹恹的死气，霎时散了个干净。

===Original===

It fell from him like a cloak, and he sat forward, wide awake and with some show of anger even.
“How do you say?” he cried, on a sharp, high note.
The captain stared at him, his pipe neglected. “I say that if so be as ye’ll pay me the same sum which your brother paid me to carry you off.﻿ ﻿…”
“My brother?” roared the knight. “Do you say my brother?”
“I said your brother.”
“Master Lionel?” the other demanded still.
“What other brothers have you?” quoth Master Leigh.
There fell a pause and Sir Oliver looked straight before him, his head sunken a little between his shoulders. “Let me understand,” he said at length. “Do you say that my brother Lionel paid you money to carry me off﻿—in short, that my presence aboard this foul hulk of yours is due to him?”
“Whom else had ye suspected? Or did ye think that I did it for my own personal diversion?”
“Answer me,” bellowed Sir Oliver, writhing in his bonds.
“I ha’ answered you more than once already. Still, I tell you once again, since ye are slow to understand it, that I was paid a matter of two hundred pound by your brother, Master Lionel Tressilian, to carry you off to Barbary and there sell you for a slave. Is that plain to you?”
“As plain as it is false. You lie, you dog!”
“Softly, softly!” quoth Master Leigh, good-humouredly.
“I say you lie!”
Master Leigh considered him a moment. “Sets the wind so!” said he at length, and without another word he rose and went to a sea-chest ranged against the wooden wall of the cabin. He opened it and took thence a leather bag. From this he produced a handful of jewels. He thrust them under Sir Oliver’s nose. “Haply,” said he, “ye’ll be acquainted with some of them. They was given me to make up the sum since your brother had not the whole two hundred pound in coin. Take a look at them.”
Sir Oliver recognized a ring and a long pear-shaped pearl earring that had been his brother’s; he recognized a medallion that he himself had given Lionel two years ago; and so, one by one, he recognized every trinket placed before him.
His head drooped to his breast, and he sat thus awhile like a man stunned. “My God!” he groaned miserably, at last. “Who, then, is left to me! Lionel too! Lionel!” A sob shook the great frame. Two tears slowly trickled down that haggard face and were lost in the stubble of beard upon his chin. “I am accursed!” he said.

===Chinese===

它像一件斗篷从他身上褪落；他朝前坐正，全醒了，甚至带了怒容。
“你说什么？”他尖着嗓门喝问。
船长瞪着他，烟斗都忘了抽。“我说，倘若您肯照您弟弟雇我劫您的那个数，付我一笔……”
“我弟弟？”爵士吼道，“你说我弟弟？”
“我说的是您弟弟。”
“莱昂内尔少爷？”对方还追着问。
“你还有几个弟弟？”利船长说。
一阵静默。奥利弗直勾勾望着前面，脑袋微微缩在两肩之间。“叫我明白明白，”他终于开口，“你是说，我弟弟莱昂内尔出钱雇你劫我——干脆说，我落在这条破船上周身，是他一手造成的？”
“不然你还疑谁？还是你以为我干这个，是图我自个儿消遣？”
“答我！”奥利弗爵士在绑绳里挣着吼。
“答过你不止一回了。也罢，再答你一回，谁叫你领会得慢：你弟弟莱昂内尔·特雷西利安少爷，给了我两百镑上下的钱，雇我把你劫到巴巴里卖作奴隶。这话够明白么？”
“明白得就像它假得一样。你撒谎，你这狗！”
“轻些，轻些！”利老板好脾气地说。
“我说你撒谎！”
利船长打量了他片刻。“原来刮的是这路风！”他末了说，一言不发地起身，走到舱壁边一口水手衣箱前，开箱取出一只皮袋，从袋里掏出一把珠宝，直送到奥利弗鼻子底下。“巧了，”他说，“这里头有几样，您兴许认得。是补齐数目给我的——您弟弟一时拿不出整整两百镑现钱。自己瞧瞧。”
奥利弗认出一枚戒指、一只梨形长珠耳环，都是弟弟的旧物；又认出一枚徽章，是他自己两年前送给莱昂内尔的；就这样，摆到眼前的一件一件，他全认了出来。
他的头垂到胸前，就那样坐了一阵，像个挨了闷棍的人。“我的上帝！”他终于凄惨地呻吟，“这下还剩谁！连莱昂内尔！莱昂内尔！”一声抽噎撼动那副大骨架。两行泪慢慢地淌下那张枯槁的脸，没进下巴的胡茬里。“我是遭了天咒的！”他说。

===Original===

Never without such evidence could he have believed this thing. From the moment that he was beset outside the gates of Godolphin Court he had conceived it to be the work of Rosamund, and his listlessness was begotten of the thought that she could have suffered conviction of his guilt and her hatred of him to urge her to such lengths as these. Never for an instant had he doubted the message delivered him by Lionel that it was Mistress Rosamund who summoned him. And just as he believed himself to be going to Godolphin Court in answer to her summons, so did he conclude that the happening there was the real matter to which she had bidden him, a thing done by her contriving, her answer to his attempt on the previous day to gain speech with her, her manner of ensuring that such an impertinence should never be repeated.
This conviction had been gall and wormwood to him; it had drugged his very senses, reducing him to a listless indifference to any fate that might be reserved him. Yet it had not been so bitter a draught as this present revelation. After all, in her case there were some grounds for the hatred that had come to take the place of her erstwhile love. But in Lionel’s what grounds were possible? What motives could exist for such an action as this, other than a monstrous, a loathly egoism which desired perhaps to ensure that the blame for the death of Peter Godolphin should not be shifted from the shoulders that were unjustly bearing it, and the accursed desire to profit by the removal of the man who had been brother, father and all else to him? He shuddered in sheer horror. It was incredible, and yet beyond a doubt it was true. For all the love which he had showered upon Lionel, for all the sacrifices of self which he had made to shield him, this was Lionel’s return. Were all the world against him he still must have believed Lionel true to him, and in that belief must have been enheartened a little. And now﻿ ﻿… His sense of loneliness, of utter destitution overwhelmed him. Then slowly of his sorrow resentment was begotten, and being begotten it grew rapidly until it filled his mind and whelmed in its turn all else. He threw back his great head, and his bloodshot, gleaming eyes fastened upon Captain Leigh, who seated now upon the sea-chest was quietly observing him and waiting patiently until he should recover the wits which this revelation had scattered.

===Chinese===

没有这样的实证，他断不会信下这件事。打在戈多尔芬宅门外被围的那一刻起，他就认定这是罗莎蒙德的手笔；他那副恹恹，正生自那个念头：她竟信了他的罪，竟恨他恨到肯把他往这般田地里推。莱昂内尔捎给他的口信，说是罗莎蒙德小姐相召，他一时一刻不曾疑过。他既信自己是应她的召唤去戈多尔芬宅，便也断定那里出的事正是召他的正题：一件出她安排的事，是对他前一天求见之举的回答，是她确保那样的唐突永不再犯的手段。
这个认定于他是胆汁、是苦艾，麻了他的知觉，把他熬成了对任何下场都无所谓的死灰。可它再苦，也苦不过眼下这一场揭底。说到底，她那样，恨替代旧爱，多少还有个由头；莱昂内尔这边，能有什么由头？除了一副怪诞的、可憎的自私——或者是要钉死那桩杀彼得·戈多尔芬的罪责，叫它永不从他冤扛着的肩上挪开，外加那该咒的贪心，要除掉那个做过他的兄长、做过他的父亲、做过他一切的人，好独占好处——还能有什么动机？他纯然是骇得发抖。这不可信，却千真万确。他把满腔的爱都浇在莱昂内尔身上，为护他舍了自己一回又一回，这就是莱昂内尔的回礼。哪怕全世界与他为敌，他也必信莱昂内尔对他忠心不改，单凭这个信，他也还能得几分宽慰。可如今……那股孤独、那股彻底的孤绝淹没了他。随后，悲哀里慢慢生出怨来；一出头便疯长，长到占满他整个心，又反过来淹没其余的一切。他猛地昂起那颗大脑袋，一双充血放光的眼睛，钉在利船长身上——船长这会儿坐在衣箱上，静静观察着他，耐着性子等他从这场惊变震散的神魂里缓回来。

===Original===

“Master Leigh,” said he, “what is your price to carry me home again to England?”
“Why, Sir Oliver,” said he, “I think the price I was paid to carry you off would be a fair one. The one would wipe out t’other as it were.”
“You shall have twice the sum when you land me on Trefusis Point again,” was the instant answer.
The captain’s little eyes blinked and his shaggy red eyebrows came together in a frown. Here was too speedy an acquiescence. There must be guile behind it, or he knew naught of the ways of men.
“What mischief are ye brooding?” he sneered.
“Mischief, man? To you?” Sir Oliver laughed hoarsely. “God’s light, knave, d’ye think I consider you in this matter, or d’ye think I’ve room in my mind for such petty resentments together with that other?”
It was the truth. So absolute was the bitter sway of his anger against Lionel that he could give no thought to this rascally seaman’s share in the adventure.
“Will ye give me your word for that?”
“My word? Pshaw, man! I have given it already. I swear that you shall be paid the sum I’ve named the moment you set me ashore again in England. Is that enough for you? Then cut me these bonds, and let us make an end of my present condition.”
“Faith, I am glad to deal with so sensible a man! Ye take it in the proper spirit. Ye see that what I ha’ done I ha’ but done in the way of my calling, that I am but a tool, and that what blame there be belongs to them which hired me to this deed.”
“Aye, ye’re but a tool﻿—a dirty tool, whetted with gold; no more. ’Tis admitted. Cut me these bonds, a God’s name! I’m weary o’ being trussed like a capon.”
The captain drew his knife, crossed to Sir Oliver’s side and slashed his bonds away without further word. Sir Oliver stood up so suddenly that he smote his head against the low ceiling of the cabin, and so sat down again at once. And in that moment from without and above there came a cry which sent the skipper to the cabin door. He flung it open, and so let out the smoke and let in the sunshine. He passed out on to the poop deck, and Sir Oliver﻿—conceiving himself at liberty to do so﻿—followed him.
In the waist below a little knot of shaggy seamen were crowding to the larboard bulwarks, looking out to sea; on the forecastle there was another similar assembly, all staring intently ahead and towards the land.

===Chinese===

“利船长，”他说，“送我回英格兰，你开个价。”
“呃，奥利弗爵士，”他说，“我想，雇您走的那笔钱，做个公道价正好。一头抵一头，两讫。”
“你再在特雷富西斯角把我放上岸，我给你双倍。”回答脱口而出。
船长的小眼睛眨了眨，乱蓬蓬的红眉毛拧成了一个疙瘩。应得太快了。这里头必有算计，否则他白活了这些年，不懂人。
“你肚子里盘算什么坏水？”他冷笑。
“坏水？冲你？”奥利弗哑声笑了。“老天在上，你这无赖，你以为这件事里我还数得着你？你以为我心里搁得下你这点小怨，同那另一桩一起？”
这是实话。他对莱昂内尔的怒火烧得太旺，独占了心，这条奸猾水手在整桩事里的那一份，他想都想不起。
“这话你敢拿人格担保？”
“人格？呸，伙计！担保早给过了。我起誓：你的脚一把我送上英格兰的岸，我点的那个数，分文不少付你。这够不够？够就割了这些绳子，叫我别再这副样子见人。”
“说实话，同这么个明白人打交道，我高兴！您这个态度就对了。您瞧，我干的事，不过是干我这一行的本分；我不过是件家伙，要论罪，罪在雇我干这票的人身上。”
“是啊，你不过是件家伙——件龌龊的家伙，拿金子开的刃；仅此而已。我认了。看在上帝份上，割绳子！我这只阉鸡当够了。”
船长抽出刀，走到奥利弗爵士身边，一言不发割断了绑绳。奥利弗爵士起身起得太猛，脑袋撞上低矮的舱顶，只得立刻又坐了回去。就在这一刻，外头上头传来一声喊，喊得船长直奔舱门。他一把推开门——放出去一舱的烟，放进来的满是阳光。他出到艉楼甲板上；奥利弗爵士自觉行动已自由，跟了出去。
下面的船腰里，一小簇毛发蓬乱的水手正挤在左舷栏边朝海上了望；前艄上也聚着差不多的另一堆，全都死死盯着前方、盯着陆地那边。

===Original===

They were off Cape Roca at the time, and when Captain Leigh saw by how much they had lessened their distance from shore since last he had conned the ship, he swore ferociously at his mate who had charge of the wheel. Ahead of them away on their larboard bow and in line with the mouth of the Tagus from which she had issued﻿—and where not a doubt but she had been lying in wait for such stray craft as this﻿—came a great tall-masted ship, equipped with topgallants, running well-nigh before the wind with every foot of canvas spread.
Close-hauled as was the *Swallow* and with her topsails and mizzen reefed she was not making more than one knot to the Spaniard’s five﻿—for that she was a Spaniard was beyond all doubt judging by the haven whence she issued.
“Luff alee!” bawled the skipper, and he sprang to the wheel, thrusting the mate aside with a blow of his elbow that almost sent him sprawling.
“ ’Twas yourself set the course,” the fellow protested.
“Thou lubberly fool,” roared the skipper. “I bade thee keep the same distance from shore. If the land comes jutting out to meet us, are we to keep straight on until we pile her up?” He spun the wheel round in his hands, and turned her down the wind. Then he relinquished the helm to the mate again. “Hold her thus,” he commanded, and bellowing orders as he went, he heaved himself down the companion to see them executed. Men sprang to the ratlines to obey him, and went swarming aloft to let out the reefs of the topsails; others ran astern to do the like by the mizzen and soon they had her leaping and plunging through the green water with every sheet unfurled, racing straight out to sea.
From the poop Sir Oliver watched the Spaniard. He saw her veer a point or so to starboard, heading straight to intercept them, and he observed that although this manoeuvre brought her fully a point nearer to the wind than the *Swallow*, yet, equipped as she was with half as much canvas again as Captain Leigh’s piratical craft, she was gaining steadily upon them nonetheless.
The skipper came back to the poop, and stood there moodily watching that other ship’s approach, cursing himself for having sailed into such a trap, and cursing his mate more fervently still.
Sir Oliver meanwhile took stock of so much of the *Swallow*’s armament as was visible and wondered what like were those on the main deck below.

===Chinese===

船那时正在罗卡角外。利船长一看上次看船以来同岸边的距离竟缩短了这么多，冲着掌舵的大副狠狠咒骂起来。前方、左舷首舷的远处，同塔霍河口正成一线——那船正是从河口出来的，多半还一直潜伏在那儿，专候“燕子号”这样的散单——来了一条高桅大船，上桅帆齐备，几乎是顺风满帆，跑得飞快。
“燕子号”抢着风，上帆和后帆又都收着，一小时不过走出一海里，那条西班牙船——看她出来的港，是西班牙船无疑——一小时走出五海里。
“转舵抢风！”船长吼着扑向舵轮，一肘子把大副顶得险些跌个趔趄。
“航线是您亲手定的。”那人叫屈。
“你这蠢货，”船长咆哮，“我叫你保持的是同岸的距离！陆地自己戳出来迎咱们，咱们还要照直走，走到撞上去不成？”他双手扳轮，掉头朝下风去，随后又把舵交还大副。“照这样把定，”他吩咐，一路吼着命令下了升降口，亲自盯着照办。水手们扑上桅索，蜂着往上爬，放开上帆收起的褶；另一些奔到船尾，去放后帆。不多时，船已张着每一面帆，在绿浪里又跳又扎，直朝大洋里奔。
奥利弗在艉楼上望着那条西班牙船。他见她朝右转了一两个罗经点，直插过来拦截；他又注意到，这一转虽叫她比“燕子号”更顶风一个点，可她多出利船长那条海盗船一半的帆面，照旧步步逼近。
船长回到艉楼，阴着脸站在那里看那条船过来，一面咒自己怎么会驶进这样一个套，一面更起劲地咒他的大副。
这厢，奥利弗爵士把“燕子号”看得见的武备打量了一遍，揣摩着下面主甲板上那些又是什么家伙。

===Original===

He dropped a question on that score to the captain, dispassionately, as though he were no more than an indifferently interested spectator, and with never a thought to his position aboard.
“Should I be racing her afore the Wind if I was properly equipped?” growled Leigh. “Am I the man to run before a Spaniard? As it is I do no more than lure her well away from land.”
Sir Oliver understood, and was silent thereafter. He observed a bo’sun and his mates staggering in the waist under loads of cutlasses and small arms which they stacked in a rack about the mainmast. Then the gunner, a swarthy, massive fellow, stark to the waist with a faded scarf tied turban-wise about his head, leapt up the companion to the brass carronade on the larboard quarter, followed by a couple of his men.
Master Leigh called up the bo’sun, bade him take the wheel, and dispatched the mate forward to the forecastle, where another gun was being prepared for action.
Thereafter followed a spell of racing, the Spaniard ever lessening the distance between them, and the land dropping astern until it was no more than a hazy line above the shimmering sea. Suddenly from the Spaniard appeared a little cloud of white smoke, and the boom of a gun followed, and after it came a splash a cable’s length ahead of the *Swallow*’s bows.
Linstock in hand the brawny gunner on the poop stood ready to answer them when the word should be given. From below came the gunner’s mate to report himself ready for action on the main deck and to receive his orders.
Came another shot from the Spaniard, again across the bows of the *Swallow*.
“ ’Tis a clear invitation to heave to,” said Sir Oliver.
The skipper snarled in his fiery beard. “She has a longer range than most Spaniards,” said he. “But I’ll not waste powder yet for all that. We’ve none to spare.”
Scarcely had he spoken when a third shot boomed. There was a splintering crash overhead followed by a sough and a thud as the maintopmast came hurtling to the deck and in its fall stretched a couple of men in death. Battle was joined, it seemed. Yet Captain Leigh did nothing in a hurry.
“Hold there!” he roared to the gunner who swung his linstock at that moment in preparation.
She was losing way as a result of that curtailment of her mainmast, and the Spaniard came on swiftly now. At last the skipper accounted her near enough, and gave the word with an oath.

===Chinese===

他朝船长丢过去一句问话，问得不动声色，仿佛自己不过是个可有可无的看客，全不把自己的处境放在话里。
“我要是家什齐备，会顶着风跟她赛跑么？”利咕哝道，“我是见了西班牙人就跑的人么？眼下这不过是把她从岸边诱远些。”
奥利弗明白了，便不再作声。他看见一名水手长带着伙计们，抱着弯刀和轻兵器在船腰里深一脚浅一脚，把它们码进主桅四周的架子里。随后炮长上来了——一个黝黑的大块头，赤着上身，头上拿一条褪色围巾缠成头巾样——跳上升降口，奔左舷后侧那门铜卡龙炮去了，身后跟着他的两名手下。
利船长把水手长唤上艉楼，叫他掌舵，又打发大副去前艄——那边另一门炮正在备战。
接下来是一场赛跑：西班牙船一步步咬近，陆地落在船尾，缩成波光粼粼的海面上一线朦胧。忽然，西班牙船上腾起一小团白烟，一声炮响跟着滚来，然后是“燕子号”船头前一个链长处激起的水柱。
艉楼上那膀大腰圆的炮长手持点火杆立着，只等一声令下便回敬。下面上来炮长的副手，禀报主甲板已备好待命，请示号令。
西班牙船上又是一炮，又是掠过“燕子号”的船头。
“这是明白地请咱们停船了。”奥利弗爵士说。
船长征他那把火红的胡子里哼了一声。“她的射程比一般的西班牙船远，”他说，“可我还不想浪费火药。咱们的火药不经费。”
话音未落，第三炮响了。头顶一声脆裂，接着呼的一响一记闷砸——主桅中段连帆带索砸下甲板，砸倒了两个人，当场砸死。看来仗是真开打了。可利船长依旧不慌。
“稳住！”他朝正抡起点火杆待发的炮长吼。
主桅这一折，“燕子号”的势头泄了，西班牙船霎时追近。末了，船长掂量她够近了，才咒了一声，下了令。

===Original===

The *Swallow* fired her first and last shot in that encounter. After the deafening thunder of it and through the cloud of suffocating smoke, Sir Oliver saw the high forecastle of the Spaniard rent open.
Master Leigh was cursing his gunner for having aimed too high. Then he signalled to the mate to fire the culverin of which he had charge. That second shot was to be the signal for the whole broadside from the main deck below. But the Spaniard anticipated them. Even as the skipper of the *Swallow* signalled the whole side of the Spaniard burst into flame and smoke.
The *Swallow* staggered under the blow, recovered an instant, then listed ominously to larboard.
“Hell!” roared Leigh. “She’s bilging!” and Sir Oliver saw the Spaniard standing off again, as if satisfied with what she had done. The mate’s gun was never fired, nor was the broadside from below. Indeed that sudden list had set the muzzles pointing to the sea; within three minutes of it they were on a level with the water. The *Swallow* had received her deathblow, and she was settling down.
Satisfied that she could do no further harm, the Spaniard luffed and hove to, awaiting the obvious result and intent upon picking up what slaves she could to man the galleys of his Catholic Majesty on the Mediterranean.
Thus the fate intended Sir Oliver by Lionel was to be fulfilled; and it was to be shared by Master Leigh himself, which had not been at all in that venal fellow’s reckoning.

===Chinese===

“燕子号”在这一场里放的头一炮，也是末一炮。震聋人的雷声过后，透过呛人的烟云，奥利弗爵士看见西班牙船高耸的前艄被撕开了。
利船长正骂炮长瞄得太高，一面又打信号叫大副开他那门长管炮。那第二炮本是下面主甲板全舷齐发的号令。可西班牙人抢了先。“燕子号”的信号才打出，西班牙船整个侧面便轰然喷出火和烟。
“燕子号”在这一击下踉跄了一下，撑住一瞬，随即不祥地朝左舷倾去。
“见鬼！”利吼道，“船底漏了！”奥利弗爵士眼见西班牙船又荡开去，仿佛对已做的功课心满意足。大副那门炮终究没有放，下面的齐发也没有来。那突兀的一倾，早把炮口压向了海面；不出三分钟，炮口已同水面齐平。“燕子号”挨了她的致命一击，正在往下沉。
西班牙船见她再也成不了祸患，便转舵停船，等着那个明摆着的结局，一心打捞她能捞的奴隶——好去补她天主教陛下在地中海的大帆船。
就这样，莱昂内尔给奥利弗爵士安排的命数，眼看要应验了；同他分担这命数的还有利老板本人——这一层，可全不在那个见钱眼开的家伙的盘算里。

## XII The Subtlety of Fenzileh / 芬齐莱的心机
===Original===

Oliver considered the woman for a long moment as she sat half-crouching on the divan, her hands locked, her face set and stony, her eyes lowered. He sighed gently and turned away. He paced to the parapet and looked out upon the city bathed in the white glare of the full risen moon. There arose thence a hum of sound, dominated, however, by the throbbing song of a nightingale somewhere in his garden and the croaking of the frogs by the pool in the valley.
Now that truth had been dragged from its well, and tossed, as it were, into Rosamund’s lap, he felt none of the fierce exultation which he had conceived that such an hour as this must bring him. Rather, indeed, was he saddened and oppressed. To poison the unholy cup of joy which he had imagined himself draining with such thirsty zest there was that discovery of a measure of justification for her attitude towards him in her conviction that his disappearance was explained by flight.
He was weighed down by a sense that he had put himself entirely in the wrong; that in his vengeance he had overreached himself; and he found the fruits of it, which had seemed so desirably luscious, turning to ashes in his mouth.
Long he stood there, the silence between them entirely unbroken. Then at length he stirred, turned from the parapet, and paced slowly back until he came to stand beside the divan, looking down upon her from his great height.
“At last you have heard the truth,” he said. And as she made no answer he continued: “I am thankful it was surprised out of him before the torture was applied, else you might have concluded that pain was wringing a false confession from him.” He paused, but still she did not speak; indeed, she made no sign that she had heard him. “That,” he concluded, “was the man whom you preferred to me. Faith, you did not flatter me, as perhaps you may have learnt.”
At last she was moved from her silence, and her voice came dull and hard. “I have learnt how little there is to choose between you,” she said. “It was to have been expected. I might have known two brothers could not have been so dissimilar in nature. Oh, I am learning a deal, and swiftly!”
It was a speech that angered him, that cast out entirely the softer mood that had been growing in him.
“You are learning?” he echoed. “What are you learning?”
“Knowledge of the ways of men.”
His teeth gleamed in his wry smile.

===Chinese===

奥利弗把那女人端详了许久：她半蜷在软榻上，双手交扣，脸绷得像块石头，眼帘垂着。他轻轻叹了口气，转过身去。他踱到栏边，望着那座浸在满月初升的白光里的城。城里腾起一片嗡嗡的市声，可压阵的，是他园子里什么地方一只夜莺婉转的啼鸣，和谷中池塘边此起彼伏的蛙鼓。
如今真相既已从井底拖出、又仿佛兜头抛进了罗莎蒙德的怀里，他却全然没有自己想象中这样的时辰必会带来的那股凶猛的快意。倒是悲凉，倒觉得压抑。为他自以为正畅饮个痛快的那杯不洁的欢乐之酒下毒的，是他发现了：她对他的那副态度，竟也情有可原——她认定他的失踪就是畏罪潜逃，这份认定自有它一番道理。
他压着一副沉甸甸的感觉：他把自己整个儿推到了理亏的一头；报起仇来，他使过了头；如今尝它的果子——先前看来那样馋人的甘美——在嘴里正化成灰。
他久久立在那里，两人之间的沉寂不曾破过一分。末了他动了动，离开栏边，慢慢踱回来，踱到软榻旁站定，从他的高处俯视着她。
“你到底听见真相了，”他说。她不作声，他便接着说：“多亏这真相赶在动刑之前从他嘴里惊了出来；不然你多半要想，是痛楚逼出来的假供。”他顿了顿，她仍不开口，甚至没有一点听见了的样子。“这，”他收了话，“就是你觉着比我强的那个人。说实话，你这可没抬举我——这一层，你大约也领教过了。”
她终于被激得开了口，嗓音又钝又硬。“我领教了你们两个之间原来没多少可挑的，”她说，“这也在意料之中。我早该想到，亲兄弟俩的天性，不可能差那样远。哦，我学的东西可多，学得可快！”
这话惹恼了他，把他心里刚生出的那点软意冲了个干净。
“你学东西？”他反问，“你学什么？”
“学男人的路数。”
他苦笑时，牙齿一闪。

“I hope the knowledge will bring you as much bitterness as the knowledge of women﻿—of one woman﻿—has brought me. To have believed me what you believed me﻿—me whom you conceived yourself to love!” He felt, perhaps the need to repeat it that he might keep the grounds of his grievance well before his mind.
“If I have a mercy to beg of you it is that you will not shame me with the reminder.”
“Of your faithlessness?” he asked. “Of your disloyal readiness to believe the worst evil of me?”
“Of my ever having believed that I loved you. That is the thought that shames me, as nothing else in life could shame me, as not even the slave market and all the insult to which you have submitted me could shame me. You taunt me with my readiness to believe evil of you.﻿ ﻿…”
“I do more than taunt you with it,” he broke in, his anger mounting under the pitiless lash of her scorn. “I lay to your charge the wasted years of my life, all the evil that has followed out of it, all that I have suffered, all that I have lost, all that I am become.”
She looked up at him coldly, astonishingly mistress of herself. “You lay all this to my charge?” she asked him.
“I do.” He was very vehement. “Had you not used me as you did, had you not lent a ready ear to lies, that whelp my brother would never have gone to such lengths, nor should I ever have afforded him the opportunity.”
She shifted on the cushions of the divan and turned her shoulder to him.
“All this is very idle,” she said coldly. Yet perhaps because she felt that she had need to justify herself she continued: “If, after all, I was so ready to believe evil of you, it is that my instincts must have warned me of the evil that was ever in you. You have proved to me tonight that it was not you who murdered Peter; but to attain that proof you have done a deed that is even fouler and more shameful, a deed that reveals to the full the blackness of your heart. Have you not proved yourself a monster of vengeance and impiety?” She rose and faced him again in her sudden passion. “Are you not﻿—you that were born a Cornish Christian gentleman﻿—become a heathen and a robber, a renegade and a pirate? Have you not sacrificed your very God to your vengeful lust?”
He met her glance fully, never quailing before her denunciation, and when she had ended on that note of question he counter-questioned her.
“And your instincts had forewarned you of all this?
