===Original===

’Tis a whim of mine, and I cannot suffer to be thwarted in my whims. To gratify this one I will pay three thousand philips.”
He looked at her and wondered what devilries might be stirring in her mind, what evil purpose she desired to serve.
“Thou’lt pay three thousand philips?” he said slowly. Then bluntly asked her: “Why?”
“To gratify a whim, to please a fancy.”
“What is the nature of this costly whim?” he insisted.
“The desire to possess her for my own,” she answered evasively.
“And this desire to possess her, whence is it sprung?” he returned, as patient as he was relentless.
“You ask too many questions,” she exclaimed with a flash of anger.
He shrugged and smiled. “You answer too few.”
She set her arms akimbo and faced him squarely. Faintly through her veil he caught the gleam of her eyes, and he cursed the advantage she had in that her face was covered from his reading.
“In a word, Oliver-Reis,” said she, “wilt sell her for three thousand philips?”
“In a word﻿—no,” he answered her.
“Thou’lt not? Not for three thousand philips?” Her voice was charged with surprise, and he wondered was it real or assumed.
“Not for thirty thousand,” answered he. “She is mine, and I’ll not relinquish her. So since I have proclaimed my mind, and since to tarry here is fraught with peril for us both, I beg thee to depart.”
There fell a little pause, and neither of them noticed the alert interest stamped upon the white face of Rosamund. Neither of them suspected her knowledge of French which enabled her to follow most of what was said in the lingua franca they employed.
Fenzileh drew close to him. “Thou’lt not relinquish her, eh?” she asked, and he was sure she sneered. “Be not so confident. Thou’lt be forced to it, my friend﻿—if not to me, why then, to Asad. He is coming for her, himself, in person.”
“Asad?” he cried, startled now.
“Asad-ed-Din,” she answered, and upon that resumed her pleading. “Come, then! It were surely better to make a good bargain with me than a bad one with the Basha.”
He shook his head and planted his feet squarely. “I intend to make no bargain with either of you. This slave is not for sale.”
“Shalt thou dare resist Asad? I tell thee he will take her whether she be for sale or not.”
“I see,” he said, his eyes narrowing. “And the fear of this, then, is the source of thy whim to acquire her for thyself. Thou art not subtle, O Fenzileh.

===Chinese===

这是我的一桩任性，我的任性受不得挫。为遂这一桩，我出三千枚金币。”
他望着她，揣摩她心里翻的是什么鬼、她想遂的是什么歹念。
“你出三千枚金币？”他慢慢地说，随后直截了当问她：“为什么？”
“遂一桩任性，讨一份欢喜。”
“这份昂贵的任性，是什么成色？”他刨根问底。
“想把她占为己有的那份念想。”她避实就虚地答。
“这份占有的念想，又是打哪儿生出来的？”他回敬，耐心同狠劲一样足。
“你问得太多了。”她勃然发作。
他耸耸肩，一笑。“你答得太少了。”
她双手叉腰，正面迎住他。隔着面纱，他隐约捕到她眼里闪动的光；他暗骂她占了便宜——那张脸罩着，不归他读。
“一句话，奥利弗-雷斯，”她说，“三千枚金币，你卖不卖她？”
“一句话——不卖。”他答。
“不卖？三千枚也不卖？”她的声音里满是惊异，他揣摩那是真的还是装的。
“三万也不卖。”他答，“她是我的，我不放手。我的意思既已亮明，你留在这里又于你我两人都凶险，我请你走。”
小小一阵沉默。两人谁都没有留意罗莎蒙德那张白脸上印着的机警——谁也没疑到她懂法文，凭这个，他们讲的混通语，她能听懂大半。
芬齐莱朝他逼进一步。“你不放手，嗯？”她问，他断定她在冷笑。“别这样自信。你会被逼着放的，我的朋友——不给我，就给阿萨德。他要亲自来取她了。”
“阿萨德？”他喊，这回真吃了一惊。
“阿萨德-丁。”她答，随即又劝起来，“来吧！同我做笔好买卖，总强过同巴沙做笔坏的。”
他摇头，双脚稳稳站定。“你们两位的买卖，我都不做。这个奴隶不卖。”
“你敢抗阿萨德？我告诉你，她卖不卖，他都要拿。”
“我明白了，”他说，眼睛眯细，“原来你这份据为己有的任性，源头是怕这个。你可不细，芬齐莱。”

===Original===

The consciousness that thine own charms are fading sets thee trembling lest so much loveliness should entirely cast thee from thy lord’s regard, eh?”
If he could not see her face, and study there the effect of that thrust of his, at least he observed the quiver that ran through her muffled figure, he caught the note of anger that throbbed in her reply﻿—“And if that were so, what is’t to thee?”
“It may be much or little,” he replied thoughtfully.
“Indeed, it should be much,” she answered quickly, breathlessly. “Have I not ever been thy friend? Have I not ever urged thy valour on my lord’s notice and wrought like a true friend for thine advancement, Sakr-el-Bahr?”
He laughed outright. “Hast thou so?” quoth he.
“Laugh as thou wilt, but it is true,” she insisted. “Lose me and thy most valuable ally is lost﻿—one who has the ear and favour of her lord. For look, Sakr-el-Bahr, it is what would befall if another came to fill my place, another who might poison Asad’s mind with lies against thee﻿—for surely she cannot love thee, this Frankish girl whom thou hast torn from her home!”
“Be not concerned for that,” he answered lightly, his wits striving in vain to plumb the depths and discover the nature of her purpose. “This slave of mine shall never usurp thy place beside Asad.”
“O fool, Asad will take her whether she be for sale or not.”
He looked down upon her, head on one side and arms akimbo. “If he can take her from me, the more easily can he take her from thee. No doubt thou hast considered that, and in some dark Sicilian way considered too how to provide against it. But the cost﻿—hast thou counted that? What will Asad say to thee when he learns how thou hast thwarted him?”
“What do I care for that?” she cried in sudden fury, her gestures becoming a little wild. “She will be at the bottom of the harbour by then with a stone about her neck. He may have me whipped. No doubt he will. But ’twill end there. He will require me to console him for his loss, and so all will be well again.”
At last he had drawn her, pumped her dry, as he imagined. Indeed, indeed, he thought, he had been right to say she was not subtle. He had been a fool to have permitted himself to be intrigued by so shallow, so obvious a purpose. He shrugged and turned away from her.
“Depart in peace, O Fenzileh,” he said. “I yield her to none﻿—be his name Asad or Shaitan.”

===Chinese===

是自知颜色将褪，才叫你发抖，怕这样一份姿色把你从你主上的宠里整个挤出去，嗯？”
面纱罩着，他读不到她的脸、研究不了他这一记的效力，但他看见那具裹得严严的身影掠过一阵哆嗦，他听见她答话里跳动的怒音——“就算是，与你何干？”
“也许干系很大，也许很小。”他若有所思地答。
“干系应该很大，”她答得又快又急，“我几时不是你的朋友？我几时不在主上面前替你的骁勇张目、不像个真朋友那样为你的高升出力，萨克埃尔-巴尔？”
他放声大笑。“你张目来着？”他说。
“你只管笑，可这是实情，”她坚称，“失了我，你就失了你最要紧的盟友——一个在她主上跟前说得上话、得着宠的盟友。你想啊，萨克埃尔-巴尔，倘若来了另一个顶了我的位，另一个拿谎话在阿萨德心里下你的蛆——她总归爱不了你，这个被你从家里掳来的法兰克丫头！”
“这个不劳挂心，”他轻巧地答，脑子却在徒劳地往深处探、想摸清她此来的真章，“我这个奴隶，永远僭不了你在阿萨德身边的位。”
“哦，你这傻瓜，她卖不卖，阿萨德都要拿。”
他低头看她，偏着头，两手叉腰。“他若能从我手里拿走她，从你手里拿走就更容易。这一层你必定掂过；还用哪一路西西里的黑心术，掂出了个防它的法子。可那代价——你算过没有？等阿萨德知道你怎样坏他的事，他会怎么对付你？”
“那关我什么事！”她勃然大怒地喊，手势都有些狂了，“到那时，她早脖子上拴着石头沉进港湾底了。他尽可以叫人鞭我。他多半会的。可也就到那儿为止。他还得要我替他排解丢人的气闷呢，到头来一切照旧。”
他到底把她钓出来了，把她掏干了——他是这样想的。的确，的确，他想，他说她不细，原没有说错。先前竟叫这么一个浅薄、这么一个一眼见底的目的吊起了兴致，他才是个傻子。他耸耸肩，从她跟前转过身去。
“平安地去吧，芬齐莱，”他说，“我不把她让给任何人——不管他叫阿萨德，还是叫撒旦。”

===Original===

His tone was final, and her answer seemed to accept at last his determination. Yet she was very quick with that answer; so quick that he might have suspected it to be preconceived.
“Then it is surely thine intent to wed her.” No voice could have been more innocent and guileless than was hers now. “If so,” she went on, “it were best done quickly, for marriage is the only barrier Asad will not overthrow. He is devout, and out of his deep reverence for the Prophet’s law he would be sure to respect such a bond as that. But be very sure that he will respect nothing short of it.”
Yet notwithstanding her innocence and assumed simplicity﻿—because of it, perhaps﻿—he read her as if she had been an open book; it no longer mattered that her face was veiled.
“And thy purpose would be equally well served, eh?” he questioned her, sly in his turn.
“Equally,” she admitted.
“Say ‘better,’ Fenzileh,” he rejoined. “I said thou art not subtle. By the Koran, I lied. Thou art subtle as the serpent. Yet I see whither thou art gliding. Were I to be guided by thine advice a twofold purpose would be served. First, I should place her beyond Asad’s reach, and second, I should be embroiled with him for having done so. What could more completely satisfy thy wishes?”
“Thou dost me wrong,” she protested. “I have ever been thy friend. I would that.﻿ ﻿…” She broke off suddenly to listen. The stillness of the night was broken by cries from the direction of the Bab El Oued. She ran swiftly to the parapet whence the gate was to be seen and leaned far out.
“Look, look!” she cried, and there was a tremor of fear in her voice. “It is he﻿—Asad-ed-Din.”
Sakr-el-Bahr crossed to her side and in a glare of torches saw a body of men coming forth from the black archway of the gate.
“It almost seems as if, departing from thy usual custom, thou hast spoken truth, O Fenzileh.”
She faced him, and he suspected the venomous glance darted at him through her veil. Yet her voice when she spoke was cold. “In a moment thou’lt have no single doubt of it. But what of me?” The question was added in a quickening tone. “He must not find me here. He would kill me, I think.”
“I am sure he would,” Sakr-el-Bahr agreed. “Yet muffled thus, who should recognize thee? Away, then, ere he comes. Take cover in the courtyard until he shall have passed. Didst thou come alone?”

===Chinese===

这话说到了头，她的回答看来也终于认了他的死心眼。可那回答来得真快；快得他本该疑心是预制好的。
“那么，你必是打定主意要娶她了。”再没有比她此刻的嗓音更无辜、更不设防的了。“若是这样，”她续道，“那就越快越好，因为婚姻是阿萨德唯一推不倒的屏障。他虔诚，出于对先知法度的深敬，他必尊重那样一重结合。可你也只管放心：比这轻一分的，他一样都不尊重。”
然而，尽管她那份无辜、那份装出来的单纯——也许正因为它——他把她读得像一本摊开的书；她蒙不蒙面纱，已不相干了。
“而你的目的也同样办妥了，嗯？”他反问过去，这回轮到他狡黠。
“同样。”她认了。
“说‘更妥’，芬齐莱，”他回敬，“我说过你不细。凭《古兰经》说，我说了谎。你细得赛蛇。可我也看得清你滑向哪边。我若听了你的指点，便是一箭双雕：头一雕，我把她挪出阿萨德的手；第二雕，我为这一挪同他结了仇。还有什么比这更称你的心？”
“你冤枉我，”她抗议，“我从来是你的朋友。我但愿……”她蓦地收声侧耳。夜的静默里，巴卜厄勒维德那头起了喊声。她疾步抢到栏边——从那里望得见那座门——探身远远张望。
“瞧，瞧！”她喊，嗓音里颤着惧意，“是他——阿萨德-丁。”
萨克埃尔-巴尔跨到她身边，在一片火把的耀光里，看见一队人正从那道黑色的拱门洞里出来。
“看来这回你一反往常，倒是说了实话，芬齐莱。”
她转过脸，他疑心面纱后头有一道毒眼射向他。可她开口时，声音冷冷的。“不出一刻，你就一丝疑心也没有了。可我怎么办？”这问题添得急促起来，“他不能在这里撞见我。他会杀了我的，我看。”
“我看也是。”萨克埃尔-巴尔附和道。“可你这身裹法，谁认得出你？走吧，趁他没到。先到院子里避一避，等他过去。你是一个人来的？”

===Original===

“Should I trust anyone with the knowledge that I had visited thee?” she asked, and he admired the strong Sicilian spirit in her that not all these years in the Basha’s harem had sufficed to extinguish.
She moved quickly to the door, to pause again on the threshold.
“Thou’lt not relinquish her? Thou’lt not.”
“Be at ease,” he answered her, on so resolved a note that she departed satisfied.

===Chinese===

“我到过你这里的秘密，难道还要托付给什么人？”她反问。他倒佩服起她那股西西里的烈性——在巴沙的内院泡了这些年，竟没能泡灭。
她快步走向门口，到门槛上又停住。
“你不放手？你不放手。”
“放心。”他答她，话音决绝，她满意地去了。

## XXII The Surrender / 投降
===Original===

Up the gangway between the lines of slumbering slaves came a quick patter of feet. Ali, who since sunset had been replacing Larocque on the heights, sprang suddenly upon the poop still shouting.
“Captain! Captain! My lord! Afoot! Up! or we are taken!”
Throughout the vessel’s length came the rustle and stir of waking men. A voice clamoured somewhere on the forecastle. Then the flap of the awning was suddenly whisked aside and Asad himself appeared with Marzak at his elbow.
From the starboard side as suddenly came Biskaine and Othmani, and from the waist Vigitello, Jasper﻿—that latest renegade﻿—and a group of alarmed corsairs.
“What now?” quoth the Basha.
Ali delivered his message breathlessly. “The galleon has weighed anchor. She is moving out of the bay.”
Asad clutched his beard, and scowled. “Now what may that portend? Can knowledge of our presence have reached them?”
“Why else should she move from her anchorage thus in the dead of night?” said Biskaine.
“Why else, indeed?” returned Asad, and then he swung upon Oliver standing there in the entrance of the poop house. “What sayest thou, Sakr-el-Bahr?” he appealed to him.
Sakr-el-Bahr stepped forward, shrugging. “What is there to say? What is there to do?” he asked. “We can but wait. If our presence is known to them we are finely trapped, and there’s an end to all of us this night.”
His voice was cool as ice, contemptuous almost, and whilst it struck anxiety into more than one it awoke terror in Marzak.
“May thy bones rot, thou ill-omened prophet!” he screamed, and would have added more but that Sakr-el-Bahr silenced him.
“What is written is written!” said he in a voice of thunder and reproof.
“Indeed, indeed,” Asad agreed, grasping at the fatalist’s consolation. “If we are ripe for the gardeners hand, the gardener will pluck us.”
Less fatalistic and more practical was the counsel of Biskaine. “It were well to act upon the assumption that we are indeed discovered, and make for the open sea while yet there may be time.”
“But that were to make certain what is still doubtful,” broke in Marzak, fearful ever. “It were to run to meet the danger.”
“Not so!” cried Asad in a loud, confident voice. “The praise to Allah who sent us this calm night. There is scarce a breath of wind. We can row ten leagues while they are sailing one.”
A murmur of quick approval sped through the ranks of officers and men.

===Chinese===

舷梯上，两排沉睡的奴隶之间，响起一阵急促的脚步声。日落以来替下拉罗克守高地的阿里，人未到艉楼，喊声先到。
“船长！船长！大人！起来！快起来！咱们叫人堵了！”
全船上下响起醒转来的窸窣和骚动。前艄什么地方有人喧嚷。随即天棚的帘子唰地掀开，阿萨德亲自现身，马尔扎克跟在肘边。
右舷那面，比斯坎和奥斯曼尼同样来得突然；船腰里来了维吉特洛、贾斯珀——那个最新的叛教者——和一群惊慌的海盗。
“出了什么事？”巴沙问。
阿里气还没喘匀就把话倒了出来。“大帆船起锚了。正开出湾去。”
阿萨德一把捋住胡子，沉下脸。“这算是什么兆头？咱们的行藏，莫非走漏到他们耳朵里了？”
“不然，深更半夜的她挪窝做什么？”比斯坎说。
“不然做什么。”阿萨德应道，随即猛地转向立在艉楼舱门口的奥利弗。“你怎么说，萨克埃尔-巴尔？”他征询他。
萨克埃尔-巴尔上前一步，耸耸肩。“有什么可说的？有什么可做的？”他问，“只有等。咱们的行藏既叫他们知道了，咱们就给关了个严实，今夜大家伙儿一起到头。”
他的嗓音冷得像冰，几乎带着鄙夷；它在不止一个人心里砸下焦虑，在马尔扎克心里砸下的却是恐惧。
“叫你的骨头烂掉，你这丧门的乌鸦嘴！”他尖叫，还想再骂，被萨克埃尔-巴尔喝住了。
“写下的就是写下的！”他雷声一般、带着训责地说。
“正是，正是，”阿萨德附和，一把抓住宿命论者的那份自解，“咱们若熟到了园丁伸手的时候，园丁自会来摘。”
比斯坎的主意就没这样宿命，更实一些。“不如就当咱们确实叫人发现了，趁还来得及，抢出海口去。”
“可那就把没准的事变成定局了，”马尔扎克插嘴，永远胆小，“那是迎着凶险跑过去。”
“不然！”阿萨德朗声喊，满有把握，“赞颂安拉，赐咱们这样一个无风的夜。风一丝也没有。他们驶一里，咱们能划十里。”
军官和行伍里飞快地起了一阵低低的赞同。

===Original===

“Let us but win safely from this cove and they will never overtake us,” announced Biskaine.
“But their guns may,” Sakr-el-Bahr quietly reminded them to damp their confidence. His own alert mind had already foreseen this one chance of escaping from the trap, but he had hoped that it would not be quite so obvious to the others.
“That risk we must take,” replied Asad. “We must trust to the night. To linger here is to await certain destruction.” He swung briskly about to issue his orders. “Ali, summon the steersmen. Hasten! Vigitello, set your whips about the slaves, and rouse them.” Then as the shrill whistle of the boatswain rang out and the whips of his mates went hissing and cracking about the shoulders of the already half-awakened slaves, to mingle with all the rest of the stir and bustle aboard the *galeasse*, the Basha turned once more to Biskaine. “Up thou to the prow,” he commanded, “and marshal the men. Bid them stand to their arms lest it should come to boarding. Go!” Biskaine salaamed and sprang down the companion. Above the rumbling din and scurrying toil of preparation rang Asad’s voice. “Crossbowmen, aloft! Gunners to the carronades! Kindle your linstocks! Put out all lights!”
An instant later the cressets on the poop rail were extinguished, as was the lantern swinging from the rail, and even the lamp in the poop house which was invaded by one of the Basha’s officers for that purpose. The lantern hanging from the mast alone was spared against emergencies; but it was taken down, placed upon the deck, and muffled.
Thus was the *galeasse* plunged into a darkness that for some moments was black and impenetrable as velvet. Then slowly, as the eyes became accustomed to it, this gloom was gradually relieved. Once more men and objects began to take shape in the faint, steely radiance of the summer night.
After the excitement of that first stir the corsairs went about their tasks with amazing calm and silence. None thought now of reproaching the Basha or Sakr-el-Bahr with having delayed until the moment of peril to take the course which all of them had demanded should be taken when first they had heard of the neighbourhood of that hostile ship. In lines three deep they stood ranged along the ample fighting platform of the prow; in the foremost line were the archers, behind them stood the swordsmen, their weapons gleaming lividly in the darkness.

===Chinese===

“只要平平安安出得这道湾，他们再也追不上咱们。”比斯坎宣布。
“可他们的炮追得上。”萨克埃尔-巴尔平静地提醒，给他们那股信心掺水。他自己那副机敏的头脑，早看见了这条脱身之机，只是他原盼着别人别把这条看得这样明白。
“这个险只好冒，”阿萨德答，“只能托付给夜色。赖在这儿，是坐着等死。”他利落地一转身，发号施令起来。“阿里，唤舵手。快！维吉特洛，把鞭子招呼到奴隶身上，都给我醒过来！”于是水手长的唿哨尖叫，伙计们的鞭子在半醒的奴隶肩头噼啪乱响，同桨帆船上其余的忙乱响成一片；巴沙又转向比斯坎。“你上船头去，”他吩咐，“把人排开。叫他们拿好兵刃，防着跳帮。去！”比斯坎行礼，跳下了升降口。备战的轰隆与奔忙之上，响着阿萨德的嗓音。“弩手，上高！炮手就位！点着火绳！灭了所有的灯！”
顷刻之间，艉楼栏上的火盆熄了，栏上荡着的那盏灯灭了，连艉楼舱里的灯也没能幸免——巴沙的一名军官专为它闯了进来。唯有桅杆上挂的那盏灯留着备急；可也摘了下来，搁在甲板上，蒙了个严实。
就这样，桨帆船坠进了一片黑暗，好一阵黑得化不开，黑得像丝绒。随后，眼睛慢慢惯了，这黑也慢慢松动了。人和物又开始在夏夜那淡淡的、钢色的微光里现出轮廓。
起初那阵骚动过后，海盗们办起差来，出奇地安静利落。如今再没有人想起要怪巴沙或萨克埃尔-巴尔拖延到险关临头，才走大家头一回听说那条敌船时就要求走的那步棋。他们三列纵深，站满船头那片宽大的接战平台：最前一列是弓手，后面立着刀手，兵刃在黑暗里泛着幽幽的光。

===Original===

They crowded to the bulwarks of the waist deck and swarmed upon the ratlines of the mainmast. On the poop three gunners stood to each of the two small cannon, their faces showing faintly ruddy in the glow of the ignited match.
Asad stood at the head of the companion, issuing his sharp brief commands, and Sakr-el-Bahr, behind him, leaning against the timbers of the poop house with Rosamund at his side, observed that the Basha had studiously avoided entrusting any of this work of preparation to himself.
The steersmen climbed to their niches, and the huge steering oars creaked as they were swung out. Came a short word of command from Asad and a stir ran through the ranks of the slaves, as they threw forward their weight to bring the oars to the level. Thus a moment, then a second word, the premonitory crack of a whip in the darkness of the gangway, and the tom-tom began to beat the time. The slaves heaved, and with a creak and splash of oars the great *galeasse* skimmed forward towards the mouth of the cove.
Up and down the gangway ran the boatswain’s mates, cutting fiercely with their whips to urge the slaves to the very utmost effort. The vessel gathered speed. The looming headland slipped by. The mouth of the cove appeared to widen as they approached it. Beyond spread the dark steely mirror of the dead-calm sea.
Rosamund could scarcely breathe in the intensity of her suspense. She set a hand upon the arm of Sakr-el-Bahr.
“Shall we elude them, after all?” she asked in a trembling whisper.
“I pray that we may not,” he answered, muttering. “But this is the handiwork I feared. Look!” he added sharply, and pointed.
They had shot clear to the headland. They were out of the cove, and suddenly they had a view of the dark bulk of the galleon, studded with a score of points of light, riding a cable’s length away on their larboard quarter.
“Faster!” cried the voice of Asad. “Row for your lives, you infidel swine! Lay me your whips upon these hides of theirs! Bend me these dogs to their oars, and they’ll never overtake us now.”
Whips sang and thudded below them in the waist, to be answered by more than one groan from the tormented panting slaves, who already were spending every ounce of strength in this cruel effort to elude their own chance of salvation and release.

===Chinese===

他们挤满船腰甲板的栏边，爬满主桅的桅索。艉楼上，两门小炮各守着三名炮手，脸在点燃的火绳映照下微微发红。
阿萨德立在升降口顶上，发着他短促的口令；萨克埃尔-巴尔在他身后，倚着艉楼舱的船板，罗莎蒙德在旁——他注意到，这些备战的差事，巴沙着意一件也没交给他。
舵手们爬进舵龛，巨大的舵桨荡出去时吱呀作响。阿萨德短短一声令下，奴隶的行列里起了一阵耸动，人人抛身向前压桨平。这样一瞬，又是第二个口令，舷梯的黑暗里鞭子先声夺人地脆响一记，鼓点便起了拍。奴隶们齐发一声喊，桨声吱嘎、水花四溅，那条大桨帆船朝湾口轻掠而去。
水手长的伙计们在舷梯上来回地跑，鞭子抽得凶，逼奴隶拿出极限的力气。船添了速。压过来的海岬滑了过去。湾口随着船的逼近仿佛张开了。湾外，铺开死平的海面，一面暗钢色的镜子。
罗莎蒙德悬心得几乎透不过气。她把手搭上萨克埃尔-巴尔的胳膊。
“我们到底还是逃得脱么？”她用发抖的气音问。
“我求上帝别叫逃脱。”他喃喃地答。“可这正是我怕的那一手。瞧！”他忽然补了一句，一指。
船已射过海岬。出了湾，大帆船那黑乎乎的一大团霎时撞进眼帘，周身缀着二十来个光点，泊在左舷后侧一链远的地方。
“快些！”阿萨德的嗓音响起来。“卖命划呀，你们这群异教猪猡！把鞭子给我下到他们皮上！把这群狗给我钉死在桨上，他们就再也追不上咱们了！”
脚下的船腰里，鞭声呼啸、闷响，应和它的，是那些被折磨得喘成一团的奴隶一声又一声的呻吟——为了脱逃这艘船，他们正榨出最后一分力气；而这艘船，本是他们自己脱身得救的唯一指望。

===Original===

Faster beat the tom-tom marking the desperate time, and faster in response to it came the creak and dip of oars and the panting, stertorous breathing of the rowers.
“Lay on! Lay on!” cried Asad, inexorable. Let them burst their lungs﻿—they were but infidel lungs!﻿—so that for an hour they but maintained the present pace.
“We are drawing away!” cried Marzak in jubilation. “The praise to Allah!”
And so indeed they were. Visibly the lights of the galleon were receding. With every inch of canvas spread yet she appeared to be standing still, so faint was the breeze that stirred. And whilst she crawled, the *galeasse* raced as never yet she had raced since Sakr-el-Bahr had commanded her, for Sakr-el-Bahr had never yet turned tail upon the foe in whatever strength he found him.
Suddenly over the water from the galleon came a loud hail. Asad laughed, and in the darkness shook his fist at them, cursing them in the name of Allah and his Prophet. And then, in answer to that curse of his, the galleon’s side belched fire; the calm of the night was broken by a roar of thunder, and something smote the water ahead of the Muslim vessel with a resounding thudding splash.
In fear Rosamund drew closer to Sakr-el-Bahr. But Asad laughed again.
“No need to fear their marksmanship,” he cried. “They cannot see us. Their own lights dazzle them. On! On!”
“He is right,” said Sakr-el-Bahr. “But the truth is that they will not fire to sink us because they know you to be aboard.”
She looked out to sea again, and beheld those friendly lights falling farther and farther astern.
“We are drawing steadily away,” she groaned. “They will never overtake us now.”
So feared Sakr-el-Bahr. He more than feared it. He knew that save for some miraculous rising of the wind it must be as she said. And then out of his despair leapt inspiration﻿—a desperate inspiration, true child of that despair of which it was begotten.
“There is a chance,” he said to her. “But it is as a throw of the dice with life and death for stakes.”
“Then seize it,” she bade him instantly. “For though it should go against us we shall not be losers.”
“You are prepared for anything?” he asked her.
“Have I not said that I will go down with you this night? Ah, don’t waste time in words!”
“Be it so, then,” he replied gravely, and moved away a step, then checked. “You had best come with me,” he said.

===Chinese===

鼓点越敲越急，敲着这不要命的拍子；应着它的桨声起落也越催越急，桨手们的喘息粗重如拉风箱。
“加鞭！加鞭！”阿萨德喊，毫不容情。叫他们把肺挣破——反正是异教徒的肺！——只要他们撑住这个势头一个钟头。
“咱们拉开啦！”马尔扎克欢呼，“赞颂安拉！”
果然拉开了。大帆船的灯火眼见着退远。她明明张着满帆，看着却像钉在原地——拨动她的那阵风太软了。她爬着，桨帆船却飞着——自萨克埃尔-巴尔接手以来，这条船从没有这样飞过，因为不论撞上多强的敌人，萨克埃尔-巴尔从没有掉过一次尾。
忽然，水面上从大帆船那边传来一声高喝。阿萨德大笑，在黑暗里朝他们挥拳，凭安拉和先知的名咒他们。而回应他这句咒的，是大帆船的侧舷喷出的火：夜的静默被一声明雷砸破，什么东西带着轰然的一声闷响，砸在穆斯林船头前面的水里。
罗莎蒙德惧得朝萨克埃尔-巴尔偎紧了些。可阿萨德又笑了。
“怕什么他们的准头，”他喊，“他们瞧不见咱们。他们自己的灯晃花了他们自己的眼。划！划！”
“他说得对，”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，“不过实情是：他们不肯开炮打沉咱们，因为他们知道你在船上。”
她又朝海上看去，看见那些友好的灯火一节一节落在船尾后头。
“咱们稳稳脱开了，”她呻吟道，“他们再也追不上了。”
萨克埃尔-巴尔怕的正是这个。他不止是怕。他知道，除非风起个奇迹，结局必如她所说。于是，从他的绝望里，跳出了灵感——一个亡命的灵感，它所由生的那场绝望的亲生儿子。
“有一条路，”他对她说，“可它是一场掷骰子，押的是你我的命。”
“那就抓牢它，”她立刻吩咐他，“就算输了，咱们也不吃亏。”
“你什么样都受得了？”他问她。
“我不是说了么，今夜我同你一起沉。啊，别把工夫浪费在嘴上！”
“那就这样定了。”他郑重地答，挪步要走，又顿住。“你最好跟我来。”他说。

===Original===

Obediently she complied and followed him, and some there were who stared as these two passed down the gangway, yet none attempted to hinder her movements. Enough and to spare was there already to engage the thoughts of all aboard that vessel.
He thrust a way for her, past the boatswain’s mates who stood over the slaves ferociously plying tongues and whips, and so brought her to the waist. Here he took up the lantern which had been muffled, and as its light once more streamed forth, Asad shouted an order for its extinction. But Sakr-el-Bahr took no least heed of that command. He stepped to the mainmast, about which the powder kegs had been stacked. One of these had been broached against its being needed by the gunners on the poop. The unfastened lid rested loosely atop of it. That lid Sakr-el-Bahr knocked over; then he pulled one of the horn sides out of the lantern, and held the now half-naked flame immediately above the powder.
A cry of alarm went up from some who had watched him. But above that cry rang his sharp command:
“Cease rowing!”
The tom-tom fell instantly silent, but the slaves took yet another stroke.
“Cease rowing!” he commanded again. “Asad!” he called. “Bid them pause, or I’ll blow you all straight into the arms of Shaitan.” And he lowered the lantern until it rested on the very rim of the powder keg.
At once the rowing ceased. Slaves, corsairs, officers, and Asad himself stood paralyzed, all at gaze upon that grim figure illumined by the lantern, threatening them with doom. It may have crossed the minds of some to throw themselves forthwith upon him; but to arrest them was the dread lest any movement towards him should precipitate the explosion that must blow them all into the next world.
At last Asad addressed him, his voice half-choked with rage.
“May Allah strike thee dead! Art thou djinn-possessed?”
Marzak, standing at his father’s side, set a quarrel to the bow which he had snatched up. “Why do you all stand and stare?” he cried. “Cut him down, one of you!” And even as he spoke he raised his bow. But his father checked him, perceiving what must be the inevitable result.
“If any man takes a step towards me, the lantern goes straight into the gunpowder,” said Sakr-el-Bahr serenely. “And if you shoot me as you intend, Marzak, or if any other shoots, the same will happen of itself.

===Chinese===

她依言跟上。这两个人顺着舷梯走下去，有几个人瞪着眼，可谁也没有拦她的动。那条船上，够每个人操心的事早已只多不少。
他替她开路，越过那些凶神恶煞地又动嘴又动鞭、看管奴隶的水手长伙计们，把她带到船腰。在那里，他提起那只蒙着的灯，灯光重新流泻出来，阿萨德喝令灭灯。可萨克埃尔-巴尔对那道命令理也不理。他走到主桅前——火药桶一桶桶码在桅子周围，其中一桶已经为艉楼的炮手备战开了口，掀松的盖子虚虚搭在顶上。萨克埃尔-巴尔一掌把那盖子打翻在地；又从灯上拆下一面角片，把如今半裸的火焰直凑到火药上方。
看见的人里发出一声惊叫。可压过那声惊叫的，是他尖利的口令：
“停桨！”
鼓声立时哑了，奴隶们却又划了一桨。
“停桨！”他又喝了一遍。“阿萨德！叫他们停，不然我把你们一股脑儿送进撒旦的怀里。”说着，他把灯又往下放，放得搁上了火药桶的边沿。
划桨应声而止。奴隶、海盗、军官，连阿萨德本人，全都僵住了，全都瞪着灯笼照亮的那道鬼影一般的身影——拿末日威吓着他们的那一个。也许有人心里闪过一念：扑上去；可拦住这一念的是那份怕：任何朝他去的动作，都可能引爆那场把他们全体送进另一个世界的轰然。
末了，阿萨德开口了，嗓音给怒气噎得半哑。
“叫安拉劈死你！你叫魔附了体么？”
马尔扎克立在父亲身边，抓起一张弩搭上了箭。“你们都站着瞪什么？”他喊，“哪个人上去砍了他！”话音里他就举起了弩。可他父亲按住了他——他看出了那必然的下场。
“谁朝我迈一步，灯就直落火药，”萨克埃尔-巴尔安安然然地说，“你若是照你的意思射我，马尔扎克，或是有别人开弓，那同一个下场，自己也会来。

===Original===

Be warned unless you thirst for the Paradise of the Prophet.”
“Sakr-el-Bahr!” cried Asad, and from its erstwhile anger his voice had now changed to a note of intercession. He stretched out his arms appealingly to the captain whose doom he had already pronounced in his heart and mind. “Sakr-el-Bahr, I conjure thee by the bread and salt we have eaten together, return to thy senses, my son.”
“I am in my sense,” was the answer, “and being so I have no mind for the fate reserved me in Algiers﻿—by the memory of that same bread and salt. I have no mind to go back with thee to be hanged or sent to toil at an oar again.”
“And if I swear to thee that naught of this shall come to pass?”
“Thou’lt be forsworn. I would not trust thee now, Asad. For thou art proven a fool, and in all my life I never found good in a fool and never trusted one﻿—save once, and he betrayed me. Yesterday I pleaded with thee, showing thee the wise course, and affording thee thine opportunity. At a slight sacrifice thou mightest have had me and hanged me at thy leisure. ’Twas my own life I offered thee, and for all that thou knewest it, yet thou knewest not that I knew.” He laughed. “See now what manner of fool art thou? Thy greed hath wrought thy ruin. Thy hands were opened to grasp more than they could hold. See now the consequence. It comes yonder in that slowly but surely approaching galleon.”
Every word of it sank into the brain of Asad thus tardily to enlighten him. He wrung his hands in his blended fury and despair. The crew stood in appalled silence, daring to make no movement that might precipitate their end.
“Name thine own price,” cried the Basha at length, “and I swear to thee by the beard of the Prophet it shall be paid thee.”
“I named it yesterday, but it was refused. I offered thee my liberty and my life if that were needed to gain the liberty of another.”
Had he looked behind him he might have seen the sudden lighting of Rosamund’s eyes, the sudden clutch at her bosom, which would have announced to him that his utterances were none so cryptic but that she had understood them.
“I will make thee rich and honoured, Sakr-el-Bahr,” Asad continued urgently. “Thou shalt be as mine own son. The Bashalik itself shall be thine when I lay it down, and all men shall do thee honour in the meanwhile as to myself.”
“I am not to be bought, O mighty Asad. I never was.

===Chinese===

不想去先知的乐园的，就都掂量掂量。”
“萨克埃尔-巴尔！”阿萨德喊道，先前那份怒气，如今换成了哀恳的调子。他朝那位他心里早已判了刑的船长，恳求地张开双臂。“萨克埃尔-巴尔，我凭你我同吃过的面包和盐起誓，求你回一回神，我的儿子。”
“我神志清楚得很，”回答道，“正因为清楚，我不想领阿尔及尔给我备下的那份下场——也凭那同一样的面包和盐。我不想跟你回去挨绞，或者再回桨凳上吃苦。”
“我若向你起誓，这些一概不来呢？”
“你必背誓。如今我信不得你了，阿萨德。因为你已被证明是个傻子；我这辈子，从没在傻子身上见过好处，也从没信过傻子——只信过一回，他出卖了我。昨天我求过你，把明智的路摆给你看，也把机会递到了你手边。你稍稍破费一点，就能拿下我，从容地吊死我。我拿的是我自己这条命送你，你虽知道，却不知道我知道。”他笑了。“如今瞧瞧你是个什么成色的傻子？你的贪，给你自己掘了坑。你那双手张得太开，想抓超出它拿得下的。如今瞧瞧后果。后果就在那边——那条稳稳地、一寸一寸逼过来的大帆船。”
字字句句，都沉进阿萨德的脑子，姗姗来迟地点亮了他。他在交并的狂怒和绝望里绞着双手。船员们惊恐无言地站着，不敢有半点可能催他们上路的动作。
“你开你自己的价，”巴沙终于喊道，“我凭先知的胡须向你起誓，必照付。”
“我昨天开过了，被你回了。我拿我的自由——若需要，再搭上我的命——换另一个人的自由。”
他若回头看一眼，就会看见罗莎蒙德眼里骤然亮起的光、她猛地按住胸口的那只手：这些都告诉他，他这些话并不怎样晦涩，她听懂了。
“我必叫你富贵、叫你尊荣，萨克埃尔-巴尔，”阿萨德急切地续道，“你就算我的亲儿子。等我放下这副担子，巴沙之位就是你的；在那之前，人人敬你如敬我自己。”
“我买不动，威严的阿萨德。从来就买不动。

===Original===

Already wert thou set upon my death. Thou canst command it now, but only upon the condition that thou share the cup with me. What is written is written. We have sunk some tall ships together in our day, Asad. We’ll sink together in our turn tonight if that be thy desire.”
“May thou burn forevermore in hell, thou black-hearted traitor!” Asad cursed him, his anger bursting all the bonds he had imposed upon it.
And then, of a sudden, upon that admission of defeat from their Basha, there arose a great clamour from the crew. Sakr-el-Bahr’s sea hawks called upon him, reminding him of their fidelity and love, and asking could he repay it now by dooming them all thus to destruction.
“Have faith in me!” he answered them. “I have never led you into aught but victory. Be sure that I shall not lead you now into defeat﻿—on this the last occasion that we stand together.”
“But the galleon is upon us!” cried Vigitello.
And so, indeed, it was, creeping up slowly under that faint breeze, her tall bulk loomed now above them, her prow ploughing slowly forward at an acute angle to the prow of the *galeasse*. Another moment and she was alongside, and with a swing and clank and a yell of victory from the English seamen lining her bulwarks her grappling irons swung down to seize the corsair ship at prow and stern and waist. Scarce had they fastened, than a torrent of men in breastplates and morions poured over her side, to alight upon the prow of the *galeasse*, and not even the fear of the lantern held above the powder barrel could now restrain the corsairs from giving these hardy boarders the reception they reserved for all infidels. In an instant the fighting platform on the prow was become a raging, seething hell of battle luridly illumined by the ruddy glow from the lights aboard the *Silver Heron*. Foremost among those who had leapt down had been Lionel and Sir John Killigrew. Foremost among those to receive them had been Jasper Leigh, who had passed his sword through Lionel’s body even as Lionel’s feet came to rest upon the deck, and before the battle was joined.
A dozen others went down on either side before Sakr-el-Bahr’s ringing voice could quell the fighting, before his command to them to hear him was obeyed.
“Hold there!” he had bellowed to his sea hawks, using the lingua franca. “Back, and leave this to me. I will rid you of these foes.”

===Chinese===

你早就打定主意要我死。如今你仍旧拿得走它，只有一个条件：同我共这一杯。写下的就是写下的。当年，咱们一块儿沉过多少高桅大船，阿萨德；今夜你要愿意，咱们自己也一块儿沉一回。”
“叫你在地狱里烧到永永远远，你这黑心肠的叛贼！”阿萨德咒他，怒气挣脱了他强加给它的全部缰绳。
紧接着，趁着巴沙这一认输，船员中炸起一片喧哗。萨克埃尔-巴尔的海鹰们朝他呼喊，提醒他自己对他们的忠和爱，问他如今竟这样把全体送上绝路，可是报答。
“信我！”他答他们，“我领你们走过的地方，从来只有胜。放心，这一回——咱们并肩的最后一回——我也不会领你们走进败局。”
“可大帆船压上来了！”维吉特洛喊。
果真压上来了：凭着那阵软风一寸寸爬上来，她高大的船身如今黑压压地罩在他们顶上，船头以一个尖角，缓缓斜插向桨帆船的船头。转瞬她已傍拢，随着一声铁响、一阵哐啷、舷栏边英格兰水手一声得胜的呐喊，她的抓钩甩了下来，船头、船尾、船腰，一把把咬住了海盗船。钩子刚扣紧，一队胸甲铜盔的汉子便翻过她的船舷，泼水似的落上桨帆船的船头。此刻，就连“灯悬在火药桶上”的那份怕，也拦不住海盗们给这伙硬跳客送上他们招待一切异教徒的那份接迎了。一瞬之间，船头的接战平台成了一锅燃烧翻滚的战斗地狱，“银鹭号”上的灯光给它泼着通红的亮。跳下来的人里冲在最前的，是莱昂内尔和约翰·基利格鲁爵士；迎他们的头一个，是贾斯珀·利——莱昂内尔的脚刚落上甲板、仗还没接上，他的剑已经穿透了莱昂内尔的身子。
两边又各倒下十来个，萨克埃尔-巴尔的洪亮嗓门才压住这场恶斗，他叫众人听命的号令才得听从。
“住手！”他朝他的海鹰们吼，用的是混通语。“退后，这个交给我。我把这些对头给你们打发走。”

===Original===

Then in English he had summoned his countrymen also to desist. “Sir John Killigrew!” he called in a loud voice. “Hold your hand until you have heard me! Call your men back and let none others come aboard! Hold until you have heard me, I say, then wreak your will.”
Sir John, perceiving him by the mainmast with Rosamund at his side, and leaping at the most inevitable conclusion that he meant to threaten her life, perhaps to destroy her if they continued their advance, flung himself before his men, to check them.
Thus almost as suddenly as it had been joined the combat paused.
“What have you to say, you renegade dog?” Sir John demanded.
“This, Sir John, that unless you order your men back aboard your ship, and make oath to desist from this encounter, I’ll take you straight down to hell with us at once. I’ll heave this lantern into the powder here, and we sink and you come down with us held by your own grappling hooks. Obey me and you shall have all that you have come to seek aboard this vessel. Mistress Rosamund shall be delivered up to you.”
Sir John glowered upon him a moment from the poop, considering. Then﻿—
“Though not prepared to make terms with you,” he announced, “yet I will accept the conditions you impose, but only provided that I have all indeed that I am come to seek. There is aboard this galley an infamous renegade hound whom I am bound by my knightly oath to take and hang. He, too, must be delivered up to me. His name was Oliver Tressilian.”
Instantly, unhesitatingly, came the answer﻿—
“Him, too, will I surrender to you upon your sworn oath that you will then depart and do here no further hurt.”
Rosamund caught her breath, and clutched Sakr-el-Bahr’s arm, the arm that held the lantern.
“Have a care, mistress,” he bade her sharply, “or you will destroy us all.”
“Better that!” she answered him.
And then Sir John pledged him his word that upon his own surrender and that of Rosamund he would withdraw nor offer hurt to any there.
Sakr-el-Bahr turned to his waiting corsairs, and briefly told them what the terms he had made.
He called upon Asad to pledge his word that these terms would be respected, and no blood shed on his behalf, and Asad answered him, voicing the anger of all against him for his betrayal.
“Since he wants thee that he may hang thee, he may have thee and so spare us the trouble, for ’tis no less than thy treachery deserves from us.”

===Chinese===

随后他又用英语，唤他的同胞们也住手。“约翰·基利格鲁爵士！”他朗声喊，“先听我一言再动手！把你的人叫回去，别再放人上船！先听我一言，我说，听完了随你们怎样。”
约翰爵士看见他在主桅边、罗莎蒙德在侧，一跳就跳到了那个最顺当的结论上：他要拿她的性命相挟，他们若再进逼，兴许就要她的命。他抢身横在自己人面前，把他们拦住。
就这样，战斗几乎同它开始时一样突然地停了。
“你有什么话说，你这叛教的狗？”约翰爵士质问。
“这个，约翰爵士：除非你下令你的人退回你船上，并发誓就此罢手，不然我立刻带你们一起直下地狱。我把这盏灯丢进这里的火药，咱们沉，你们叫自己的抓钩拖着，一起下来。听我的，这船上你们要寻的一切，你们都到手。罗莎蒙德小姐交还给你们。”
约翰爵士从艉楼上恶狠狠瞪了他半晌，掂量着。随后——
“同你讲条件，本不干我事，”他宣布，“可你开出的这条件，我收下；但有一个前提：我来此要寻的，必须一样不少。这条船上有个恶名昭彰的叛教恶狗，我的骑士之誓绑着我，要拿住他、吊死他。这人，你也必须交出来。他的名字，从前叫奥利弗·特雷西利安。”
回答来得又快又决——
“他也交给你，只要你起誓：交人之后即刻撤走，不再伤这里任何人。”
罗莎蒙德倒抽一口气，一把攥住萨克埃尔-巴尔提灯的那条胳膊。
“小心，小姐，”他厉声道，“不然你把咱们全毁了。”
“毁了倒好！”她答他。
于是约翰爵士对他立了信约：只要他同罗莎蒙德两人交出，他便撤兵，不伤在场任何人。
萨克埃尔-巴尔转向他候命的海盗们，三言两语，把讲下的条件告诉了他们。
他要阿萨德立约担保条件得到遵守、不为他流一滴血；阿萨德这样答他，替全船说出了对他的怒——
“他要你，为的是吊死你；那就给他，也省了咱们动手——你的背叛，从我们这里领这个，不多不少，正正好。”

===Original===

“Thus, then, I surrender,” he announced to Sir John, and flung the lantern overboard.
One voice only was raised in his defence, and that voice was Rosamund’s. But even that voice failed, conquered by weary nature. This last blow following upon all that lately she had endured bereft her of all strength. Half swooning she collapsed against Sakr-el-Bahr even as Sir John and a handful of his followers leapt down to deliver her and make fast their prisoner.
The corsairs stood looking on in silence; the loyalty to their great captain, which would have made them spend their last drop of blood in his defence, was quenched by his own act of treachery which had brought the English ship upon them. Yet when they saw him pinioned and hoisted to the deck of the *Silver Heron*, there was a sudden momentary reaction in their ranks. Scimitars were waved aloft, and cries of menace burst forth. If he had betrayed them, yet he had so contrived that they should not suffer by that betrayal. And that was worthy of the Sakr-el-Bahr they knew and loved; so worthy that their love and loyalty leapt full-armed again upon the instant.
But the voice of Asad called upon them to bear in mind what in their name he had promised, and since the voice of Asad alone might not have sufficed to quell that sudden spark of revolt, there came down to them the voice of Sakr-el-Bahr himself issuing his last command.
“Remember and respect the terms I have made for you! *Mektub!* May Allah guard and prosper you!”
A wail was his reply, and with that wail ringing in his ears to assure him that he did not pass unloved, he was hurried below to prepare him for his end.
The ropes of the grapnels were cut, and slowly the galleon passed away into the night, leaving the galley to replace what slaves had been maimed in the encounter and to head back for Algiers, abandoning the expedition against the argosy of Spain.
Under the awning upon the poop Asad now sat like a man who has awakened from an evil dream. He covered his head and wept for one who had been as a son to him, and whom through his madness he had lost. He cursed all women, and he cursed destiny; but the bitterest curse of all was for himself.

===Chinese===

“那么，我投降了。”他向约翰爵士宣布，随手把灯抛进了海里。
起来为他辩护的，只有一个声音，是罗莎蒙德的。可连那个声音也撑不住，叫疲惫的天性压倒了。这最后一击，叠在她新近受过的那一切上，把她最后的力气也抽干了。约翰爵士带着几名手下跳下来解救她、捆他们的囚徒时，她半晕着，瘫软在萨克埃尔-巴尔身上。
海盗们默然看着。对他们那位大船长的忠心，本是肯为他流尽最后一滴血的，如今却被他亲手那桩背叛——那桩引来英格兰船的背叛——浇熄了。可待到看见他被缚住、被吊上“银鹭号”的甲板，他们的行伍里陡然又起了一阵反潮。弯刀纷纷举向半空，威吓的呐喊迸了出来。他纵然背叛了他们，可他把这一场安排得他们半根毫毛不曾伤。这，才配他们识得、他们敬爱的那个萨克埃尔-巴尔；配得太过了，那份爱与忠心霎时又披挂整齐、活了过来。
可阿萨德的声音唤他们记起他以他们的名义许下的诺言；而单凭阿萨德的声音，未必压得住那一点突起的火星，于是又有另一个声音落下来——萨克埃尔-巴尔自己的声音，发着他最后的军令。
“记住并遵守我为你们讲下的条件！这是命！愿安拉看顾你们、助你们成事！”
回答他的是一声哀号。这一声哀号在耳边回响，叫他知道自己走得不乏人爱；他被人匆匆押下舱去，为他自己的终局作准备。
抓钩的绳索斩断，大帆船缓缓退进夜色，撇下这条桨帆船：补上接战中残废的桨手，掉头回阿尔及尔——对西班牙商船的出征，就此作罢。
艉楼天棚下，阿萨德坐着，像一个从噩梦里醒来的人。他蒙住头，为那个曾如儿子一般待他、又被他亲手疯掉的人落泪。他咒一切女人，咒命运；可最苦的那声咒，留给了他自己。

===Original===

In the pale dawn they flung the dead overboard and washed the decks, nor did they notice that a man was missing in token that the English captain, or else his followers, had not kept strictly to the letter of the bond.
They returned in mourning to Algiers﻿—mourning not for the Spanish argosy which had been allowed to go her ways unmolested, but for the stoutest captain that ever bared his scimitar in the service of Islam. The story of how he came to be delivered up was never clearly told; none dared clearly tell it, for none who had participated in the deed but took shame in it thereafter, however clear it might be that Sakr-el-Bahr had brought it all upon himself. But, at least, it was understood that he had not fallen in battle, and hence it was assumed that he was still alive. Upon that presumption there was built up a sort of legend that he would one day come back; and redeemed captives returning a half-century later related how in Algiers to that day the coming of Sakr-el-Bahr was still confidently expected and looked for by all true Muslimeen.

===Chinese===

灰蒙蒙的黎明里，他们把死者抛下海，冲洗甲板；却没有发觉少了一个人——这便是一个凭据：那位英格兰船长，或是他的部下，并没有一字不差地守住契约。
他们挂着一船的哀恸回到阿尔及尔——哀的不是那条放走了、任它自去西班牙商船，而是伊斯兰的刀刃底下从没有出过的那条最硬的船长。他怎样被交出去的，始终没有人说得清楚；也没人敢说清楚，因为凡是那桩事里沾过手的，事后无不引以为耻——尽管明摆着，这一切都是萨克埃尔-巴尔自己招上身的。但至少，人们知道他不是阵亡的，于是便推想他还活着。在这个推想上，渐渐搭起了一则传说：说他总有一天要回来。半个世纪后赎身归来的俘虏们讲起：直到那些年，阿尔及尔的真信士们，还在笃定地盼着、等着萨克埃尔-巴尔的归来。

## XI The Truth / 真相
===Original===

The sun was dipping swiftly to the world’s rim when Sakr-el-Bahr with his Nubians and his little retinue of corsairs came to the gates of that white house of his on its little eminence outside the Bab El Oued and beyond the walls of the city.
When Rosamund and Lionel, brought in the wake of the corsair, found themselves in the spacious courtyard beyond the dark and narrow entrance, the blue of the sky contained but the paling embers of the dying day, and suddenly, sharply upon the evening stillness, came a *muezzin*’s voice calling the faithful unto prayer.
Slaves fetched water from the fountain that played in the middle of the quadrangle and tossed aloft a slender silvery spear of water to break into a myriad gems and so shower down into the broad marble basin. Sakr-el-Bahr washed, as did his followers, and then he went down upon the praying mat that had been set for him, whilst his corsairs detached their cloaks and spread them upon the ground to serve them in like stead.
The Nubians turned the two slaves about, lest their glances should defile the orisons of the faithful, and left them so facing the wall and the green gate that led into the garden whence were wafted on the cooling air the perfumes of jessamine and lavender. Through the laths of the gate they might have caught a glimpse of the riot of colour there, and they might have seen the slaves arrested by the Persian waterwheel at which they had been toiling and chanting until the call to prayer had come to strike them into statues.
Sakr-el-Bahr rose from his devotions, uttered a sharp word of command, and entered the house. The Nubians followed him, urging their captives before them up the narrow stairs, and so brought them out upon the terrace on the roof, that space which in Eastern houses is devoted to the women, but which no woman’s foot had ever trodden since this house had been tenanted by Sakr-el-Bahr the wifeless.
This terrace, which was surrounded by a parapet some four feet high, commanded a view of the city straggling up the hillside to eastward, from the harbour and of the island at the end of the mole which had been so laboriously built by the labour of Christian slaves from the stones of the ruined fortress﻿—the *Peñon*, which Kheyr-ed-Din Barbarossa had wrested from the Spaniards.

===Chinese===

太阳正急急沉向天边，萨克埃尔-巴尔带着他的努比亚人和那一小队海盗，到了他那座白宅的门前——宅子踞在巴卜厄勒维德外、城墙之外的一座小小高地上。
罗莎蒙德和莱昂内尔跟在海盗身后，穿过幽暗狭窄的门洞，站进那方宽敞的院子时，蓝天里只剩垂死白昼的余烬，正一点点淡下去；忽然，一声锐利的宣礼员嗓音劈开黄昏的宁静，唤信士们去祷告。
奴隶们从泉水里打来水——那泉在院子正中喷涌，抛起一支纤细银亮的水矛，在半空碎成万点珠玉，洒落宽宽的大理石盆。萨克埃尔-巴尔净了身，他的部下也净了身；随后他跪上替他铺好的拜毯，他的海盗们解下斗篷铺在地上，权充拜毯。
努比亚人把两个奴隶拨转过身去，免得他们的目光玷了信士的祷词，让他们面朝院墙和那扇通往园子的绿门——渐凉的空气里，正是从那园中送来茉莉和薰衣草的香。透过绿门的板条，他们本可以瞥见里头那一片泼泼洒洒的颜色，也可以望见那些奴隶在波斯水车前定了格——直到祷告的召唤来时，他们还在那儿推着唱着，一喊便成了石像。
萨克埃尔-巴尔祷毕起身，短促一声令下，进了宅子。努比亚人跟着他，把俘虏推在身前，赶上窄窄的楼梯，带上屋顶的露台——东方宅子里，这一片向来是留给女眷的；只是自打这座宅子住了不娶妻的萨克埃尔-巴尔，还不曾有女人的脚踏上来过。
这道露台围着约四英尺高的栏墙，东面收进眼底：那座城沿着山坡一路蔓上去，直到港口，直到防波堤头那座小岛——堤是基督徒奴隶的苦役、拿破败堡垒“佩尼翁”的石头筑起来的；那堡垒，是海雷丁·巴巴罗萨从西班牙人手里夺下来的。

===Original===

The deepening shroud of evening was now upon all, transmuting white and yellow walls alike to a pearly greyness. To westward stretched the fragrant gardens of the house, where the doves were murmuring fondly among the mulberries and lotus trees. Beyond it a valley wound its way between the shallow hills, and from a pool fringed with sedges and bullrushes above which a great stork was majestically sailing came the harsh croak of frogs.
An awning supported upon two gigantic spears hung out from the southern wall of the terrace which rose to twice the height of that forming the parapet on its other three sides. Under this was a divan and silken cushions, and near it a small Moorish table of ebony inlaid with mother-of-pearl and gold. Over the opposite parapet, where a lattice had been set, rioted a trailing rose-tree charged with bloodred blossoms, though now their colours were merged into the all-encompassing greyness.
Here Lionel and Rosamund looked at each other in the dim light, their faces gleaming ghostly each to each, whilst the Nubians stood like twin statues by the door that opened from the stairhead.
The man groaned, and clasped his hands before him. The doublet which had been torn from him in the *sôk* had since been restored and temporarily repaired by a strand of palmetto cord. But he was woefully bedraggled. Yet his thoughts, if his first words are to be taken as an indication of them were for Rosamund’s condition rather than his own.
“O God, that you should be subjected to this!” he cried. “That you should have suffered what you have suffered! The humiliation of it, the barbarous cruelty! Oh!” He covered his haggard face with his hands.
She touched him gently on the arm.
“What I have suffered is but a little thing,” she said, and her voice was wonderfully steady and soothing. Have I not said that these Godolphins were brave folk? Even their women were held to have something of the male spirit in their breasts; and to this none can doubt that Rosamund now bore witness. “Do not pity me, Lionel, for my sufferings are at an end or very nearly.” She smiled strangely, the smile of exaltation that you may see upon the martyr’s face in the hour of doom.
“How?” quoth he, in faint surprise.
“How?” she echoed. “Is there not always a way to thrust aside life’s burden when it grows too heavy﻿—heavier than God would have us bear?”
His only answer was a groan.

===Chinese===

夜幕愈合愈拢，白墙黄墙一概换上了一身珍珠似的灰。西面铺展着宅子芬芳的园子，鸽子在桑树和忘忧树间咕咕呢喃。园外，一道山谷在浅丘之间蜿蜒；一座芦苇香蒲围着的池塘上头，一只大鹳庄严地滑翔，池里送来蛙声阵阵。
露台南面的墙升到两倍高，从墙里挑出一方天棚，撑在两根巨矛上。棚下设着软榻和丝垫，近旁一张乌木嵌螺钿和金丝的小摩尔桌。对面栏墙上装着一格花窗，一株攀缘的玫瑰在窗上开得泼辣，缀满了血红的花——此刻，那些颜色也都融进四合的灰里去了。
就在这里，莱昂内尔和罗莎蒙德在昏光里对望，两张脸彼此望来都像鬼影一般白；努比亚人立在楼梯口的门边，像一对孪生的石像。
男人呻吟一声，双手交握在胸前。市场上被撕去的那件紧身上衣，后来发还了他，拿一截棕榈绳草草缀上了。可他还是狼狈得可怜。不过，若把他头一句话当作凭据，他心里想的是罗莎蒙德的处境，不是自己的。
“哦，上帝，竟叫你受这个！”他喊，“竟叫你受了你所受的这一切！这般的羞辱，这般野蛮的残暴！哦！”他双手捂住枯槁的脸。
她轻轻碰了碰他的胳膊。
“我受过的不过是小事，”她说，嗓音出奇地稳、出奇地温。我不是说过么，戈多尔芬家是勇敢的人家？连他们的女人，胸腔里也素来认定存着几分男儿气；这一点，此刻的罗莎蒙德便是明证，谁也不能怀疑。“别可怜我，莱昂内尔，我的苦已经完了，或者快完了。”她古怪地一笑，那是一种升华的笑，殉道者在死囚的时辰脸上会有的那种笑。
“怎么？”他弱弱地一怔。
“怎么？”她应道，“生的担子压得太沉——沉过上帝要我们背的分量时，不是总有一条路，把它卸下去么？”
他唯一的回答，是一声呻吟。

===Original===

Indeed, he had done little but groan in all the hours they had spent together since they were brought ashore from the carack; and had the season permitted her so much reflection, she might have considered that she had found him singularly wanting during those hours of stress when a man of worth would have made some effort, however desperate, to enhearten her rather than repine upon his own plight.
Slaves entered bearing four enormous flaming torches which they set in iron sconces protruding from the wall of the house. Thence they shed a lurid ruddy glow upon the terrace. The slaves departed again, and presently, in the black gap of the doorway between the Nubians, a third figure appeared unheralded. It was Sakr-el-Bahr.
He stood a moment at gaze, his attitude haughty, his face expressionless; then slowly he advanced. He was dressed in a short white caftan that descended to his knees, and was caught about his waist in a shimmering girdle of gold that quivered like fire in the glow of the torches as he moved. His arms from the elbow and his legs from the knee were bare, and his feet were shod with gold-embroidered red Turkish slippers. He wore a white turban decked by a plume of osprey attached by a jewelled clasp.
He signed to the Nubians and they vanished silently, leaving him alone with his captives.
He bowed to Rosamund. “This, mistress,” he said, “is to be your domain henceforth which is to treat you more as wife than slave. For it is to Muslim wives that the housetops in Barbary are allotted. I hope you like it.”
Lionel staring at him out of a white face, his conscience bidding him fear the very worst, his imagination painting a thousand horrid fates for him and turning him sick with dread, shrank back before his half-brother, who scarce appeared to notice him just then.
But Rosamund confronted him, drawn to the full of her splendid height, and if her face was pale, yet it was as composed and calm as his own; if her bosom rose and fell to betray her agitations yet her glance was contemptuous and defiant, her voice calm and steady, when she answered him with the question﻿—
“What is your intent with me?”
“My intent?” said he, with a little twisted smile. Yet for all that he believed he hated her and sought to hurt, to humble and to crush her, he could not stifle his admiration of her spirit’s gallantry in such an hour as this.

===Chinese===

说实在的，自从从卡拉维尔船上被带上岸，两人相处的这几个钟头里，他做的事少，呻吟多。时节若容她多想，她兴许早掂量出来了：在这般煎熬的时辰，一条有分量的汉子总该想点什么办法——哪怕再绝望——来给她打气，而不是一味哀叹自家的命；可他偏没有。
奴隶们进来，擎着四支巨大的火把，插上从宅墙上探出的铁架。火把给露台泼下一片通红的亮。奴隶们退了下去；不多时，努比亚人之间那道黑洞洞的门里，第三个身影悄没声地出现了。是萨克埃尔-巴尔。
他立了片刻，端详着，姿态倨傲，面无表情；随后缓缓上前。他穿一袭白色短长袍，长及膝盖，腰间束一条金光闪烁的腰带，在火把光里随着他的走动颤动如火。他的臂自肘以下、腿自膝以下都赤着，脚上一双绣金的红色土耳其便鞋。头上一顶白缠头，缀着一支鱼鹰翎，用一枚宝石扣别住。
他朝努比亚人一摆手，两人无声地退去，留下他单独对着他的俘虏。
他向罗莎蒙德一躬。“小姐，从此这里归您管——我会待您如妻子，多于如奴隶。巴巴里的屋顶，本来就是拨给穆斯林妻室的。但愿您中意。”
莱昂内尔从一张白脸上瞪着他：良心叫他往最坏处怕，想象力给他描出一千种可怕的下场，怕得他发恶心。他在异母哥哥面前朝后缩，而那人此刻几乎没有留意他。
罗莎蒙德却迎面对住他，挺足了她那出众的身量。她的脸纵然苍白，却同他自己的脸一样安详镇定；胸口起伏泄露了她的激荡，可她的目光鄙夷而不屈，嗓音平静而稳——她这样回他的问：
“你要把我怎样？”
“我把你怎样？”他说，嘴角挂着一丝歪扭的笑。尽管他自信恨她、存心伤她、辱她、碾碎她，可在这样的时辰，她这股气概的英姿，他还是压不住心头的赞赏。

===Original===

From behind the hills peeped the edge of the moon﻿—a sickle of burnished copper.
“My intent is not for you to question,” he replied. “There was a time, Rosamund, when in all the world you had no slave more utter than was I. Yourself in your heartlessness, and in your lack of faith, you broke the golden fetters of that servitude. You’ll find it less easy to break the shackles I now impose upon you.”
She smiled her scorn and quiet confidence. He stepped close to her.
“You are my slave, do you understand?﻿—bought in the marketplace as I might buy me a mule, a goat, or a camel﻿—and belonging to me body and soul. You are my property, my thing, my chattel, to use or abuse, to cherish or break as suits my whim, without a will that is not my will, holding your very life at my good pleasure.”
She recoiled a step before the dull hatred that throbbed in his words, before the evil mockery of his swarthy bearded face.
“You beast!” she gasped.
“So now you understand the bondage into which you are come in exchange for the bondage which in your own wantonness you dissolved.”
“May God forgive you,” she panted.
“I thank you for that prayer,” said he. “May He forgive you no less.”
And then from the background came an inarticulate sound, a strangled, snarling sob from Lionel.
Sakr-el-Bahr turned slowly. He eyed the fellow a moment in silence, then he laughed.
“Ha! My sometime brother. A pretty fellow, as God lives is it not? Consider him Rosamund. Behold how gallantly misfortune is borne by this pillar of manhood upon which you would have leaned, by this stalwart husband of your choice. Look at him! Look at this dear brother of mine.”
Under the lash of that mocking tongue Lionel’s mood was stung to anger where before it had held naught but fear.
“You are no brother of mine,” he retorted fiercely. “Your mother was a wanton who betrayed my father.”
Sakr-el-Bahr quivered a moment as if he had been struck. Yet he controlled himself.
“Let me hear my mother’s name but once again on thy foul tongue, and I’ll have it ripped out by the roots. Her memory, I thank God, is far above the insults of such a crawling thing as you. None the less, take care not to speak of the only woman whose name I reverence.”
And then turning at bay, as even the rat will do, Lionel sprang upon him, with clawing hands outstretched to reach his throat.

===Chinese===

山后探出月亮的边——一弯磨亮的铜镰。
“我要怎样，轮不到你来问。”他答。“罗莎蒙德，从前，全世界再没有一个奴隶，比得上我那样彻底。是你自己，拿你的狠心、你的寡信，挣断了那副金镣。如今我给你上的这副铁镣，你挣断起来，可没那么容易。”
她报之一笑，笑里满是轻蔑和笃定。他逼近一步。
“你是我的奴隶，明白么？——市场上买来的，同我买一头骡子、一只山羊、一峰骆驼一样——连身带魂，都归了我。你是我的产业、我的物件、我的动产，随我的高兴使唤或作践、珍惜或毁掉；除了我的意志，你没有意志；连你那条命，也捏在我的高兴里。”
他话里那股阴沉的恨，他那张黝黑须髯脸上恶毒的讥诮，逼得她倒退一步。
“你这畜生！”她喘着说。
“如今你懂了：你拿自己的任性解开的那副枷，换来的是怎样一副枷。”
“愿上帝饶恕你。”她喘息道。
“多谢你这句祷词，”他说，“愿祂同样饶恕你。”
就在这时，暗处起了一声不成话的响动——莱昂内尔一声哽住的、带着咆哮的抽噎。
萨克埃尔-巴尔慢慢转过身。他默然把那人打量了片刻，随后笑了。
“哈！我从前的弟弟。一副体面人物，老天在上，是不是？相看相看吧，罗莎蒙德。瞧瞧这条你想倚靠的男子汉柱子、这位你选中的壮硕丈夫，把不幸担得多么英武。瞧瞧他！瞧瞧我这位亲爱的弟弟。”
那条讥诮的舌头一鞭子抽下去，莱昂内尔先前那一腔的怕，被抽成了怒。
“你不是我的什么哥哥，”他狠狠回嘴，“你娘是个狐狸精，骗了我父亲。”
萨克埃尔-巴尔一颤，像挨了一记。可他按住了自己。
“再让我从我听你那条脏舌头上，听见一次我母亲的名字，我就叫人把它连根拔了。感谢上帝，她的名字高得很，轮不到你这种爬虫来辱慢。不过你还是当心：别提那位唯一叫我敬重的女人。”
莱昂内尔被逼到墙根——连耗子被逼急了也要回身——他猛扑上来，张开十指直取他的咽喉。

===Original===

But Sakr-el-Bahr caught him in a grip that bent him howling to his knees.
“You find me strong, eh?” he gibed. “Is it matter for wonder? Consider that for six endless months I toiled at the oar of a galley, and you’ll understand what it was that turned my body into iron and robbed me of a soul.”
He flung him off, and sent him crashing into the rosebush and the lattice over which it rambled.
“Do you realize the horror of the rower’s bench? to sit day in day out, night in night out, chained naked to the oar, amid the reek and stench of your fellows in misfortune, unkempt, unwashed save by the rain, broiled and roasted by the sun, festering with sores, lashed and cut and scarred by the boatswain’s whip as you faint under the ceaseless, endless, cruel toil?”
“Do you realize it?” From a tone of suppressed fury his voice rose suddenly to a roar. “You shall. For that horror which was mine by your contriving shall now be yours until you die.”
He paused; but Lionel made no attempt to avail himself of this. His courage all gone out of him again, as suddenly as it had flickered up, he cowered where he had been flung.
“Before you go there is something else,” Sakr-el-Bahr resumed, “something for which I have had you brought hither tonight.
“Not content with having delivered me to all this, not content with having branded me a murderer, destroyed my good name, filched my possessions and driven me into the very path of hell, you must further set about usurping my place in the false heart of this woman I once loved.
“I hope,” he went on reflectively, “that in your own poor way you love her, too, Lionel. Thus to the torment that awaits your body shall be added torment for your treacherous soul﻿—such torture of mind as only the damned may know. To that end have I brought you hither. That you may realize something of what is in store for this woman at my hands; that you may take the thought of it with you to be to your mind worse than the boatswain’s lash to your pampered body.”
“You devil!” snarled Lionel. “Oh, you fiend out of hell!”
“If you will manufacture devils, little toad of a brother, do not upbraid them for being devils when next you meet them.”
“Give him no heed, Lionel!” said Rosamund. “I shall prove him as much a boaster as he has proved himself a villain. Never think that he will be able to work his evil will.”

===Chinese===

可萨克埃尔-巴尔一把攥住他，那一攥之力，压得他嚎着跪了下去。
“觉得我力气大，嗯？”他嘲弄道，“奇怪么？想想我在帆船的桨上苦了整整六个月，你就明白，是什么把我的身子炼成了铁，又把我炼掉了魂。”
他把他一甩，甩得他撞进蔷薇丛、连同蔷薇爬满的那格花窗。
“你可知道桨凳的滋味？日复一日、夜复一夜，赤条条锁在桨上，泡在难友们的恶臭里，蓬头垢面，除了雨再没有水洗，叫日头烤、太阳燎，浑身烂着疮，熬不住那没完没了的苦役晕过去时，还要挨水手长的鞭子、挨刀、落疤——你可知道？”
“你可知道？”他从强压的怒声里陡然拔成一声咆哮。“你就要知道了。那场由你一手安排给我的苦难，如今轮到你，一直到死。”
他顿住；莱昂内尔却无意利用这个空当。他方才闪起的那点勇气又熄了，熄得同闪起时一样突然，蜷在他被甩落的地方。
“你上那儿去之前，还有一桩事，”萨克埃尔-巴尔续道，“我今晚把你带到这儿来，正为此。
“把我送进这一切还不算，把我钉成杀人犯、毁我的名声、偷我的家产、把我一路逼进地狱的门还不算，你还得再进一步，篡我在这个虚伪女人心里的位子——这个我曾经爱过的女人。
“我希望，”他若有所思地说，“你用你那可怜的法子，也爱她，莱昂内尔。这样，等着你皮肉的那份罪之外，还要给你那条奸诈的魂添一份罪——唯有沉沦者才知道的那种心刑。为这个我才把你带到这里来。叫你多少看明白，这个女人在我手里等着的是什么；叫你带着这份念想走，在你心里，它比水手长的鞭子落在你那娇养的皮肉上还要狠。”
“你这魔鬼！”莱昂内尔咬牙道，“哦，你这从地狱里放出来的恶鬼！”
“既是你要造魔鬼，小癞蛤蟆般的弟弟，就别埋怨下回撞见它们时，它们是魔鬼。”
“别理他，莱昂内尔！”罗莎蒙德说，“我来证明他是个吹牛的，正如他已经证明了自己是个恶棍。永远别信他能行他的凶。”

===Original===

“ ’Tis you are the boaster there,” said Sakr-el-Bahr. “And for the rest, I am what you and he, between you, have made me.”
“Did we make you liar and coward?﻿—for that is what you are indeed,” she answered.
“Coward?” he echoed, in genuine surprise. “ ’Twill be some lie that he has told you with the others. In what, pray, was I ever a coward?”
“In what? In this that you do now; in this taunting and torturing of two helpless beings in your power.”
“I speak not of what I am,” he replied, “for I have told you that I am what you have made me. I speak of what I was. I speak of the past.”
She looked at him and she seemed to measure him with her unwavering glance.
“You speak of the past?” she echoed, her voice low. “You speak of the past and to me? You dare?”
“It is that we might speak of it together that I have fetched you all the way from England; that at last I may tell you things I was a fool to have kept from you five years ago; that we may resume a conversation which you interrupted when you dismissed me.”
“I did you a monstrous injury, no doubt,” she answered him, with bitter irony. “I was surely wanting in consideration. It would have become me better to have smiled and fawned upon my brother’s murderer.”
“I swore to you, then, that I was not his murderer,” he reminded her in a voice that shook.
“And I answered you that you lied.”
“Ay, and on that you dismissed me﻿—the word of the man whom you professed to love, the word of the man to whom you had given your trust weighing for naught with you.”
“When I gave you my trust,” she retorted, “I did so in ignorance of your true self, in a headstrong wilful ignorance that would not be guided by what all the world said of you and your wild ways. For that blind wilfulness I have been punished, as perhaps I deserved to be.”
“Lies﻿—all lies!” he stormed. “Those ways of mine﻿—and God knows they were none so wild, when all is said﻿—I abandoned when I came to love you. No lover since the world began was ever so cleansed, so purified, so sanctified by love as was I.”
“Spare me this at least!” she cried on a note of loathing.
“Spare you?” he echoed. “What shall I spare you?”
“The shame of it all; the shame that is ever mine in the reflection that for a season I believed I loved you.”
He smiled. “If you can still feel shame, it shall overwhelm you ere I have done. For you shall hear me out.

===Chinese===

“吹牛的是你，”萨克埃尔-巴尔说，“再说，我是你们两个合力造出来的这个我。”
“我们把造成骗子、懦夫了么？——你这才是呢。”她答。
“懦夫？”他应声，这一惊倒是真的。“又是他编给你的谎话之一吧。我几时懦过，倒要请教？”
“几时？就现在：拿两个落在你手里的无助的人，这样讥、这样磨。”
“我说的不是如今的我，”他答，“我已经告诉过你，我是你们造的。我说的是从前的我，说的是过去。”
她望着他，那毫不游移的目光像是在丈量他。
“你说过去？”她应道，嗓音低了，“你同我说过去？你敢？”
“正是为了同你说说它，我才把你从英格兰一路带到这里来；为了我终于可以告诉你一些事——五年前我把它们瞒下，是我蠢；为了咱们接着谈那一场谈话——你打发我走的时候，叫你打断了的那一场。”
“不错，我是大大地亏待了你，”她答他，话里的讥讽又苦又冷，“我实在欠考虑。我原该堆着笑、谄着媚，去讨好我哥哥的凶手才是。”
“我当时对你起过誓，说我不是凶手，”他提醒她，嗓音发着颤。
“我答你的，是你在撒谎。”
“是啊，你就拿这一句打发了我——你自称爱的那个人的话、你交过心的那个人的话，在你那里一个钱不值。”
“我交心给你的时候，”她反诘，“我还不识你的真面目；那是任性的、一意孤行的无知——全世界说你和你那副荒唐做派的话，我一概不听。就为那份盲目的任性，我受了罚——多半也是我该受的。”
“谎——全是谎！”他发作了。“我那副做派——老天知道，说到底也没那样荒唐——爱上一你，我就丢开了。开天辟地以来，再没有哪个情人，像我那样，被爱洗得那样净、炼得那样纯、祝得那样圣。”
“至少这一样饶了我吧！”她喊，话音里满是憎恶。
“饶了你？”他应道，“饶你哪一样？”
“饶了我这满身的羞；一想到我竟有一季信过自己爱你，那份叫我抬不起头的羞。”
他笑了。“你要还知羞，等我说完，羞也要淹死你。因为你要听我把话说完。

===Original===

Here there are none to interrupt us, none to thwart my sovereign will. Reflect then, and remember. Remember what a pride you took in the change you had wrought in me. Your vanity welcomed that flattery, that tribute to the power of your beauty. Yet, all in a moment, upon the paltriest grounds, you believed me the murderer of your brother.”
“The paltriest grounds?” she cried, protesting almost despite herself.
“So paltry that the Justices at Truro would not move against me.”
“Because,” she cut in, “they accounted that you had been sufficiently provoked. Because you had not sworn to them as you swore to me that no provocation should ever drive you to raise your hand against my brother. Because they did not realize how false and how forsworn you were.”
He considered her a moment. Then he took a turn on the terrace. Lionel crouching ever by the rose-tree was almost entirely forgotten by him now.
“God give me patience with you!” he said at length. “I need it. For I desire you to understand many things this night. I mean you to see how just is my resentment; how just the punishment that is to overtake you for what you have made of my life and perhaps of my hereafter. Justice Baine and another who is dead, knew me for innocent.”
“They knew you for innocent?” There was scornful amazement in her tone. “Were they not witnesses of the quarrel betwixt you and Peter and of your oath that you would kill him?”
“That was an oath sworn in the heat of anger. Afterwards I bethought me that he was your brother.”
“Afterwards?” said she. “After you had murdered him?”
“I say again,” Oliver replied calmly, “that I did not do this thing.”
“And I say again that you lie.”
He considered her for a long moment; then he laughed. “Have you ever,” he asked, “known a man to lie without some purpose? Men lie for the sake of profit, they lie out of cowardice or malice, or else because they are vain and vulgar boasters. I know of no other causes that will drive a man to falsehood, save that﻿—ah, yes!﻿—” (and he flashed a sidelong glance at Lionel)﻿—“save that sometimes a man will lie to shield another, out of self-sacrifice. There you have all the spurs that urge a man to falsehood. Can any of these be urging me tonight? Reflect! Ask yourself what purpose I could serve by lying to you now.

===Chinese===

这里没有人来打岔，没有人拦得住我的意志。那你就想想，就记得吧。记得你在我身上促成的改变，你多么引以为傲。你的虚荣心吃足了那份奉承、那份献给你美貌的贡赋。可一转眼，凭那最不成话的一点由头，你就信了我是杀你哥哥的凶手。”
“最不成话的由头？”她喊，几乎不由自主地抗辩起来。
“不成话到特鲁罗的治安官都不肯动手拿我。”
“那是因为，”她插进来，“他们认为我挑衅得你已经够多了。因为你没有对着他们起过誓——你对我起过的那种誓：说什么挑衅也断不能叫你对我哥哥抬一根指头。因为他们不知道你有多假、多背誓。”
他把端详了片刻，随后在露台上踱了个来回。一直蜷在蔷薇树旁的莱昂内尔，此刻早被他忘了个干净。
“上帝赐我对你的耐性吧！”他终于说，“我用得着。因为今夜我要你明白许多事。我要你看到，我的怨多么公正；等着你的那场惩罚多么公正——为你把我的一生、兴许还有我的来世，造的这个样子。贝恩治安官，还有一个已经死了的人，早知道我是清白的。”
“他们知道你清白？”她的话音里满是鄙夷的惊异，“你同彼得那场争吵、你那要杀他的誓言，他们不都是见证么？”
“那是一句怒头上起的誓。过后我想起来，他是你的哥哥。”
“过后？”她说，“你杀了他之后？”
“我再说一遍，”奥利弗平静地答，“这事不是我干的。”
“我也再说一遍，你在撒谎。”
他把她端详了许久；随后他笑了。“你见过，”他问，“一个人无缘无故撒谎么？人为利撒谎，为怯、为恶撒谎，再不然就是虚荣、爱吹。除了这些——啊，对了！”（他斜扫了莱昂内尔一眼）——“还有一条：有时人为护另一个人，出于自我牺牲，肯撒谎。催人撒谎的鞭子，全在这里了。这几条，今夜可有哪一条在抽我？想想吧！问问你自己，我如今对你撒谎，图得上什么。

===Original===

Consider further that I have come to loathe you for your unfaith; that I desire naught so much as to punish you for that and for all its bitter consequences to me that I have brought you hither to exact payment from you to the uttermost farthing. What end then can I serve by falsehood?”
“All this being so, what end could you serve by truth?” she countered.
“To make you realize to the full the injustice that you did. To make you understand the wrongs for which you are called to pay. To prevent you from conceiving yourself a martyr; to make you perceive in all its deadly bitterness that what now comes to you is the inevitable fruit of your own faithlessness.”
“Sir Oliver, do you think me a fool?” she asked him.
“Madam, I do﻿—and worse,” he answered.
“Ay, that is clear,” she agreed scornfully, “since even now you waste breath in attempting to persuade me against my reason. But words will not blot out facts. And though you talk from now till the day of judgment no word of yours can efface those bloodstains in the snow that formed a trail from that poor murdered body to your own door; no word of yours can extinguish the memory of the hatred between him and you, and of your own threat to kill him; nor can it stifle the recollection of the public voice demanding your punishment. You dare to take such a tone as you are taking with me? You dare here under Heaven to stand and lie to me that you may give false gloze to the villainy of your present deed﻿—for that is the purpose of your falsehood, since you asked me what purpose there could be for it. What had you to set against all that, to convince me that your hands were clean, to induce me to keep the troth which﻿—God forgive me!﻿—I had plighted to you?”
“My word,” he answered her in a ringing voice.
“Your lie,” she amended.
“Do not suppose,” said he, “that I could not support my word by proofs if called upon to do so.”
“Proofs?” She stared at him, wide-eyed a moment. Then her lip curled. “And that no doubt was the reason of your flight when you heard that the Queen’s pursuivants were coming in response to the public voice to call you to account.”
He stood at gaze a moment, utterly dumbfounded. “My flight?” he said. “What fable’s that?”
“You will tell me next that you did not flee. That that is another false charge against you?”
“So,” he said slowly, “it was believed I fled!”

===Chinese===

再想想：我已经恨透了你的负心；我最想要的，就是为它、为它落到我头上的那一切苦果惩罚你，才把你带到这里来，一个子儿一个子儿地，向你收足这笔账。那么撒谎对我还有什么用？”
“既然如此，说真话对你又有什么用？”她反诘。
“叫你把你干下的不公，掂个十足。叫你明白你凭什么挨这笔讨还。免得你把自己当成殉道者；叫你看得清清楚楚、苦到根：今日落到你头上的，是你自己负心结出的、躲不掉的果子。”
“奥利弗爵士，你当我是傻子么？”她问他。
“小姐，当你是——还不止。”他答。
“是啊，这看得明白，”她鄙夷地附和，“都这会儿了，你还费口水，想说服我违逆我的理智。可话抹不掉事实。你就是从今天一直讲到审判日，你那一个字，也抹不掉雪地上那串血迹——它从那具可怜的、被杀的尸身，一路通到你自家门口；抹不掉众人记得的你同他的仇、你亲口要杀他的威吓；也抹不掉人人口中喊着要办你的那片声。你还敢拿这样的口气同我说话？你还敢在青天底下站在这里对我撒谎，好给你眼下这桩恶行涂脂抹粉——你既然问我撒谎图什么，这才是你那谎的用处。面对这一切，你拿什么给我，叫我相信你双手干净、叫我守住那份——上帝饶恕我！——我许过你的信约？”
“我的话。”他答，声音铮铮。
“你的谎。”她纠正。
“别以为，”他说，“真要我对质，我拿不出凭据来撑我的话。”
“凭据？”她瞪着他，一时睁大了眼。随后嘴唇一撇。“难怪你一听说女王的缉捕官应众人之声要来拿你，就撒腿跑了。”
他怔怔立着，全然懵了。“我跑了？”他说，“这是什么天方夜谭？”
“你下一步是不是要说，你没跑？说那又是别人安在你头上的一桩冤枉？”
“原来，”他慢慢地说，“人人当我是跑了！”

===Original===

And then light burst upon him, to dazzle and stun him. It was so inevitably what must have been believed, and yet it had never crossed his mind. O the damnable simplicity of it! At another time his disappearance must have provoked comment and investigation, perhaps. But, happening when it did, the answer to it came promptly and convincingly and no man troubled to question further. Thus was Lionel’s task made doubly easy, thus was his own guilt made doubly sure in the eyes of all. His head sank upon his breast. What had he done? Could he still blame Rosamund for having been convinced by so overwhelming a piece of evidence? Could he still blame her if she had burnt unopened the letter which he had sent her by the hand of Pitt? What else indeed could any suppose, but that he had fled? And that being so, clearly such a flight must brand him irrefutably for the murderer he was alleged to be. How could he blame her if she had ultimately been convinced by the only reasonable assumption possible?
A sudden sense of the wrong he had done rose now like a tide about him.
“My God!” he groaned, like a man in pain. “My God!”
He looked at her, and then averted his glance again, unable now to endure the haggard, strained yet fearless gaze of those brave eyes of hers.
“What else, indeed, could you believe?” he muttered brokenly, thus giving some utterance to what was passing through his mind.
“Naught else but the whole vile truth,” she answered fiercely, and thereby stung him anew, whipped him out of his sudden weakening back to his mood of resentment and vindictiveness.
She had shown herself, he thought in that moment of reviving anger, too ready to believe what told against him.
“The truth?” he echoed, and eyed her boldly now. “Do you know the truth when you see it? We shall discover. For by God’s light you shall have the truth laid stark before you now, and you shall find it hideous beyond all your hideous imaginings.”
There was something so compelling now in his tone and manner that it drove her to realize that some revelation was impending. She was conscious of a faint excitement, a reflection perhaps of the wild excitement that was astir in him.
“Your brother,” he began, “met his death at the hands of a false weakling whom I loved, towards whom I had a sacred duty. Straight from the deed he fled to me for shelter.

===Chinese===

随即，一道光炸开在他头上，炸得他眼花、炸得他发懵。事情本该如此被认定——这再顺理成章不过，可他偏偏从没想到过。哦，这简单得多该死！换了别的时候，他的失踪多半要惹人议论、惹人查问；可它偏生在那样的关口，答案来得又快又叫人信，谁也懒得再问第二句。就这样，莱昂内尔的事好办了一倍；就这样，他的罪，在众人眼里也坐实了一倍。他的头垂到胸前。他都干了些什么？这样的铁证摆在眼前，他还能怪罗莎蒙德信了么？她把皮特捎去的那封信原封没拆就烧了，他还能怪她么？换了谁，还能作别的想——除了他畏罪潜逃？而既是这样，那样一场逃跑，自然就等于把他钉死成传说中的凶手。到最后，她是被唯一讲得通的推断说服的——这个，他还能怪她么？
一股亏欠之感，此刻像潮水一样从四下漫上来，淹了他。
“我的上帝！”他呻吟，像个痛极了的人，“我的上帝！”
他望了望她，随即又把目光挪开——她那双勇敢的眼睛，憔悴、绷紧、却毫不畏缩的逼视，他此刻受不住了。
“是啊，不然你还能信什么？”他断断续续地低语，把心里淌过的，吐出了一口。
“不然就只有那整个的、肮脏的真相。”她狠狠地答，这一句又刺了他一鞭，把他刚软下来的那一阵，抽回了怨毒和报复的心气。
她这也太急着信那些说他的坏话了——他此刻回涨的怒气里这样想。
“真相？”他应声，此刻放胆直视她，“你见了真相，认得么？咱们这就见分晓。老天在上，我要把真相赤条条摆到你面前，叫你发现：它比你一切狰狞的想象，还要狰狞。”
他话音和气度里那股不容分说的东西，逼得她意识到：一场揭底就要来了。她觉出一阵隐隐的激动——多半是映照着他心里翻腾的那场狂澜。
“你的哥哥，”他开了头，“死在了一个虚伪的懦夫手里——一个我爱过的、对他负着神圣本分的人。他行凶之后，径直逃到我这里来避难。

===Original===

A wound he had taken in the struggle left that trail of blood to mark the way he had come.” He paused, and his tone became gentler, it assumed the level note of one who reasons impassively. “Was it not an odd thing, now, that none should ever have paused to seek with certainty whence that blood proceeded, and to consider that I bore no wound in those days? Master Baine knew it, for I submitted my body to his examination, and a document was drawn up and duly attested which should have sent the Queen’s pursuivants back to London with drooping tails had I been at Penarrow to receive them.”
Faintly through her mind stirred the memory that Master Baine had urged the existence of some such document, that in fact he had gone so far as to have made oath of this very circumstance now urged by Sir Oliver; and she remembered that the matter had been brushed aside as an invention of the Justice’s to answer the charge of laxity in the performance of his duty, particularly as the only co-witness he could cite was Sir Andrew Flack, the parson, since deceased. Sir Oliver’s voice drew her attention from that memory.
“But let that be,” he was saying. “Let us come back to the story itself. I gave the craven weakling shelter. Thereby I drew down suspicion upon myself, and since I could not clear myself save by denouncing him, I kept silent. That suspicion drew to certainty when the woman to whom I was betrothed, recking nothing of my oaths, freely believing the very worst of me, made an end of our betrothal and thereby branded me a murderer and a liar in the eyes of all. Indignation swelled against me. The Queen’s pursuivants were on their way to do what the Justices of Truro refused to do.
“So far I have given you facts. Now I give you surmise﻿—my own conclusions﻿—but surmise that strikes, as you shall judge, the very bull’s-eye of truth. That dastard to whom I had given sanctuary, to whom I had served as a cloak, measured my nature by his own and feared that I must prove unequal to the fresh burden to be cast upon me. He feared lest under the strain of it I should speak out, advance my proofs, and so destroy him. There was the matter of that wound, and there was something still more unanswerable he feared I might have urged.

===Chinese===

搏斗里他带了一处伤，那一路血迹，就是给他引路的。”他顿了顿，语气缓了下来，换上一副不动声色讲理人的平调。“说起来怪不怪：竟没有一个人停下来查个确实，那血是从哪儿来的；也没有一个人想一想，我那时身上并没有伤？贝恩少爷知道，因为我把身子交给他验看过；还有一份文书，拟好、签押齐全——我若在佩纳罗候着，本可以把女王的缉捕官夹着尾巴打发回伦敦的。”
她心里隐隐记起：贝恩少爷当年就坚称有这么一份文书，甚至为这一节立过誓——正是奥利弗爵士此刻申说的这一节；她又记起，那事当时被当成治安官为搪塞渎职之斥编出来的话，轻轻挥开了，尤其因为他能引的唯一同证，是彭林的牧师安德鲁·弗拉克爵士，如今也已亡故。奥利弗爵士的声音把她从这份回想里引开了。
“可那个不去说它，”他说，“咱们回到事情本身。我收留了那个怯懦的软骨头。就这样，嫌疑落到我头上；我不除掉他就洗不清自己，我便缄了口。随后，同我订了婚的那个女人，把我的誓言当个零，痛痛快快信了最坏的我，亲手掐了我们的婚约——嫌疑就此坐成了铁案，我在众人眼里既是凶手，又是骗子。群情汹汹，要办我。女王的缉捕官已经在路上，特鲁罗的治安官不肯做的，他们来做。
“到这里，我给你的都是实事。现在我给你推测——我自己下的结论——但这推测，你听了自会评判，箭箭钉在真相的靶心上。那个我给过庇护、给遮过风雨的懦夫，拿他自己的心思量我的心，怕我担不起新压上来的担子。他怕我受不住那份重，会开口、会亮出凭据、会毁了他。那一处伤是一桩；还有一桩他更没法招架的，他怕我端出来。

===Original===

There was a certain woman﻿—a wanton up at Malpas﻿—who could have been made to speak, who could have revealed a rivalry concerning her betwixt the slayer and your brother. For the affair in which Peter Godolphin met his death was a pitifully, shamefully sordid one at bottom.”
For the first time she interrupted him, fiercely. “Do you malign the dead?”
“Patience, mistress,” he commanded. “I malign none. I speak the truth of a dead man that the truth may be known of two living ones. Hear me out, then! I have waited long and survived a deal that I might tell you this.
“That craven, then, conceived that I might become a danger to him; so he decided to remove me. He contrived to have me kidnapped one night and put aboard a vessel to be carried to Barbary and sold there as a slave. That is the truth of my disappearance. And the slayer, whom I had befriended and sheltered at my own bitter cost, profited yet further by my removal. God knows whether the prospect of such profit was a further temptation to him. In time he came to succeed me in my possessions, and at last to succeed me even in the affections of the faithless woman who once had been my affianced wife.”
At last she started from the frozen patience in which she had listened hitherto. “Do you say that﻿ ﻿… that Lionel﻿ ﻿… ?” she was beginning in a voice choked by indignation.
And then Lionel spoke at last, straightening himself into a stiffly upright attitude.
“He lies!” he cried. “He lies, Rosamund! Do not heed him.”
“I do not,” she answered, turning away.
A wave of colour suffused the swarthy face of Sakr-el-Bahr. A moment his eyes followed her as she moved away a step or two, then they turned their blazing light of anger upon Lionel. He strode silently across to him, his mien so menacing that Lionel shrank back in fresh terror.
Sakr-el-Bahr caught his brother’s wrist in a grip that was as that of a steel manacle. “We’ll have the truth this night if we have to tear it from you with red-hot pincers,” he said between his teeth.
He dragged him forward to the middle of the terrace and held him there before Rosamund, forcing him down upon his knees into a cowering attitude by the violence of that grip upon his wrist.
“Do you know aught of the ingenuity of Moorish torture?” he asked him. “You may have heard of the rack and the wheel and the thumbscrew at home.

===Chinese===

马尔帕斯山上有一个女人——一个荡妇——本可以叫她开口的，本可以由她抖出凶手同你哥哥为她争风的一段。因为彼得·戈多尔林丧命的那桩事，根底里又腌臜、又下作，腌臜下作得可怜。”
她头一回打断他，劈头盖脸：“你又糟践死人？”
“别急，小姐，”他吩咐道，“我不糟践谁。我说一个死人的实话，好叫两个活人知道真相。听我说完！我等了很久、熬过了许多，才等到把这话告诉你。
“那个懦夫，掂量着我会变成他的祸事，便决意除掉我。他设局，一夜之间把我绑走，弄上一条船，运到巴巴里卖作奴隶。这就是我失踪的真相。而这个凶手——我不惜血本结交过、庇护过的——又靠我的消失又发了一笔。天知道那份进项的前景，是不是又一层诱他下手的饵。后来他继承了我的家产；到最后，连那个负心的女人——曾经是我未婚妻的那个——的感情，也叫他继承了去。”
她终于从那冻住般的静听里惊起。“你是说……是莱昂内尔……”她开了腔，嗓音叫义愤堵着。
这时莱昂内尔终于开口了，直挺挺把自己撑成一根棍。
“他撒谎！”他喊，“他撒谎，罗莎蒙德！别听他的。”
“我不听。”她答，把脸掉开。
一股血色涌上萨克埃尔-巴尔黝黑的脸。他的眼睛跟着她挪开的一两步追了一瞬，随即把那冒火的光调转过来，钉在莱昂内尔身上。他默默大步跨过去，那副架势凶得莱昂内尔又生出新的一层怕，直往后缩。
萨克埃尔-巴尔攥住弟弟的手腕，那一攥，像一副钢镣。“今晚咱们要真相，”他咬着牙说，“必要的话，拿烧红的铁钳，从你肉里撕出来。”
他把他拖到露台正中，按在罗莎蒙德面前，凭腕上那一攥的蛮力，把他压得跪了下去，跪成一团瑟缩。
“你可知道摩尔人刑罚的花样？”他问他，“在家乡，你或许多少听说过拉肢架、轮子和夹指钳。

===Original===

They are instruments of voluptuous delight compared with the contrivances of Barbary to loosen stubborn tongues.”
White and tense, her hands clenched, Rosamund seemed to stiffen before him.
“You coward! You cur! You craven renegade dog!” she branded him.
Oliver released his brother’s wrist and beat his hands together. Without heeding Rosamund he looked down upon Lionel, who cowered shuddering at his feet.
“What do you say to a match between your fingers? Or do you think a pair of bracelets of living fire would answer better, to begin with?”
A squat, sandy-bearded, turbaned fellow, rolling slightly in his gait, came﻿—as had been prearranged﻿—to answer the corsair’s summons.
With the toe of his slipper Sakr-el-Bahr stirred his brother.
“Look up, dog,” he bade him. “Consider me that man, and see if you know him again. Look at him, I say!” And Lionel looked, yet since clearly he did so without recognition his brother explained: “His name among Christians was Jasper Leigh. He was the skipper you bribed to carry me into Barbary. He was taken in his own toils when his ship was sunk by Spaniards. Later he fell into my power, and because I forebore from hanging him he is today my faithful follower. I should bid him tell you what he knows,” he continued, turning to Rosamund, “if I thought you would believe his tale. But since I am assured you would not, I will take other means.” He swung round to Jasper again. “Bid Ali heat me a pair of steel manacles in a brazier and hold them in readiness against my need of them.” And he waved his hand.
Jasper bowed and vanished.
“The bracelets shall coax confession from your own lips, my brother.”
“I have naught to confess,” protested Lionel. “You may force lies from me with your ruffianly tortures.”
Oliver smiled. “Not a doubt but that lies will flow from you more readily than truth. But we shall have truth, too, in the end, never doubt it.” He was mocking, and there was a subtle purpose underlying his mockery. “And you shall tell a full story,” he continued, “in all its details, so that Mistress Rosamund’s last doubt shall vanish. You shall tell her how you lay in wait for him that evening in Godolphin Park; how you took him unawares, and.﻿ ﻿…”
“That is false!” cried Lionel in a passion of sincerity that brought him to his feet.
