同巴巴里撬顽固舌头的那些家什比起来，它们都算温柔乡了。”
罗莎蒙德绷得白而紧，双手攥死，像在他面前僵住了。
“你这懦夫！你这恶狗！你这下作的叛教畜生！”她给他烙下这几个字。
奥利弗撒开弟弟的手腕，击了两下掌。他不理会罗莎蒙德，低头看着蜷在他脚边打颤的莱昂内尔。
“手指头里来根点着的火柴，你说如何？还是先来一对活火的手镯，更对你胃口？”
一个矮墩墩、沙色胡、缠头巾的家伙，走路微微摇晃着，应声而来——这一场，原是他预先排好的。
萨克埃尔-巴尔拿便鞋尖拨了拨弟弟。
“抬头，狗，”他吩咐，“把我想成那个人，看你认不认得。瞧他，我说！”莱昂内尔瞧了，可显然没认出来，他哥哥便解说：“他在基督徒那边的名字，叫贾斯珀·利。就是你出钱雇他、把我运进巴巴里的那个船长。后来他的船叫西班牙人打沉，他自己落进了自家的网。再后来他落到我手里；我没吊死他，所以他今天是我忠心的部下。”他转向罗莎蒙德，续道：“我本该叫他把他知道的讲给你听，若是我当你肯信。可我算准了你不肯，便另换法子。”他又转向贾斯珀。“叫阿里给我把一副钢镣在火盆里烧上，备着，等我随时要用。”说罢一挥手。
贾斯珀一躬，去了。
“那副镯子，会哄得你自己招供，我的弟弟。”
“我没什么可招的，”莱昂内尔抗议，“你那些恶刑，逼出来的只能是谎。”
奥利弗一笑。“毫无疑问，谎比真话流得快。可真话咱们最后也要拿到手，这个你不用疑。”他是在嘲弄，那嘲弄底下埋着一层细致的用意。“你要讲一个全乎的故事，”他接着说，“一枝一叶都讲到，好叫罗莎蒙德小姐最后一个疑影也散了。你要讲给她听：那天傍晚你怎样在戈多尔芬园子里埋伏他；怎样趁他不备下的手，还有……”
“那是假的！”莱昂内尔喊，那股真诚的激愤把他带了起来。

===Original===

It was false, indeed, and Oliver knew it, and deliberately had recourse to falsehood, using it as a fulcrum upon which to lever out the truth. He was cunning as all the fiends, and never perhaps did he better manifest his cunning.
“False?” he cried with scorn. “Come, now, be reasonable. The truth, ere torture sucks it out of you. Reflect that I know all﻿—exactly as you told it me. How was it, now? Lurking behind a bush you sprang upon him unawares and ran him through before he could so much as lay a hand to his sword, and so.﻿ ﻿…”
“The lie of that is proven by the very facts themselves,” was the furious interruption. A subtle judge of tones might have realized that here was truth indeed, angry indignant truth that compelled conviction. “His sword lay beside him when they found him.”
But Oliver was loftily disdainful. “Do I not know? Yourself you drew it after you had slain him.”
The taunt performed its deadly work. For just one instant Lionel was carried off his feet by the luxury of his genuine indignation, and in that one instant he was lost.
“As God’s my witness, that is false!” he cried wildly. “And you know it. I fought him fair.﻿ ﻿…” He checked on a long, shuddering, indrawn breath that was horrible to hear.
Then silence followed, all three remaining motionless as statues: Rosamund white and tense, Oliver grim and sardonic, Lionel limp, and overwhelmed by the consciousness of how he had been lured into self-betrayal.
At last it was Rosamund who spoke, and her voice shook and shifted from key to key despite her strained attempt to keep it level.
“What﻿ ﻿… what did you say, Lionel?” she asked.
Oliver laughed softly. “He was about to add proof of his statement, I think,” he jeered. “He was about to mention the wound he took in that fight, which left those tracks in the snow, thus to prove that I lied﻿—as indeed I did﻿—when I said that he took Peter unawares.
“Lionel!” she cried. She advanced a step and made as if to hold out her arms to him, then let them fall again beside her. He stood stricken, answering nothing. “Lionel!” she cried again, her voice growing suddenly shrill. “Is this true?”
“Did you not hear him say it?” quoth Oliver.
She stood swaying a moment, looking at Lionel, her white face distorted into a mask of unutterable pain. Oliver stepped towards her, ready to support her, fearing that she was about to fall.

===Chinese===

那的确是假的，奥利弗知道；他是故意借假，拿它当撬棍，把真话撬出来。他狡诈得赛过群魔，而他的狡诈，也许从没有比这回更显过身手。
“假的？”他轻蔑地喊，“来，放明白些。趁刑罚还没把它吸出来，先吐真的。想想我知道底细——正是你亲口告诉我的那一套。是怎么说的来着？你埋伏在树丛后头，趁他不备猛扑上去，他还来不及摸剑，你就把他捅了个透心凉，于是……”
“这话的假，事实本身就戳穿了，”是怒气冲冲的抢白。善听声气的人本可以听出来：这一句是实打实的真话，是又怒又愤、叫人不能不信的真话。“他们找到他的时候，他的剑就躺在身边。”
可奥利弗高高地不屑。“我岂不知道？是你杀了他之后，自己把剑给他抽出来的。”
这一记讥讽，干成了它歹毒的活。只一瞬，莱昂内尔叫他那份真愤慨抬了轿，就在那一瞬，他完了。
“上帝作证，那是假的！”他狂喊，“你自己也知道！我是公平同他交的手……”他在一声长长的、发抖的、可怕之极的吸气上收住了。
随后是寂静，三个人都像石像一样不动：罗莎蒙德白而绷紧；奥利弗冷峻而讥诮；莱昂内尔瘫软着，被“自己是怎么叫人钓得自供”的那份自知，压得抬不起头。
末了开口的是罗莎蒙德，她的声音抖着，变着调，尽管她竭力要它放平。
“你……你方才说什么，莱昂内尔？”她问。
奥利弗轻轻笑了。“我猜他正要给他的话补上凭据，”他嘲弄道，“他正要提他那一战里带的那处伤——那落雪地上留脚印的那处——好证明我方才说他趁人不备，是撒谎；那一节我确实撒了。
“莱昂内尔！”她喊。她抢上一步，抬臂像要朝他张开的模样，又让两臂落回身边。他呆立着，一言不答。“莱昂内尔！”她又喊，嗓音陡然拔尖，“这是真的么？”
“你不是听见他自己说了么？”奥利弗道。
她摇摇地立了一阵，望着莱昂内尔，白脸上绞出一副说不出的痛。奥利弗跨向她，预备扶她，怕她要倒。

===Original===

But with an imperious hand she checked his advance, and by a supreme effort controlled her weakness. Yet her knees shook under her, refusing their office. She sank down upon the divan and covered her face with her hands.
“God pity me!” she moaned, and sat huddled there, shaken with sobs.
Lionel started at that heartbroken cry. Cowering, he approached her, and Oliver, grim and sardonic, stood back, a spectator of the scene he had precipitated. He knew that given rope Lionel would enmesh himself still further. There must be explanations that would damn him utterly. Oliver was well content to look on.
“Rosamund!” came Lionel’s piteous cry. “Rose! Have mercy! Listen ere you judge me. Listen lest you misjudge me!”
“Ay, listen to him,” Oliver flung in, with his soft hateful laugh. “Listen to him. I doubt he’ll be vastly entertaining.”
That sneer was a spur to the wretched Lionel. “Rosamund, all that he has told you of it is false. I﻿ ﻿… I﻿ ﻿… It was done in self-defence. It is a lie that I took him unawares.” His words came wildly now. “We had quarrelled about﻿ ﻿… about﻿ ﻿… a certain matter, and as the devil would have it we met that evening in Godolphin Park, he and I. He taunted me; he struck me, and finally he drew upon me and forced me to draw that I might defend my life. That is the truth. I swear to you here on my knees in the sight of Heaven! And.﻿ ﻿…”
“Enough, sir! Enough!” she broke in, controlling herself to check these protests that but heightened her disgust.
“Nay, hear me yet, I implore you; that knowing all you may be merciful in your judgment.”
“Merciful?” she cried, and almost seemed to laugh.
“It was an accident that I slew him,” Lionel raved on. “I never meant it. I never meant to do more than ward and preserve my life. But when swords are crossed more may happen than a man intends. I take God to witness that his death was an accident resulting from his own fury.”
She had checked her sobs, and she considered him now with eyes that were hard and terrible.
“Was it also an accident that you left me and all the world in the belief that the deed was your brother’s?” she asked him.
He covered his face, as if unable to endure her glance. “Did you but know how I loved you﻿—even in those days, in secret﻿—you would perhaps pity me a little,” he whimpered.
“Pity?” She leaned forward and seemed to spit the word at him. “ ’Sdeath, man!

===Chinese===

可她一只专断的手止住了他，凭一记绝大的努力，把那阵虚弱压了下去。只是膝盖在裙下打着颤，不肯当差了。她跌坐在软榻上，双手掩住脸。
“上帝怜我！”她呜咽，蜷在那里，抽噎一阵一阵撼着她。
莱昂内尔被这声断肠的哭喊惊起。他缩着，蹭到她跟前；奥利弗冷峻而讥诮地退在一旁，看着他亲手掀起的这一幕。他知道，绳子放得够长，莱昂内尔自会把自己缠得更死。后面必还有解释，一桩一桩把他钉死。奥利弗乐得看着。
“罗莎蒙德！”传来莱昂内尔凄惨的呼喊。“罗丝！发发慈悲！先听我说，再论断我。听了，免得你论断错了！”
“是啊，听他说，”奥利弗插进他那软绵绵、可恨的笑，“听他说。我疑心他会热闹得很。”
这句挖苦，鞭子似的抽在那可怜虫身上。“罗莎蒙德，他讲的那一切全是假的。我……我……那是自卫。说我趁他不备，是谎。”他的话此刻说得上气不接下气。“我们吵了一场，为的是……为一桩……一桩事；偏赶巧，那天傍晚我和他在戈多尔芬园子里撞上了。他讥我、打我，末了他拔了剑，逼得我也拔剑保命。这是实情。我跪在这里、当着上天的面对你起誓！还有……”
“够了，先生！够了！”她打断，强自按住，不让这些辩白再翻搅她的恶心。
“不，再听我一句，求你；知道了全部，你论断起来才下得了慈悲。”
“慈悲？”她喊，几乎要笑出来。
“我杀他是失手，”莱昂内尔乱嚷着往下说，“我从来没有那个心。我一心只想招架、只想保命。可刀剑相交，出头的事往往过了人的本心。我请上帝作证：他的死是一场意外，是他自己的狂怒招的。”
她收住了抽噎，此刻拿一双又硬又可怕的眼睛端详他。
“叫我和全世界都当你哥哥是凶手，也是失手么？”她问他。
他捂住脸，像受不住她的逼视。“你若知道我怎样爱你——连那些日子都在暗里爱——你也许还会可怜我一分的。”他呜咽道。
“可怜？”她探身向前，像要把这个字啐到他脸上。“该死的，男人！

===Original===

Do you sue for pity﻿—you?”
“Yet you must pity me did you know the greatness of the temptation to which I succumbed.”
“I know the greatness of your infamy, of your falseness, of your cowardice, of your baseness. Oh!”
He stretched out suppliant hands to her; there were tears now in his eyes. “Of your charity, Rosamund.﻿ ﻿…” he was beginning, when at last Oliver intervened:
“I think you are wearying the lady,” he said, and stirred him with his foot. “Relate to us instead some more of your astounding accidents. They are more diverting. Elucidate the accident, by which you had me kidnapped to be sold into slavery. Tell us of the accident by which you succeeded to my property. Expound to the full the accidental circumstances of which throughout you have been the unfortunate victim. Come, man, ply your wits. ’Twill make a pretty tale.”
And then came Jasper to announce that Ali waited with the brazier and the heated manacles.
“They are no longer needed,” said Oliver. “Take this slave hence with you. Bid Ali to take charge of him, and at dawn to see him chained to one of the oars of my *galeasse*. Away with him.”
Lionel rose to his feet, his face ashen. “Wait! Ah, wait! Rosamund!” he cried.
Oliver caught him by the nape of his neck, spun him round, and flung him into the arms of Jasper. “Take him away!” he growled, and Jasper took the wretch by the shoulders and urged him out, leaving Rosamund and Oliver alone with the truth under the stars of Barbary.

讨可怜的——凭你也配？”
“可你若知道降服我的那诱惑有多大，你就必得可怜我了。”
“我知道的是你的无耻有多大、你的虚伪有多大、你的怯懦和下贱有多大。哦！”
他朝她伸出哀恳的双手，眼里这时也有了泪。“行行好，罗莎蒙德……”他刚起了个头，奥利弗终于插了进来：
“我看你把小姐磨乏了，”他说，拿脚尖拨了拨他，“不如再给咱们讲讲你那些了不得的‘意外’。那个更助兴。讲讲你雇人绑了我去卖作奴隶的那场意外；讲讲你继承我家产的那场意外；把你一以贯之的那副受害者的倒霉，前前后后摆摆清楚。来呀，伙计，动动脑子。保管是篇妙文。”
这时贾斯珀进来禀报：阿里同火盆、烧好的铁镣，都在外头候着了。
“用不着了，”奥利弗说，“把这奴隶带走。交给阿里看管；天一亮，锁上我桨帆船的一支桨。带走。”
莱昂内尔站起身，脸灰得像死。“等等！啊，等等！罗莎蒙德！”他喊。
奥利弗攥住他的后颈，一把拧转，把他甩进贾斯珀怀里。“带走！”他低喝。贾斯珀抓住那可怜虫的双肩，把他搡了出去；露台上，只剩罗莎蒙德和奥利弗，同巴巴里星空下的真相待在一处。

## XV The Voyage / 航程
===Original===

His resolve being taken, Asad drew Tsamanni aside and spent some moments in talk with him, giving him certain instructions for the conduct of affairs ashore during his absence. That done, and the *wazeer* dismissed, the Basha himself gave the order to cast off, an order which there was no reason to delay, since all was now in readiness.
The gangway was drawn ashore, the boatswains whistle sounded, and the steersmen leapt to their niches in the stern, grasping the shafts of the great steering-oars. A second blast rang out, and down the gangway-deck came Vigitello and two of his mates, all three armed with long whips of bullock hide, shouting to the slaves to make ready. And then, on the note of a third blast of Larocque’s whistle, the fifty-four poised oars dipped to the water, two hundred and fifty bodies bent as one, and when they heaved themselves upright again the great *galeasse* shot forward and so set out upon her adventurous voyage. From her mainmast the red flag with its green crescent was unfurled to the breeze, and from the crowded mole, and the beach where a long line of spectators had gathered, there burst a great cry of valediction.
That breeze blowing stiffly from the desert was Lionel’s friend that day. Without it his career at the oar might have been short indeed. He was chained, like the rest, stark naked, save for a loincloth, in the place nearest the gangway on the first starboard bench abaft the narrow waist deck, and ere the *galeasse* had made the short distance between the mole and the island at the end of it, the boatswain’s whip had coiled itself about his white shoulders to urge him to better exertion than he was putting forth. He had screamed under the cruel cut, but none had heeded him. Lest the punishment should be repeated, he had thrown all his weight into the next strokes of the oar, until by the time the *Peñon* was reached the sweat was running down his body and his heart was thudding against his ribs. It was not possible that it could have lasted, and his main agony lay in that he realized it, and saw himself face to face with horrors inconceivable that must await the exhaustion of his strength. He was not naturally robust, and he had led a soft and pampered life that was very far from equipping him for such a test as this.

===Chinese===

主意既定，阿萨德把察曼尼拉到一旁，同他密谈了片刻，交代了几条他出海期间岸上事务的方略。交割完毕，维齐尔退下，巴沙亲自下令解缆——也没有理由再拖，万事早已齐备。
跳板收上岸，水手长唿哨一响，舵手们跃入船尾的舵龛，攥住大舵桨的桨杆。第二声哨响起，维吉特洛带着两名伙计从舷道甲板走下来，三个都提着牛皮长鞭，喝叫奴隶们预备。随后，拉罗克第三声哨的尾音上，五十四支平悬的桨一齐入水，二百五十条身子如一地俯下去；待他们再直起身来，那条大桨帆船已经射了出去，就此踏上她这场冒险的航程。主桅上，绿新月红旗迎风展开；摩肩接踵的防波堤上、围成长排看客的滩头，爆发出一片响亮的送别。
那阵从沙漠吹来的硬风，那天成了莱昂内尔的朋友。没有它，他桨上的生涯只怕短得很。他同众人一样锁着，赤条条只余一条缠腰布，位子在右舷头一排、紧挨舷道、窄窄的船腰甲板后头；桨帆船从防波堤到堤头小岛这短短一段路还没走完，水手长的鞭子已经在他白生生的肩头上盘过一回，催他拿出比眼下更卖力的卖力。那一记狠抽之下他尖叫了，可没有谁理会。为免那顿鞭子再来，接下来几桨他压上了全身的分量；待船到“佩尼翁”，汗水已经顺着身子淌，心口擂着肋骨直撞。这样的力气断不能持久，而他最深的苦正于此：他明白这一点，眼睁睁看着自己直面着种种无法想象的恐怖——它们正候着他力气枯竭的那一刻。他天生不算强壮，又过惯了娇养软和的日子，离撑过这样一场考验，差得太远。

===Original===

But as they reached the *Peñon* and felt the full vigour of that warm breeze, Sakr-el-Bahr, who by Asad’s command remained in charge of the navigation, ordered the unfurling of the enormous lateen sails on main and foremasts. They ballooned out, swelling to the wind, and the *galeasse* surged forward at a speed that was more than doubled. The order to cease rowing followed, and the slaves were left to return thanks to Heaven for their respite, and to rest in their chains until such time as their sinews should be required again.
The vessel’s vast prow, which ended in a steel ram and was armed with a culverin on either quarter, was crowded with lounging corsairs, who took their ease there until the time to engage should be upon them. They leaned on the high bulwarks or squatted in groups, talking, laughing, some of them tailoring and repairing garments, others burnishing their weapons or their armour, and one swarthy youth there was who thrummed a *gimbri* and sang a melancholy Shilha love-song to the delight of a score or so of bloodthirsty ruffians squatting about him in a ring of variegated colour.
The gorgeous poop was fitted with a spacious cabin, to which admission was gained by two archways curtained with stout silken tapestries upon whose deep red ground the crescent was wrought in brilliant green. Above the cabin stood the three cressets or stern lamps, great structures of gilded iron surmounted each by the orb and crescent. As if to continue the cabin forward and increase its size, a green awning was erected from it to shade almost half the poop deck. Here cushions were thrown, and upon these squatted now Asad-ed-Din with Marzak, whilst Biskaine and some three or four other officers who had escorted him aboard and whom he had retained beside him for that voyage, were lounging upon the gilded balustrade at the poop’s forward end, immediately above the rowers’ benches.
Sakr-el-Bahr alone, a solitary figure, resplendent in caftan and turban that were of cloth of silver, leaned upon the bulwarks of the larboard quarter of the poop deck, and looked moodily back upon the receding city of Algiers which by now was no more than an agglomeration of white cubes piled up the hillside in the morning sunshine.
Asad watched him silently awhile from under his beetling brows, then summoned him. He came at once, and stood respectfully before his prince.

===Chinese===

可一到“佩尼翁”，吃足了那阵暖风的劲，奉阿萨德之命仍掌着航务的萨克埃尔-巴尔，便下令张开主桅前桅上那两张巨大的斜挂大帆。帆鼓成了球，迎风涨满，桨帆船猛地添了一倍多的速度。停桨令跟着下来，奴隶们得空谢一谢上天的缓刑，戴着镣铐歇下，直歇到他们的筋腱再被需要的时候。
船头那高大的冲角顶着钢嘴，两舷各装一门长管炮，此刻懒洋洋坐满了海盗，养精蓄锐，专候接战的时辰。他们或倚着高栏，或成堆蹲坐，说笑打诨；有的缝补衣裳，有的擦拭兵刃甲胄；还有一个黝黑的少年，弹着吉他拉琴，唱一支希勒哈语的相思小调，叫围成五色一圈、蹲坐四周的二十来个嗜血的匪徒听得心花怒放。
华美的艉楼上设着一间宽敞的舱房，两道拱门进出，门上挂着厚实的丝织挂毯，深红的底子上，翠绿的新月闪闪。舱房顶上立着三座火盆——也叫艉灯——鎏金铁架的大家伙，各各顶着一颗宝球和新月。仿佛要把舱房往前续、把它扩大似的，一道绿天棚从舱前张开，遮了艉楼甲板的小半。棚下四散扔着坐垫，此刻坐在上面的，是阿萨德-丁和马尔扎克；比斯坎和随船陪同的三四名军官，倚在艉楼前端的鎏金栏上——正底下便是桨手的条凳。
唯有萨克埃尔-巴尔独自一人，一身银锦的长袍和缠头，光彩照人，凭倚在艉楼左舷后侧的栏边，郁郁地回望那座正在退远的阿尔及尔城——到这时，它已经只是晨光里沿山坡堆叠起来的一堆白方盒了。
阿萨德从浓眉底下默默望了他一阵，随后唤他。他立刻过来，恭恭敬敬立在主上面前。

===Original===

Asad considered him a moment solemnly, whilst a furtive malicious smile played over the beautiful countenance of his son.
“Think not, Sakr-el-Bahr,” he said at length, “that I bear thee resentment for what befell last night or that that happening is the sole cause of my present determination. I had a duty﻿—a long-neglected duty﻿—to Marzak, which at last I have undertaken to perform.” He seemed to excuse himself almost, and Marzak misliked both words and tone. Why, he wondered, must this fierce old man, who had made his name a terror throughout Christendom, be ever so soft and yielding where that stalwart and arrogant infidel was concerned?
Sakr-el-Bahr bowed solemnly. “My lord,” he said, “it is not for me to question thy resolves or the thoughts that may have led to them. It suffices me to know thy wishes; they are my law.”
“Are they so?” said Asad tartly. “Thy deeds will scarce bear out thy protestations.” He sighed. “Sorely was I wounded yesternight when thy marriage thwarted me and placed that Frankish maid beyond my reach. Yet I respect this marriage of thine, as all Muslims must﻿—for all that in itself it was unlawful. But there!” he ended with a shrug. “We sail together once again to crush the Spaniard. Let no ill will on either side o’er-cloud the splendour of our task.”
“*Ameen* to that, my lord,” said Sakr-el-Bahr devoutly. “I almost feared.﻿ ﻿…”
“No more!” the Basha interrupted him. “Thou wert never a man to fear anything, which is why I have loved thee as a son.”
But it suited Marzak not at all that the matter should be thus dismissed, that it should conclude upon a note of weakening from his father, upon what indeed amounted to a speech of reconciliation. Before Sakr-el-Bahr could make answer he had cut in to set him a question laden with wicked intent.
“How will thy bride beguile the season of thine absence, O Sakr-el-Bahr?”
“I have lived too little with women to be able to give thee an answer,” said the corsair.
Marzak winced before a reply that seemed to reflect upon himself. But he returned to the attack.
“I compassionate thee that art the slave of duty, driven so soon to abandon the delight of her soft arms. Where hast thou bestowed her, O captain?”
“Where should a Muslim bestow his wife but according to the biddings of the Prophet﻿—in the house?”
Marzak sneered. “Verily, I marvel at thy fortitude in quitting her so soon!”

===Chinese===

阿萨德庄严地把他端详了片刻，他儿子那张俊脸上，掠过一丝鬼祟的歹笑。
“你别以为，萨克埃尔-巴尔，”他终于说，“我记恨昨夜的事，也别以为那桩事是我今日这决定的唯一因由。我对马尔扎克有一份本分——一份荒疏已久的本分——如今我总算着手来尽了。”他几乎像是在自辩；马尔扎克对这话、这口气都不受用。他纳闷：这个把名字变成基督世界一声惊雷的凶悍老者，怎的偏偏在那个倨傲的异教壮汉面前，总是这样软、这样让？
萨克埃尔-巴尔庄重地一躬。“主上，”他说，“您的决断、决断背后的心思，都轮不到我来置喙。知悉您的意愿于我足矣；它就是我的法。”
“是么？”阿萨德辛辣地说，“你的行止可撑不起你的表白。”他叹了口气。“昨夜你的婚事拦了我、把那法兰克姑娘挪出我手边，我是狠狠挨了一刀。可我尊重你这桩婚事，凡是穆斯林都得尊重——尽管它本身就于法不合。罢了！”他耸耸肩，收了话。“咱们又同船出海，去碾碎西班牙人。谁也别叫心头的那点怨，罩了咱们这桩大业的彩头。”
“阿敏，愿如此，主上。”萨克埃尔-巴尔虔敬地说，“我险些怕了……”
“不必再说！”巴沙截住他。“你从来不是个怕事的人，正为这个，我一直爱你如子。”
可事情这样收场，落在马尔扎克眼里全不对味：居然以他父亲的一步软话收梢，简直就是一篇修好的致辞了。不等萨克埃尔-巴尔回话，他便抢进来，把一个包着祸心的问句递了过去。
“你这位新娘子，要怎样打发你不在的那些时日呢，萨克埃尔-巴尔？”
“我跟女人过的日子太少，答不上你这个。”海盗说。
马尔扎克在这一句仿佛扫着他自己的回答前缩了一下。可他卷土重来。
“我可怜你，做本分的奴隶，这么快就撇下她那温柔臂膀里的快活。你把她安置在哪儿了，船长？”
“一个穆斯林安置他的妻子，还能安置在哪儿？照先知的吩咐——在家里。”
马尔扎克冷笑。“说真的，你这样快就离了她，你这忍功叫我叹服！”

===Original===

But Asad caught the sneer, and stared at his son. “What cause is there to marvel in that a true Muslim should sacrifice his inclinations to the service of the Faith?” His tone was a rebuke; but it left Marzak undismayed. The youth sprawled gracefully upon his cushions, one leg tucked under him.
“Place no excess of faith in appearances, O my father!” he said.
“No more!” growled the Basha. “Peace to thy tongue, Marzak, and may Allah the All-knowing smile upon our expedition, lending strength to our arms to smite the infidel to whom the fragrance of the garden is forbidden.”
To this again Sakr-el-Bahr replied “*Ameen*,” but an uneasiness abode in his heart summoned thither by the questions Marzak had set him. Were they idle words calculated to do no more than plague him, and to keep fresh in Asad’s mind the memory of Rosamund, or were they based upon some actual knowledge?
His fears were to be quickened soon on that same score. He was leaning that afternoon upon the rail, idly observing the doling out of the rations to the slaves, when Marzak came to join him.
For some moments he stood silently beside Sakr-el-Bahr watching Vigitello and his men as they passed from bench to bench serving out biscuits and dried dates to the rowers﻿—but sparingly, for oars move sluggishly when stomachs are too well nourished﻿—and giving each to drink a cup of vinegar and water in which floated a few drops of added oil.
Then he pointed to a large palmetto bale that stood on the waist deck near the mainmast about which the powder barrels were stacked.
“That pannier,” he said, “seems to me oddly in the way yonder. Were it not better to bestow it in the hold, where it will cease to be an encumbrance in case of action?”
Sakr-el-Bahr experienced a slight tightening at the heart. He knew that Marzak had heard him command that bale to be borne into the poop cabin, and that anon he had ordered it to be fetched thence when Asad had announced his intention of sailing with him. He realized that this in itself might be a suspicious circumstance; or, rather, knowing what the bale contained, he was too ready to fear suspicion. Nevertheless he turned to Marzak with a smile of some disdain.
“I understood, Marzak, that thou art sailing with us as apprentice.”
“What then?” quoth Marzak.
“Why merely that it might become thee better to be content to observe and learn.

===Chinese===

可阿萨德捉住了那声冷笑，瞪住他儿子。“一个真穆斯林为服侍正教、牺牲一己之所好，这有什么可叹服的？”他的语气是申斥；马尔扎克却并不气馁。少年懒洋洋摊在坐垫上，一条腿收在身下。
“外表上的事，父亲大人，可别信得太过！”他说。
“够了！”巴沙低吼，“舌头安分些，马尔扎克。愿全知的安拉对我们这趟出征微笑，添咱们臂膀的力气，好痛击那些不配闻园中芬芳的异教徒。”
对这一句，萨克埃尔-巴尔又应了一声“阿敏”；可马尔扎克那几个问题，已经在他心里唤起一份不安，赖着不走了。那些是闲话么——只打算磨磨他、只打算把罗莎蒙德的影子在阿萨德心里焐热？还是底下垫着一些实在的知情？
在这个题目上，他的怕很快又要添柴。当天下午，他正倚着栏，闲看给奴隶分口粮，马尔扎克过来了。
他在萨克埃尔-巴尔身边默默站了片刻，看着维吉特洛和他的人挨着条凳走，给桨手发饼干和干枣——发得抠，肚子喂得太饱，桨就走得懒——又给每人一杯掺水的醋，面上浮着几滴添的油。
随后他一指主桅旁船腰甲板上立着的那只大棕榈席包——火药桶正围着它码了一圈。
“那只驮篮，”他说，“我看它搁在那儿碍事碍得古怪。收进底舱去不好么？真接了仗，它也不当道。”
萨克埃尔-巴尔的心微微一紧。他知道马尔扎克听见过他吩咐把那只包抬进艉楼舱，也知道后来阿萨德一宣布随船，他又吩咐把它从那儿抬了出来。他明白，单这一节本身就惹疑；或者说，正因为知道那包里装的是什么，他太容易疑心生暗鬼。可他仍旧转向马尔扎克，带着几分不屑一笑。
“我记得，马尔扎克，你这趟随船，是当学徒来的。”
“那又怎样？”马尔扎克说。
“不怎么，只是：安分看着、学着，更配你的身份。

===Original===

Thou’lt soon be telling me how grapnels should be slung, and how an action should be fought.” Then he pointed ahead to what seemed to be no more than a low cloud-bank towards which they were rapidly skimming before that friendly wind. “Yonder,” he said, “are the Balearics. We are making good speed.”
Although he said it without any object other than that of turning the conversation, yet the fact itself was sufficiently remarkable to be worth a comment. Whether rowed by her two hundred and fifty slaves, or sailed under her enormous spread of canvas, there was no swifter vessel upon the Mediterranean than the *galeasse* of Sakr-el-Bahr. Onward she leapt now with bellying lateens, her well-greased keel slipping through the wind-whipped water at a rate which perhaps could not have been bettered by any ship that sailed.
“If this wind holds we shall be under the Point of Aguila before sunset, which will be something to boast of hereafter,” he promised.
Marzak, however, seemed but indifferently interested; his eyes continued awhile to stray towards that palmetto bale by the mainmast. At length, without another word to Sakr-el-Bahr, he made his way abaft, and flung himself down under the awning, beside his father. Asad sat there in a moody abstraction, already regretting that he should have lent an ear to Fenzileh to the extent of coming upon this voyage, and assured by now that at least there was no cause to mistrust Sakr-el-Bahr. Marsak came to revive that drooping mistrust. But the moment was ill-chosen, and at the first words he uttered on the subject, he was growled into silence by his sire.
“Thou dost but voice thine own malice,” Asad rebuked him. “And I am proven a fool in that I have permitted the malice of others to urge me in this matter. No more, I say.”
Thereupon Marzak fell silent and sulking, his eyes ever following Sakr-el-Bahr, who had descended the three steps from the poop to the gangway and was pacing slowly down between the rowers’ benches.
The corsair was supremely ill at ease, as a man must be who has something to conceal, and who begins to fear that he may have been betrayed. Yet who was there could have betrayed him? But three men aboard that vessel knew his secret﻿—Ali, his lieutenant, Jasper, and the Italian Vigitello.

回头你就要教我抓钩该怎样甩、仗该怎样打了。”说着他朝前方一指——那看着不过一带低云的地方，船正乘着这阵顺风，飞快朝它掠去。“那边，”他说，“是巴利阿里群岛。咱们船速不错。”
他这话本只为岔开话头，可这事实本身就值得说道说道。无论靠二百五十名奴隶划，还是靠那一大展帆走，地中海再没有一条船快过萨克埃尔-巴尔的桨帆船。此刻她鼓着大斜帆一路飞跃，抹足了油的龙骨，从风鞭打过的水面上滑过去，那份快，天下的船怕是没一条追得上。
“这风若撑得住，日落前咱们就到鹰角外了，往后也够夸口的。”他许诺道。
马尔扎克却兴味寥寥；他的眼睛还朝主桅边那只棕榈席包溜了一阵。末了，也不同萨克埃尔-巴尔再说什么，他径自往后走，一歪身坐进天棚下他父亲身旁。阿萨德郁郁地坐在那里出神，已经在后悔不该听芬齐莱听到登船这一步，而且到这时已经认定：至少眼下，没有理由疑萨克埃尔-巴尔。马尔扎克来，正是要救活那份蔫下去的疑。可他挑的时机不巧，这个题目上他才起了个头，就叫他老子一声低吼轰哑了。
“你嘴里跑的，全是你自家的恶意，”阿萨德训他，“而我已证明是个傻子——竟叫旁人的恶意撺掇到这一步。够了，我说。”
马尔扎克于是哑了、蔫了，眼睛却一直跟着萨克埃尔-巴尔——那人已下了艉楼的三级台阶到舷道，正沿着桨凳之间缓缓踱下去。
这位海盗心里极不塌实，凡藏着事、又开始疑心自己叫人卖了的人，都难免这样。可谁卖得了他呢？这条船上知道他秘密的，拢共三人：他的副手阿里、贾斯珀，还有那个意大利人维吉特洛。

===Original===

And Sakr-el-Bahr would have staked all his possessions that neither Ali nor Vigitello would have betrayed him, whilst he was fairly confident that in his own interests Jasper also must have kept faith. Yet Marzak’s allusion to that palmetto bale had filled him with an uneasiness that sent him now in quest of his Italian boatswain whom he trusted above all others.
“Vigitello,” said he, “is it possible that I have been betrayed to the Basha?”
Vigitello looked up sharply at the question, then smiled with confidence. They were standing alone by the bulwarks on the waist deck.
“Touching what we carry yonder?” quoth he, his glance shifting to the bale. “Impossible. If Asad had knowledge he would have betrayed it before we left Algiers, or else he would never have sailed without a stouter bodyguard of his own.
“What need of bodyguard for him?” returned Sakr-el-Bahr. “If it should come to grips between us﻿—as well it may if what I suspect be true﻿—there is no doubt as to the side upon which the corsairs would range themselves.”
“Is there not?” quoth Vigitello, a smile upon his swarthy face. “Be not so sure. These men have most of them followed thee into a score of fights. To them thou art the Basha, their natural leader.”
“Maybe. But their allegiance belongs to Asad-ed-Din, the exalted of Allah. Did it come to a choice between us, their faith would urge them to stand beside him in spite of any past bonds that may have existed between them and me.”
“Yet there were some who murmured when thou wert superseded in the command of this expedition,” Vigitello informed him. “I doubt not that many would be influenced by their faith, but many would stand by thee against the Grand Sultan himself. And do not forget,” he added, instinctively lowering his voice, “that many of us are renegadoes like myself and thee, who would never know a moment’s doubt if it came to a choice of sides. But I hope,” he ended in another tone, “there is no such danger here.”
“And so do I, in all faith,” replied Sakr-el-Bahr, with fervour. “Yet I am uneasy, and I must know where I stand if the worst takes place. Go thou amongst the men, Vigitello, and probe their real feelings, gauge their humour and endeavour to ascertain upon what numbers I may count if I have to declare war upon Asad or if he declares it upon me. Be cautious.”
Vigitello closed one of his black eyes portentously.

阿里和维吉特洛会卖他——他情愿押上全部家当打赌不会；贾斯珀出于自身利害，也必守口，这个他也有八分把握。可马尔扎克那一提那只棕榈席包，已经灌了他一腔的不安，此刻把他催着去找他那意大利水手长——船上他最信的人。
“维吉特洛，”他说，“我有没有可能，已经叫人卖到巴沙耳朵里了？”
维吉特洛闻声猛抬头，随即含着把握一笑。两人单独站在船腰甲板的栏边。
“为咱们那边载着的那件东西？”他说，眼睛朝那只包一溜。“不可能。阿萨德若知情，出阿尔及尔之前就点破了；再不然，他绝不会不带一支更硬的贴身卫队出海。
“他哪用得着卫队？”萨克埃尔-巴尔回道，“真到你我掰腕子的地步——我若疑得实，多半要到那地步——海盗们站哪边，没有半点悬念。”
“没有么？”维吉特洛说，黝黑的脸上带笑，“别这样笃定。这些人多半跟着你打过二十来仗。在他们眼里，你才是巴沙，天生的头领。”
“也许。可他们的忠，属于阿萨德-丁，安拉所扬的那位。真到你我二选一，他们的信仰会拽他们站到他那边，不管他们同我从前有过什么香火情。”
“可你被褫了这趟的帅印时，也有人嘀咕来着，”维吉特洛告诉他，“我毫不疑心，很多人会叫信仰牵着走；可也有很多人，站在你这边，大苏丹亲来也照站。别忘了，”他压低嗓门添了一句，近乎本能，“咱们里头像你我这样的叛教者不少，真到选边，我们半秒钟都不会含糊。不过我希望，”他换了口气收尾，“这里没有那样的凶险。”
“我也希望，千真万确地希望。”萨克埃尔-巴尔回得恳切。“可我不安生，我得知道万一走到最坏，我立在什么地方。你到弟兄们中间走一遭，维吉特洛，探探他们的真心，掂掂他们的心气，设法摸清：倘使我得对阿萨德开战，或他对我开战，我数得上几个数。放仔细些。”
维吉特洛煞有介事地闭了一只黑眼睛。

===Original===

“Depend upon it,” he said, “I’ll bring you word anon.”
On that they parted, Vigitello to make his way to the prow and there engage in his investigations, Sakr-el-Bahr slowly to retrace his steps to the poop. But at the first bench abaft the gangway he paused, and looked down at the dejected, white-fleshed slave who sat shackled there. He smiled cruelly, his own anxieties forgotten in the savour of vengeance.
“So you have tasted the whip already,” he said in English. “But that is nothing to what is yet to come. You are in luck that there is a wind today. It will not always be so. Soon shall you learn what it was that I endured by your contriving.”
Lionel looked up at him with haggard, blood-injected eyes. He wanted to curse his brother, yet was he too overwhelmed by the sense of the fitness of this punishment.
“For myself I care nothing,” he replied.
“But you will, sweet brother,” was the answer. “You will care for yourself most damnably and pity yourself most poignantly. I speak from experience. ’Tis odds you will not live, and that is my chief regret. I would you had my thews to keep you alive in this floating hell.”
“I tell you I care nothing for myself,” Lionel insisted. “What have you done with Rosamund?”
“Will it surprise you to learn that I have played the gentleman and married her?” Oliver mocked him.
“Married her?” his brother gasped, blenching at the very thought. “You hound!”
“Why abuse me? Could I have done more?” And with a laugh he sauntered on, leaving Lionel to writhe there with the torment of his half-knowledge.
An hour later, when the cloudy outline of the Balearic Isles had acquired density and colour, Sakr-el-Bahr and Vigitello met again on the waist deck, and they exchanged some few words in passing.
“It is difficult to say exactly,” the boatswain murmured, “but from what I gather I think the odds would be very evenly balanced, and it were rash in thee to precipitate a quarrel.”
“I am not like to do so,” replied Sakr-el-Bahr. “I should not be like to do so in any case. I but desired to know how I stand in case a quarrel should be forced upon me.” And he passed on.
Yet his uneasiness was no whit allayed; his difficulties were very far from solved.

“放心，”他说，“回头我就给你回话。”
两人在此分手：维吉特洛往船头去办他的差；萨克埃尔-巴尔慢慢往艉楼折返。可走到舷道后第一张条凳，他停了步，低头看那个锁在那儿的、白肉垂头丧气的奴隶。他残忍地笑了——复仇的滋味里，自家的焦虑暂且忘了。
“这么快就尝过鞭子了，”他用英语说，“可这比起后头的，什么都不是。今天有风，算你走运。不会总有风的。很快你就晓得，你那手安排叫我受的是哪一种。”
莱昂内尔抬起头，一双血丝密布的枯眼望着他。他想咒这条哥哥，却又被“这惩罚来得正是地方”的感觉压得咒不出口。
“我自己怎么样，我不在乎。”他答。
“你会在乎的，好弟弟，”回答道，“你会把自己在乎得要命，把自己可怜得锥心。我这话是拿亲身经验说的。多半你活不下来，这是我最大的遗憾。但愿你有我这副筋骨，好撑着活过这座浮起的地狱。”
“我告诉你，我不在乎自己，”莱昂内尔还说，“你把罗莎蒙德怎么了？”
“听说我行了个绅士的体面、把她娶了，你吃惊么？”奥利弗嘲他。
“娶了她？”他弟弟倒抽一口气，一念及此，脸都白了，“你这恶狗！”
“骂我做什么？我难道还能做得更周到？”说罢他一笑，信步走了，撇下莱昂内尔在那半知半晓的煎熬里扭着。
一个钟头后，巴利阿里群岛那团云影有了分量、有了颜色，萨克埃尔-巴尔同维吉特洛在船腰甲板上又碰了头，擦肩换了几句。
“实数难说，”水手长咕哝道，“可依我摸的，两边怕是半斤八两；你先挑起争端，是莽撞。”
“我也不想挑，”萨克埃尔-巴尔答道，“本来就不想。我不过要弄清：万一一桩争端强按到我头上，我立在哪里。”说罢他走了过去。
可他的不安分毫未减；他的难处，离解开还远得很。

===Original===

He had undertaken to carry Rosamund to France or Italy; he had pledged her his word to land her upon one or the other shore, and should he fail, she might even come to conclude that such had never been his real intention. Yet how was he to succeed, now, since Asad was aboard the *galeasse*? Must he be constrained to carry her back to Algiers as secretly as he had brought her thence, and to keep her there until another opportunity of setting her ashore upon a Christian country should present itself? That was clearly impracticable and fraught with too much risk of detection. Indeed, the risk of detection was very imminent now. At any moment her presence in that pannier might be betrayed. He could think of no way in which to redeem his pledged word. He could but wait and hope, trusting to his luck and to some opportunity which it was impossible to foresee.
And so for a long hour and more he paced there moodily to and fro, his hands clasped behind him, his turbaned head bowed in thought, his heart very heavy within him. He was taken in the toils of the evil web which he had spun; and it seemed very clear to him now that nothing short of his life itself would be demanded as the price of it. That, however, was the least part of his concern. All things had miscarried with him and his life was wrecked. If at the price of it he could ensure safety to Rosamund, that price he would gladly pay. But his dismay and uneasiness all sprang from his inability to discover a way of achieving that most desired of objects even at such a sacrifice. And so he paced on alone and very lonely, waiting and praying for a miracle.

===Chinese===

他许过罗莎蒙德送她去法国或意大利；他立过约，要把她送上这一岸或那一岸。倘若办不到，她说不定竟会认定：那从来就不是他的真心。可如今，阿萨德就在这条桨帆船上，他还怎样办得到？难道要他像把她偷运出来那样，再把她偷偷运回阿尔及尔，藏在那里，等下一个把她送上基督徒国土的机会？那分明行不通，被撞破的风险太大。说实在的，被撞破的风险眼下已经迫在眉睫：她在那只驮篮里的事，随时可能叫人捅出去。他想不出一个法子来赎回他许下的诺言。他只能等、只能盼，把自己的运气、把一个无法预见的机缘，托付给天。
就这样，一个多钟头，他郁郁地在那里来回踱着，双手背在身后，缠着头巾的头低垂着想心事，心口沉得很。他落进了自己织的那张毒网的网眼里；此刻他看得清清楚楚：这张网赎身的价码，少说也是他这条命本身。然而，那是他最不在意的一节。他事事失手，这条命早已翻了船。倘若拿它换得罗莎蒙德的平安，这个价他出得心甘情愿。可他的惶恐和不安，全生自这一处：连这样的牺牲，他也寻不出一条路，去换那件他最想要的东西。于是他独自一人、孤单得很，一路踱下去，等着一场奇迹，求着一场奇迹。

## VII Trepanned / 诱拐
===Original===

Master Lionel was absent most of the following day from Penarrow, upon a pretext of making certain purchases in Truro. It would be half-past seven when he returned; and as he entered he met Sir Oliver in the hall.
“I have a message for you from Godolphin Court,” he announced, and saw his brother stiffen and his face change colour. “A boy met me at the gates and bade me tell you that Mistress Rosamund desires a word with you forthwith.”
Sir Oliver’s heart almost stopped, then went off at a gallop. She asked for him! She had softened perhaps from her yesterday’s relentlessness. She would consent at last to see him!
“Be thou blessed for these good tidings!” he answered on a note of high excitement. “I go at once.” And on the instant he departed. Such was his eagerness, indeed, that under the hot spur of it he did not even stay to fetch that parchment which was to be his unanswerable advocate. The omission was momentous.
Master Lionel said no word as his brother swept out. He shrank back a little into the shadows. He was white to the lips and felt as he would stifle. As the door closed he moved suddenly. He sprang to follow Sir Oliver. Conscience cried out to him that he could not do this thing. But Fear was swift to answer that outcry. Unless he permitted what was planned to take its course, his life might pay the penalty.
He turned, and lurched into the dining room upon legs that trembled.
He found the table set for supper as on that other night when he had staggered in with a wound in his side to be cared for and sheltered by Sir Oliver. He did not approach the table; he crossed to the fire, and sat down there holding out his hands to the blaze. He was very cold and could not still his trembling. His very teeth chattered.
Nicholas came in to know if he would sup. He answered unsteadily that despite the lateness of the hour he would await Sir Oliver’s return.
“Is Sir Oliver abroad?” quoth the servant in surprise.
“He went out a moment since, I know not whither,” replied Lionel. “But since he has not supped he is not like to be long absent.”
Upon that he dismissed the servant, and sat huddled there, a prey to mental tortures which were not to be repressed. His mind would turn upon naught but the steadfast, unwavering affection of which Sir Oliver ever had been prodigal towards him.

===Chinese===

第二天，莱昂内尔少爷大半日不在佩纳罗，托词去特鲁罗采买些东西。他回来时该是七点半；一进门，便在大厅撞见奥利弗爵士。
“我给你捎来戈多尔芬宅的一个口信，”他宣布道，眼看哥哥浑身一僵、变了脸色。“一个孩子在门口拦我，叫我告诉你：罗莎蒙德小姐有话，即刻要见你。”
奥利弗爵士的心几乎停了一拍，随即狂奔起来。她找他！她那昨日还硬到底的心肠，兴许软了。她总算肯见他一面了！
“报这个喜信的，愿上天赐福你！”他答，声音里透着高亢的兴奋。“我这就去。”话音未落人已动身。他心急到这个地步：那股热辣辣的鞭子抽着，连那张羊皮文书都没顾上取——那本该是他无往不利的辩护士。这一漏，干系重大。
哥哥大步出门，莱昂内尔少爷一言不发。他朝阴影里缩了缩。他白到嘴唇，胸口闷得像要憋死。门一关上，他猛地一动，弹起来要去追奥利弗爵士。良心朝他呐喊：这事做不得。可恐惧应声就来，盖过了那声呐喊：放任那盘算照计而行，不然，赔的可能是他的命。
他转过身，拖着两条打颤的腿，一步一晃挪进餐厅。
餐桌照旧摆着晚饭，同那一夜一模一样——那一夜，他捂着腰上的伤摇摇晃晃进来，由奥利弗爵士照料、庇护。他没有近桌，径直挪到炉火边坐下，朝火伸出双手。他冷得很，抖个不住，牙齿都在磕碰。
尼古拉斯进来问他用不用饭。他答得七颠八倒：时辰虽晚，他要等奥利弗爵士回来。
“奥利弗爵士出门了？”仆人诧异道。
“方才出去的，去哪儿我不知道，”莱昂内尔答，“可他晚饭还没吃，想必去不了多久。”
说罢他打发走仆人，自己蜷在那里，任心头那压不下去的酷刑撕扯。他的念头翻来覆去，只在一样东西上打转：奥利弗爵士对他，从来挥霍不完的那份恒久不动摇的手足之情。

===Original===

In this very matter of Peter Godolphin’s death, what sacrifices had not Sir Oliver made to shield him? From so much love and self-sacrifice in the past he inclined to argue now that not even in extreme peril would his brother betray him. And then that bad streak of fear which made a villain of him reminded him that to argue thus was to argue upon supposition, that it would be perilous to trust such an assumption; that if, after all, Sir Oliver should fail him in the crucial test, then was he lost indeed.
When all is said, a man’s final judgment of his fellows must be based upon his knowledge of himself; and Lionel, knowing himself incapable of any such sacrifice for Sir Oliver, could not believe Sir Oliver capable of persisting in such a sacrifice as future events might impose. He reverted to those words Sir Oliver had uttered in that very room two nights ago, and more firmly than ever he concluded that they could have but one meaning.
Then came doubt, and, finally, assurance of another sort, assurance that this was not so and that he knew it; assurance that he lied to himself, seeking to condone the thing he did. He took his head in his hands and groaned loud. He was a villain, a black-hearted, soulless villain! He reviled himself again. There came a moment when he rose shuddering, resolved even in this eleventh hour to go after his brother and save him from the doom that awaited him out yonder in the night.
But again that resolve was withered by the breath of selfish fear. Limply he resumed his seat, and his thoughts took a fresh turn. They considered now those matters which had engaged them on that day when Sir Oliver had ridden to Arwenack to claim satisfaction of Sir John Killigrew. He realized again that Oliver being removed, what he now enjoyed by his brother’s bounty he would enjoy henceforth in his own unquestioned right. The reflection brought him a certain consolation. If he must suffer for his villainy, at least there would be compensations.
The clock over the stables chimed the hour of eight. Master Lionel shrank back in his chair at the sound. The thing would be doing even now. In his mind he saw it all﻿—saw his brother come running in his eagerness to the gates of Godolphin Court, and then dark forms resolve themselves from the surrounding darkness and fall silently upon him.

===Chinese===

单说彼得·戈多尔芬这条命案，奥利弗爵士为护他，什么没舍过？念着旧日这许多的爱和舍己，他此刻倒倾向于这样推断：即便到了绝境，哥哥也不会卖他。可他那根把人变成恶棍的怕的毒筋，又来提醒他：这样推断，是在拿假设当凭据，把宝押在这上头，凶险；万一到了那最要命的一关，奥利弗爵士到底没能撑住，那他就真的完了。
说到底，一个人对旁人的终审，只能拿他对自己的认识打底；莱昂内尔自知：换成是他，绝不肯为奥利弗爵士舍这么多。于是他便不信奥利弗爵士肯把这样的牺牲一路撑到底——往后的事态要他舍多少，是个无底洞。他又想起两夜前奥利弗爵士就在这间屋里说的那些话；比先前更硬地认定：那些话只有一个意思。
跟着来了疑心；再跟着，来了另一种“笃定”——笃定事情并不是这样、笃定他自己心里明白；笃定他在骗自己，好给他正做的事开脱。他抱住头，放声呻吟。他是个恶棍，一个黑心烂肺、没有魂的恶棍！他又骂起自己来。有一刻，他哆嗦着站起身，哪怕到了这最后关头，也要去追哥哥，把他从外头黑夜里等着他的那场劫数里救出来。
可那份自私的怕，一口气又把这点决心吹枯了。他软软地坐回去，念头转了个新方向，转回奥利弗爵士骑马去阿文纳克找约翰·基利格鲁爵士讨公道那天，占据过它的那些内容。他重新掂量出：奥利弗一去，他如今靠哥哥的慷慨享用着的一切，往后就名正言顺、无人置喙地归他自己了。这个念头给了他几分安慰。罪是要遭报的，可至少，有贴补。
马厩那头的钟敲了八点。莱昂内尔少爷闻声朝椅背里一缩。这时候，那件事正在办了。他心里看得一清二楚——看见哥哥满心欢喜地跑到戈多尔芬宅门口，看见黑影从四下的黑暗里凝出人形，无声无息地扑上去。

===Original===

He saw him struggling a moment on the ground, then, bound hand and foot, a gag thrust into his mouth, he beheld him in fancy borne swiftly down the slope to the beach and so to the waiting boat.
Another half-hour sat he there. The thing was done by now, and this assurance seemed to quiet him a little.
Then came Nicholas again to babble of some possible mischance having overtaken his master.
“What mischance should have overtaken him?” growled Lionel, as if in scorn of the idea.
“I pray none indeed,” replied the servant. “But Sir Oliver lacks not for enemies nowadays, and ’tis scarce zafe for he to be abroad after dark.”
Master Lionel dismissed the notion contemptuously. For pretence’s sake he announced that he would wait no longer, whereupon Nicholas brought in his supper, and left him again to go and linger about the door, looking out into the night and listening for his master’s return. He paid a visit to the stables, and knew that Sir Oliver had gone forth afoot.
Meanwhile Master Lionel must make pretence of eating though actual eating must have choked him. He smeared his platter, broke food, and avidly drank a bumper of claret. Then he, too, feigned a growing anxiety and went to join Nicholas. Thus they spent the weary night, watching for the return of one who Master Lionel knew would return no more.
At dawn they roused the servants and sent them to scour the countryside and put the news of Sir Oliver’s disappearance abroad. Lionel himself rode out to Arwenack to ask Sir John Killigrew bluntly if he knew aught of this matter.
Sir John showed a startled face, but swore readily enough that he had not so much as seen Sir Oliver for days. He was gentle with Lionel, whom he liked, as everybody liked him. The lad was so mild and kindly in his ways, so vastly different from his arrogant overbearing brother, that his virtues shone the more brightly by that contrast.
“I confess it is natural you should come to me,” said Sir John. “But, my word on it, I have no knowledge of him. It is not my way to beset my enemies in the dark.”
“Indeed, indeed, Sir John, I had not supposed it in my heart,” replied the afflicted Lionel. “Forgive me that I should have come to ask a question so unworthy. Set it down to my distracted state. I have not been the same man these months, I think, since that happening in Godolphin Park. The thing has preyed upon my mind.

看见他在地上挣了一阵，随后被捆了手脚、嘴里塞了布团；他想象中看见他被飞快抬下坡去，抬到滩头，抬上那条候着的船。
他又干坐了半个钟头。到这时，事已办完；这份笃定倒叫他稍许平静了些。
随后尼古拉斯又来了，唠叨着主人只怕遭了什么意外。
“能遭什么意外？”莱昂内尔哼道，像在讥这个念头。
“俺也求着别有，”仆人答道，“可奥利弗爵士如今仇人不少，天黑了还在外头，总归不大稳当。”
莱昂内尔少爷不屑地把这念头挥开。为了做戏，他宣称不再等了；尼古拉斯便端进他的晚饭，又退出去守在门边，朝夜色里张望，竖着耳朵听主人的动静。他到马厩看了一遭，知道奥利弗爵士是步行出去的。
这厢，莱昂内尔少爷只得做吃的样子，真吃是咽不下去的。他把盘子抹乱，掰碎些吃食，又急急灌下一大杯红酒。随后他也装出越等越焦的模样，去同尼古拉斯作伴。两人就这样熬着这难熬的长夜，等一个莱昂内尔少爷明知再不会回来的人回来。
天一亮，他们叫起全宅仆人，打发他们搜遍乡野，把奥利弗爵士失踪的消息传扬出去。莱昂内尔自己骑马去了阿文纳克，直截了当问约翰·基利格鲁爵士：这事他知道不知道。
约翰爵士露出吃惊的脸，却起誓起得很痛快：连着好几天，他连奥利弗爵士的影子都没见过。他对莱昂内尔很和气——他喜欢这少年，人人都喜欢他。这少年做派温良和善，同他那位倨傲霸道的哥哥天差地远；两相一衬，他的美德愈发亮得晃眼。
“不瞒你说，你来问我，也是自然，”约翰爵士道，“可我拿话担保：我毫不知情。在黑地里埋伏仇家，不是我的路数。”
“可不是，可不是，约翰爵士，我心里也断没有这样想，”受难的莱昂内尔答，“原谅我竟来问了这样一句不体面的话。都算在我心绪迷乱头上吧。打戈多尔芬园子里出了那桩事，这几个月我怕是换了个人了。这事在我心里啃。

===Original===

It is a fearsome burden to know your own brother﻿—though I thank God he is no more than my half-brother﻿—guilty of so foul a deed.”
“How?” cried Killigrew, amazed. “You say that? You believed it yourself?”
Master Lionel looked confused, a look which Sir John entirely misunderstood and interpreted entirely in the young man’s favour. And it was thus and in that moment that was sown the generous seed of the friendship that was to spring up between these two men, its roots fertilized by Sir John’s pity that one so gentle-natured, so honest, and so upright should be cursed with so villainous a brother.
“I see, I see,” he said. And he sighed. “You know that we are daily expecting an order from the Queen to her Justices to take the action which hitherto they have refused against your﻿ ﻿… against Sir Oliver.” He frowned thoughtfully. “D’ye think Sir Oliver had news of this?”
At once Master Lionel saw the drift of what was in the other’s mind.
“I know it,” he replied. “Myself I bore it him. But why do you ask?”
“Does it not help us perhaps to understand and explain Sir Oliver’s disappearance? God lack! Surely, knowing that, he were a fool to have tarried here, for he would hang beyond all doubt did he stay for the coming of her grace’s messenger.”
“My God!” said Lionel, staring. “You﻿ ﻿… you think he is fled, then?”
Sir John shrugged. “What else is to be thought?”
Lionel hung his head. “What else, indeed?” said he, and took his leave like a man overwrought, as indeed he was. He had never considered that so obvious a conclusion must follow upon his work so fully to explain the happening and to set at rest any doubt concerning it.
He returned to Penarrow, and bluntly told Nicholas what Sir John suspected and what he feared himself must be the true reason of Sir Oliver’s disappearance. The servant, however, was none so easy to convince.
“But do ee believe that he done it?” cried Nicholas. “Do ee believe it, Master Lionel?” There was reproach amounting to horror in the servant’s voice.
“God help me, what else can I believe now that he is fled.”
Nicholas sidled up to him with tightened lips. He set two gnarled fingers on the young man’s arm.
“He’m not fled, Master Lionel,” he announced with grim impressiveness. “He’m never a turntail. Sir Oliver he don’t fear neither man nor devil, and if so be him had killed Master Godolphin, he’d never ha’ denied it.

===Chinese===

眼看着自己的哥哥——感谢上帝，好歹只是异母的哥哥——背着这样一桩肮脏的罪，这份担子，压得人怕。”
“怎么？”基利格鲁惊呼，“你这话？连你自己都信了？”
莱昂内尔少爷露出惶惑之色；约翰爵士全然会错了意，往对这少年大大有利的方向解了去。正是在那一刻，播下了两人后来那段深交的宽厚的种子；滋养它根须的，是约翰爵士的怜惜：这样一位温和、诚实、端正的人，偏偏摊上那样一个恶棍哥哥。
“我明白，我明白，”他说，叹了口气。“你知道，我们天天盼着女王下旨给治安官们，叫他们办这件他们至今不肯办的事——办你那位……办奥利弗爵士。”他若有所思地皱起眉。“你说，奥利弗爵士会不会得了这个信？”
莱昂内尔少爷立时就看见了对方心里那道流向。
“我知道这个，”他答，“是我亲自捎给他的。可你为什么问这个？”
“这不正好帮咱们理解、解释奥利弗爵士的失踪么？老天！知道了这个还赖着不走的，那才是傻子；他若留下等女王陛下的使者来，铁定上绞架。”
“我的上帝！”莱昂内尔瞪着眼说，“你……你是想，他跑了？”
约翰爵士耸耸肩。“不然还能怎么想？”
莱昂内尔垂下头。“不然还能怎么想，可不是。”他说着告辞，一副心力交瘁的样子——他倒真没全装。他从来没有想过：他那件活计之后，这样一个明摆着的结论必会跟上来，把事情解释得妥妥帖帖，把一切疑心安顿得服服帖帖。
他回到佩纳罗，把约翰爵士的猜疑、连同他自己怕是实情的那层猜疑，直统统告诉了尼古拉斯。可那仆人没那么好说服。
“可您当真信是他干的？”尼古拉斯喊，“您当真信么，莱昂内尔少爷？”仆人的话音里，那责备几乎成了惊骇。
“上帝帮帮我，他都跑了，如今我还能怎么想。”
尼古拉斯抿紧嘴唇，侧身凑近，两根虬结的手指搭上少年的胳膊。
“他没跑，莱昂内尔少爷，”他板着脸、一字一顿地宣布，“他绝不是那号夹尾巴的东西。奥利弗爵士天不怕地不怕；他要是真杀了戈多尔芬少爷，他死也不会赖账。

===Original===

Don’t ee believe Sir John Killigrew. Sir John ever hated he.”
But in all that countryside the servant was the only one to hold this view. If a doubt had lingered anywhere of Sir Oliver’s guilt, that doubt was now dispelled by this flight of his before the approach of the expected orders from the Queen.
Later that day came Captain Leigh to Penarrow inquiring for Sir Oliver.
Nicholas brought word of his presence and his inquiry to Master Lionel, who bade him be admitted.
The thickset little seaman rolled in on his bowed legs, and leered at his employer when they were alone.
“He’s snug and safe aboard,” he announced. “The thing were done as clean as peeling an apple, and as quiet.”
“Why did you ask for him?” quoth Master Lionel.
“Why?” Jasper leered again. “My business was with him. There was some talk between us of him going a voyage with me. I’ve heard the gossip over at Smithick. This will fit in with it.” He laid that finger of his to his nose. “Trust me to help a sound tale along. ’Twere a clumsy business to come here asking for you, sir. Ye’ll know now how to account for my visit.”
Lionel paid him the price agreed and dismissed him upon receiving the assurance that the *Swallow* would put to sea upon the next tide.
When it became known that Sir Oliver had been in treaty with Master Leigh for a passage overseas, and that it was but on that account that Master Leigh had tarried in that haven, even Nicholas began to doubt.
Gradually Lionel recovered his tranquillity as the days flowed on. What was done was done, and, in any case, being now beyond recall, there was no profit in repining. He never knew how fortune aided him, as fortune will sometimes aid a villain. The royal pour-suivants arrived some six days later, and Master Baine was the recipient of a curt summons to render himself to London, there to account for his breach of trust in having refused to perform his sworn duty. Had Sir Andrew Flack but survived the chill that had carried him off a month ago, Master Justice Baine would have made short work of the accusation lodged against him. As it was, when he urged the positive knowledge he possessed, and told them how he had made the examination to which Sir Oliver had voluntarily submitted, his single word carried no slightest conviction.

您别听约翰·基利格鲁爵士的。约翰爵士向来恨他。”
可在那一带乡里，持这个看法的，只有这仆人一个。倘使对奥利弗爵士的罪还残存着一丝疑，他赶在女王那道众人盼着的谕令之前的一逃，也把那丝疑吹得干干净净。
当天晚些时候，利船长到佩纳罗来，找奥利弗爵士。
尼古拉斯把他的来意禀给莱昂内尔少爷，少爷吩咐放他进来。
那个矮墩墩的水手迈着两条弯腿滚了进来，同雇主单独相对时，斜眼一笑。
“他在船上，稳稳当当，”他宣布，“活计办得干净，削苹果似的，还没个响。”
“那你找我哥做什么？”莱昂内尔少爷问。
“做什么？”贾斯珀又斜眼一笑，“我的买卖本来就在他身上。我们俩先头谈过他随我出海一趟的事。史密尼克那边的闲话我也听见了。这正好接上。”他把那根手指往鼻子上一搭。“编圆一个故事，看我的。直接上这儿来找您，那才叫笨呢，先生。往后我这趟来访，您就知道该怎么交代了。”
莱昂内尔付清讲好的价，得了“燕子号”下个潮水就开船的保票，打发他走了。
待到风声传开——奥利弗爵士原来同利少爷接洽过渡海的事，利少爷逗留那处港湾也正为这个——连尼古拉斯也开始动摇了。
日子一天天淌过去，莱昂内尔渐渐回了神。做下的已经做下了；横竖收不回来，悔也无益。他从不晓得命运怎样帮了他——命运有时候就是偏帮恶棍。王家的缉捕官约六天后到了，贝恩少爷接了一纸冷冰冰的传召，命他赴伦敦，为他不肯履行宣誓职责的失职做出交代。倘若安德鲁·弗拉克爵士没有一个月前死于那场风寒，贝恩治安官本可以把泼向他罪状三言两语打发掉。如今呢，他申说握有确凿的知情、讲明他验看经过（奥利弗爵士是自愿受验的）时，他孤身一言，没有半分人信。

===Original===

Not for a moment was it supposed that this was aught but the subterfuge of one who had been lax in his duty and who sought to save himself from the consequences of that laxity. And the fact that he cited as his fellow-witness a gentleman now deceased but served to confirm his judges in this opinion. He was deposed from his office and subjected to a heavy fine, and there the matter ended, for the hue-and-cry that was afoot entirely failed to discover any trace of the missing Sir Oliver.
For Master Lionel a new existence set in from that day. Looked upon as one in danger of suffering for his brother’s sins, the countryside determined to help him as far as possible to bear his burden. Great stress was laid upon the fact that after all he was no more than Sir Oliver’s half-brother; some there were who would have carried their kindness to the lengths of suggesting that perhaps he was not even that, and that it was but natural that Ralph Tressilian’s second wife should have repaid her husband in kind for his outrageous infidelities. This movement of sympathy was led by Sir John Killigrew, and it spread in so rapid and marked a manner that very soon Master Lionel was almost persuaded that it was no more than he deserved, and he began to sun himself in the favour of a countryside that hitherto had shown little but hostility for men of the Tressilian blood.

===Chinese===

众人一口咬定：这不过是一个渎职者自保的花招，想逃脱渎职的后果。他引为同证的偏偏又是一位已故的绅士，这只会叫他的法官们愈发认定如此。他被削了职，罚了一大笔款，事情就此了结；至于四处张开的缉捕网，到底也没捞着失踪的奥利弗爵士半根毫毛。
对莱昂内尔少爷来说，从那天起，一重新日子开始了。乡里把他当作一个险些替哥哥的罪受过的人，打定了主意，要尽着力帮他挑这副担子。人们重重地强调：他到底不过是奥利弗爵士的异母弟弟；更有几个，好心一路使到底，索性暗示：只怕连这个也不是——拉尔夫·特雷西利安那样无法无天地偷汉，续弦太太礼尚往来、还他一报，也是天经地义。这股同情的潮，由约翰·基利格鲁爵士领着头，涨得又快又显；不多时，莱昂内尔少爷几乎被哄得信了：这原是他应得的待遇。他开始舒舒服服晒起乡里的恩宠来——对特雷西利安家血脉，那地方先前是向来没什么好脸色的。
